The proportion of permutations fixing a k k -set
Ben Green, Mehtaab Sawhney

Abstract
. Denote by p ⁡ ( k ) p(k) the limit, as n → ∞ n\rightarrow\infty , of the probability that a random permutation on a set of size n n has an invariant set of size k k . We give an asymptotic formula for p ⁡ ( k ) p(k) , showing that it is asymptotically f ( { log 2 k } ) k − δ ( log k ) − 3 / 2 f(\{\log_{2}k\})k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2} where δ = 1 − 1 + log ⁡ log ⁡ 2 log ⁡ 2 ≈ 0.086 \delta=1-\frac{1+\log\log 2}{\log 2}\approx 0.086 and f f is a smooth, positive, function on ℝ / ℤ \mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z} , which we will describe explicitly. The function f f satisfies max ⁡ f min ⁡ f < 1 + 2 × 10 − 7 \frac{\max f}{\min f}<1+2\times 10^{-7} and we conjecture that it is not constant. Estimating p ⁡ ( k ) p(k) is a model for the more well-known question which asks for an estimation of M ⁡ ( n ) M(n) , the number of distinct elements in the n n -by- n n multiplication table. By elaborating on the techniques in this paper, we will give an asymptotic for M ⁡ ( n ) M(n) in forthcoming work.



Part I
Introduction and setup

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main result and history

The well-known multiplication table problem of Erdős
[
17
,
16
]
asks for an asymptotic for
M(n)
, the number of elements in the
n
-by-
n
multiplication table. In forthcoming work
[
27
]
we offer a solution to this problem.

It is well-known that (at least in certain regimes) both the logs of the prime factors of a random integer and the cycle lengths of a random permutation have Poisson behaviour. A consequence of this is an analogy between certain problems about the divisors of typical integers (which the estimation of
M(n)
can easily be reduced to) and problems about invariant sets of random permutations. In our forthcoming paper we will go into much greater detail on this connection; the introduction to
[
15
]
may also be consulted. The objective of the present work is to fully explore the (limiting version of) the permutation analogue of the multiplication table problem, which we now describe.

Denote by
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
the symmetric group of permutations on
n
letters. Let
i(n,k)
denote the probability that
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
has an invariant set of size
k
. Let
p(k)=\lim_{n\to\infty}i(n,k)
; this limit exists, as we will recall below, and we have (for example)
p(1)=1-\frac{1}{e}
and
p(2)=1-2e^{-3/2}
.

Our main interest in this paper will be the estimation of
p(k)
. We believe this is of interest in its own right, and indeed several previous works such as
[
14
,
15
,
41
]
are devoted to the problem. However, our main rationale is that the methods we develop are relatively clean when it comes to estimating
p(k)
, but will adapt to solve the more well-known (but more technical) question of estimating
M(n)
. Large parts of the present paper can be reused almost verbatim for the latter question.

The following is our main theorem. Let

(1.1)

\delta=1-\frac{1+\log\log 2}{\log 2}

be the “Erdős–Ford–Tenenbaum constant”. In this theorem, and throughout the paper, we write
\log_{2}k
for log to base 2 (
not

\log\log k
as one occasionally sees in analytic number theory). We also write
\{x\}
for the fractional part of a real number
x
, that is to say
0\leqslant\{x\}<1
and
x-\{x\}\in\mathbb{Z}
; we have
\{x\}=x-\lfloor x\rfloor
where the
\lfloor x\rfloor
is the greatest integer less than or equal to
x
.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, nowhere zero, such that

p(k)=(1+o(1))f(\{\log_{2}k\})k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}.

The function
f
can be expressed as
c_{0}g\ast\mu\ast\mu^{\prime}
, where
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is the function defined by

(1.2)

g(x):=\sum_{D\in\mathbb{Z}}(\log 2)^{D-x}(1-e^{-2^{D-x}}),

c_{0}=(\frac{\pi}{2})^{1/2}(\log 2)^{3/2}e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}
, and
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
are two nonzero, positive Borel measures
1
1

1

Positive
means that
\mu(E)\geqslant 0
for any measurable set
E
.
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
which we will define in
Propositions

1.3
and
1.4
below. We have

\frac{\max f}{\min f}\leqslant\frac{\max g}{\min g}<1+2\cdot 10^{-7}.

This may be compared with the main result of
[
15
]
where, using the methods of Ford
[
21
]
, it was shown that
k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}\ll p(k)\ll k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}
. It is a slightly odd historical quirk of the subject that this was only worked out
after
Ford proved the strictly more difficult corresponding result for
M(n)
. (That this should be possible was anticipated by Diaconis and Soundararajan, as noted in
[
35
]
.)

We make some remarks on the theorem.

(1)

We presume that
f
is not the constant function, but we have not been able to prove this. We will show that
\widehat{g}(m)\neq 0
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, which means that
f
is constant if and only if
\mu\ast\mu^{\prime}
is the uniform measure. Proving this, or obtaining any numerical simulations of
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
, remains an interesting open problem.

(2)

The most interesting feature of
Theorem

1.1
is arguably the appearance of a periodic function of
\{\log_{2}k\}
. This sort of behaviour has been seen in related problems before, notably in the work of Balazard, Nicolas, Pomerance and Tenenbaum
[
6
]
where they examine the number of
n\leqslant X
with
\tau(n)\geqslant\log X
, where
\tau
is the divisor function. Citing that paper, Brent, Pomerance, Purdum and Webster
[
9
]
speculate that the asymptotic for
M(n)
may have an oscillatory term.
For squarefree
n
, the condition
\tau(n)\geqslant\log X
is the same as
\omega(n)\geqslant\log\log X/\log 2
, where
\omega
denotes the number of prime factors. The asymptotic turns out to be dominated by the contribution of
n
for which
\omega(n)=\log\log X/\log 2+O(1)
. Since
\omega(n)
is constrained to be an integer, the fractional part of
\log\log X/\log 2
starts to have a significant effect on the distribution. The same phenomenon is at play in the present paper, with
\omega(n)
replaced by the number of cycles of the permutation
\pi
of length
\leqslant k
, and the important contribution being from
\pi
with
\log_{2}k+O(1)
such cycles.

(3)

The almost constant nature of
g
seemed extremely surprising and mysterious to us at first sight. One explanation for this, as we shall see below, is that the nonzero Fourier coefficients
\widehat{g}(m)
are given by values of
\Gamma
with imaginary part
2\pi m/\log 2
, and so are very small when
m\neq 0
. Moreover, it transpires that similar phenomena have been observed on a number of occasions before, with the earliest source we are aware of being formula (2.11.2) in Hardy’s explanation
[
28
]
of how Ramanujan failed to prove the prime number theorem. Several further related references are given on
[
19
, p 311]
. As pointed out to us by Soundararajan, the exact same function
g
appears as
\psi_{1}^{*}
in the work of Granville, Sedunova and Sabuncu
[
26
]
; although the contexts are somewhat related, this appears to be at least partially coincidental. Finally, we remark that Sean Eberhard pointed out to us that similar functions can arise in rather basic problems, such as the following question on “binomial thinning”. Suppose we start on day 0 with a population of size
k
. On day
i
, each remaining member of the population is eliminated with probability
1/2
, these events being independent. What is the probability that there is some day on which precisely one person remains? It is not hard to see that the answer tends to
g_{0}(\{\log_{2}k\})
where
g_{0}(t):=\sum_{D\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{D+t}e^{-2^{D+t}}
as
k
grows.

An immediate corollary of
Theorem

1.1
is the following.

Corollary 1.2
.

There is a positive constant
C
such that we have the asymptotic
p(k)=(C+o(1))k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
along powers of two.

Although
Corollary

1.2
is an immediate consequence of
Theorem

1.1
, it is in fact a slightly easier result (we shall see the proof at the end of
Section

14
).

1.2.
The
\varrho
statistics and the measure
\mu

We turn now to a description of the measure
\mu
in the statement of our main result.

Let
u=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of real numbers. In our applications, this will either be the arrival times of a rate 1 Poisson process on
[0,\infty)
or a minor variant of this. Then for each positive integer
\ell
we define

(1.3)

\varrho_{u}(\ell):=2^{u_{\ell}-\ell}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}1\big(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon_{i}2^{-u_{i}}\in[1-2^{-u_{\ell}},1]\big),

where
1(E)
denotes the indicator of the event
E
.
Since we will typically have
u_{i}\approx i
, roughly speaking one expects
\varrho_{u}(\ell)
to have size
\asymp 1
. One should think of the
\varrho_{u}(\ell)
as describing some sort of “density” of the set of subset sums
\sum_{i\in I}2^{-u_{i}}
near
1
.

From now on we fix the
upper process

\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{u}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
to be precisely the sequence of arrival times of a rate 1 Poisson process on
[0,\infty)
. The following is our main result concerning the measure
\mu
. Here, and throughout the paper,
x^{+}:=\max(x,0)
.

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
c:=-\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}
. For every Lipschitz function
\psi\in\operatorname{Lip}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
the measure
\mu
satisfies

(1.4)

\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu:=(2/\pi)^{1/2}\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)),

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}
is over the random process
\mathbf{u}
, and
x^{+}:=\max(x,0)
. We adopt the convention that the integrand is zero when
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)=0
.

In particular, the Fourier coefficients of
\mu
are given by

\widehat{\mu}(r)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)^{c-\frac{2\pi ir}{\log 2}}

for
r\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The existence of these limits is part of the proposition. Moreover, the bounded Lipschitz norm
\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}
is bounded, and
\mu
is not the zero measure.

Remarks
.

For the definition of the bounded Lipschitz norm
\|\cdot\|_{\operatorname{BL}}
of a Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, see
Appendix

E
.

At first sight, it might appear that the limit as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
in
Equation

1.4
diverges. However, this is not the case, essentially since if
\mathbf{u}_{\ell}
is significantly smaller than
\ell
then
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)
is only nonzero with rather small probability; for rigorous instances of this assertion, see
Lemma

7.2
.

A remark of a different nature is that, despite the existence of the above formulæ for
\mu
, obtaining any interesting numerical or computational data about
\mu
seems a very difficult task. This is because for any sequence
u
the determination of
\varrho_{u}(\ell)
is closely related to the subset sum problem and all known algorithms for this have complexity exponential in
\ell
, whilst we expect the rate of convergence in
Equation

1.4
to be only polynomial.

1.3.
The
\tau
statistics and the measure
\mu^{\prime}

Now let
x=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of positive integers. Then for each positive integer
\ell
we define

(1.5)

\tau_{x}(\ell):=2^{-\ell}\#\{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon_{i}x_{i}:\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\}.

That is, the number of
distinct
subset sums
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon_{i}x_{i}
, normalised by the number of such sums. Note in particular that
0\leqslant\tau_{x}(\ell)\leqslant 1
for all
\ell
. It is also not hard to see that
\tau_{x}(\ell)
is a non-increasing function of
\ell
.

Typically we will have
x_{i}\asymp 2^{i}
and so roughly speaking one expects
\tau_{x}(\ell)
to have size
\asymp 1
(that is, the subset sums
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon_{i}x_{i}
have a good chance of being mostly distinct; for comparison, when
x_{i}=2^{i}
exactly, these sums are genuinely distinct).

Define the
lower process

\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
by ordering the elements of a random (multi-)set of positive integers, where the multiplicity of
i
is a
\operatorname{Pois}(1/(i\log 2))
random variable, with these variables being independent. Here is our main result concerning the measure
\mu^{\prime}
.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
c=-\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}
. For every Lipschitz function
\psi\in\operatorname{Lip}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, the measure
\mu^{\prime}
satisfies

\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}:=(2/\pi)^{1/2}\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}(\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)).

In particular the Fourier coefficients of
\mu^{\prime}
are given by

\widehat{\mu^{\prime}}(r)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}(\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)^{c-\frac{2\pi ir}{\log 2}}.

The existence of these limits is part of the proposition. Moreover, the bounded Lipschitz norm
\|\mu^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{BL}}
is bounded, and
\mu^{\prime}
is not the zero measure.

1.5.
Notation

The following pieces of notation will be in force throughout the paper. The letter
k
will always be as in
Theorem

1.1
(that is, we want to compute
p(k)
). We always write

(1.6)

k=2^{n+\xi}

where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\xi\in[0,1)
. That is,
\xi=\{\log_{2}k\}
.

Bold font.
There are many random variables in the paper. We have tried to consistently use bold font to denote these, which hopefully helps avoid some confusion, particularly when various conditionings are in place. To avoid conflict we have used blackboard bold for
\mathbb{N},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{R}
(the naturals, integers and reals) and for
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{E}
(probability and expectation).

Asymptotic notation.
We will use the asymptotic notations
\gg,\ll
and
O(\cdot)
in the standard manner for analytic number theorists. That is,
X=O(Y)
and
X\ll Y
both mean that
|X|\leqslant CY
for some absolute constant
C
(which may change from line to line). If
X,Y
are positive quantities then we write
X\asymp Y
to mean
Y\ll X\ll Y
. We will sometimes use
\Omega(X)
to mean a quantity which is bounded below by
cX
for some absolute constant
c>0
(which may change from line to line). We will use the
o(\cdot)
notation only sparingly;
o(1)
means a quantity which tends to
0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. The notations
\ggg
and
\lll
are informal and mean ‘much greater than’ and ‘much less than’ respectively.

Further notation.
We will use the following conventions and further pieces of notation.

•

Write
H_{X}=\sum_{j\leqslant X}1/j
; here
X
need not necessarily be an integer.

•

1_{S}(x)
and
1_{x\in S}
both mean
1
if
x\in S
and
0
otherwise.

•

If
N
is a positive integer,
[N]=\{1,\dots,N\}
.

•

If
f:[N]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a function (for some
N
) then
\argmin(f)
denotes the first
q
for which
f(q)=\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}f(i)
.

•

If
E_{1},E_{2}
are two events,
\mathbb{P}(E_{1},E_{2})
denotes the probability that both
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
occur. Occasionally in more complicated expressions we will write
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}\cap E_{2})
for the same thing. We sometimes write
\neg E
for the complement of the event
E
.

•

|S|
denotes the cardinality of the set
S
. This notation will also be used for multisets, in which case elements are counted with multiplicity. The notation
\#\{x:\text{conditions on $x$}\}
denotes the cardinality of the set of
x
satisfying the specified conditions.

•

\operatorname{Supp}(f)
denotes the support of the function
f
, that is to say
\{x:f(x)\neq 0\}
.

•

x^{+}
denotes
\max(x,0)
.

•

\mathbf{X}\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(\lambda)
means that
\mathbf{X}
is a random variable with the
\operatorname{Pois}(\lambda)
distribution, that is to say
\mathbb{P}(X=i)=e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{i}/i!
for nonnegative integers
i
. Occasionally we will abuse notation by writing
\operatorname{Pois}(\lambda)
to mean a random variable with the
\operatorname{Pois}(\lambda)
distribution. Most particularly, we will talk about random walks with
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
steps, which means the increments
\bm{\xi}
are distributed as
X-1
with
X\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(1)
. Similarly a walk with
1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)
steps has increments
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}
distributed as
1-X
with
X\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(1)
.

Equations

8.1
and
8.2

\Sigma(S)

set of subset sums of
S

Definition

2.1

2.
Heuristic explanation

The form of our main result is complicated. We offer here a heuristic explanation for the result, which also serves as a rather rough overview of the actual argument. This heuristic makes certain simplifications and should not be taken too literally; a more technical overview may be found in
Section

4
below.

Let us first recall from
[
15
]
that we can immediately pass from the language of permutations to a formulation in terms of a so-called Poisson random multiset. A multiset
S\subset\mathbb{N}
has the usual definition;
r_{S}(i)
denotes the multiplicity of
i
in
S
.

Definition 2.1
.

If
S
is a multiset (finite or infinite), let
s_{1},s_{2},\dots
be some listing of its elements (with multiplicity). We define the set
\Sigma(S)
of subset sums to consist of all sums
\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}s_{i}
with
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}
and all but finitely many
\varepsilon_{i}
equal to
0
.

Remark
.

Note carefully that we always regard
\Sigma(S)
as a
set
and not a multiset.

Now let
\mathbf{A}_{0}\subset\mathbb{N}
denote the random multiset with
r_{\mathbf{A}_{0}}(i)\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(1/i)
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, with these variables being independent. We then have

(2.1)

p(k)=\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})).

This follows from the fact that a permutation
\pi
has an invariant set of size
k
if and only if it has disjoint cycles of lengths summing to
k
, together with the theorem
[
5
]
that the distribution of the number of cycles of length
i
,
i=1,\dots,k
, of a random
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}
tends to that of independent
\operatorname{Pois}(1/i)
random variables in the large
n
limit. We will not mention permutations again, and from now on our focus will be on
Equation

2.1
.

An observation which in some form goes back to Erdős
[
17
]
is that the most important contribution to
p(k)
comes from instances of
\mathbf{A}_{0}
with
|\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|\approx\log_{2}k
, that is to say from sets which are bigger by a factor
\frac{1}{\log 2}
than the expected size. We will explain why this is so in
Section

3
below.

It is tempting to model the relevant sets
\mathbf{A}_{0}
by ‘changing measure’ and using
\operatorname{Pois}(1/(i\log 2))
variables to define a random multiset
\mathbf{A}_{1}
. However, to better capture the large-scale behaviour we use the following variant of this. First we recall the definitions of the upper and lower processes
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x}
from the introduction.

Definition 2.2
.

Define the
upper process

\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{u}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
to be simply the sequence of arrival times of a rate 1 Poisson process on
[0,\infty)
. Define the
lower process

\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
by ordering the elements of a random (multi-)set of positive integers, where the multiplicity of
i
is a
\operatorname{Pois}(1/(i\log 2))
random variable, with these variables being independent.

Now we define our random multiset
\mathbf{A}_{1}
. Pick some
k_{0}
with
1\lll k_{0}\lll k
. For definiteness one could take
k_{0}:=\sqrt{k}
, although the precise choice is unimportant (
k_{0}
does not need to be an integer). The ‘small’ elements of
\mathbf{A}_{1}
are simply the elements
\mathbf{x}_{i}
of the lower process which are less than or equal to
k_{0}
, which we write
\mathbf{x}_{1}\leqslant\mathbf{x}_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\mathbf{x}_{L^{\prime}}
in order (where
L^{\prime}
is the number of such elements, which is itself a random variable). The ‘large’ elements of
\mathbf{A}_{1}
are
\lfloor k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i}}\rfloor
, where
\mathbf{u}_{1}\leqslant\mathbf{u}_{2}\leqslant\dots\leqslant\mathbf{u}_{L}
are the elements of the upper process which are less than
\log_{2}(k/k_{0})
. Again,
L
is a random variable. We remark that the probability that some
i\geqslant k_{0}
lies in this upper portion of
\mathbf{A}_{1}
equals the probability that some element of
\mathbf{u}
lies in the interval
(\log 2)^{-1}\big(\log(k/i)-\log(1+\frac{1}{i}),\log(k/i)\big]
, which is
\frac{1}{i\log 2}+O(i^{-2})
.

One may compute that this change of measure affects probabilities as follows. If
|A|=n+D
then we have

(2.2)

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]=A)\approx e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}k^{-\delta}(\log 2)^{D-\xi}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}_{1}=A),

where
\gamma
is Euler’s constant. (An essentially identical computation to this one is done carefully at the end of
Section

3
.) Thus we see that the desired quantity
p(k)
can be understood with a sufficiently accurate understanding of the probabilities
\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1}),\;|\mathbf{A}_{1}|=n+D)
.

The next observation is (in slightly different language) the key advance of Kevin Ford
[
21
]
, which is to establish that the main contribution is from sets
\mathbf{A}_{1}
for which roughly speaking we have

(2.3)

\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{i}\geqslant i-O(1)

and

(2.4)

\mathbf{u}_{i}\leqslant i+O(1)

for all
i
. Let us briefly explain in rather heuristic terms where this comes from. In what follows we condition to
|\mathbf{A}_{1}|=n+D
with
D=O(1)
; thus

L+L^{\prime}=n+D.

For the discussion, we will otherwise ignore the effect of this conditioning on the distribution of the
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{u}_{i}
variables.

Suppose first that
Equation

2.3
is violated, say
\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{q}\leqslant q-m
for some
m
(which we think of as “not
O(1)
”) and some
q\leqslant L^{\prime}
. Then, since the
\mathbf{x}_{i}
grow roughly dyadically, one expects
\Sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{q})
to be contained in
[0,O(2^{q-m})]
, and hence to have size
\lessapprox 2^{q-m}
. It then follows that

|\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1})|\leqslant|\Sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{q})|\cdot|\Sigma(\mathbf{x}_{q+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L^{\prime}})|\cdot|\Sigma(\mathbf{u}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{L})|\lessapprox 2^{q-m}\cdot 2^{n+D-q}\sim k2^{-m}.

This leads us to expect that

(2.5)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1}))\lessapprox 2^{-m},

or in other words the contribution from those
\mathbf{x}
for which
Equation

2.3
is violated is quite small.

Now suppose that
Equation

2.4
is violated in the sense that
\mathbf{u}_{q}\geqslant q+m
for some
q\leqslant L
. First observe that
k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1})
requires that

(2.6)

\big|k-\sum_{i=1}^{L}\varepsilon_{i}\lfloor k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i}}\rfloor\big|=\sum_{i=1}^{L^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}\lessapprox k_{0}

for some
\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, where the second inequality is what we typically expect since the
\mathbf{x}_{i}
grow dyadically and the largest is
\mathbf{x}_{L^{\prime}}\leqslant k_{0}
.
Now one expects
\sum_{i\geqslant q}\lfloor k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i}}\rfloor\lessapprox k2^{-q-m}
, and so
Equation

2.6
requires

(2.7)

\big|1-\sum_{i=1}^{q-1}\varepsilon_{i}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i}}\big|\lessapprox 2^{-q-m}

for some
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{q-1}\in\{0,1\}
. There are
2^{q-1}
choices for
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i})_{i=1}^{q-1}
, and for each the inequality
Equation

2.7
can typically only happen if
\varepsilon_{i}\neq 0
for some
i=O(1)
, since
\mathbf{u}_{i}\approx i
. For any such
\varepsilon
, by revealing all variables except
\mathbf{u}_{i}
one sees that the probability of
Equation

2.7
is
\lessapprox 2^{-q-m}
. Summing over
\varepsilon
again yields a bound like
Equation

2.5
.

This concludes our discussion of why the important contributions come from
Equations

2.3
and
2.4
; as previously remarked, a rigorous version of this analysis and its implications was one of the key advances of Ford
[
21
]
.

Now we come to the new part of the analysis. The point of departure is that random
\mathbf{A}_{1}
satisfying the properties
Equations

2.3
and
2.4
will in fact (with high probability) robustly satisfy them in the sense that

(2.8)

\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{i}\geqslant i+i^{1/2-\eta}

and

(2.9)

\mathbf{u}_{i}\leqslant i-i^{1/2-\eta}

for large
i
. (Here
\eta
is a small constant, say
\eta=\frac{1}{100}
.)
These statements are closely analogous to the fact that (under mild hypotheses on the increments) a mean zero random walk
\bm{\beta}(1),\bm{\beta}(2),\dots,\bm{\beta}(N)
of length
N
, conditioned to stay positive, will almost surely have
\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant i^{1/2-\eta}
for all large
i
. (In fact we will make extensive use of the detailed theory of random walks conditioned to stay almost positive in our arguments.)

The conditions
Equations

2.8
and
2.9
(or closely related ones) have two important ramifications for us. First, they imply a stability property for the
\varrho
and
\tau
statistics defined in
Equations

1.3
and
1.5
; roughly speaking we have

(2.10)

\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)\approx\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell^{\prime})

for
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\ggg 1
and similarly

(2.11)

\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)\approx\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell^{\prime}).

Heuristically, the explanation for
Equation

2.10
is that the slightly faster-than-
2^{i}
growth of the
\mathbf{x}_{i}
means that new coincidences
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}
become unlikely as
\ell
increases. A similar heuristic can be provided for
Equation

2.11
.
We caution that
Equations

2.10
and
2.11
should be considered only as heuristics; the actual statements, established in
Sections

6
and
7
, are more technical.

The second important ramification of
Equations

2.8
and
2.9
, and in some sense the heart of the paper, is that with conditions such as these in place we are able to make the following heuristic rigorous.

Consider the portion of
\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1})
coming from the
\mathbf{x}_{i}
, that is to say

\Sigma_{\operatorname{small}}(\mathbf{A}_{1}):=\{\sum_{i=1}^{L^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}:\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\in\{0,1\}\}.

Observe that by definition
Equation

1.5
we have

(2.12)

|\Sigma_{\operatorname{small}}(\mathbf{A}_{1})|=2^{L^{\prime}}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(L^{\prime}).

The event
k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1})
is then precisely the event that there is some
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i})_{i=1}^{L}
such that

(2.13)

k-\sum_{i=1}^{L}\varepsilon_{i}\lfloor k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i}}\rfloor\in\Sigma_{\operatorname{small}}(\mathbf{A}_{1}).

Let
\psi(k)
be some very slowly-growing function. Then almost surely we have
\max(\Sigma_{\operatorname{small}}(\mathbf{A}_{1}))\leqslant\psi(k)k_{0}\leqslant\psi(k)k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{L}}
, since
\mathbf{x}_{L^{\prime}}\leqslant k_{0}\leqslant k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{L}}
and the
\mathbf{x}_{i}
grow roughly as powers of two. That is, the condition
Equation

2.13
is contained in the condition

(2.14)

k-\sum_{i=1}^{L}\varepsilon_{i}\lfloor k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i}}\rfloor\in[0,\psi(k)k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{L}}].

By the definition
Equation

1.3
of
\varrho
, we expect the number of
\varepsilon
satisfying this weaker condition to be roughly
r:=\psi(k)2^{L-\mathbf{u}_{L}}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(L)
. (Here we are assuming, for the purpose of the heuristic, that
Equation

1.3
does not change much if we replace the interval
[1-2^{-\mathbf{u}_{\ell}},1]
with
[1-(j+1)2^{-\mathbf{u}_{\ell}},1-j2^{-\mathbf{u}_{\ell}}]
for
j=1,\dots,\psi(k)-1
.)

We now adopt the most naïve heuristic, namely that the probability that a given
\varepsilon
satisfying
Equation

2.14
also satisfies
Equation

2.13
should be

\approx\lambda:=\frac{|\Sigma_{\operatorname{small}}(\mathbf{A}_{1})|}{\psi(k)k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{L}}}.

Assuming suitable independence, the natural guess for the probability that
Equation

2.13
holds for at least one of the
r
choices of
\varepsilon
for which
Equation

2.14
holds is
1-(1-\lambda)^{r}\approx 1-e^{-\lambda r}
, that is to say

(2.15)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1}))\approx 1-e^{-\lambda r}.

Note that using
Equation

2.12
we have

\lambda r=\frac{|\Sigma_{\operatorname{small}}(\mathbf{A}_{1})|}{\psi(k)k2^{-\mathbf{u}_{L}}}\psi(k)2^{L-\mathbf{u}_{L}}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(L)=\frac{1}{k}2^{L+L^{\prime}}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(L^{\prime})\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(L).

Since
L+L^{\prime}=n+D
and
k=2^{n+\xi}
, it follows that

\lambda r=2^{D-\xi}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(L^{\prime})\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(L).

Finally, using the invariance properties
Equations

2.10
and
2.11
, it follows from the above that (heuristically!)

(2.16)

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1})\mid|\mathbf{A}_{1}|=n+D\big)\approx 1-\exp\big({-}2^{D-\xi}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)\big),

where here
1\lll\ell\lll L,L^{\prime}
(one should think of
\ell
as being ‘fixed but large’ while if
k_{0}=\sqrt{k}
then we expect
L,L^{\prime}\sim\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}k
to grow with
k
).

Now that we have written the key relation
Equation

2.16
, for the rest of this heuristic discussion we are no longer conditioning on
|\mathbf{A}_{1}|=n+D
.

Now condition to
\mathbf{x}_{1}=x_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{\ell}=x_{\ell}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}=u_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{\ell}=u_{\ell}
for fixed choices of
x_{1}\leqslant\dots\leqslant x_{\ell}
and
u_{1}\leqslant\dots\leqslant u_{\ell}
. We wish to calculate the probability that
|\mathbf{A}_{1}|=n+D
, or in other words that
L+L^{\prime}=n+D
. In order to do this we will use the following two facts. Here,
W(t):=te^{-t^{2}/2}1_{t\geqslant 0}
denotes the density function of the Rayleigh distribution.

Fact 1.
Conditioned on
\mathbf{u}_{1}=u_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{\ell}=u_{\ell}
with
0\leqslant u_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant u_{\ell}<\ell
, the probability that
\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}\leqslant\ell^{\prime}
for all
\ell^{\prime}
with
\ell\leqslant\ell^{\prime}\leqslant L
and a given value of
L
is
\approx(2/\pi)^{1/2}(\ell-u_{\ell})N^{-1}W(\frac{L-N}{\sqrt{N}})
, where
N=\lfloor\log_{2}(k/k_{0})\rfloor
.

Fact 2.
Conditioned on
\mathbf{x}_{1}=x_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{\ell}=x_{\ell}
with
0\leqslant x_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant x_{\ell}
and
\log_{2}x_{\ell}>\ell
, the probability that
\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{\ell^{\prime}}\geqslant\ell^{\prime}
for all
\ell^{\prime}
with
\ell\leqslant\ell^{\prime}\leqslant L^{\prime}
and a given
L^{\prime}
is
\approx(2/\pi)^{1/2}(\log_{2}x_{\ell}-\ell)(N^{\prime})^{-1}W(\frac{N^{\prime}-L^{\prime}}{\sqrt{N^{\prime}}})
where
N^{\prime}=\lfloor\log_{2}k_{0}\rfloor
.

Both of these facts come from the same source, which is the behaviour of random walks conditioned to stay (almost) positive. For Fact 1, there is a natural random walk whose
i
th step is the number of elements of
\mathbf{u}
in the interval
[u_{\ell}+i-1,u_{\ell}+i]
, minus 1; the increments of this walk are i.i.d.
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
variables, and its length
T
is
\approx\log_{2}(k/k_{0})-u_{\ell}
. The conditions
u_{\ell^{\prime}}\leqslant\ell^{\prime}
are then essentially that this random walk stays above
-(\ell-u_{\ell})
, and the event that we are interested in is essentially that the walk ends at
L-\ell-T
. We use the approximations
T\sim N
and
L-\ell-T\sim L-N
. Fact 1 is then a consequence of known results about the distribution of the endpoint of random walk of length
N
, conditioned to stay above
-m
. (For precise statements of the relevant statements, which are consequences of work of Denisov, Tarasov and Wachtel
[
11
]
, see
Section

8
and in particular
Propositions

8.1
and
8.8
). The explanation of Fact 2 is analogous and we omit a detailed discussion.

We wish to sum the probabilities in Facts 1 and 2 over
L,L^{\prime}
such that
L+L^{\prime}=n+D
(where here
D=O(1)
). Since the
W(\cdot)
terms vary on a scale of
\sqrt{N}
or
\sqrt{N^{\prime}}
, which we assume
\ggg 1
, this sum does not essentially depend on
D
; ignoring minor issues of integer parts, we may thus assume
L=N+t
and
L^{\prime}=N^{\prime}-t
(so
L+L^{\prime}=N+N^{\prime}=\log_{2}k=n+O(1)
). The sum is then

\frac{2}{\pi}(\ell-u_{\ell})(\log_{2}x_{\ell}-\ell)\sum_{t}N^{-1}(N^{\prime})^{-1}W\big(\frac{t}{\sqrt{N}}\big)W\big(\frac{t}{\sqrt{N^{\prime}}}\big).

Writing
M:=\big(\frac{NN^{\prime}}{N+N^{\prime}}\big)^{1/2}
and approximating the sum by an integral, the sum over
t
is

\displaystyle(NN^{\prime})^{-3/2}\sum_{t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}}t^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}(\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1}{N^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{M^{2}}{(NN^{\prime})^{3/2}}\sum_{t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}}\big(\frac{t}{M}\big)^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(t/M)^{2}}\approx\big(\frac{M^{2}}{NN^{\prime}}\big)^{3/2}\int^{\infty}_{0}x^{2}e^{-x^{2}/2}dx

(2.17)

\displaystyle=(\pi/2)^{1/2}(N+N^{\prime})^{-3/2}\approx(\pi/2)^{1/2}(\log_{2}k)^{-3/2}.

In summary, conditioned on
\mathbf{x}_{1}=x_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{\ell}=x_{\ell}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}=u_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{\ell}=u_{\ell}
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(|\mathbf{A}_{1}|=n+D\big)\approx(2/\pi)^{1/2}(\log_{2}k)^{-3/2}(\ell-u_{\ell})(\log_{2}x_{\ell}-\ell).

We now combine this with
Equation

2.16
and undo the conditioning to
\mathbf{x}_{1}=x_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{\ell}=x_{\ell}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}=u_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{\ell}=u_{\ell}
. This gives that
\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{1}),\,|\mathbf{A}_{1}|=n+D\big)
is approximately

\approx\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}(\log_{2}k)^{-3/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{u}}(\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}\big(1-\exp\big({-}2^{D-\xi}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)\big)\big).

Finally, summing over
D
using
Equation

2.2
gives

p(k)=\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0}))\approx f(\xi)k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2},

where

f(\xi)=(\frac{\pi}{2})^{1/2}(\log 2)^{3/2}e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{u}}(\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}g_{\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)}(\xi),

with

g_{\lambda}(\xi):=\sum_{D\in\mathbb{Z}}(\log 2)^{D-\xi}(1-e^{-\lambda 2^{D-\xi}}).

Note that

(2.18)

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{D\in\mathbb{Z}}(\log 2)^{D-\xi}(1-e^{-2^{D-\xi+\log_{2}\lambda}})

(2.19)

\displaystyle=(\log 2)^{-\log_{2}\lambda}\sum_{D\in\mathbb{Z}}(\log 2)^{D-\xi+\log_{2}\lambda}(1-e^{-2^{D-\xi+\log_{2}\lambda}})=\lambda^{-\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}}g(\xi-\log_{2}\lambda)

with
g
the function in
Theorem

1.1

and so we indeed see that
f=c_{0}g\ast\mu\ast\mu^{\prime}
with
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
defined as in
Propositions

1.3
and
1.4
and
c_{0}=(\frac{\pi}{2})^{1/2}(\log 2)^{3/2}e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}
.

Remark
.

Properties of uniform order statistics analogous to
Equations

2.8
and
2.9
(so-called “strong barrier conditions”) have been exploited in a related context by Schlitt in recent work
[
34
]
; see in particular
[
34
, page 5]
for some further references.

3.
Changing measure

We will give a detailed outline of the rest of the paper in
Section

4
below. However, it is expedient to first give a proper treatment of the ‘change of measure’ step (that is, the move from
\mathbf{A}_{0}
to
\mathbf{A}_{1}
) described at the start of
Section

2
, since this sits slightly to one side of the rest of the paper.

Rather than working with exactly the random mulitset
\mathbf{A}_{1}
as described in
Section

2
, we will instead consider the random multiset
\mathbf{A}
in which

(3.1)

r_{\mathbf{A}}(i)\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}\big(\frac{n}{iH_{k}}\big)

for
i\in[k]
, where
H_{k}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}1/i
is the harmonic sum. Note here that
n/H_{k}\approx 1/\log 2
, so
\mathbf{A}
is very closely related to
\mathbf{A}_{1}
. Observe that

\big|\mathbf{A}\big|\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}\big(\frac{n}{H_{k}}\sum_{i\leqslant k}\frac{1}{i}\big)=\operatorname{Pois}(n).

The fact that
n
is an integer is the reason for this particular rescaling; this will enable us to make effective use of embedded random walks, where we can bring into play the existing literature on the distribution of almost positive walks.

In this short section, we accomplish two things. First, we show that only the contribution to
Equation

2.1
from sets with
|\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|\approx\log_{2}k
is important. Second, for sets of roughly this critical size, we analyse the change in passing from the random multiset
\mathbf{A}_{0}
to the reweighted random multiset
\mathbf{A}
. The following is the key result of the section, and the only one we will need in the rest of the paper. Recall that
\gamma
is Euler’s constant and
\delta
the Erdős-Ford-Tenenbaum constant
Equation

1.1
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Write
k=2^{n+\xi}
. We have

\displaystyle p(k)=\Big(1+O\big(\frac{\log\log k}{\log k}\big)\Big)e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}k^{-\delta}\sum_{|D|\leqslant 20\log n}(\log 2)^{D-\xi}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}),\,|\mathbf{A}|=n+D\big)

\displaystyle+O\big(k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-2}\big).

Proof.

The starting point is of course
Equation

2.1
. We first eliminate the contribution from sets with size not close to
\log_{2}k
. The key claim is that

(3.2)

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\;\mbox{and}\;\big||\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|-n\big|\geqslant 20\log n\big)\ll k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-2}.

A short computation with Stirling’s formula using the fact that
|\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(H_{k})
shows that

\mathbb{P}\big(|\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|-n\geqslant 20\log n\big)\ll k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-2},

so it is enough to show

(3.3)

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\;\mbox{and}\;|\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|\leqslant m\big)\ll k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-2},

where, for brevity, we have written
m:=n-20\log n
. Define
\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}:=\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[1,\frac{k}{2\log k})
and
\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}:=\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[\frac{k}{2\log k},k]
. Then the LHS of
Equation

3.3
is bounded above by

(3.4)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leqslant m\\
1\leqslant r\leqslant m^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\mid|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}|=r,\,|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}|=m^{\prime}-r\big)\cdot\mathbb{P}\big(|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}|=r,\,|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}|=m^{\prime}-r\big);

the key point here is that we do not need to include
r=0
since, if
\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}
is empty and
|\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|\leqslant m
then
\max\big(\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\cap[k]\big)\leqslant\frac{mk}{2\log k}<3k/4
, so in particular
k\notin\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})
.

Conditioned on
|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}|=r
, we can write
\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}=\{\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{r}\}
, where the
\mathbf{a}_{j}
are independent samples from
\mathbb{Z}\cap[\frac{k}{2\log k},k]
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{a}_{j}=x)
proportional to
1/x
. (This is the standard relation between the conditioned Poisson and multinomial distributions.) Further conditioning on a fixed choice
A_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}
of
\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}
of size
m^{\prime}-r
, we have

(3.5)

\{k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\}=\bigcup_{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^{r}\setminus\{0^{r}\}}\bigcup_{s\in\Sigma(A_{0}^{\operatorname{small}})}\{k=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{j}\mathbf{a}_{j}+s\}.

For each
\varepsilon
, select the minimum
i
with
\varepsilon_{i}\neq 0
, and reveal all
\mathbf{a}_{j}
except for
j=i
; since the probability mass function of
\mathbf{a}_{i}
is pointwise bounded by
O(\frac{\log k}{k})
, we see that
\mathbb{P}\big(k=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{j}\mathbf{a}_{j}+s\big)\ll\frac{\log k}{k}
. Therefore, from
Equation

3.5
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\mid|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}|=r,\,\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}=A^{\operatorname{small}}_{0}\big)\ll 2^{m^{\prime}}\frac{\log k}{k}

and so, summing over all
A^{\operatorname{small}}_{0}
weighted by
\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}=A^{\operatorname{small}}_{0})
,

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\mid|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}|=r,\,|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}|=m^{\prime}-r\big)\ll 2^{m^{\prime}}\frac{\log k}{k}.

Substituting into
Equation

3.4
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\;\mbox{and}\;|\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|

\displaystyle\leqslant m\big)\ll\frac{\log k}{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leqslant m\\
1\leqslant r\leqslant m^{\prime}\end{subarray}}2^{m^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{large}}|=r,\,|\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\operatorname{small}}|=m^{\prime}-r\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\log k}{k}\sum_{m^{\prime}\leqslant m}2^{m^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(|\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|=m^{\prime}\big)=\frac{\log k}{k}\sum_{m^{\prime}\leqslant m}\frac{e^{-H_{k}}(2H_{k})^{m^{\prime}}}{(m^{\prime})!}.

Using the bounds
e^{-H_{k}}\ll 1/k
and
(m^{\prime})!\geqslant(m^{\prime}/e)^{m^{\prime}}
, one may see that this is bounded above by
\ll\frac{\log k}{k^{2}}\sum_{m^{\prime}\leqslant m}(2eH_{k}/m^{\prime})^{m^{\prime}}
. Since
(C/x)^{x}
is increasing on
[0,C/e]
, and since
m=(1+o(1))\frac{\log k}{\log 2}
, this is
\ll\frac{(\log k)^{2}}{k^{2}}(2eH_{k}/m)^{m}
. Noting that
(2e\log k/m_{0})^{m_{0}}=k^{2-\delta}
, where
m_{0}:=\log k/\log 2
, we can rewrite this bound as

(\log k)^{2}k^{-\delta}\cdot\big(\frac{m_{0}}{m}\big)^{m}e^{m-m_{0}}\cdot\big(\frac{H_{k}}{\log k}\big)^{m}\cdot(2\log 2)^{m-m_{0}}.

Using
(m_{0}/m)^{m}e^{m-m_{0}}\leqslant 1
and
(H_{k}/\log k)^{m}=(1+O(1/\log k))^{m}\ll 1
, we see that this is
\ll(\log k)^{2}k^{-\delta}(2\log 2)^{-20\log\log k}<k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-2}
. This completes the proof of the claim
Equation

3.2
.

From this and
Equation

2.1
we evidently have

p(k)=\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{0})\;\mbox{and}\;\big||\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]|-n\big|\leqslant 20\log n\big)+O\big(k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-2}\big).

Now we change measure and express the probabilities using
\mathbf{A}
as given by the law
Equation

3.1
.

If
A
is a fixed multiset of elements from
[k]
(with the multiplicity of
i
in
A
being
r_{A}(i)
) then

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]=A)=\prod_{i\in[k]}\frac{e^{-1/i}(1/i)^{r_{A}(i)}}{r_{A}(i)!},\qquad\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}=A)=\prod_{i\in[k]}\frac{e^{-n/H_{k}i}(n/(H_{k}i))^{r_{A}(i)}}{r_{A}(i)!},

and so if
|A|=n+D
then

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]=A)=e^{n-H_{k}}\big(\frac{H_{k}}{n}\big)^{n+D}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}=A).

Using that

n=\frac{\log k}{\log 2}-\xi,\quad\frac{H_{k}}{n}=\Big(1+\frac{1}{\log k}(\gamma+\xi\log 2)+O\big(\frac{1}{\log^{2}k}\big)\Big)\log 2,

it follows that we have the key relation

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}_{0}\cap[k]=A)=\Big(1+O\big(\frac{1+|D|}{\log k}\big)\Big)e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}(\log 2)^{D-\xi}k^{-\delta}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{A}=A).

Summing over all sets
A
with cardinality in the interval
[n-20\log n,n+20\log n]
(and thus
|D|\ll\log\log k
) and with
k\in\Sigma(A)
, and applying
Equation

3.2
, the proof of
Proposition

3.1
is complete.
∎

4.
Outline of the rest of the paper

We now give an outline of the rest of the paper.
Proposition

3.1
has reduced our main task of proving
Theorem

1.1
to that of estimating
\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))
(and that
|\mathbf{A}|
has a certain fixed size close to
\frac{\log k}{\log 2}
), where the random (multi-)set
\mathbf{A}
is defined using
Equation

3.1
. An important tool for understanding
\mathbf{A}
– and indeed the reason for the particular definition
Equation

3.1
– will be the use of random walks.

Roughly, the idea is that an equivalent way to sample
\mathbf{A}
is via the use of two random walks
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
. The random walk
\bm{\beta}=(\bm{\beta}(i))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a random walk with
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
steps
\bm{\xi}_{i}=\bm{\beta}(i)-\bm{\beta}(i-1)
, where we adopt the convention that
\bm{\beta}(0)=0
deterministically. Similarly
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
has
1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)
steps
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}
.

Only the
\bm{\xi}_{i}
with
i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil
and the
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}
with
i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor
will be relevant. The choice of
n/2
here is somewhat arbitrary; any pair of cutoffs summing to
n
and both tending to infinity would do, and our particular choice corresponds to taking
k_{0}\sim\sqrt{k}
in the heuristic of
Section

2
.

The precise manner in which this sampling of
\mathbf{A}
is done is a little technical and is described carefully in
Section

5
below. Roughly, one should think of
1+\bm{\xi}_{i}
being the number of elements of
\mathbf{A}
of size
\sim k2^{-i}
, and
1-\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}
the number of elements of
\mathbf{A}
of size
\sim 2^{i}
. The precise meaning of
\sim
will be clarified in
Section

5
but the definitions will be such that we have

(4.1)

|\mathbf{A}|=\sum_{i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil}(1+\bm{\xi}_{i})+\sum_{i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(1-\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i})=n+\bm{\beta}(\lceil n/2\rceil)-\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(\lfloor n/2\rfloor).

Note that
1+\bm{\xi}_{i},1-\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(1)
, which makes this modelling of
\mathbf{A}
natural. The reason for using two ‘opposite’ random walks (rather than simply
\operatorname{Pois}(1)
random variables) is completely analogous to the modelling of
\mathbf{A}_{1}
using the upper and lower processes
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x}
as described in
Section

2
, and has to do with the fact that we will exploit positivity properties of these walks.

Throughout many of our arguments we will condition to a fixed choice
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
of (the initial segments of)
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})
. We use the term
upper walk
to describe any possible instance
\beta
of
\bm{\beta}
; this is simply a sequence
(\beta(i))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
whose increments
\xi_{i}:=\beta(i)-\beta(i-1)
are bounded below by
-1
. Similarly a lower walk is any sequence
(\beta^{\prime}(i))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
whose increments
\xi^{\prime}_{i}=\beta^{\prime}(i)-\beta^{\prime}(i-1)
are bounded above by
1
.

Write
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
for the corresponding (random) choice of
\mathbf{A}
. The definition of this object is described carefully in
Section

5.3
below, but this outline can be followed without detailed knowledge of that section. By
Equation

4.1
we have

\#(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})=n+\beta(\lceil n/2\rceil)-\beta^{\prime}(\lfloor n/2\rfloor).

We therefore have from
Proposition

3.1
that

\displaystyle p(k)=\Big(

\displaystyle 1+O\big(\frac{\log\log k}{\log k}\big)\Big)e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}k^{-\delta}\sum_{|D|\leqslant 20\log n}(\log 2)^{D-\xi}\times

(4.2)

\displaystyle\times\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta,\beta^{\prime}\\
\beta(\lceil n/2\rceil)-\beta^{\prime}(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)=D\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)+O(k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-2}).

This formula will be the basis for much of our analysis, and in particular the bulk of our paper will be devoted to trying to understand the quantity

(4.3)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})).

Broadly, our strategy for doing this will follow the initial steps of the heuristic laid out in
Section

2
. However, by conditioning to fixed
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
the actual details look somewhat different.

The analogue of
Equations

2.3
and
2.4
is that the main contribution will come from
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
which are almost positive in the sense that

(4.4)

\min\big(\min_{i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil}\beta(i),\min_{i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\beta^{\prime}(i)\big)\geqslant-O(1).

The main point of departure of the paper will then be the fact that most such walks satisfy
\beta(i),\beta^{\prime}(i)\geqslant i^{1/2-\frac{1}{100}}
for large
i
, which is the appropriate analogue of
Equations

2.8
and
2.9
, as well as some weak boundedness properties on their increments. We give the appropriate technical definitions now, since they will feature throughout the paper. In the following definitions
\kappa
and
\eta
are fixed small positive constants, global to the paper. We can take
\kappa=\eta=\frac{1}{100}
, but it makes the exposition clearer to keep these constants unspecified (and separate).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
R\geqslant 1
be a parameter and let
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\beta
be a walk (upper or lower) with increments
\xi_{i}=\beta(i)-\beta(i-1)
. Then we say that
\beta
is
R
-bounded to length
L
if
|\xi_{i}|\leqslant Ri^{\kappa}
for
i=1,\dots,L
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
T\geqslant 0
be a parameter and let
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\beta
be a walk (upper or lower) with increments
\xi_{i}=\beta(i)-\beta(i-1)
. Then we say that
\beta
is
T
-
positive
to length
L
if
-T+\ell^{1/2-\eta}\leqslant\beta(\ell)\leqslant T+\ell^{1/2+\eta}

for
\ell=1,\dots,L
.

Continuing to follow the heuristic in
Section

2
, the next task is to define appropriate analogues of the
\tau
and
\varrho
statistics. These will be called
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
and
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
respectively, where
\ell
is a positive integer parameter. They are defined, and their key properties explored, in
Section

6
and
Section

7
respectively. In particular, we will show that if
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
are appropriately bounded and positive then these statistics have good convergence properties as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
, statements which correspond to the heuristic assertions
Equations

2.10
and
2.11
. The precise statements here are
Lemma

6.4
and
Lemma

7.3
.

Part

II
of the paper is an interlude consisting of three sections, whose purpose is to develop various background material. These three sections may be read independently of each other and the rest of the paper.

Section

8
contains material on almost positive random walks, in particular concerning boundedness and positivity properties of such walks. Rigorous statements to the effect that most almost positive walks are appropriately bounded (resp. positive) may be found in
Lemma

8.12
and
Lemma

8.15
respectively.
Section

8
also introduces the technical concept of a
jump step
. This is a single time
t
at which the walk
\beta
has a large increment, followed by suitable boundedness and positivity properties; the definition is
Definition

8.17
. Almost positive walks generically have a jump step in appropriate ranges (see
Lemma

8.19
).

Section

9
assembles various bounds for
\{0,1\}
-matrices satisfying linear-algebraic conditions, and finally
Section

10
gives the basic properties of the function
g
featuring in our main result (see
Equation

1.2
).

Part

III
of the paper, consisting of two sections
Sections

11
and
12
, is the beating heart of the paper. This is the portion of the paper where the analysis corresponding to
Equation

2.12
to
Equation

2.16
in our heuristic overview is carried out rigorously. In particular, the phrase ‘assuming suitable independence’ leading up to
Equation

2.15
is justified in
Section

11
by a (lengthy) local limit theorem analysis; the main result is
Lemma

11.2
. Here, the existence of a jump step
t
is used crucially in order to characterise the relevant distributions as Gaussians.

The analysis is continued in
Section

12
, where the heuristic
Equation

2.15
(suitably rephrased in the random walk formalism) is justified via an inclusion–exclusion argument. The apparent dependence on the location of the jump step
t
is shown to be illusory, and the main result of the section is
Proposition

12.1
, which is a rigorous version of
Equation

2.16
. Roughly speaking it provides the desired expression for
Equation

4.3
, namely that under suitable conditions we have

(4.5)

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)\approx 1-\mathbb{E}\exp\big(-2^{D-\xi}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)\big),

where here
\xi=\{\log_{2}k\}
,
D=\beta(\lceil n/2\rceil)-\beta^{\prime}(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)
and
L
is an appropriate large parameter. This should be compared with
Equation

2.16
.

Part

IV
of the paper contains the main synthesis, substituting
Equation

4.5
(or rather the more technical
Proposition

12.1
) into
Proposition

3.1
in order to prove the weak form of
Theorem

1.1
in which the measures
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
are shown to exist, but are not yet described explicitly. The first task here, accomplished in
Section

13
, is to reduce to the case where (essentially)
D=O(1)
and
Equation

4.4
holds. Here, with an eye to applying the main results from
Part

III
, one must additionally restrict to walks which are suitably bounded, positive, and have a jump step.

Section

14
then contains the main analysis, which is parallel to the leveraging of Facts 1 and 2 in our heuristic discussion. The point here is to evaluate the probability that two walks
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
of lengths
\lceil n/2\rceil,\lfloor n/2\rfloor
are almost positive and satisfy
\bm{\beta}(\lceil n/2\rceil)-\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)=D
. This is done by using a suitable local limit theorem, which states that the endpoints of the two almost positive walks, appropriately scaled, each have a Rayleigh distribution. The result we apply follows from work of Denisov, Tarasov and Wachtel
[
11
]
, though we will give our own self-contained treatment. The endpoints of the two walks may then be coupled via a fairly straightforward analysis very similar to
Equation

2.17
.

Throughout the above argument, the walks
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
under consideration are bounded below by
-M
, for some parameter
M
. By the end of
Section

14
we have established an approximate form of
Theorem

1.1
in which “
f
” is of the form
g\ast\mu_{M}\ast\mu^{\prime}_{M}+O(e^{-cM})
for certain Borel measures
\mu_{M},\mu^{\prime}_{M}
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. To conclude the proof of
Theorem

1.1
(and, in particular, to show that
f
actually exists) we must show appropriate convergence of
\mu_{M},\mu^{\prime}_{M}
to nonzero limit measures
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
as
M\rightarrow\infty
. This task, which is rather delicate, is accomplished in
Section

15
. It makes considerable further use of the results from
Sections

6
,
7
, and
8
.

The final part of the main paper,
Part

V
, gives the explicit characterisations of the measures
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
as detailed in
Proposition

1.3
and
Proposition

1.4
respectively. The proofs occupy
Sections

16
and
17
respectively. These involve a translation from the formalism of random walks
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
to the language of sequences
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x}
as in the statements of
Propositions

1.3
and
1.4
(and the heuristic sketch). A surprising amount of care is required to carry this out correctly. A key technical issue is that when defining the measures
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
we have not specified that the corresponding walks are “almost positive” and one needs to prove that the contribution from remaining paths is essentially negligible.

There are six appendices.
Appendix

A
collects various facts about random walks and a basic estimate from renewal theory.
Appendix

B
then gives a self-contained account of a version of the local limit theorem of Denisov, Tarasov and Wachtel which is sufficient for our purposes.
Appendix

C
pulls together some Fourier estimates of fairly standard type.
Appendix

D
gives assorted real-variable inequalities whose proofs would have diverted attention to too great an extent if given in the main text.
Appendix

E
gives the basic properties of the so-called bounded Lipschitz norm on Borel measures, which we use in
Section

15
to study the convergence of the measures
\mu_{M},\mu^{\prime}_{M}
, and again in
Sections

16
and
17
when characterising
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
. Finally,
Appendix

F
collects some standard large deviation estimates.

5.
Sampling using walks

In this section we give the details of how the random (multi-)set
\mathbf{A}
defined by
r_{\mathbf{A}}(i)\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}\big(\frac{n}{iH_{k}}\big)
(that is, as in
Equation

3.1
) may be sampled via a pair of random walks
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

As in the previous section, and for the rest of the paper,
\bm{\beta}
denotes a random walk with
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
steps
\bm{\xi}_{i}=\bm{\beta}(i)-\bm{\beta}(i-1)
, and
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
has
1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)
steps
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}=\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)-\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i-1)
. By convention,
\bm{\beta}(0)=\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(0)=0
. The walks
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
are independent.

At the same time we will show how to sample the upper and lower processes (see
Definition

2.2
)
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x}
in a similar manner. More precisely we will explain how to couple them to
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
respectively.

5.1.
The upper and lower processes

To couple the upper process
\mathbf{u}
to
\bm{\beta}
, we first sample
\bm{\beta}
and then, for each
i
, sample
1+\bm{\xi}_{i}
uniform random elements from
(i-1,i]
and order them to create
\mathbf{u}\cap(i-1,i]
. It is a standard fact that
\mathbf{u}
is indeed a rate 1 Poisson process. Observe that
\bm{\beta}(i)=\#(\mathbf{u}\cap[0,i])-i
.

Handling the lower process is a little more involved. We begin by associating to
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
an auxiliary rate 1 Poisson process
\mathbf{s}
by first sampling
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
, then for each
i
sampling
1-\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}
uniform random elements from
(i-1,i]
and ordering them to create
\mathbf{s}\cap(i-1,i]
. Again,
\mathbf{s}
is a rate 1 Poisson process, and now
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=i-\#(\mathbf{s}\cap[0,i])
.

We then introduce the following pair of closely related maps. Here,
H_{N}
denotes the harmonic sum and by convention
H_{0}=0
.

Definition 5.1
.

Define maps
\phi,\tilde{\phi}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
as follows.
\phi(0)=\tilde{\phi}(0)=1
, and if
x>0
then
\tilde{\phi}
is the unique map so that
x\in(\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{\tilde{\phi}(x)-1},\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{\tilde{\phi}(x)}]
.
\phi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is the unique map defined by
x\in(\frac{1}{\log 2}H_{\phi(x)-1},\frac{1}{\log 2}H_{\phi(x)}]
.

We will use
\phi
now, and
\tilde{\phi}
will be used later when discussing how to sample
\mathbf{A}
.
For orientation, we note that
\phi(x),\tilde{\phi}(x)\asymp 2^{x}
, and also that
\tilde{\phi}
depends on
k
, but
\phi
is ‘universal’. For a detailed proof, see
Lemma

5.3
below.

We now couple the lower process
\mathbf{x}
(
Definition

2.2
) to
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
as follows. First sample
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
, then associate the auxiliary process
\mathbf{s}
. Define
\mathbf{x}=\phi(\mathbf{s})
. We observe that, unravelling the definitions,

r_{\mathbf{x}}(i)=\big|\mathbf{s}\cap\frac{1}{\log 2}(H_{i-1},H_{i}]\big|\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(1/(i\log 2)).

Thus
\mathbf{x}
indeed has the required distribution of the lower process.

5.2.
Sampling
\mathbf{A}

We now turn to the discussion of sampling
\mathbf{A}
(the random multiset defined by
Equation

3.1
) via the random walks
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
. Let
\mathbf{u}
be the upper process coupled to
\bm{\beta}
as above, and let
\mathbf{s}
be the auxiliary rate 1 Poisson process coupled to
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
, again as described above. Now set

\mathbf{A}=\tilde{\phi}\big(\mathbf{s}\cap[0,\lfloor n/2\rfloor]\big)\cup\tilde{\phi}\big(n-(\mathbf{u}\cap[0,\lceil n/2\rceil])\big).

That is, we use
(\bm{\beta}(i))_{i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil}
to describe the large elements of
\mathbf{A}
, and
(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i))_{i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
to describe the small elements.
Observe that
[0,\lfloor n/2\rfloor]\cup(n-[0,\lceil n/2\rceil])=[0,n]
and so the distribution of
\mathbf{A}
is exactly that of
\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{p}\cap[0,n])
, where
\mathbf{p}
is a Poisson process of rate
1
. Therefore we have

r_{\mathbf{A}}(i)=r_{\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{p}\cap[0,n])}(i)=\big|\mathbf{p}\cap\frac{n}{H_{k}}(H_{i-1},H_{i}]\big|\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}\big(\frac{n}{H_{k}i}\big)

for
i=1,\dots,k
, and so this
\mathbf{A}
indeed coincides with the random multiset defined by
Equation

3.1
.

5.3.
Conditioning to fixed walks.

We recall in a formal definition the notions of upper and lower walk.

Definition 5.2
.

An
upper walk
of length
N
is simply a sequence
(\beta(i))_{i=1}^{N}
where the increments
\xi_{i}:=\beta(i)-\beta(i-1)
are integers satisfying
\xi_{i}\geqslant-1
for all
i
. A
lower walk
of length
N^{\prime}
is a sequence
(\beta^{\prime}(i))_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}
where the increments
\xi^{\prime}_{i}:=\beta^{\prime}(i)-\beta^{\prime}(i-1)
are integers satisfing
\xi^{\prime}_{i}\leqslant 1
for all
i
. By convention we set
\beta(0)=\beta^{\prime}(0)=0
.

For much of the analysis we will condition to the event that
(\bm{\beta}(i))_{i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil}=(\beta(i))_{i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil}
and that
(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i))_{i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor}=(\beta^{\prime}(i))_{i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
for some pair of upper- and lower walks
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
, which we will always take to have lengths
\lceil n/2\rceil
,
\lfloor n/2\rfloor
respectively. It is convenient to write
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
for this event.

We now consider the upper and lower processes
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x}
and random multiset
\mathbf{A}
conditioned to fixed walks. We take the opportunity to spell out these definitions and at the same time specify notation for listing their elements.

Conditioned upper process.
The conditioned upper process
(\mathbf{u}\mid\bm{\beta}=\beta)
, which we write
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
for brevity, is obtained by sampling, for each
i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil
,
1+\xi_{i}
uniform random elements from
(i-1,i]
, and ordering them. (We remark that we will never need to discuss any values of
\mathbf{u}
outside the interval
[0,\lceil n/2\rceil]
, so we happily ignore the slight inconsistency of notation here.) We write the elements of
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
as
(\mathbf{u}_{i,j})_{i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}
, but leave these randomly ordered on each
(i-1,i]
. When this notation is used, the underlying
\beta
will be clear from context and so we do not indicate an explicit dependence. Thus
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
is uniform on
(i-1,i]
, and these variables are independent for
j=1,2,\dots,1+\xi_{i}
.

Conditioned lower process.
To describe
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})=(\mathbf{x}\mid\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime})
we first consider the conditioned auxiliary Poisson process
(\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime})
. This is obtained by sampling
1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}
uniform random elements from
(i-1,i]
,
i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor
. We write its elements as
(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})_{i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}
, again randomly ordered on
(i-1,i]
. Thus
\mathbf{s}_{i,j}
is uniform on
(i-1,i]
, and these variables are independent for
j=1,2,\dots,1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}
. We have, by definition,
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})=\phi(\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime})
. We write
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=\phi(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})
,
i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor
and
j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]
for the elements of
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
.

Conditioned random multiset.
Now we define
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
for
\mathbf{A}
conditioned to
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
. First of all we introduce notation which unifies the
\lceil n/2\rceil
steps of
\beta
and the
\lfloor n/2\rfloor
steps of
\beta^{\prime}
which are relevant to the construction. Thus we define non-negative integers
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
by

(5.1)

b_{i}:=1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}\;\;(i=1,\dots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)\;\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\;b_{n+1-i}:=1+\xi_{i}\;\;(i=1,\dots,\lceil n/2\rceil).

b_{i}
should be thought of as roughly the number of elements of
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
which are of size
\asymp 2^{i}
, as we will see in
Lemma

5.5
below.

The elements of
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
may be listed as
(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{i\in[n],j\in[b_{i}]}
with

(5.2)

\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})&1\leqslant i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor;\\
\tilde{\phi}(n-\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j})&\lfloor n/2\rfloor<i\leqslant n.\end{array}\right.

Note in particular that we have

(5.3)

\#(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}.

5.4.
Size and anticoncentration estimates

We will make extensive use of
Lemma

5.5
which gives the approximate distribution of the
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
. In the proof of this we will require some simple properties of the function
\tilde{\phi}
. We give these, together with essentially identical properties of
\phi
, in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.3
.

Uniformly for
x\in[0,n]
we have

(5.4)

\log_{2}\phi(x),\log_{2}\tilde{\phi}(x)=x+O(1).

Thus
\phi(x),\tilde{\phi}(x)\asymp 2^{x}
.

Proof.

We give the proofs for
\tilde{\phi}
. Recall throughout that
n=\lfloor\log_{2}k\rfloor
. We have
\frac{n}{H_{k}}=\frac{1}{\log 2}+O(\frac{1}{n})
and
H_{\tilde{\phi}(x)}=\log\tilde{\phi}(x)+O(1)
. Thus from
x\leqslant\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{\tilde{\phi}(x)}
we obtain
x\leqslant(\frac{1}{\log 2}+O(\frac{1}{n}))(\log\tilde{\phi}(x)+O(1))=\log_{2}\tilde{\phi}(x)+O(1)
, or in other words the RHS of
Equation

5.4
is at most the LHS. Here, we used that
\tilde{\phi}(x)\leqslant k
for
x\leqslant n
in order to bound the cross term
O(\frac{1}{n})\log\tilde{\phi}(x)
by an absolute constant.

In a very similar manner one may obtain an inequality in the opposite direction as well, and putting these together gives
Equation

5.4
.

The proof for
\phi
is extremely similar (and slightly simpler). This estimate in fact holds for all
x
.
∎

Before turning to
Lemma

5.5
itself, we first give distributional estimates for the components
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}
of the conditioned lower process
(\mathbf{x}\mid\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.4
.

We have

(5.5)

\mathbf{x}_{i,j}\asymp 2^{i}

deterministically and

(5.6)

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=t)\ll 2^{-i}.

Proof.

For both parts we use that
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=\phi(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})
. Item
Equation

5.5
is immediate from
Lemma

5.3
and the fact that
\mathbf{s}_{i,j}\in(i-1,i]
. For
Equation

5.6
, observe that
\mathbb{P}(\phi(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})=t)
is the probability that a uniform random variable on
(i-1,i]
lies in
(\frac{1}{\log 2}H_{t-1},\frac{1}{\log 2}H_{t}]
, which is an interval of length
1/t\log 2
. For this interval to intersect
(i-1,i]
we must (since
\phi
is nondecreasing) have
t\geqslant\phi(i-1)
, and so the desired probability is at most
1/\phi(i-1)\log 2
. The stated bound
Equation

5.6
then follows using
Lemma

5.3
.
∎

Now we give the key estimate on the
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
, the elements of the conditioned set
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Deterministically we have

(5.7)

\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\asymp 2^{i}.

We also have the anticoncentration estimate

(5.8)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x)\ll 2^{-i}.

Here, the implied constants are absolute
(
and, in particular, do not depend on
k
)
.

Proof.

Item
Equation

5.7
is immediate from
Lemmas

5.3
and
5.2
and the fact that
\mathbf{s}_{i,j},\mathbf{u}_{i,j}=i+O(1)
.

For
Equation

5.8
, suppose first that
\lfloor n/2\rfloor<i\leqslant n
. Observe that

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x)=\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\phi}(n-\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j})=x)=\mathbb{P}(n-\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j}\in I)

where
I:=(\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{x-1},\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{x}]
. Now
\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j}
is uniformly distributed on
(n-i,n+1-i]
, so it follows that

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x)\leqslant|I|=\frac{n}{H_{k}x}\ll\frac{1}{x}.

Since
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
is deterministically
\asymp 2^{i}
, the result follows.
To proof in the case
i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor
is very similar and left to the reader.
∎

5.5.
Coupling top and bottom to universals

The material in the following subsection will not be required until the end of
Section

12
. The idea is to couple
\mathbf{A}
, whose definition is quite heavily dependent on
k
, to the ‘universal’ upper and lower processes
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x}
. Essentially this consists of relating
\mathbf{A}
to the set
\mathbf{A}_{1}
which featured in our heuristic discussion.

We first handle the large elements of
\mathbf{A}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
be upper and lower walks, and consider the conditioned upper process
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
and the conditioned random multiset
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
. Let
i
satisfy
\lfloor n/2\rfloor<i\leqslant n
. Then
|\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}-2^{-\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j}}|\ll 2^{i-n}(n+1-i)/n
deterministically.

Proof.

By
Equation

5.2
we have
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=\tilde{\phi}(n-\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j})
. From
Definition

5.1
, this implies that
n-\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j}\in(\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{\mathbf{a}_{i,j}-1},\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{\mathbf{a}_{i,j}}]
.
Since
H_{t},H_{t-1}=\log t+\gamma+O(\frac{1}{t})
uniformly for
t\geqslant 2
, it follows that

n-\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j}=\frac{n}{H_{k}}\big(\log\mathbf{a}_{i,j}+\gamma+O(2^{-i})\big).

Multiplying by
\frac{H_{k}}{n}
and using
H_{k}=\log k+\gamma+O(\frac{1}{k})
,
\frac{H_{k}}{n}=\log 2+O(\frac{1}{n})
and
\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j}\leqslant n+1-i
, this implies that

\log k-\mathbf{u}_{n+1-i,j}\log 2=\log\mathbf{a}_{i,j}+O\big(\frac{n+1-i}{n}\big).

That is, writing
x:=\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
and
y:=2^{-\mathbf{u}_{n+i-1,j}}
, we have
|\log x-\log y|\ll\frac{n+1-i}{n}
. The desired result then follows using the inequality
|x-y|\leqslant\max(x,y)|\log x-\log y|
(valid for all real
x,y\in(0,1]
) and the fact that
x,y\asymp 2^{i-n}
.
∎

Now we turn to the small elements of
\mathbf{A}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
be upper and lower walks, and consider the conditioned lower process
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta)
, with elements
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}
, and the conditioned random multiset
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
, with elements
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
. Then, uniformly for
i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{x}_{i,j}\neq\mathbf{a}_{i,j})\ll i/n
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime})=(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})_{i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}
be the auxiliary process introduced above. Thus
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=\phi(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})
and
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})
. Thus we must bound
\mathbb{P}(\phi(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})\neq\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{s}_{i,j}))
; that is, it suffices to prove that

\sum_{x}\big|\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})=x)-\mathbb{P}(\phi(\mathbf{s}_{i,j})=x)\big|\ll i/n.

From the definitions (
Definition

5.1
) of
\phi,\tilde{\phi}
, the task is to show

\sum_{x}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{s}_{i,j}\in(\frac{1}{\log 2}H_{x-1},\frac{1}{\log 2}H_{x}]\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{s}_{i,j}\in(\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{x-1},\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{x}]\big)\Big|\ll i/n.

Recall that here
\mathbf{s}_{i,j}
is uniform on
(i-1,i]
. In particular, both probabilities vanish unless
x\asymp 2^{i}
. For each
x
, the difference in probabilities is the length of the symmetric difference between the two intervals, and therefore the above expression is bounded by

\sum_{x\asymp 2^{i}}\Big|\frac{n}{H_{k}}-\frac{1}{\log 2}\Big|\big(H_{x-1}+H_{x}\big)\ll\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\asymp 2^{i}}\big(H_{x-1}+H_{x}\big)\ll\frac{i}{n},

as required.
∎

6.
The random variables
\tau
and
\tau^{*}

In this section we develop the basic properties of the
\tau
-statistics as defined in
Equation

1.5
, which feature prominently in the measure
\mu^{\prime}
(see
Proposition

1.4
).

Let
\beta^{\prime}
be a lower walk with increments
\xi^{\prime}_{i}=\beta^{\prime}(i)-\beta^{\prime}(i-1)
. In this section we suppose that
\beta^{\prime}
is infinite (rather than of length
\lfloor n/2\rfloor
) although we will only be dealing with finite segments of
\beta^{\prime}
.

Recall the definition of the conditioned lower process
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
and its elements
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}
,
j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]
, as described in
Section

5.3
. We define two statistics related to
\tau
, as defined in
Equation

1.5
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. We define

(6.1)

\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell):=2^{\beta^{\prime}(\ell)-\ell}\#\big\{\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{i,j}:\varepsilon_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}\big\}.

By convention we set
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(0)=\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(0):=1
. Suppose that

(6.2)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}(j-\beta^{\prime}(j))=\infty.

Then we also define

(6.3)

\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell):=2^{-\ell}\#\big\{\sum_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{i,j}:\varepsilon_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}\big\},

where the
(i,j)
-sum is taken over the
\ell
smallest
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}
s.

Remarks
.

The assumption
Equation

6.2
guarantees that
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
is defined for all
\ell
. We note that the definition of
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}
is compatible with the definition
Equation

1.5
, being the
\tau
statistic defined there applied to the conditioned sequence
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
.
We think of
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
as rather analogous to
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
, but ‘adapted to the random walk’; instead of working with the first
\ell
elements of
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
, we work with the elements up to
\ell
.

Observe that the
\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)
elements
(\mathbf{x}_{i,j})_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}
are precisely the initial
\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)
elements of the conditioned lower process
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
. Therefore we have

(6.4)

\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)=\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)).

It is straightforward to check the monotonicity properties

(6.5)

1=\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(0)\geqslant\dots\geqslant\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\geqslant\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell+1)\geqslant\dots\geqslant 0

and

(6.6)

1=\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(0)\geqslant\dots\geqslant\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\geqslant\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell+1)\geqslant\dots\geqslant 0.

The main aim in this section is to develop some basic properties of the variables
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
and
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
, particularly the latter. The first statement says that, under reasonable conditions,
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\lessapprox 2^{-m}
if
\beta^{\prime}
attains the value
-m
. This should be considered a rigorous version of the heuristics just before
Equation

2.5
.

Recall from
Definition

4.1
the notion of a walk being
R
-bounded to some length
L
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
m\geqslant 0
be an integer. Suppose that
\beta^{\prime}
is a lower walk and that
\beta^{\prime}(q)=-m
for some
q
with
0\leqslant q\leqslant\ell
. Suppose that
\beta^{\prime}
is
R
-bounded to length
\ell
. Then

(6.7)

\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\ll R(1+q)^{\kappa}2^{-m}.

Proof.

If
q=0
then
m=0
and the result is trivial from
Equation

6.6
. Suppose, then, that
q\geqslant 1
. By the monotonicity property
Equation

6.6
it suffices to handle the case
\ell=q
. By
Equation

5.5
we have

\big|\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{i,j}\big|\ll\sum_{i\leqslant\ell}2^{i}|1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}|\ll R2^{\ell}\ell^{\kappa},

where in the last step we used the
R
-boundedness of
\beta^{\prime}
. In particular the number of distinct elements
\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{i,j}
is
\ll R2^{\ell}\ell^{\kappa}
. From the definition
Equation

6.1
, it follows that
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\leqslant 2^{\beta^{\prime}(\ell)}R\ell^{\kappa}=2^{-m}R\ell^{\kappa}
, which implies
Equation

6.7
.
∎

We will also require the following variant for the unstarred
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}
quantities.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
m\geqslant 0
and
\ell\geqslant 1
be integers. Suppose that
Equation

6.2
holds, and denote by
\ell^{\prime}
the smallest integer such that
\ell^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
. Suppose that
\beta^{\prime}(q)=-m
for some
q
with
0\leqslant q\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1
, and that
\beta^{\prime}
is
R
-bounded to length
\ell^{\prime}-1
. Then

\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\ll R(1+q)^{\kappa}2^{-m}.

Proof.

By the monotonicity of the
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
in
\ell
, the definition of
\ell^{\prime}
, and
Equation

6.4
, we have

\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\leqslant\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime}-1-\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime}-1))=\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime}-1).

The result now follows immediately from
Lemma

6.2
, replacing
\ell
by
\ell^{\prime}-1
in that lemma.
∎

The next fact is more difficult. It allows us to establish rapid convergence of
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
to the limit as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
under a suitable condition on the lower walk
\beta^{\prime}
(that
\beta^{\prime}
is suitably positive in the sense of
Definition

4.2
).

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\beta^{\prime}
be a lower walk. Then uniformly for non-negative integers
\ell
we have

(6.8)

\mathbb{E}\big(\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)-\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell+1)\big)\ll 2^{-\beta^{\prime}(\ell+1)}.

Here, the expectation is over random choices of
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})=(\mathbf{x}_{i,j})_{i;j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}
.

Proof.

For an integer
\ell\geqslant 1
, denote
\varepsilon_{\leqslant\ell}=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{i\leqslant\ell,j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}
and for
t\in\mathbb{Z}
write

r_{\mathbf{x},\ell}(t):=\sum_{\varepsilon_{\leqslant\ell}}1\big(\sum_{i\leqslant\ell,j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=t\big).

(Thus
r_{\mathbf{x},\ell}(t)
is a random variable.) Define also
r_{\mathbf{x},0}(t)=1_{t=0}
. Thus by
Equation

6.1
we have, for all
\ell\geqslant 0
, that

(6.9)

\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)=2^{\beta^{\prime}(\ell)-\ell}\big|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})\big|,

where
\operatorname{Supp}
denotes support.
Define

f_{\mathbf{x},\ell}(t):=\sum_{\varepsilon_{\ell+1}}1_{\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})}\big(t-\sum_{j=1}^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\varepsilon_{\ell+1,j}\mathbf{x}_{\ell+1,j}\big),

where
\varepsilon_{\ell+1}
denotes
(\varepsilon_{\ell+1,j})_{j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}]}
. Since

r_{\mathbf{x},\ell+1}(t)=\sum_{\varepsilon_{\ell+1}}r_{\mathbf{x},\ell}\big(t-\sum_{j=1}^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\varepsilon_{\ell+1,j}\mathbf{x}_{\ell+1,j}\big),

we see that

(6.10)

\operatorname{Supp}(f_{\mathbf{x},\ell})=\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell+1})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sum_{t}f_{\mathbf{x},\ell}(t)=2^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\big|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})\big|.

By expansion and a substitution, we have

\sum_{t}f_{\mathbf{x},\ell}(t)^{2}=\sum_{\varepsilon_{\ell+1},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\sum_{t}1_{\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})}(t)1_{\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})}\Big(t-\sum_{j=1}^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}(\varepsilon_{\ell+1,j}-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell+1,j})\mathbf{x}_{\ell+1,j}\Big),

whence

\displaystyle\sum_{t}

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{x},\ell}(t)^{2}-2^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\big|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})\big|=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varepsilon_{\ell+1}\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\sum_{t}1_{\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})}(t)1_{\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})}\Big(t-\sum_{j=1}^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}(\varepsilon_{\ell+1,j}-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell+1,j})\mathbf{x}_{\ell+1,j}\Big).

Denote the two equal expressions here by
E(\mathbf{x})
.
We may write

(6.11)

E(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\varepsilon_{\ell+1}\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}}1_{\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})}(t)1_{\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})}(t^{\prime})1\Big(t-t^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}(\varepsilon_{\ell+1,j}-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell+1,j})\mathbf{x}_{\ell+1,j}\Big).

By Cauchy-Schwarz we have
