\big|\operatorname{Supp}(f_{\mathbf{x},\ell})\big|\geqslant\big(\sum_{t}f_{\mathbf{x},\ell}(t)\big)^{2}/\sum_{t}f_{\mathbf{x},\ell}(t)^{2}
, and using this,
Equation

6.10
and the definition of
E(\mathbf{x})
, it follows that

\big|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell+1})\big|\geqslant\frac{2^{2(1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1})}|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})|^{2}}{2^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\big|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})\big|+E(\mathbf{x})},

and hence from
Equation

6.9
(and using
\ell+1-\beta^{\prime}(\ell+1)=\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)+(1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1})
) that

\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\leqslant\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell+1)\Big(1+\frac{E(\mathbf{x})}{2^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})|}\Big).

Taking expectations over the random choice of
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
and using the trivial bound
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell+1)\leqslant 1
on the second term gives

(6.12)

\mathbb{E}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\leqslant\mathbb{E}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell+1)+\mathbb{E}\Big(\frac{E(\mathbf{x})}{2^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x},\ell})|}\Big).

The remaining task is to bound the final average here. Consider again the definition
Equation

6.11
of
E(\mathbf{x})
. Condition to
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=x_{i,j}
for
i\leqslant\ell
and
j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]
. From
Equation

6.11
and the anticoncentration estimate
Equation

5.6
it follows that

\mathbb{E}\big(E(\mathbf{x})\mid\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=x_{i,j}\;\mbox{for $i\leqslant\ell$ and $j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]$}\big)\ll 2^{2(1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1})-\ell}\big|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{x,\ell})\big|^{2};

note here that we have written
r_{x,\ell}
rather than
r_{\mathbf{x},\ell}
since this quantity depends only on the fixed variables
x_{i,j}
with
i\leqslant\ell
and
j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]
. Consequently

\mathbb{E}\bigg(\frac{E(\mathbf{x})}{2^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{x,\ell})|}\mid\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=x_{i,j}\;\mbox{for $i\leqslant\ell$, $j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]$}\bigg)\ll 2^{1-\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1}-\ell}\big|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{x,\ell})\big|\ll 2^{-\beta^{\prime}(\ell+1)},

where here we used the trivial bound
\big|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{x,\ell})\big|\leqslant 2^{\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)}
.
Undoing the conditioning and recalling
Equation

6.12
, the estimate
Equation

6.8
follows.

∎

7.
The random variables
\varrho
and
\varrho^{*}

In this section we develop the basic properties of the
\varrho
-statistics as defined in
Section

1.2
, which feature prominently in the measure
\mu
(see
Proposition

1.3
).

Let
\beta
be an upper walk with increments
\xi_{i}=\beta(i)-\beta(i-1)
. In this section we suppose that
\beta
is infinite (rather than of length
\lceil n/2\rceil
) although we will only be dealing with finite segments of
\beta
. Recall the definition of the conditioned upper process
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
and its elements
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
,
j\in[1+\xi_{i}]
, as described in
Section

5.3
.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\beta
be an upper walk and let
\ell\geqslant 1
be an integer. Then we define the random variable
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
by

(7.1)

\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell):=2^{-\beta(\ell)}\sum_{\varepsilon}1\Big(\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in[1-2^{-\ell},1]\Big).

Here,
\varepsilon
ranges over all
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}
with
\varepsilon_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}
.
By convention we set
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(0):=1
.

A very closely-related definition is that of
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
, which is defined as in
Equation

1.3
, with
u
being the sequence
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
. It is slightly annoying to express this using the
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
notation for the elements of
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
; we do this now. Fix the upper walk
\beta
. In order for the quantities
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
to be well-defined, we will need to assume that

(7.2)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}(j+\beta(j))=\infty.

Given
\ell\geqslant 1
, let
\ell^{\prime}
be minimal so that
\ell^{\prime}+\beta(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
. (Note carefully that the definition of
\ell^{\prime}
here, in the context of an
upper
walk
\beta
, differs from the definition in the previous section, but there should be no danger of confusion.) Define an integer
r\geqslant 1
by

(7.3)

\ell^{\prime}-1+\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)=\ell-r.

We have
r\leqslant\max(\xi_{\ell^{\prime}}+1,\ell)
. Define
\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},1},\dots,\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},r}
to be the
r
smallest elements among the
\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime},j}
,
j\in[1+\xi_{\ell^{\prime}}]
, listed in ascending order so that
\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},r}
is the largest of these elements, and therefore the
\ell
th element of
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
when these are listed in ascending order. Then from the definition
Equation

1.3
we have

(7.4)

\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)=2^{\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},r}-\ell}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}1\big(\sum_{i<\ell^{\prime};j}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime},j}2^{-\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},j}}\in[1-2^{-\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},r}},1]\big).

We now provide a lemma giving upper bounds for the
\varrho
and
\varrho^{*}
statistics. The gist of this is that these variables are bounded in expectation (assuming weak boundedness properties of the walk
\beta
) and that both have only a small (
\sim 2^{-m}
) probability of being nonzero if
\beta
assumes the value
-m
. This last point corresponds to a rigorous version of the heuristic around
Equations

2.6
and
2.7
.

The proofs for
\varrho,\varrho^{*}
are slightly different in minor details but are best given together.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
m\geqslant 0
and
\ell\geqslant 1
be integers and let
R\geqslant 1
be a parameter. Suppose that
\beta
is an upper walk which is
R
-bounded to length
\ell
. Then

(7.5)

\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\ll R^{2},

where the expectation is over random choices of
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
. Suppose in addition that
\beta(q)=-m
for some
q
with
0\leqslant q\leqslant\ell
. Then

(7.6)

\mathbb{P}\big(\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0\big)\ll R^{3}(1+q)^{\kappa}2^{-m}.

Now suppose that
\beta
satisfies
Equation

6.2
, and that
\beta
is
R
-bounded to length
\ell^{\prime}
, where
\ell^{\prime}
is minimal so that
\ell^{\prime}+\beta(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
. Then

(7.7)

\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\ll R^{2}.

Suppose in addition that
\beta(q)=-m
for some
q
with
0\leqslant q\leqslant\ell^{\prime}
. Then

(7.8)

\mathbb{P}\big(\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0\big)\ll\max(\ell,\ell^{\prime})^{3}2^{-m}.

Proof.

An important tool in the proof will be the anticoncentration estimate

(7.9)

\mathbb{P}(2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in I)\ll 2^{i}|I|

uniformly for all intervals
I
and all
i
. This follows quickly from the fact that
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
is uniformly distributed on
(i-1,i]
. An important technical point is that when
r
is small there may not be a similar concentration estimate for the
\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell,j}
, which are likely to be concentrated at one end of the interval. Throughout the proof we may assume without loss of generality that
R
is sufficiently large.

Proof of
Equation

7.5
.
We observe that using the
R
-boundedness property we have (deterministically)

(7.10)

\sum_{i>2\log_{2}R;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\leqslant\sum_{i>2\log_{2}R}(1+\xi_{i})2^{1-i}\leqslant\sum_{i>2\log_{2}R}(1+Ri^{\kappa})2^{1-i}<\tfrac{1}{2}.

To see the second inequality, one can bound the sum by
\ll R^{-1/2}
, then use the assumption that
R
is sufficiently large. Now suppose that
\varepsilon
counts towards
Equation

7.1
, that is to say
\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in[1-2^{-\ell},1]
. In particular,
\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
. It follows from this and
Equation

7.10
that some
\varepsilon_{a,b}
is nonzero with
a\leqslant\min(2\log_{2}R,\ell)
. We then have

\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in[1-2^{-\ell},1]\big)\ll 2^{a-\ell}\ll R^{2}2^{-\ell};

this follows by revealing all
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
with
(i,j)\neq(a,b)
then using
Equation

7.9
. Therefore

\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in[1-2^{-\ell},1]\big)\ll R^{2}2^{-\ell},

uniformly for all
\varepsilon
. Summing over the
2^{\sum_{i\leqslant\ell}(1+\xi_{i})}=2^{\ell+\beta(\ell)}
choices of
(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{i\leqslant\ell,j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}
, and multiplying by the normalising factor
2^{-\beta(\ell)}
in the definition
Equation

7.1
, we obtain
Equation

7.5
.

Proof of
Equation

7.7
.
This is similar but slightly more technical due to the failure of
Equation

7.9
for the
\mathbf{u}^{*}
variables. First note the trivial bound

(7.11)

\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\leqslant 2^{\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},r}}\leqslant 2^{\ell^{\prime}}.

If
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant 3
, this immediately implies the required bound (since we are assuming
R\geqslant 1
), so suppose in what follows that
\ell^{\prime}\geqslant 4
.
Suppose that
\varepsilon
counts towards
Equation

7.4
. Since
\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},r}\geqslant\ell^{\prime}-1\geqslant 3
, we have

(7.12)

\sum_{i<\ell^{\prime};j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime},j}2^{-\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},j}}\geqslant\tfrac{3}{4}.

We divide into two cases: Case 1 is that there is some nonzero
\varepsilon_{a,b}
with
a\leqslant\min(2\log_{2}R,\ell^{\prime}-1)
, and Case 2 is that this fails to be the case. In Case 1, we may argue as before using
Equation

7.9
. If we are in Case 2 then, arguing as in the proof of
Equation

7.5
, we have
\sum_{i<\ell^{\prime};j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, and hence from
Equation

7.12
it follows that
\sum_{j=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime},j}2^{-\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},j}}\geqslant\frac{1}{4}
. Then, since
\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime},j}^{*}\geqslant\ell^{\prime}-1
, we have
r\geqslant 2^{\ell^{\prime}-3}
. By
R
-boundedness we have
r\leqslant 1+\xi_{\ell^{\prime}}\leqslant 1+R(\ell^{\prime})^{\kappa}
. Since
r\geqslant 2^{\ell^{\prime}-3}\geqslant 2
, this implies that
R\gg 2^{\ell^{\prime}/2}
, and then
Equation

7.7
follows using
Equation

7.11
.

Proof of
Equation

7.6
.
Suppose first that
q\leqslant 2\log_{2}R
. Since all steps of
\beta
are at least
-1
, we have
q\geqslant m
, and so
R^{2}2^{-m}\geqslant 1
. The stated bound is therefore trivial in this case, so we assume from now on that
q>2\log_{2}R
.

Suppose that
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0
. Then there is some
\varepsilon
such that
\sum_{i\leqslant\ell}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in[1-2^{-\ell},1]
. As before, this implies that
\varepsilon_{a,b}\neq 0
for some
a\leqslant\min(2\log_{2}R,\ell)
. In particular,
a\leqslant q
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}\geqslant i-1
,
\sum_{i\leqslant q}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}
lies in the interval
I:=[1-\sum_{i\geqslant q+1}(1+\xi_{i})2^{1-i}-2^{-\ell},1]
. By revealing all
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
except
\mathbf{u}_{a,b}
, it follows (since
q\geqslant a
) that
\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{i\leqslant q}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in I\big)\ll R^{2}|I|
. Summing over all possibilities for
(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{i\leqslant q,j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}
, of which there are
2^{\sum_{i=1}^{q}(1+\xi_{i})}=2^{q+\beta(q)}=2^{q-m}
, it follows that

(7.13)

\mathbb{P}(\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0)\ll R^{2}2^{q-m}|I|=R^{2}2^{-m}\big(\sum_{i\geqslant q+1}(1+\xi_{i})2^{q-i}+2^{q-\ell}\big).

Note that
2^{q-\ell}\leqslant 1
. Moreover, the
R
-boundedness assumption implies that

(7.14)

\sum_{i\geqslant q+1}(1+\xi_{i})2^{q-i}\leqslant q^{\kappa}\sum_{j\geqslant 1}2^{-j}\Big(\frac{1+R(j+q)^{\kappa}}{q^{\kappa}}\Big)\ll Rq^{\kappa}

The desired bound
Equation

7.6
therefore follows from
Equation

7.13
.

Proof of
Equation

7.8
.
Since the steps of
\beta
are bounded below by
-1
, we have
\ell^{\prime}\geqslant q\geqslant m
. Since
Equation

7.8
is vacuous for
m\leqslant 3
, we may assume
\ell^{\prime}\geqslant 3
. From the definition of
\ell^{\prime}
we have that
\beta(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)
, so the smallest
q^{\prime}
for which
\beta(q^{\prime})\leqslant-m
has
q^{\prime}\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1
. Replacing
q
by
q^{\prime}
and
m
by
m^{\prime}=-\beta(q^{\prime})
, we may assume that
q\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1
. Write
\tilde{R}
for the smallest quantity such that
\beta
is
\tilde{R}
bounded to length
\ell^{\prime}-1
. Since
\sum_{i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1}(\xi_{i}+1)=(\ell^{\prime}-1)+\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)<\ell
, we certainly have
\xi_{i}\leqslant\ell
for all
i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1
and so (crudely)
\tilde{R}\leqslant\ell
.

Suppose that
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0
. Then there is some
\varepsilon
such that

(7.15)

\sum_{i<\ell^{\prime};j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime},j}2^{-\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},j}}\in[1-2^{1-\ell^{\prime}},1].

Since
\ell^{\prime}\geqslant 3
, we must again be in at least one of the two cases, Case 1 and Case 2, as described following
Equation

7.12
.

We first treat Case 1. Using
Equation

7.10
(with
\tilde{R}
replacing
R
) we see that in this case there is some nonzero
\varepsilon_{a,b}
with
a\leqslant\min(2\log_{2}\tilde{R},\ell^{\prime}-1)
. Suppose that
q<a
. Then
q\leqslant 2\log_{2}\tilde{R}
. Since
q\geqslant m
,
\tilde{R}^{2}2^{-m}\geqslant 1
and hence
\ell^{2}2^{-m}\geqslant 1
. The bound
Equation

7.8
is therefore trivial in this case, so we assume from now on that
q\geqslant a
.

By
Equation

7.15
,
\sum_{i\leqslant q}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}
lies in the interval
\tilde{I}:=[1-\sum_{q+1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1}(1+\xi_{i})2^{1-i}-(r+1)2^{1-\ell^{\prime}},1]
. Here, it is important to note that
q\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1
. Arguing as in
Equation

7.13
(using here that
q\geqslant a
, and also that
\tilde{R}\leqslant\ell
) we obtain

\mathbb{P}\big(\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0\big)\ll\ell^{2}2^{-m}\big(\sum_{q+1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1}(1+\xi_{i})2^{q-i}+(r+1)2^{q-\ell^{\prime}}\big).

We have
(r+1)2^{q-\ell^{\prime}}<r+1\ll\ell
, so the contribution from this term is acceptable. The contribution from the sum over
i
may be bounded exactly as in
Equation

7.14
(with
\tilde{R}
replacing
R
), and this term is also acceptable. This concludes the analysis of Case 1.

Finally, we consider Case 2. As before,
r>2^{\ell^{\prime}-3}
. We also have
r\leqslant\ell
. Furthermore, since
\beta
is an upper walk we have
\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}}\beta(i)\geqslant{-}\ell^{\prime}
, and so
m\leqslant\ell^{\prime}
. Combining these facts gives
2^{m}\leqslant 8\ell
, and so
Equation

7.8
is trivial in this case.∎

Now we come to the second main result of the section, which asserts that
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
has good convergence properties as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
, under the assumption that
\beta
is appropriately
T
-positive in the sense of
Definition

4.2
. We will also need a related statement involving the
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
and, as with
Lemma

7.2
, it makes sense to prove this at the same time.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\ell\geqslant 1
be an integer and let
T\geqslant 1
be a parameter. Then there is some absolute constant
c\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
such that the following are true.

Suppose that
\beta
is an upper walk which is
T
-positive up to
\ell
. Then we have

(7.16)

\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\big|^{2}\ll 2^{3T-c\ell^{1/4}}.

Now suppose that
Equation

7.2
holds. Given
\ell
and
\beta
, we again define
\ell^{\prime}
to be minimal such that
\ell^{\prime}+\beta(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
. Suppose that
\beta
is
T
-positive up to
\ell^{\prime}
. Then we have

(7.17)

\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime}-1)-\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\big|^{2}\ll 2^{3T-c\ell^{\prime 1/4}}.

Proof.

We begin by observing that in both situations, the positivity of
\beta
implies some weak boundedness properties. Starting with
Equation

7.16
, from the definition
Equation

7.1
we have the trivial bound
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\leqslant 2^{\ell}
, and so
Equation

7.16
is vacuous unless
T\leqslant\ell
. Thus we may assume
T\leqslant\ell
throughout. But then, using the
T
-positive nature of
\beta
, we have
\xi_{i}=\beta(i)-\beta(i-1)\leqslant 2T+\ell^{1/2+\eta}\leqslant 3\ell
for all
i\leqslant\ell
. This implies that
\beta
is
R
-bounded up to
\ell
for
R:=3\ell
.

An essentially identical remark applies regarding
Equation

7.17
, here using the trivial bound
Equation

7.11
; in this case we may assume that
\beta
is
R^{\prime}:=3\ell^{\prime}
-bounded up to
\ell^{\prime}
.

We now begin the proof proper, starting with
Equation

7.17
, indicating as we go the (minor) modifications required to prove
Equation

7.16
. From this point on in the proof it is convenient to adopt the following shorthand for intervals:
a+b[\eta]
denotes the interval
[a,a+b\eta]\subset\mathbb{R}
. Here,
b
is allowed to be negative (in which case we mean the interval
[a+b\eta,a]
).

Let
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
L\in\mathbb{N}
(we will have
L\in\{\ell^{\prime},\ell\}
later on). Set
\varepsilon_{<L}=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{i<L;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\in\{0,1\}^{L-1+\beta(L-1)}
. Denote by
E(\mathbf{u},\varepsilon_{<L},\lambda,x)
the event (for random
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)=(\mathbf{u}_{i,j})_{i,j}
) that

(7.18)

\sum_{i<L;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in 1-x-2^{-\lambda}[2^{1-L}].

Denote
\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}}=(\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime},j})_{j=1}^{r}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
.
By mild rearrangement of
Equation

7.4
using
Equation

7.3
, we have

(7.19)

\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)=2^{-\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Av}_{\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}}}2^{\lambda_{0}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})}1\big(E(\mathbf{u},\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}},\lambda_{0}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}),x_{0}(\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}},\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})\big)

where here
\lambda_{0}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}):=\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},r}-(\ell^{\prime}-1)\in[0,1]
and
x_{0}(\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}},\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}):=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime},j}2^{-\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime},j}}
, and
\operatorname{Av}_{\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}}}
denotes the average over all tuples in
\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
.
On the other hand from the definition
Equation

7.1
we have

(7.20)

\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime}-1)=2^{-\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Av}_{\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}}}2^{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})}1\big(E(\mathbf{u},\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}},\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}),x_{1}(\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}},\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})\big)

with
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})=x_{1}(\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}},\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}):=0
.
Subtracting
Equation

7.20
from
Equation

7.19
gives

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)

\displaystyle-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime}-1)

(7.21)

\displaystyle=2^{-\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)}\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{\delta}\sum_{\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Av}_{\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}}}2^{\lambda_{\delta}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})}1\big(E(\mathbf{u},\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}},\lambda_{\delta}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}),x_{\delta}(\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}},\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})\big).

Squaring and taking expectations over the random choice of
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime}-1)\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=2^{-2\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)}\sum_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{\delta+\delta^{\prime}}\times

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<\ell^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Av}_{\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}}p_{\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<\ell^{\prime}}}\big(x_{\delta}(\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}},\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}),x_{\delta^{\prime}}(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}},\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}),\lambda_{\delta}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}}\big),\lambda_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})\big),

where

(7.22)

p_{\varepsilon_{<L},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<L}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}):=2^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(E(\mathbf{u},\varepsilon_{<L},\lambda,x)\cap E(\mathbf{u},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<L},\lambda^{\prime},x^{\prime})\big).

Since
\sum_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(-1)^{\delta+\delta^{\prime}}=0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime}-1)\big|^{2}\ll

(7.23)

\displaystyle\ll 2^{-2\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<\ell^{\prime}}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant x,x^{\prime}\leqslant 2R\ell^{\prime}2^{-\ell^{\prime}}\\
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in[0,1]\end{subarray}}\big|p_{\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<\ell^{\prime}}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})-p_{\varepsilon_{<\ell^{\prime}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<\ell^{\prime}}}(0,0,0,0)\big|.

Here, the constraints on
x,x^{\prime}
come from the definition of
x_{0}(\;,\;)
; we have
|x_{0}(\varepsilon_{\ell^{\prime}},\mathbf{u}^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}})|\leqslant r2^{1-\ell^{\prime}}\leqslant 2R(\ell^{\prime})^{\kappa}2^{-\ell^{\prime}}
.

To complete the proof of
Equation

7.17
, it therefore suffices to show that the RHS of
Equation

7.23
is
\ll 2^{3T-\Omega(\ell^{\prime 1/4})}
. At this point,
\ell^{\prime}
can be thought of as simply a dummy variable (rather than specifically the smallest
\ell^{\prime}
such that
\ell^{\prime}+\beta(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
). Replacing it by
L
(in both
Equation

7.22
and
Equation

7.23
), the task is therefore to show that if
\beta
is
R
-bounded and
T
-positive up to
L
, where
R:=3L
and
T\leqslant L
, then

(7.24)

2^{-2\beta(L-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon_{<L},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<L}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant x,x^{\prime}\leqslant 2RL2^{-L}\\
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in[0,1]\end{subarray}}\big|p_{\varepsilon_{<L},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<L}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})-p_{\varepsilon_{<L},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<L}}(0,0,0,0)\big|\ll 2^{3T-\Omega(L^{1/4})}.

Before turning to the proof of this, we claim that
Equation

7.16
also follows from it in the case
L=\ell
. For this, first recall
Equation

7.20
(with the dashes dropped from the
\ell
s):

\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell-1)=2^{-\beta(\ell-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon_{<\ell}}\operatorname{Av}_{\varepsilon_{\ell}}2^{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{\ell})}1\big(\sum_{i<\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in 1-x_{1}(\varepsilon_{\ell},\mathbf{u}_{\ell})-2^{-\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{\ell})}[2^{1-\ell}]\Big),

where
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{\ell})=x_{1}(\varepsilon_{\ell},\mathbf{u}_{\ell})=0
.
On the other hand,

\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)=2^{-\beta(\ell-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon_{<\ell}}\operatorname{Av}_{\varepsilon_{\ell}}2^{\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{\ell})}1\big(\sum_{i<\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in 1-x_{2}(\varepsilon_{\ell},\mathbf{u}_{\ell})-2^{-\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{\ell})}[2^{1-\ell}]\Big)

where
\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{\ell})=1
and
x_{2}(\varepsilon_{\ell},\mathbf{u}_{\ell}):=\sum_{j=1}^{\xi_{\ell}+1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{\ell,j}}
. That
Equation

7.16
is a consequence of
Equation

7.24
then follows almost verbatim as in the argument from
Equation

7.21
to
Equation

7.23
above.

To complete the proof of
Lemma

7.3
, then, the remaining task is to prove
Equation

7.24
. The bound is trivial for
L=O(1)
, so we may assume
L
sufficiently large. To ease notation, we write
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{<L}
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{<L}
for the rest of the proof.

For any of the
p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(\cdots)
terms in
Equation

7.24
to be nonzero (for a given fixed
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
) we must have that some event
E(\mathbf{u},\varepsilon_{<L},\lambda,x)
with
0\leqslant x\leqslant 2RL2^{-L}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
is nontrivial, which implies
\sum_{i<L;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{1-i}\geqslant 1-4RL2^{-L}>\frac{1}{2}
, using here that
R=3L
and that
L
is sufficiently large. By
Equation

7.10
, it follows that there is some
(i_{0},j_{0})
with
i_{0}\leqslant 2\log_{2}R
(
<L
) such that
\varepsilon_{i_{0},j_{0}}\neq 0
. Say that
\varepsilon
is
good
if it has this property. Thus we may restrict the sum in
Equation

7.24
to
\varepsilon
good, and similarly also to
\varepsilon^{\prime}
good.

We first consider the contribution to
Equation

7.24
from the diagonal terms with
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}
(with both being good). The number of such pairs is
<2^{L+\beta(L-1)}
. For fixed
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}
, pick some
(i_{0},j_{0})
with
\varepsilon_{i_{0},j_{0}}\neq 0
and
i_{0}\leqslant 2\log_{2}R
. Revealing all
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
except
\mathbf{u}_{i_{0},j_{0}}
, it follows from
Equation

7.9
that

p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\ll\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{i<L;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in 1-x-2^{-\lambda}[2^{1-L}]\big)\ll 2^{i_{0}-L}\ll R^{2}2^{-L}.

Thus the contribution of these terms to
Equation

7.24
is
\ll R^{2}2^{-\beta(L-1)}
, which is
\ll R^{2}2^{T-\Omega(L^{1/4})}
by the fact that
\beta
is a
T
-positive walk. Thus the contribution of the diagonal terms to
Equation

7.24
is acceptable.

Now consider the non-diagonal terms with
\varepsilon\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}
and both
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
good. Order the pairs
(i,j)
with
i<L
and
j\in[1+\xi_{i}]
lexicographically. Let
(a,j_{a})
be the first pair in this ordering for which at least one of
\varepsilon_{a,j_{a}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{a,j_{a}}
is not zero. Let
(b,j_{b})
be the first pair for which at least one of
\varepsilon_{b,j_{b}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{b,j_{b}}
is not zero, and for which
(\varepsilon_{a,j_{a}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{a,j_{a}})\neq(\varepsilon_{b,j_{b}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{b,j_{b}})
. These pairs must exist since
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
are distinct and neither is the zero tuple (since they are both good).

For future reference we count the number of pairs
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
corresponding to a given
(a,b)
. For such pairs we have
\varepsilon_{i,j}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}=0
for
i\leqslant a-1
, whilst

(\varepsilon_{i,j},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})\in\{(0,0),(\varepsilon_{a,j_{a}},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{a,j_{a}})\}
for
a\leqslant i\leqslant b-1
, so the number of such choices is bounded by

(7.25)

2^{(b+\beta(b-1))-(a+\beta(a-1))}2^{2(L+\beta(L-1))-2(b+\beta(b-1))}.

Expanding out the definitions (that is,
Equation

7.22
and the definition
Equation

7.18
of the event
E(\cdots)
), one sees that

p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})=2^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\big)

where

Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
is the event that

\varepsilon_{a,j_{a}}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{a,j_{a}}}+\varepsilon_{b,j_{b}}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{b,j_{b}}}+t(\mathbf{u})\in 1-x-2^{-\lambda}[2^{1-L}]

and

\varepsilon^{\prime}_{a,j_{a}}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{a,j_{a}}}+\varepsilon^{\prime}_{b,j_{b}}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{b,j_{b}}}+t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u})\in 1-x^{\prime}-2^{-\lambda^{\prime}}[2^{1-L}],

where

t(\mathbf{u}):=\sum_{(i,j)\neq(a,j_{a}),(b,j_{b})}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}},\quad t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u}):=\sum_{(i,j)\neq(a,j_{a}),(b,j_{b})}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}.

Write
M=M(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime})
for the (nonsingular)
2\times 2
matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varepsilon_{a,j_{a}}&\varepsilon_{b,j_{b}}\\
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{a,j_{a}}&\varepsilon^{\prime}_{b,j_{b}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Then we can recast the definition of
Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
as the event that

(7.26)

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{a,j_{a}}}\\
2^{-\mathbf{u}_{b,j_{b}}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in D_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}-M^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x+t(\mathbf{u})\\
x^{\prime}+t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u})\end{smallmatrix}\right),

where
D_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}:=M^{-1}\big((1-2^{-\lambda}[2^{1-L}])\times(1-2^{-\lambda^{\prime}}[2^{1-L}])\big)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
To continue the computation we condition on the values of
t=t(\mathbf{u})
and
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u})
, thus we write

(7.27)

p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})=2^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\int f(t,t^{\prime})\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\mid t(\mathbf{u})=t,t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u})=t^{\prime}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}t^{\prime},

where
f
is the probability density function of the variable
(t(\mathbf{u}),t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u}))
.
We divide into two cases for the pair
(t,t^{\prime})
via the following definition.

Definition 7.4
.

We say that a pair
(t,t^{\prime})
is an
edge pair
if at least one of the sets
S_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},x,x^{\prime}}:=D_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}-M^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x+t\\
x^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in[0,1]
,
0\leqslant x,x^{\prime}\leqslant 2RL2^{-L}
, has a point outside the rectangle
[2^{-a},2^{1-a}]\times[2^{-b},2^{1-b}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and also one of the
S_{\tilde{\lambda},\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime},\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime}}
has a point inside this rectangle.

Using this definition and
Equation

7.27
we may decompose
p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{edge}}+p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{nonedge}}
in the obvious way. By the triangle inequality, it is sufficient to show the bound
Equation

7.24
with
p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
replaced by each of
p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{edge}}
and
p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{nonedge}}
separately.

The edge case.
We begin by handling the contribution of
p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{edge}}
. One may compute that the distance between any point in
S_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},x,x^{\prime}}
and any point in
S_{\tilde{\lambda},\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime},\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime}}
is
\ll RL2^{-L}
. Therefore if
(t,t^{\prime})
is an edge pair,
M^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1-t\\
1-t^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
(which lies in
S_{0,0,0,0}
) lies in one of the four thin rectangles
[2^{-a}-O(RL2^{-L}),2^{-a}]\times[2^{-b},2^{1-b}]
,
[2^{1-a},2^{1-a}+O(RL2^{-L})]\times[2^{-b},2^{1-b}]
,
[2^{-a},2^{1-a}]\times[2^{-b}-O(RL2^{-L}),2^{-b}]
or
[2^{-a},2^{1-a}]\times[2^{1-b},2^{1-b}+O(RL2^{-L})]
. Since the possible rows of
M^{-1}
are
\pm(0,1)
,
\pm(1,0)
and
\pm(1,-1)
, we see that one of
t,t^{\prime},t-t^{\prime}
is constrained to some interval of length
O(RL2^{-L})
. We call these Cases 1,2 and 3 respectively.

Case 1.
Observe that
\varepsilon_{i_{1},j_{1}}\neq 0
for some
(i_{1},j_{1})
with
i_{1}\leqslant b+2\log_{2}R
: this is a consequence of the restriction to
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
being good. Revealing all
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
except
\mathbf{u}_{i_{1},j_{1}}
,
\mathbf{u}_{a,j_{a}}
,
\mathbf{u}_{b,j_{b}}
, it follows that the probability of
M^{-1}(t(\mathbf{u}),t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u}))
lying in one of the four thin rectangles is
\ll R^{3}L2^{b-L}
.

Case 2.
The analysis is identical to Case 1 except now we use a non-zero value of
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i_{1},j_{1}}
.

Case 3.
Note that
\varepsilon_{i_{1},j_{1}}-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\neq 0
for some
(i_{1},j_{1})
with
i_{1}\leqslant b+2\log_{2}R
, using the definition of
b
. The rest of the analysis is as in Case 1.

By the above discussion,

(7.28)

\mathbb{P}\big((t(\mathbf{u}),t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u}))\;\mbox{is an edge pair}\big)\ll R^{3}L2^{b-L}.

Now note that uniformly we have

\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\mid t(\mathbf{u})=t,\,t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u})=t^{\prime}\big)\ll 2^{a+b-2L}.

This follows by observing that any translate of
D_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}
is contained in some
I_{1}\times I_{2}
, where
I_{1},I_{2}
are intervals of length
2^{2-L}
, and so by
Equation

7.9
we have

(7.29)

\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\mid t(\mathbf{u})=t,t^{\prime}(\mathbf{u})=t^{\prime}\big)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(2^{-\mathbf{u}_{a,j_{a}}}\in I_{1})\mathbb{P}(2^{-\mathbf{u}_{b,j_{b}}}\in I_{2})\ll 2^{a-L}\cdot 2^{b-L}.

Putting
Equations

7.28
and
7.29
together, for a given
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
corresponding to
(a,b)
, uniformly in
x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
we have

p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{edge}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda)\ll R^{3}L2^{a+2b-3L}.

In particular this holds with
x=x^{\prime}=\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}=0
.

We now bound the total contribution to
Equation

7.24
from the
p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{edge}}
terms by summing the above bound over
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
corresponding to a given pair
(a,b)
, then finally summing over
(a,b)
. Using
Equation

7.25
, the contribution to
Equation

7.24
for a fixed
(a,b)
is bounded by

\ll R^{3}L\cdot 2^{-2\beta(L-1)}\cdot 2^{(b+\beta(b-1))-(a+\beta(a-1))}\cdot 2^{2(L+\beta(L-1))-2(b+\beta(b-1))}\cdot 2^{a+2b-3L},

which simplifies to
R^{3}L2^{b-\beta(a-1)-\beta(b-1)-L}
.
Finally, we sum over all
(a,b)
with
a\leqslant b\leqslant L
. The contribution from
b<L/2
is negligible (using the
T
-positive nature of
\beta
to bound
-\beta(a-1)-\beta(b-1)\leqslant 2T
). For the contribution from
b\geqslant L/2
, we use
b-L\leqslant 0
and
-\beta(a-1)-\beta(b-1)\leqslant 2T-(L/2-1)^{1/2-\eta}
, which again follows from the
T
-positive nature of
\beta
. Using these estimates we obtain our sum over all
(a,b)
, that is to say the total contribution from the edge cases, is
\ll R^{3}2^{2T-\Omega(L^{1/4})}
. Since
R=3L
, the
R^{3}
factor can be absorbed by reducing the
\Omega
constant, and this is acceptable towards
Equation

7.24
.

The non-edge case.
In this case, using
Equation

7.26
we see that

\displaystyle 2^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\mid t(\mathbf{u})=t,\,t(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=t^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(0,0,0,0)\mid t(\mathbf{u})=t,\,t(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=t^{\prime}\big)

(7.30)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\log 2)^{2}}\Big(2^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\frac{dydy^{\prime}}{yy^{\prime}}-\int_{\mathcal{R}}\frac{dydy^{\prime}}{yy^{\prime}}\Big),

where
\mathcal{R}:=D_{0,0}-M^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t\\
t^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}:=D_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}-M^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x+t\\
x^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and both regions of integration are contained in
[2^{-a},2^{1-a}]\times[2^{-b},2^{1-b}]
. Set
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
u^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right):=M^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1-t\\
1-t^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{R}
; thus
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
u^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in[2^{-a},2^{1-a}]\times[2^{-b},2^{1-b}]
. If
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
y^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
then
|u-y|,|u^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\ll RL2^{-L}
. Now since
a\leqslant b
, both first partial derivatives of
1/yy^{\prime}
on
[2^{-a},2^{1-a}]\times[2^{-b},2^{1-b}]
are bounded by
\ll 2^{a+2b}
, and therefore on
\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
we have

\frac{1}{yy^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{uu^{\prime}}+O(RL2^{-L}2^{a+2b}).

Substituting into
Equation

7.30
, the contributions from the two
\frac{1}{uu^{\prime}}
terms cancel since
\mu(\mathcal{R})=2^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\mu(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
(here
\mu
is Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
). The contribution from the
O(RL2^{-L}2^{a+2b})
error is
\ll RL2^{a+2b-3L}
, since
\mu(\mathcal{R}),\mu(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})\ll 2^{-2L}
.

Combining these observations gives

\displaystyle\Big|2^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\mid t(\mathbf{u})=t,\,t(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=t^{\prime}\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle\mid t(\mathbf{u})=t,\,t(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=t^{\prime}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\ll RL2^{a+2b-3L}.

Integrating over all non-edge
(t,t^{\prime})
and using
\int f(t,t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}=1
, we have

\big|p^{\operatorname{nonedge}}_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda)-p^{\operatorname{nonedge}}_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(0,0,0,0)\big|\ll RL2^{a+2b-3L}.

Exactly as before, we may sum this over all
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
corresponding to a given
(a,b)
, and then over all
(a,b)
, obtaining the same bound
2^{2T-\Omega(L^{1/4})}
for the contribution of the
p_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{nonedge}}
terms to
Equation

7.24
.

This concludes the proof of
Equation

7.24
and hence of
Lemma

7.3
.
∎

A corollary of
Lemma

7.3
is a square mean bound for
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
, which we will need in
Section

15
.

Corollary 7.5
.

Let
\ell
be sufficiently large and let
T\geqslant 1
be a parameter. Suppose that
\beta
is an upper walk which is
T
-positive up to
\ell
. Then we have

\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{2}\ll 2^{3T}.

Proof.

We use the inequalities

\mathbb{E}|X_{N}-X_{0}|^{2}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{N}|X_{i}-X_{i-1}|\big)^{2}\leqslant\big(\sum_{i=1}^{N}i^{-2}\big)\big(\sum_{i=1}^{N}i^{2}\mathbb{E}|X_{i}-X_{i-1}|^{2}\big)

and

\mathbb{E}|X_{N}|^{2}\leqslant 2\mathbb{E}|X_{N}-X_{0}|^{2}+2\mathbb{E}|X_{0}|^{2}
, valid for any sequence
(X_{i})_{i=0}^{N}
of real-valued random variables. Applying these with
N=\ell
and
X_{i}=\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(i)
, the result follows from
Lemma

7.3
.
∎

Remark
.

A somewhat shorter, direct, proof of
Corollary

7.5
is possible, along the lines of
Lemma

7.3
but without the careful analysis of ‘edge’ cases. We leave the details to the interested reader.

Part II
Background material

Each section of this part of the paper may be read independently of the rest of the paper. Our aim here is to assemble various background ingredients for the remaining parts of the argument.

8.
Properties of random walks

In this section we collect the results we need on random walks constrained to be almost positive. Throughout the section
\bm{\beta}
will be a random walk with increments
\bm{\xi}_{i}\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
, and
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
a random walk with increments
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}\overset{d}{=}1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)
, though much of the discussion would apply to rather general random walks.

For integers
m
, we write

(8.1)

h(m)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)

and

(8.2)

h^{\prime}(m)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

The following proposition assembles the basic properties of these quantities.

Proposition 8.1
.

We have the following statements.

(1)

h(m)
,
h^{\prime}(m)
exist for all
m\geqslant 0

(
that is, the limits in
Equations

8.1
and
8.2
exist for
m\geqslant 0
)
.

(2)

For
m\geqslant 0
we have
h(m)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}(m+1)
and
h^{\prime}(m)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}(m+O(1))
.

(3)

Let
0\leqslant m\leqslant\sqrt{N}
. Then, uniformly,

(8.3)

N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\asymp m+1,

and similarly for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
. Moreover we have the upper bound

(8.4)

N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll m+1

with no restriction on
m
.

Proof.

These may mostly be regarded as classical results from the fluctuation theory of random walks. However, it is not easy to locate appropriate references. See
Appendix

A
for further details and proofs. We remark that
Equation

8.4
is a trivial consequence of
Equation

8.3
.
∎

Once it is established that
h(m)
exists for all
m\geqslant 0
, it follows by conditioning on the first step of the walk that
h(m)
exists for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and that we have the recurrence relation

(8.5)

h(m)=\sum_{r\geqslant-m}\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1=r)h(m+r)

(and similarly for
h^{\prime}
).
h(-1)
is a convenient shorthand for
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)>0\big)
, and similarly for
h^{\prime}(-1)
. We remark that
h^{\prime}(m)=0
for
m\leqslant-2
, since
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(1)\leqslant 1
.

8.1.
Spaces of almost positive paths

In this subsection we describe how, associated to random walks such as
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
, there are certain measures on
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}
supported on sequences which are bounded below. One should heuristically think of this as allowing us to sample ‘a random
\bm{\beta}
which is bounded below’, though some care should be taken with this interpretation since these are not probability measures. The construction is mostly standard and is an example of Doob conditioning; we give brief details for the convenience of the reader.

Let
m\geqslant-1
. Denote by
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}
the space of sequences
\beta=(\beta(i))_{i=1}^{\infty}
with
\beta(i)\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\beta(i)\geqslant-m
for all
i
.
Consider the
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}
generated by ‘cylinder’ sets of the form
\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell}):=\{\beta\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}:\beta(i)=c_{i}\;\mbox{for $1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell$}\}
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, where here we may restrict to
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}c_{i}\geqslant-m
.

Now consider the upper random walk
\bm{\beta}
, that is to say the random walk with
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
increments. Define
\omega_{m}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m})=h(m)
and

(8.6)

\omega_{m}\big(\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell})\big):=h(m+c_{\ell})\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(i)=c_{i}\;\mbox{for $1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell$}\big).

Lemma 8.2
.

\omega_{m}
is well-defined and extends to a measure on
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}

(
with the
\sigma
-algebra described above
)
.
\omega_{m}
is supported on sequences
\beta
for which
\beta(i+1)\geqslant\beta(i)-1
for all
i
.

Proof.

By the Daniell-Kolmogorov extension theorem it is enough to check the compatibility condition

\sum_{c_{\ell+1}}\omega_{m}\big(\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell},c_{\ell+1})\big)=\omega_{m}\big(\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell})\big).

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{c_{\ell+1}}\omega_{m}\big(\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell},c_{\ell+1})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c_{\ell+1}}h(m+c_{\ell+1})\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(i)=c_{i}\;\mbox{for $1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell+1$}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c_{\ell+1}}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\xi}_{\ell+1}=c_{\ell+1}-c_{\ell}\big)h(m+c_{\ell+1})\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(i)=c_{i}\;\mbox{for $1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell$}\big)

\displaystyle=h(m+c_{\ell})\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(i)=c_{i}\;\mbox{for $1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell$}\big)=\omega_{m}\big(\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell})\big),

where the
\bm{\xi}_{i}
are the increments of
\bm{\beta}
and in the penultimate step we used
Equation

8.5
with
m
replaced by
m+c_{\ell}
and the dummy variable
r
equal to
c_{\ell+1}-c_{\ell}
. The key point to note is that this is valid since
m+c_{\ell+1}\geqslant 0
by assumption.
∎

In an essentially identical manner we define measures
\omega^{\prime}_{m}
associated to the lower walk
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
(in fact a similar construction holds for any random walk
\bm{\gamma}
with identical increments satisfying suitable assumptions, but we only need
\omega_{m}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{m}
in the present paper).

The following lemma provides a reasonably intuitive way to think about these measures.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
m\geqslant-1
and
L\geqslant 1
be integers, and suppose that
S\subset[-m,\infty)^{L}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{L}
. Denote by
E
the set of walks
\beta
with
(\beta(1),\dots,\beta(L))\in S
. Then

(8.7)

\omega_{m}\{\beta\in E\}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\in E,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

Suppose moreover that
L\leqslant N/2
and that
m\leqslant\sqrt{N}
. Then

(8.8)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\in E,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll N^{-1/2}\omega_{m}\{\beta\in E\}.

Analogous results hold for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{m}\{\beta^{\prime}\in E\}
.

Proof.

We first establish
Equation

8.7
. First of all, fix
(c_{1},\dots,c_{L})\in S
, and let
N
be much larger than
L
. Considering the translated walk
\bm{\gamma}(i):=\bm{\beta}(i+L)-\bm{\beta}(L)
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(1)=

\displaystyle c_{1},\dots,\bm{\beta}(L)=c_{L},\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(1)=c_{1},\dots,\bm{\beta}(L)=c_{L}\big)\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-L}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant-m-c_{L}\big).

By the definitions of
h(m+c_{L})
and of
\omega_{m}
, this is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(1)=c_{1},

\displaystyle\dots,\bm{\beta}(L)=c_{L}\big)\cdot(1+o_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1))(N-L)^{-1/2}h(m+c_{L})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(1)=c_{1},\dots,\bm{\beta}(L)=c_{L}\big)\cdot(1+o_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1))N^{-1/2}h(m+c_{L})

\displaystyle=(1+o_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1))N^{-1/2}\omega_{m}(\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{L})).

Summing over all
(c_{1},\dots,c_{L})\in E
gives
Equation

8.7
. The proof of
Equation

8.8
is almost identical, but now using the second statement of
Proposition

8.1
(2) and (3) in place of the definition of
h(\cdot)
, and applying the bound
N\ll N-L
, which is valid in the range
L\leqslant N/2
.
∎

Path spaces.
We recall that, associated to
\bm{\beta}
, there is the standard notion of the path space
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}
together with its associated measure which, with a slight abuse of notation, we denote by
\mathbb{P}
. Here the
\sigma
-algebra is generated by cylinder sets
\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell})
(but now in
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}
) and
\int 1_{\Gamma(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell})}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\beta):=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(1)=c_{1},\dots,\bm{\beta}(\ell)=c_{\ell})
. It follows from the definitions that if
F
is a function supported on paths
\beta
with
\beta(i)\geqslant-m
for all
i
, but otherwise depending only on
\beta(1),\dots,\beta(\ell)
, then

(8.9)

\int F(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}=\int F(\beta)h(m+\beta(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\beta).

There is a similar construction with respect to
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
, and we will use
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
to denote the corresponding path measure.

8.2.
Crude bounds for endpoints of almost positive walks

The following lemma may be found in various places, for instance
[
39
, Lemma 20]
and
[
2
]
. A very succinct argument is presented as Theorem 1 in the unpublished note
[
1
]
; we sketch this in
Section

A.3
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Uniformly for integers
N\geqslant 1
and
x\geqslant 0
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0\big)\ll\min\big(N^{-1},(1+x)N^{-3/2}\big),

and similarly for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

See the references above or
Section

A.3
.∎

We will need the following generalisation in which the condition on
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)
is relaxed.

Lemma 8.5
.

Uniformly for integers
N\geqslant 1
,
m\geqslant 0
and
x\geqslant 0
we have

(8.10)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll(1+m)^{2}(1+x+m)N^{-3/2}.

and

(8.11)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll(1+m)^{2}N^{-1}.

Similar estimates hold for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

In both cases we may assume that
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-s
for some
s
with
0\leqslant s\leqslant m
since the contribution from walks for which this is not the case can be bounded using
Lemma

8.4
. For
j\in[N]
define the probability

p(j,s,x):=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\,\bm{\beta}(j)=\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-s\big).

Thus it suffices to obtain upper bounds on

(8.12)

\sum_{0\leqslant s\leqslant m}\sum_{1\leqslant j\leqslant N}p(j,s,x).

To bound
p(j,s,x)
we employ a reversal principle, running a walk with
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}
increments backwards from
j
and a walk with
\bm{\xi}
increments forwards. (Specifically, these walks are given by
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}(i):=\bm{\beta}(j-i)+s
and
\bm{\gamma}(i):=\bm{\beta}(j+i)+s
respectively.) From this we see that

(8.13)

p(j,s,x)=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}(j)=s,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j}\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant 0\big)\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\gamma}(N-j)=x+s,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-j}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant 0\big),

where the second term is absent (equal to 1) when
j=N
, and for the first term to be valid we needed
s\geqslant 0
.

The second probability term in
Equation

8.13
is
\ll(N+1-j)^{-1}
by
Lemma

8.4
(considering the cases
j<N
and
j=N
separately). The first probability term in
Equation

8.13
is
\ll(1+s)j^{-3/2}
, again using
Lemma

8.4
. Inputting these bounds into
Equation

8.13
gives the estimate

(8.14)

p(j,s,x)\ll(1+s)j^{-3/2}(N+1-j)^{-1}.

It then follows easily that the sum in
Equation

8.12
is
\ll(1+m)^{2}N^{-1}
, which gives
Equation

8.11
.

Alternatively, one can use the second estimate in
Lemma

8.4
on the second probability term in
Equation

8.13
, obtaining

p(j,s,x)\ll(1+s)(1+x+s)j^{-3/2}(N+1-j)^{-3/2}.

From this it follows that the sum in
Equation

8.12
is
\ll(1+m)^{2}(1+x+m)N^{-3/2}
, which gives
Equation

8.10
.
∎

Remarks
.

The statements
Equations

8.10
and
8.11
are provided as natural generalisations of
Lemma

8.4
. In certain applications below one could save occasional powers of
1+m
by slightly more efficient use of the same underlying ideas. However, these powers of
1+m
are not important to us in this paper. The bound
Equation

8.14
will be used a few times later on in its own right. Finally, we note that a similar statement with a similar proof may be found as
[
22
, Theorem 2]
.

As an application, we have the following bounds for measures of certain sets of walks.

Lemma 8.6
.

Uniformly for
m\geqslant 0
,
i\geqslant 1
and
j\geqslant-m
we have

(8.15)

\omega_{m}\{\beta\colon\beta(i)=j\}\ll(1+m)^{2}(1+j+m)^{2}i^{-3/2},

and similar bounds hold for
\omega^{\prime}_{m}
.
In particular for all
q\geqslant 0
we have

(8.16)

\omega_{m}\{\beta\colon\beta(q)=-m\}\ll(1+m)^{2}(1+q)^{-3/2}.

Proof.

For
Equation

8.15
, the value is
h(m+j)\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(i)=j,\;\min_{1\leqslant i^{\prime}\leqslant i}\bm{\beta}(i^{\prime})\geqslant-m)
.
We have
h(m+j)\ll 1+m+j
by
Proposition

8.1
(2), and the probability term may be bounded using
Equation

8.10
. This gives
Equation

8.15
. The bound
Equation

8.16
follows from
Equation

8.15
by taking
i=q
and
j=-m
.
∎

We also have the following statement about the position of the minimum.

Lemma 8.7
.

Uniformly for integers
N\geqslant 1
,
0\leqslant q\leqslant N
, and
m\geqslant 0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(q)=-m,\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll(1+m)(1+q)^{-3/2}(N+1-q)^{-1/2}.

Proof.

When
q=0
we must have
m=0
, and the result is immediate from the existence of
h(0)
. Suppose that
q\geqslant 1
. Then by
Equation

8.10
we have
\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(q)=-m,\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)\ll(1+m)^{2}q^{-3/2}
. When
q=N
this gives the result immediately. Suppose that
q<N
. The shifted walk
\bm{\gamma}(i):=\bm{\beta}(q+i)-\bm{\beta}(q)
of length
N-q
must have
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-q}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant 0
which, by the existence of
h(0)
, occurs with probability
\ll(N-q)^{-1/2}\ll(N+1-q)^{-1/2}
. Since this walk is independent of
(\bm{\beta}(i))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}
, the stated bound follows.
∎

8.3.
Local limit theorems for almost positive walks and applications

The main result of this subsection is a quantitative version of the local limit theorem for random walks constrained to be almost positive. Here is the statement we need. In this result

(8.17)

W(x)=xe^{-x^{2}/2}1_{x\geqslant 0}

denotes the density of the Rayleigh distribution.

Proposition 8.8
.

There is an absolute constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that the following holds.
Uniformly for integers
m,x,N
with
m,x\geqslant 0
and
N\geqslant 1
we have

(8.18)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)=N^{-1}h(m)W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O\big((1+m)^{O(1)}N^{-1-\varepsilon_{0}}\big)

and

(8.19)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(N)=x,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m\big)=N^{-1}h^{\prime}(m)W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O\big((1+m)^{O(1)}N^{-1-\varepsilon_{0}}\big).

Remarks
.

For the proof, see
Section

A.4
and
Appendix

B
. We give some brief bibliographical remarks here. The result also holds in the case
m=-1
(with
(1+m)^{O(1)}
replaced by
1
) and this is a result of Denisov, Tarasov and Wachtel
[
11
, Theorem 1]
; however, this is a very precise asymptotic with a correspondingly lengthy proof, and moreover depends on
[
13
]
. For these reasons, we give an essentially self-contained account in our appendices. Other relevant references are Caravenna
[
10
]
(who was seemingly the first to prove a local limit theorem for random walks conditioned to stay positive, but with no quantitative dependencies) and Grama and Xiao
[
25
, Theorem 6.1]
, which (with a little work) would imply
Proposition

8.8
. Let us be clear that we claim no originality for this result.

For the remainder of the paper
\varepsilon_{0}
will denote a fixed constant for which
Proposition

8.8
holds true.

8.4.
Coupling results

We now derive some results about coupling
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
together to have nearby endpoints. These will be critical inputs in our argument.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
\varepsilon_{1}>0
be a sufficiently small constant. Let
m,m^{\prime}
be non-negative integers. Let
L,N,N^{\prime}
be positive integers with
|N^{\prime}-N|\leqslant 1
. Let
c_{1},\dots,c_{L}
and
c^{\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
c_{i}\geqslant-m
and
c^{\prime}_{i}\geqslant-m^{\prime}
for all
i
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant L
. If
m>0
suppose that
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L}c_{i}=-m
, and if
m^{\prime}>0
that
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L}c^{\prime}_{i}=-m^{\prime}
. Assume that

\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L}c_{i},\;\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L}c^{\prime}_{i}\leqslant N^{\varepsilon_{1}}\quad\mbox{and that}\quad L,|d|,m,m^{\prime}\leqslant N^{\varepsilon_{1}}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N^{\prime}}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)

\displaystyle=-m^{\prime},\;\bm{\beta}(N)-\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(N^{\prime})=d\mid\bm{\beta}(i)=c_{i},\;\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=c^{\prime}_{i},\;i=1\dots,L\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}N^{-3/2}h(m+c_{L})h^{\prime}(m^{\prime}+c^{\prime}_{L})+O(N^{-3/2-\varepsilon_{1}}).

Proof.

The result is vacuous for
N=O(1)
so we assume
N
sufficiently large throughout.
Let
\bm{\gamma}
be the random walk of length
N-L
defined by
\bm{\gamma}(i):=\bm{\beta}(L+i)-c_{L}
, thus
\bm{\gamma}
has
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
increments. Define
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
similarly. The condition that
\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m
is then precisely the condition that
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-L}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant-m-c_{L}
, and similarly for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.
Therefore the probability we are interested in equals

\sum_{x-x^{\prime}=d}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\gamma}(N-L)=x,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-L}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant-m-c_{L}\big)\cdot\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\gamma}(N^{\prime}-L)=x^{\prime},\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N^{\prime}-L}\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m^{\prime}-c^{\prime}_{L}\big).

We can localise the sum to
x,x^{\prime}\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}
with simple large deviation estimates on
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\gamma}(N-L)\geqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon})
and the corresponding estimate with
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
(see
Lemma

F.3
).
By
Proposition

8.8
, the remaining sum is

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x-x^{\prime}=d\\
x,x^{\prime}\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\Big(\frac{h(m+c_{L})}{N-L}W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N-L}}\big)+O\big(N^{C\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{0}-1}\big)\Big)\Big(\frac{h(m^{\prime}+c^{\prime}_{L})}{N^{\prime}-L}W\big(\frac{x^{\prime}}{\sqrt{N^{\prime}-L}}\big)+O\big(N^{C\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{0}-1}\big)\Big)

for some absolute constant
C
.
By the assumptions and
Proposition

8.1
(2) we have
h(m+c_{L}),h^{\prime}(m^{\prime}+c^{\prime}_{L})\ll N^{\varepsilon_{1}}
and so one may see that the contribution from all terms involving one of the
O(\cdot)
errors is acceptable, provided
\varepsilon_{1}
is sufficiently small in terms of
\varepsilon_{0}
. We are then left with the sum

\frac{h(m+c_{L})h^{\prime}(m^{\prime}+c^{\prime}_{L})}{(N-L)(N^{\prime}-L)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x-x^{\prime}=d\\
x,x^{\prime}\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}\end{subarray}}W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N-L}}\big)W\big(\frac{x^{\prime}}{\sqrt{N^{\prime}-L}}\big).

Now one may check that
\frac{x^{\prime}}{\sqrt{N^{\prime}-L}},\frac{x}{\sqrt{N-L}}=\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}+O(N^{\varepsilon_{1}-1/2})
in the range of summation, and so the above is

N^{-2}h(m+c_{L})h^{\prime}(m^{\prime}+c^{\prime}_{L})\big(1+O(\frac{L}{N})\big)\sum_{x,x-d\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}}\Big(W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)^{2}+O(N^{\varepsilon_{1}-1/2})\Big).

The contributions from both the
O(L/N)
and the
O(N^{\varepsilon_{1}-1/2})
terms are easily seen to be acceptable, so we are left with

N^{-2}h(m+c_{L})h^{\prime}(m^{\prime}+c^{\prime}_{L})\sum_{x,x-d\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}}W(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}})^{2}.

Comparing the sum to an integral we see that

N^{-1/2}\sum_{x,x-d\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}}W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)^{2}=\int^{\infty}_{0}W(t)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+O(N^{-1/2})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}+O(N^{-1/2}).

This concludes the proof.
∎

The next result allows us to dispense with ‘rare’ events in coupled situations like the above.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
M,N,N^{\prime}
be positive integers with
|N^{\prime}-N|\leqslant 1
. Suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is an event on the sample space of walks
\bm{\beta}
of lengths
\leqslant N
. Then uniformly for nonnegative integers
m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M
and
d\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{E},\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N^{\prime}}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=-m^{\prime},\;\bm{\beta}(N)-\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(N^{\prime})=d\big)

\displaystyle\ll M^{2}N^{-1}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{E},\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)\ll M^{3}N^{-3/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{E}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

There is a symmetric estimate with the roles of
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
reversed.

Proof.

We can write the probability as

\sum_{x}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{E},\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\bm{\beta}(N)=x\big)\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N^{\prime}}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=-m^{\prime},\;\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(N^{\prime})=x-d\big).

Applying
Equation

8.11
to the second factor gives the first estimate. To get the second estimate we relax the
\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m
condition to
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m
and apply
Equation

8.4
.
∎

8.5.
Boundedness estimates

In this section we bound the probability of an almost positive walk having an unusually large increment. We start with a definition which is closely related to
Definition

4.1
. Recall that
\kappa=\frac{1}{100}
is a globally defined small constant.

Definition 8.11
.

Let
\beta
be a walk. Then we define
R(\beta)
to be the minimal positive integer
R
for which
|\xi_{i}|\leqslant Ri^{\kappa}
for all
i
. Set
R(\beta):=\infty
if no such
R
exists. More generally denote by
R_{\leqslant L}(\beta)
the minimal positive integer for which this holds for all
i\leqslant L
, that is to say the minimal positive integer
R
for which
\beta
is
R
-bounded to length
L
.

Remark
.

The restriction of the values of
R
to a discrete set (the integers) is a technical convenience.

Lemma 8.12
.

Uniformly in integers
L,N
with
1\leqslant L\leqslant N
and non-negative integers
m,r
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(R_{\leqslant L}(\bm{\beta})>r,\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll(m+r+1)e^{-r}N^{-1/2}.

A similar estimate holds for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We may assume
r\geqslant 1
, since for
r=0
the result is immediate from
Equation

8.4
. If
R_{\leqslant L}(\bm{\beta})>r
then there is some (minimal) index
\ell
,
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant L
, such that
|\xi_{\ell}|\geqslant r\ell^{\kappa}
. Suppose that
|\xi_{\ell}|=i
. Then, by the minimality of
\ell
, we have
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant|\xi_{1}|+\cdots+|\xi_{\ell}|\leqslant r\ell^{2}+i
. The shifted walk
\bm{\gamma}(j):=\bm{\beta}(\ell+j)-\bm{\beta}(\ell)
of length
N-\ell
must therefore stay above
-(m+r\ell^{2}+i)
, and so the contribution of a given pair
(\ell,i)
to our probability is, by
Equation

8.4
,

\ll\mathbb{P}\big(|\bm{\xi}_{\ell}|=i\big)\cdot(m+r\ell^{2}+i)(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}\ll\frac{1}{(i+1)!}(m+r\ell^{2}+i)(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}.

(Note that this estimate does hold when
\ell=N
, which is why we included the
+1
term.)
To complete the proof, we must show that the sum of the RHS over all
(\ell,i)
is bounded by
\ll(m+r+1)e^{-r}N^{-1/2}
.

The contribution from
\ell\leqslant N/2
is

\ll N^{-1/2}\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\sum_{i\geqslant r\ell^{\kappa}}\frac{1}{(i+1)!}(m+r\ell^{2}+i)\leqslant N^{-1/2}\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\sum_{i\geqslant r\ell^{\kappa}}\frac{1}{(i+1)!}(m+r+1)\ell^{2}i.

Using the bounds
\sum_{i\geqslant X}\frac{1}{i!}\ll e^{-X}
and
\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\ell^{2}e^{-r\ell^{\kappa}}\ll e^{-r}
, this is seen to be acceptable.

To estimate the contribution from
N/2<\ell\leqslant N
we can use very crude bounds. We may bound this by

\displaystyle\ll N\sum_{i\geqslant r(N/2)^{\kappa}}\frac{1}{(i+1)!}

\displaystyle(m+rN^{2}+i)\leqslant(m+r+1)N^{3}\sum_{i\geqslant r(N/2)^{\kappa}}\frac{1}{i!}

\displaystyle\ll(m+r+1)N^{3}e^{-r(N/2)^{\kappa}}\ll(m+r+1)e^{-r}N^{-10}.

This is (amply) acceptable and the proof is complete. The proof for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
is identical since
|\bm{\xi}_{\ell}|
and
|\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{\ell}|
have the same distribution.
∎

A consequence of this is the following bound on the
\omega
-measure of walks which are not
r
-bounded.

Corollary 8.13
.

Suppose that
m,r\geqslant 0
are integers. Then uniformly in
L\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\omega_{m}\{R_{\leqslant L}(\beta)>r\},\,\omega^{\prime}_{m}\{R_{\leqslant L}(\beta^{\prime})>r\}\ll(m+r+1)e^{-r}.

Similar estimates hold for
\omega_{m}\{R(\beta)>r\}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{m}\{R(\beta^{\prime})>r\}
.

Proof.

The required bounds are immediate from
Equation

8.7
and
Lemma

8.12
.∎

8.6.
Positivity estimates

In this section we show that an almost positive random walk almost surely has a parabolic shape. The following definition is very closely related to the definition of
T
-positive walk, which is
Definition

4.2
.
Recall that
\eta=\frac{1}{100}
is a globally-defined small parameter.

Definition 8.14
.

Denote by
T(\beta)
the smallest integer
T
for which
-T+\ell^{1/2-\eta}\leqslant\beta(\ell)\leqslant T+\ell^{1/2+\eta}
for all
\ell
, and set
T(\beta):=\infty
if no such integer exists. More generally, define
T_{\leqslant L}(\beta)
to be the smallest integer
T
for which this holds for all
\ell\leqslant L
, that is to say for which
\beta
is
T
-positive to length
L
.

Lemma 8.15
.

Suppose that
m\geqslant 0
and
T,N>0
are integers. Then
We have

\mathbb{P}\big(T_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})>T,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll(1+m)^{O(1)}T^{-\eta/2}N^{-1/2}.

A similar statement holds for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

When
T<2m
, the result follows immediately from
Proposition

8.1
by simply ignoring the condition
T_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})>T
on the LHS. Thus we assume that
T\geqslant 2m
in what follows. Once again the same proof applies to both the dashed and undashed statements, so we prove only the former.
We first consider the ‘upper’ failure of positivity in which there is some
\ell\leqslant N
such that
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\geqslant T+\ell^{1/2+\eta}
. In fact we will handle the event
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\geqslant T+\ell^{1/2}\log\ell
. By
Proposition

8.1
applied to the shifted walk
\bm{\gamma}(\cdot):=\bm{\beta}(\ell+\cdot)-\bm{\beta}(\ell)
of length
N-\ell
, and by
Lemma

F.3
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\leqslant N}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(\ell)\geqslant T+\ell^{1/2}\log\ell,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\ell\leqslant N}(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{i\geqslant T+\ell^{1/2}\log\ell}(m+i)\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(\ell)=i)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\ell\leqslant N}(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}\!\!\!\!\sum_{i\geqslant T+\ell^{1/2}\log\ell}(m+i)e^{-i^{2}/4\ell}.

To bound this, set
i:=T+\lceil\ell^{1/2}\log\ell\rceil+j
and note that
i^{2}\geqslant T^{2}+j^{2}+\ell\log^{2}\ell
; the sum is therefore bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\leqslant N}(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}\ell^{-10}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\big(m+T+\ell^{1/2}\log\ell+j\big)e^{-T^{2}/4\ell}e^{-j^{2}/4\ell}

\displaystyle\ll(1+m)T\sum_{\ell\leqslant N}(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}\ell^{-9}e^{-T^{2}/4\ell}\sum_{j\geqslant 0}(j+1)e^{-j^{2}/4\ell},

using here that
m+T+\ell^{1/2}\log\ell+j\leqslant(1+m)T\ell(j+1)
. Since
\sum_{j}je^{-j^{2}/4\ell}\ll\ell
, this is bounded by

\ll(1+m)T\sum_{\ell\leqslant N}(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}\ell^{-8}e^{-T^{2}/4\ell}.

For
\ell\leqslant T
we use
e^{-T^{2}/4\ell}\leqslant e^{-T/4}
and
\sum_{\ell\leqslant N}(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}\ell^{-8}\ll N^{-1/2}
, and for
\ell\geqslant T
we use
e^{-T^{2}/4\ell}\leqslant 1
and
\sum_{T\leqslant\ell\leqslant N}(N+1-\ell)^{-1/2}\ell^{-8}\ll T^{-7}N^{-1/2}
. Thus the probability of this ‘upper failure’ is
\ll(m+1)T^{-6}N^{-1/2}
, which is substantially smaller than the stated bound.

We turn now to bounds for the more subtle ‘lower’ failure of positivity. This fact, namely that almost positive walks almost surely have nearly squareroot growth, is critical for the whole paper. Our argument is closely related to that of Ritter
[
33
]
. Suppose that for some
\ell
,
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant N
, we have
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant-T+\ell^{1/2-\eta}
. Since
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\geqslant\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m
, we have
\ell\geqslant(T-m)^{2}\geqslant T^{2}/4
, so
T\leqslant 2N^{1/2}
, and (obviously)
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant\ell^{1/2-\eta}
. We will bound the measure of walks for which there is an
\ell
with these last two properties. The argument will be slightly different according to whether
\ell\leqslant N/8
(say) or not.

The case
\ell\leqslant N/8
.
Suppose that
k\leqslant N/4
is a power of two (here
k
is just a dummy variable, not the global variable
k
from the main paper). Let
j
be an integer with
j\in[k/2,2k]
. Denote by
Z_{j,k}(\bm{\beta})
the indicator of the event that
\bm{\beta}(j)\leqslant 2k^{1/2-\eta}
. Using
Proposition

8.1
(applied to the shifted walk
\bm{\gamma}(\cdot):=\bm{\beta}(j+\cdot)-\bm{\beta}(j)
, which has length
N-j\geqslant N/2
and minimum
\geqslant-u-m
) and then
Equation

8.10
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(

\displaystyle Z_{j,k}(\bm{\beta})=1,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll N^{-1/2}\!\!\!\!\sum_{-m\leqslant u\leqslant 2k^{1/2-\eta}}(1+m+u)\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(j)=u,\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)

\displaystyle\ll(1+m)^{2}N^{-1/2}\sum_{-m\leqslant u\leqslant 2k^{1/2-\eta}}(m+u+1)^{2}j^{-3/2}\ll(1+m)^{5}N^{-1/2}k^{-3\eta}.

Denote

Z_{k}(\bm{\beta}):=\sum_{j\in[k/2,2k]}Z_{j,k}(\bm{\beta}).

It follows that for any
\delta\in(0,1)
we have

(8.20)

\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{k}(\bm{\beta})\geqslant\delta k,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll(1+m)^{5}\delta^{-1}N^{-1/2}k^{-3\eta}.

We say that
\bm{\beta}
is
reasonable
at scale
k
if
\bm{\beta}(j_{2})-\bm{\beta}(j_{1})\leqslant k^{1/2-\eta}
and
\bm{\beta}(j_{1})\leqslant k
whenever
j_{1},j_{2}
satisfy
k/2\leqslant j_{1}<j_{2}\leqslant 2k
and
j_{2}-j_{1}\leqslant k^{1-2\eta}(\log k)^{-2}
. We claim the following estimate:

(8.21)

\mathbb{P}\big(\mbox{$\bm{\beta}$ unreasonable at scale $k$},\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll(1+m)^{2}k^{-10}N^{-1/2}.

To see this we use a union bound over the
\ll k^{2}
choices of
j_{1},j_{2}
. For each choice, we need to bound

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}-m\leqslant i_{1}\leqslant k\\
i_{2}-i_{1}>k^{1/2-\eta}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(j_{1})=i_{1},\bm{\beta}(j_{2})=i_{2},\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)+\sum_{i_{1}>k}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(j_{1})=i_{1},\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

For this we apply
Proposition

8.1
to the walk shifted by
j_{2}
(for the first term) or
j_{1}
(for the second term). Since
N-j_{1},N-j_{2}\gg N
we see that this is

(8.22)

\ll N^{-1/2}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}-m\leqslant i_{1}\leqslant k\\
i_{2}-i_{1}>k^{1/2-\eta}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!(m+1+i_{2})\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(j_{1})=i_{1},\bm{\beta}(j_{2})=i_{2}\big)+N^{-1/2}\sum_{i_{1}>k}(m+1+i_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(j_{1})=i_{1}\big).

By
Lemma

F.5
we have
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(j_{1})=i_{1})\ll e^{-i_{1}/10}
uniformly for
j_{1}\in[k/2,2k]
and
i_{1}>k
, and so (after summing over the
O(k^{2})
choices of
j_{1},j_{2}
) the contribution of the second term here is bounded as claimed in
Equation

8.21
. To bound the first term in
Equation

8.22
we set
i=i_{2}-i_{1}
and sum out the
i_{1}
variable, obtaining a bound

\ll N^{-1/2}(k+m)\sum_{i>k^{1/2-\eta}}(m+k+i)\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(j_{2}-j_{1})=i).

By a standard large deviation estimate (see
Lemma

F.3
) this is

\ll N^{-1/2}(k+m)\sum_{i>k^{1/2-\eta}}(m+k+i)e^{-i^{2}/4(j_{2}-j_{1})},

which in turn is bounded by

\ll N^{-1/2}(k+m)^{2}\sum_{i>k^{1/2-\eta}}ie^{-i^{2}(\log k)^{2}/4k^{1-2\eta}}<N^{-1/2}(1+m)^{2}k^{-100}.

Summing over the
O(k^{2})
choices for
j_{1},j_{2}
, this completes the proof of
Equation

8.21
.

Now suppose that
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant\ell^{1/2-\eta}
(with
\ell\leqslant N/8
) and let
k
be the unique power of two such that
\ell\in(k/2,k]
. Thus
k\leqslant N/4
, and
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant k^{1/2-\eta}
. Suppose that
\bm{\beta}
is reasonable at scale
k
. Then
\bm{\beta}(j)\leqslant 2k^{1/2-\eta}
for all
j
with
0\leqslant j-\ell\leqslant k^{1-2\eta}(\log k)^{-2}
, and so
Z_{k}(\bm{\beta})\geqslant k^{1-2\eta}(\log k)^{-2}
. By
Equation

8.20
with
\delta:=k^{-2\eta}(\log k)^{-2}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m,\;\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant\ell^{1/2-\eta}

\displaystyle\,\mbox{for some $\ell\in(k/2,k]$},\;\mbox{$\bm{\beta}$ reasonable at scale $k$}\big)

\displaystyle\ll(1+m)^{5}N^{-1/2}k^{-\eta}(\log k)^{2}

for all (powers of two)
k\leqslant N/4
. Putting this together with
Equation

8.21
gives

\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m,\;\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant\ell^{1/2-\eta}\,\mbox{for some $\ell\in(k/2,k]$}\big)\ll(1+m)^{5}N^{-1/2}k^{-\eta}(\log k)^{2}.

Summing
k
over powers of two with
k\geqslant(T-m)^{2}
yields

\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m,\;\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant\ell^{1/2-\eta}\,\mbox{for some $\ell$, $N/8\geqslant\ell\geqslant(T-m)^{2}$}\big)\ll(1+m)^{5}N^{-1/2}T^{-\eta/2}.

This is the desired bound in the case
\ell\leqslant N/8
.

The case
N/8<\ell\leqslant N
.
The proof in this case is somewhat similar. In this case, we say that
\bm{\beta}
is
reasonable
if
|\bm{\beta}(j_{2})-\bm{\beta}(j_{1})|\leqslant N^{1/2-\eta}
whenever
j_{1},j_{2}\in[N/8,N]
and
|j_{2}-j_{1}|\leqslant N^{1-5\eta/2}
. By another large deviation estimate (see
Corollary

F.4
),
\bm{\beta}
is reasonable with probability
\geqslant 1-N^{-10}
.

If
\bm{\beta}
is reasonable, and if
\bm{\beta}(\ell)\leqslant\ell^{1/2-\eta}
for some
\ell\in[N/8,N]
, then by appropriate rounding there is some
j=\lambda\lfloor N^{1-5\eta/2}\rfloor
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
,
N-j\in[N/8,N]
, with
\bm{\beta}(N-j)\leqslant 2N^{1/2-\eta}
.

Now for a fixed such
j
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N-j)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2N^{1/2-\eta},\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)=\sum_{-m\leqslant u\leqslant 2N^{1/2-\eta}}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N-j)=u,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{-m\leqslant u\leqslant 2N^{1/2-\eta}}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N-j)=u,\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-j}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\cdot(m+u+1)(1+j)^{-1/2}

by applying
Proposition

8.1
to the walk of length
j
starting after
N-j
. (Here we used
(1+j)^{-1/2}
so that the result is true for
j=0
). By
Equation

8.10
, and since
N-j\gg N
, this is

\ll(1+m)^{2}\sum_{-m\leqslant u\leqslant 2N^{1/2-\eta}}(1+u+m)^{2}N^{-3/2}(1+j)^{-1/2}\ll(1+m)^{5}N^{-3\eta}(1+j)^{-1/2}.

Summing over
j=\lambda\lfloor N^{1-5\eta/2}\rfloor
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
such that
N-j\in[N/8,N]
(thus
\lambda\ll N^{5\eta/2}
) gives
\ll(1+m)^{5}N^{-1/2-\eta/2}
. Since
T\leqslant 2N^{1/2}
, this is
\ll(1+m)^{5}N^{-1/2}T^{-\eta}
, which implies the desired bound in this case.∎

Corollary 8.16
.

Let
m\geqslant 0
and
T\geqslant 1
be integers.
We have

\omega_{m}\{T(\beta)>T\},\;\omega^{\prime}_{m}\{T(\beta^{\prime})>T\}\ll(1+m)^{O(1)}T^{-\eta/2}.

Proof.

Once again the proofs of the two statements are the same so we handle only the first one.
It follows immediately from
Lemma

8.15
that if
L\leqslant N
then

N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(T_{\leqslant L}(\bm{\beta})>T,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll(1+m)^{O(1)}T^{-\eta/2}.

The result now follows from
Equation

8.7
by letting
N\rightarrow\infty
, and then letting
L\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

8.7.
Constructing a jump step

We now come to the most technical part of the section. We start with a definition. Here,
\eta=\kappa=\frac{1}{100}
are the global constants involved in
Definitions

4.1
and
4.2
.

Definition 8.17
.

Let
V\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\beta
be a walk of length
N
with increments
\xi_{i}=\beta(i)-\beta(i-1)
. Then we say that an index
t
is a
V
-jump step if
1+\xi_{t}=V
and if, for all
\ell
with
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant N-t
, we have

(8.23)

\beta(t+\ell)-\beta(t)\geqslant\ell^{1/2-2\eta},

and if
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant N-t
we have

(8.24)

\xi_{t+\ell}\leqslant\ell^{2\kappa}.

We will show that a random
\bm{\beta}
almost surely has a jump step in a suitable range. This will be done by recursive application of the following result.

Lemma 8.18
.

Suppose that
N,V
are sufficiently large integers and let
\bm{\beta}
be a random walk of length
N
with
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
increments. Then we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\mbox{index $1$ is a $V$-jump step for $\bm{\beta}$}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0\big)\geqslant V^{-V}.

Proof.

Choose absolute constants
R,T
such that

(8.25)

\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0,\;\bm{\beta}\;\mbox{is $R$-bounded and $T$-positive up to $N$}\big)\gg N^{-1/2},

uniformly for
N
sufficiently large. That such
R,T
exist follows from
Lemma

8.12
and
Lemma

8.15
with
m=0
, together with the existence of
h(0)
, which implies that
\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0\big)\gg N^{-1/2}
. Set
L:=\lceil\max(10,2T,R^{1/\kappa})\rceil
(thus
L
is still an absolute constant).

The walk with
\bm{\beta}(i)=V-2+i
for
i=1,\dots,N
satisfies
Equations

8.23
and
8.24
for
t=1
. This walk occurs with probability
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(1)=V)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(1)=2)^{N-1}
which, if
V
is sufficiently large, is
\geqslant V^{-V}
for any
N\leqslant 2L
. Therefore we may assume
N>2L
in what follows.

Now by the existence of
h(0)
we have
\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0\big)\leqslant CN^{-1/2}
for some absolute
C
, so it suffices to show that

(8.26)

\mathbb{P}\big(\mbox{index $1$ is a $V$-jump step for $\bm{\beta}$},\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0\big)\geqslant CV^{-V}N^{-1/2}.

Consider the shifted walk
\bm{\gamma}(i):=\bm{\beta}(L+i)-\bm{\beta}(L)
and let its increments be
\tilde{\bm{\xi}}_{i}:=\bm{\xi}_{L+i}
. We claim that the event on the left in
Equation

8.26
contains the event
\mathcal{E}
defined by

\{\bm{\xi}_{1}=V-1,\bm{\xi}_{2}=\cdots\bm{\xi}_{L}=1\}\cap\{\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-L}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant 0,\;\bm{\gamma}\;\mbox{is $R$-bounded and $T$-positive up to $N-L$}\}.

This is enough to complete the proof, since (using
Equation

8.25
applied to
\bm{\gamma}
) we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\gg\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(1)=V)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(1)=2)^{L-1}(N-L)^{-1/2},

so
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant CV^{-V}N^{-1/2}
provided
V
is sufficiently large.

To prove the claim, suppose that
\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{E}
. First note that we clearly have
1+\bm{\xi}_{1}=V
. Now for
\ell
with
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant L-1
, we have
\bm{\beta}(1+\ell)-\bm{\beta}(1)=\ell\geqslant\ell^{1/2-2\eta}
. Thus
Equation

8.23
holds for
\ell\leqslant L-1
. Moreover for
i=0,\dots,N-L
we have, using the
T
-positive nature of
\bm{\gamma}
and the fact that
L\geqslant\max(10,2T)
, that

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}(L+i)-\bm{\beta}(1)

\displaystyle=\bm{\gamma}(i)+(\bm{\beta}(L)-\bm{\beta}(1))\geqslant-T+i^{1/2-\eta}+(L-1)

\displaystyle\geqslant L^{1/2-\eta}+i^{1/2-\eta}\geqslant(L+i)^{1/2-\eta}.

Thus
Equation

8.23
holds for
L\leqslant\ell\leqslant N-1
as well.

The inequality
Equation

8.24
certainly holds for
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant L-1
. For
i=0,\dots,N-L
we have

\bm{\xi}_{1+L+i}=\tilde{\bm{\xi}}_{1+i}\leqslant R(1+i)^{\kappa}<(L+i)^{2\kappa},

and so
Equation

8.24
also holds for
L\leqslant\ell\leqslant N-1
. This completes the proof of the claim, and hence of
Lemma

8.18
.
∎

Now we turn to the main result of this subsection.

Lemma 8.19
.

There exists an absolute constant
C
such that the following holds. Let
N,V
be sufficiently large integer parameters. Let
m\geqslant 0
be an integer, and suppose that
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{10})
. Let
t_{\min},t_{\max}
be two integer thresholds, and suppose that

(8.27)

N>t_{\max}>t_{\min}\geqslant\Big(\frac{8(m+2)^{C}V^{V}}{\delta}\Big)^{4/\eta}

and

(8.28)

\log\log t_{\max}-\log\log t_{\min}\geqslant 20V^{V}\log(1/\delta).

Then
