\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\;\mbox{does not have a $V$-jump step in $[t_{\min},t_{\max}]$}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll\delta.

Proof.

For brevity in what follows set
\varepsilon:=V^{-V}
.
Define thresholds
T_{1}:=t_{\min}
and
T_{j+1}:=T_{j}^{4}
,
j=1,2,\dots
. We assume in what follows that any such threshold under discussion is
\leqslant t_{\max}
; this will ultimately turn out to be the case by use of
Equation

8.28
, as we shall show later. Set
I_{j}:=[T_{j},T_{j+1})
. We say that
\bm{\beta}
has a
local jump step in
I_{j}
if there is some
t\in[T_{j},T_{j}^{2}]
such that
1+\bm{\xi}_{t}=V
and such that
Equations

8.23
and
8.24
are satisfied for all
\ell
with
1\leqslant\ell<T_{j+1}-t
. (Note the case
\ell=0
of
Equation

8.23
is trivial.)

Denote by
\mathcal{J}_{j}
the event that
\bm{\beta}
has a local jump step in the interval
I_{j}
. Set

\tau_{j}:=\mathbb{P}\big(\neg\mathcal{J}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{j}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

Observe that
\tau_{1}\geqslant\tau_{2}\geqslant\dots
and that for
j\geqslant 2
we have

\displaystyle\tau_{j-1}-\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{J}_{j}\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{j-1}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)

(8.29)

\displaystyle=\sum_{T_{j}\leqslant s\leqslant N}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{J}_{j}\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{j-1}\cap\argmin_{T_{j}\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=s\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

(That is,
s
is the first
s\in[T_{j},N]
for which
\bm{\beta}
attains its minimum on that interval.) To estimate
Equation

8.29
from below, we first consider the sum over
s\geqslant T_{j}^{2}
. For this, we simply ignore the
\mathcal{J}
events, bounding the sum by

(8.30)

\mathbb{P}\big(\argmin_{T_{j}\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\in[T_{j}^{2},N]\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

Suppose that
\bm{\beta}
is
t_{\min}^{1/2}
-positive up to
N
(see
Definition

4.2
). Then
\bm{\beta}(T_{j})\leqslant T_{j}^{1/2+\eta}+t_{\min}^{1/2}
and
\bm{\beta}(s)\geqslant(T_{j}^{2})^{1/2-\eta}-t_{\min}^{1/2}
for all
s
with
T_{j}^{2}\leqslant s\leqslant N
. Since
T_{j}\geqslant t_{\min}>2^{8}
and
\eta\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
, we have
T_{j}^{1/2+\eta}+t_{\min}^{1/2}<(T_{j}^{2})^{1/2-\eta}-t_{\min}^{1/2}
, and so in this case we do not have
\argmin_{T_{j}\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\in[T_{j}^{2},N]
. Thus we can bound
Equation

8.30
by

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\;\mbox{is not $t_{\min}^{1/2}$-positive up to $N$}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

Using
Proposition

8.1
(3) and
Lemma

8.15
, we see that the probability in
Equation

8.30
is
\leqslant(m+2)^{C}t_{\min}^{-\eta/4}
for some absolute
C
. Thus we have shown that

(8.31)

\sum_{T_{j}^{2}\leqslant s\leqslant N}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{J}_{j}\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{j-1}\cap\argmin_{T_{j}\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=s\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\leqslant(m+2)^{C}t_{\min}^{-\eta/4}.

Consider now the portion of the sum in
Equation

8.29
over the range
T_{j}\leqslant s<T_{j}^{2}
.
We bound this below by

(8.32)

\sum_{T_{j}\leqslant s<T_{j}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{J}^{(s)}_{j}\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{j-1}\cap\argmin_{T_{j}\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=s\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big),

where
\mathcal{J}^{(s)}_{j}
is the event that
t=s+1
is a local jump step; this event is obviously contained in
\mathcal{J}_{j}
.

We the apply the chain rule for conditional probability

\mathbb{P}(A\cap B\cap E_{s}\mid M)=\mathbb{P}(B\mid M)\cdot\mathbb{P}(E_{s}\mid B\cap M)\cdot\mathbb{P}(A\mid B\cap E_{s}\cap M),

where
A=\mathcal{J}^{(s)}_{j}
,
B=\neg\mathcal{J}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{j-1}
,
E_{s}=(\argmin_{T_{j}\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=s)
and
M=(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m)
. Observe that
\mathbb{P}(B\mid M)=\tau_{j-1}
. Denote
\mathbb{P}(E_{s}\mid B\cap M):=p(s)
. Then
Equation

8.32
is

(8.33)

\tau_{j-1}\sum_{T_{j}\leqslant s<T_{j}^{2}}p(s)\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{J}^{(s)}_{j}\mid\neg\mathcal{J}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{j-1},\;\argmin_{T_{j}\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=s,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big).

The event we are conditioning on here has the form
\mathcal{E}_{s}(\bm{\beta}(1),\dots,\bm{\beta}(s))\cap\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-s}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant 0)
, where
\bm{\gamma}_{s}(i):=\bm{\beta}(s+i)-\bm{\beta}(s)
is the shifted walk starting at
s
and

\mathcal{E}_{s}:=\neg\mathcal{J}_{1}\cap\cdots\cap\neg\mathcal{J}_{j-1}\cap(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant s}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m)\cap\big(\bm{\beta}(i)>\bm{\beta}(s)\;\mbox{for $T_{j}\leqslant i<s$}\big);

note carefully that, by the definition of local jump step,
\mathcal{E}_{s}
really does depend only on the values
\bm{\beta}(i)
for
i\leqslant s
. The event
\mathcal{J}^{(s)}_{j}
depends only on
\bm{\gamma}_{s}
(the portion of
\bm{\beta}
after
s
), and so
Equation

8.33
is

\tau_{j-1}\sum_{T_{j}\leqslant s<T_{j}^{2}}p(s)\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{J}^{(s)}_{j}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-s}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant 0\big)\geqslant\varepsilon\tau_{j-1}\sum_{T_{j}\leqslant s<T_{j}^{2}}p(s),

where the second step follows from
Lemma

8.18
applied to
\bm{\gamma}
(recalling here that
\varepsilon=V^{-V}
).

Combining the upper bound
Equation

8.31
with the lower bound we have just obtained for
Equation

8.32
via
Equation

8.33
, and substituting in to
Equation

8.29
, we have shown that

(8.34)

\tau_{j-1}-\tau_{j}\geqslant\varepsilon\tau_{j-1}\big(\sum_{T_{j}\leqslant s<T_{j}^{2}}p(s)\big)-(m+2)^{C}t_{\min}^{-\eta/4}.

We must now give a lower bound for the sum over
p(s)
. Note first of all that

(8.35)

\sum_{T_{j}\leqslant s\leqslant N}p(s)=1,

since precisely one of the events
E_{s}
occurs for any
\bm{\beta}
. Now for each
s
we have

(8.36)

p(s)=\mathbb{P}(E_{s}\mid B\cap M)=\frac{\mathbb{P}(E_{s}\cap B\cap M)}{\mathbb{P}(B\cap M)}\leqslant\frac{\mathbb{P}(E_{s}\cap M)}{\mathbb{P}(B\cap M)}=\frac{\mathbb{P}(E_{s}\mid M)}{\mathbb{P}(B\mid M)}=\frac{1}{\tau_{j-1}}\mathbb{P}(E_{s}\mid M).

Furthermore,
\sum_{T_{j}^{2}\leqslant s\leqslant N}\mathbb{P}(E_{s}\mid M)
is precisely the probability in
Equation

8.30
, which we have already shown (in the analysis leading up to
Equation

8.31
) to be at most
(m+2)^{C}t_{\min}^{-\eta/4}
. Combining this with
Equations

8.34
,
8.35
, and
8.36
gives

\tau_{j-1}-\tau_{j}\geqslant\varepsilon\tau_{j-1}-2(m+2)^{C+1}t_{\min}^{-\eta/4}\geqslant\varepsilon\tau_{j-1}-\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{4},

where this last inequality follows from
Equation

8.27
. By induction,
\tau_{j}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{4}+(1-\frac{\delta}{4})(1-\varepsilon)^{j-1}
, and so
\tau_{j}\leqslant\delta/2
for all
j\geqslant 10\varepsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta)
, that is to say

(8.37)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\;\mbox{has no local jump step in any $I_{j}$, $j\leqslant 10\varepsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta)$}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\leqslant\delta/2.

Suppose now that
\bm{\beta}
has a local jump step in
I_{j}
, but does not have an actual jump step in
I_{j}
. This means that either
Equation

8.23
or
Equation

8.24
fails for some
\ell
with
T_{j+1}-t\leqslant\ell\leqslant N-t
and for some
t\in[T_{j},T_{j}^{2}]
. That is, either

(8.38)

\bm{\beta}(t+\ell)\leqslant\bm{\beta}(t)+\ell^{1/2-2\eta}\quad\mbox{or}\quad\bm{\xi}_{t+\ell}>\ell^{2\kappa}

for some such
\ell
. We additionally observe that
\ell
is much larger than
t
; certainly
\ell\geqslant t
since
t\leqslant T_{j}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}T_{j+1}
.

Suppose the first of the alternatives in
Equation

8.38
holds. We claim that either
\bm{\beta}(t+\ell)\leqslant(t+\ell)^{1/2-\eta}-t_{\min}
or
\bm{\beta}(t)\geqslant t^{2/3}+t_{\min}
. To see this, first note that

(8.39)

\tfrac{1}{4}(t+\ell)^{1/2-\eta}\geqslant\max\big(t^{2/3},\ell^{1/2-2\eta},2t_{\min}\big).

The first inequality here follows using
t+\ell\geqslant T_{j+1}
and
t\leqslant T_{j}^{2}
and the fact that
T_{j+1}:=T_{j}^{4}
. The second inequality follows from
\frac{1}{4}(t+\ell)^{1/2-\eta}>\frac{1}{4}\ell^{1/2-2\eta}
and the fact that
\ell
is sufficiently large. The third inequality following using
t+\ell\geqslant T_{j+1}
and
t_{\min}=T_{1}
. Adding the inequalities in
Equation

8.39
gives
(t+\ell)^{1/2-\eta}-t_{\min}\geqslant t^{2/3}+t_{\min}+\ell^{1/2-2\eta}
, which implies the claim. The claim implies that
\bm{\beta}
fails to be
t_{\min}
-positive in the sense of
Definition

4.2
. By another application of
Proposition

8.1
and
Lemma

8.15
, the probability of this event conditional on
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m
is
\leqslant(m+2)^{C}t_{\min}^{-\eta/2}\leqslant\delta/4
.

Now suppose the second option in
Equation

8.38
holds. Then
\bm{\beta}
fails to be
t_{\min}^{\kappa/2}
-bounded in the sense of
Definition

4.1
; this is because
\ell^{2\kappa}>t_{\min}^{\kappa/2}(t+\ell)^{\kappa}
, since
\ell^{2/3}\geqslant(T_{j+1}/2)^{2/3}>T^{1/2}_{1}=t^{1/2}_{\min}
and
\ell^{4/3}>2\ell\geqslant t+\ell
. By
Proposition

8.1
(3) and
Lemma

8.12
, the probability of this conditional on
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m
is certainly
\leqslant\delta/4
; the key point here is that the assumption
Equation

8.27
implies that the term
e^{-t_{\min}^{\kappa/2}}
arising from the application of
Lemma

8.12
is massively smaller than
\delta
, and also that
N
is massively larger than
m
and
1/\delta
.

The conclusion of the above analysis is that

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\;\mbox{has a local but not a global jump step in some $I_{j}$}\mid\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\leqslant\delta/2.

Combining with
Equation

8.37
proves that, conditioned on
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m
, the probability that
\bm{\beta}
has no (global) jump step in any
I_{j}
with
j\leqslant 10\varepsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta)
is at most
\delta
. The proof is concluded by noting that, under condition
Equation

8.28
, all such intervals
I_{j}
are contained in
[t_{\min},t_{\max}]
.
∎

9.
Bounds on
\{0,1\}
-matrices

This section assembles various ingredients that will be used in
Part

III
of the paper, having to do with bounds on the number of
\{0,1\}
-matrices satisfying various linear-algebraic conditions.

Let
M
be an
\ell\times N
matrix. Write
r
for its rank (thus
r\leqslant\ell
), and for
d
with
1\leqslant d\leqslant r
denote by
q_{d}
the least index such that the first
q_{d}
columns of
M
span a subspace of
\mathbb{Q}^{\ell}
of dimension
d
. Set
q_{r+1}:=N+1
. We call
(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})
the
rank profile
of
M
and denote it by
\operatorname{rp}(M)
.

Lemma 9.1
.

We have the following statements. Suppose that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

(i)

The number of
\ell
-by-
N
matrices
M
with
\{0,1\}
-entries and
\operatorname{rp}(M)=(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})
is bounded above by
2^{\ell^{2}}2^{rN-\sum_{d=1}^{r}q_{d}}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
N\geqslant 10\ell
and that
1\leqslant r<\ell
. The number of
\ell
-by-
N
matrices
M
with
\{0,1\}
-entries and rank
r
, and at least
r+1
different nonzero rows, is
\ll 2^{\ell^{2}}2^{(r-\frac{1}{10})N}
.
(
By different rows we mean distinct as elements of
\mathbb{Q}^{N}
.
)

Proof.

(i) We uncover the columns of
M
one-by-one. Consider the
q
th column. If
q
is one of the
q_{d}
then we bound the number of choices trivially by
2^{\ell}
. Otherwise, suppose that
q_{d}<q<q_{d+1}
; then the
q
th column lies in the linear span of the previous columns, which has dimension
d
. It is a folklore fact (see, for instance,
[
20
, Lemma 5.1]
) that any subspace of
\mathbb{Q}^{\ell}
of dimension
d
intersects the cube
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
in at most
2^{d}
points, and so there are at most
2^{d}
choices for the
q
th column. Therefore the quantity we seek is bounded above by

2^{\sum_{d=1}^{r}d(q_{d+1}-q_{d}-1)+\ell r}=2^{rN-\sum_{d=1}^{r}q_{d}-\sum_{d=1}^{r}d+\ell r}\leqslant 2^{\ell^{2}}2^{rN-\sum_{d=1}^{r}q_{d}}

as claimed.

(ii) There is some set of
r
rows which span the row space of
M
; this can be selected in
\binom{\ell}{r}
ways. Fix such a set of rows. Consider the induced
r
-by-
N
submatrix
M^{\prime}
.

Say that an
r
-by-
N
matrix is
typical
if the intersection of the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear span of its rows with
\{0,1\}^{N}
consists of only the rows themselves and zero. We claim that a random
r
-by-
N
matrix with
\{0,1\}
-entries is typical with probability
\geqslant 1-2^{-N/10}
.

Given the claim, we may conclude the proof of (ii) as follows. If
M^{\prime}
is typical, then
M
can only have
r
different nonzero rows, since all rows of
M
are in the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear span of
M^{\prime}
. If
M^{\prime}
is not typical, then by the claim there are
\leqslant 2^{(r-\frac{1}{10})N}
choices for
M^{\prime}
. Each of the remaining
\ell-r
rows of
M
is drawn from the intersection of
\{0,1\}^{N}
with the row space of
M^{\prime}
, which has size at most
2^{r}
by the folklore fact above. This gives
\leqslant(2^{r})^{\ell-r}
ways to complete
M^{\prime}
to
M
. Since
\binom{\ell}{r}(2^{r})^{\ell-r}\leqslant 2^{\ell^{2}}
, the result follows.

It remains to prove the claim. Let
Q
be a random
r
-by-
N
matrix with
\{0,1\}
-entries. Let
N^{\prime}
be an integer with
N/3\leqslant N^{\prime}\leqslant N/2
. We first reveal the
r
-by-
N^{\prime}
submatrix
Q^{\prime}
formed from the first
N^{\prime}
columns. The probability that
Q^{\prime}
does not have full rank is bounded above by
2^{r-N^{\prime}}
, even over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
: each of the
2^{r}-1
possible annihilators of the column space occurs with probability
2^{-N^{\prime}}
.

Suppose now that
Q^{\prime}
has been revealed and that it does have full rank. Fix a set
S
of
r
columns which span. We now reveal the remaining elements of
Q\setminus Q^{\prime}
. Consider a hypothetical element of
\{0,1\}^{N}
in the row span of
Q
, not zero or one of rows. Suppose that it is
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}v_{i}
where
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
are the rows of
Q
and at least two of the
\lambda_{i}
are not zero. Restricting to the columns in
S
, we see that there are at most
2^{r}
choices for
(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r})
. Fix such a choice. We now proceed to reveal the
N-N^{\prime}
columns in
Q\setminus Q^{\prime}
. Each of these is a vector
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{r})\in\{0,1\}^{r}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}
. Observe that we have
\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^{r}}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}\big)\leqslant\frac{3}{4}
since, if
\lambda_{u},\lambda_{v}\neq 0
, then the set
\{0,\lambda_{u},\lambda_{v},\lambda_{u}+\lambda_{v}\}
has size at least
3
, hence for every
x=\sum_{i\neq u,v}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
at least one of
x,x+\lambda_{u},x+\lambda_{v},x+\lambda_{u}+\lambda_{v}
is not in
\{0,1\}
. It follows that the probability that
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}v_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
is
\leqslant(3/4)^{N-N^{\prime}}
. Summing over the possible choices of
(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r})
gives that the probability of
Q
failing to be typical (for fixed
Q^{\prime}
of full rank) is
\leqslant 2^{r}(3/4)^{N-N^{\prime}}
.

Combining the above estimates, we see that the probability that
Q
is not typical is
\leqslant 2^{r-N^{\prime}}+2^{r}(3/4)^{N-N^{\prime}}
. Using the bounds
r\leqslant N/10
and the choice of
N^{\prime}
, the result follows.
∎

10.
The function
g

In this section we give the basic properties of the function
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
featuring in our main theorem, and defined as in
Equation

1.2
. We have already noted the transformation law
Equation

2.19
. The other key properties we need are contained in the following lemma.

Lemma 10.1
.

The Fourier coefficients
\widehat{g}(m):=\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}g(x)e^{-2\pi imx}dx
are given by

(10.1)

\widehat{g}(m)={-}\frac{1}{\log 2}\Gamma\big(\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}+\frac{2\pi m}{\log 2}i\big).

In particular,
\widehat{g}(m)\neq 0
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. We have the bounds

(10.2)

|\widehat{g}(m)|<\frac{6}{\log 2}e^{-\pi^{2}|m|/\log 2}.

We have

(10.3)

\frac{\max g}{\min g}\leqslant 1+2\times 10^{-7}.

Remark
.

Note that since the Fourier coefficients
|\widehat{g}(m)|
decay quicker than any fixed power of
m
, the function
g
is indeed smooth with
g^{(j)}(\theta)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}(2\pi im)^{j}\widehat{g}(m)e^{2\pi i\theta m}
(see for instance
[
24
, Proposition 3.3.12]
).

Proof.

From the definition
Equation

1.2
and unfolding the
\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}
and
\sum_{D\in\mathbb{Z}}
into an integral over the real line, it follows that for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\widehat{g}(m)=\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}(\log 2)^{x}(1-e^{-2^{x}})e^{2\pi imx}\,\mathrm{d}x=(\log 2)^{-1}\int^{\infty}_{0}u^{\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}+\frac{2\pi im}{\log 2}-1}(1-e^{-u})\,\mathrm{d}u.

To investigate this, it makes sense to consider more generally the integral

(10.4)

I_{s}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{s-1}(1-e^{-u})\,\mathrm{d}u

for
\operatorname{Re}s\in(-1,0)
. It turns out that this can be explicitly evaluated in terms of the
\Gamma
-function, namely
I_{s}={-}\Gamma(s)
for
-1<\operatorname{Re}s<0
, and from this
Equation

10.1
follows immediately.

The evaluation of
I_{s}
can be justified
2
2

2

As an aside for the reader, the authors originally discovered that
g
was almost constant via the use of GPT o4-mini. Upon being asked to prove this fact, the model suggested the broad strategy but miscomputed this integral (instead replacing it by the computation of the integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{s-1}}{e^{x}-1}\,\mathrm{d}x=\zeta(s)\Gamma(s)
); this embarrassingly tricked the second author until clarification by the first author. Upon prompting the model with the relevant Wikipedia page on Mellin transforms, the model was able to complete the proof and give the counter integral argument to bound the
\Gamma(\cdot)
(which is of course completely standard).
as follows:

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{0}u^{s-1}(1-e^{-u})\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{0}u^{s-1}\int^{u}_{0}e^{-t}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}u=\int^{\infty}_{0}e^{-t}\int^{\infty}_{t}u^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{0}e^{-t}\big(-\frac{t^{s}}{s}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t={-}\frac{1}{s}\Gamma(s+1)={-}\Gamma(s).

Turning to
Equation

10.2
, presumably one can look up precise numerical asymptotics for
\Gamma
in the relevant strip
-1<\operatorname{Re}s<0
. However, the integral representation
Equation

10.4
allows us to derive an appropriate bound from first principles with minimal effort via a contour integration argument, which we give now.

Write
s=\sigma+it
and assume
t>0
(the case
t<0
can be handled symmetrically or by taking complex conjugates). One may note that the integral here indeed converges for
\sigma\in(-1,0)
; as
u\rightarrow\infty
, the condition
\sigma<0
guarantees convergence, whilst as
u\rightarrow 0
we have
1-e^{-u}\ll u
and the condition
\sigma>-1
guarantees it.
To estimate
I_{\sigma+it}
we perform a contour integration in the upper right quadrant. Denote by
\mathcal{C}_{r}=\{re^{i\theta}:0\leqslant\theta\leqslant\pi/2\}
the quarter circle of radius
r
. Let
0<r<1<R
. Then applying Cauchy’s theorem around the contour formed by
\mathcal{C}_{r},\mathcal{C}_{R}
and the portions of the real and imaginary axes between
r
and
R
, we see that

\displaystyle\int^{R}_{r}

\displaystyle u^{\sigma-1+it}(1-e^{-u})\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=i\int^{R}_{r}(ui)^{\sigma-1+it}(1-e^{-ui})\,\mathrm{d}u-\int_{\mathcal{C}_{R}}u^{\sigma-1+it}(1-e^{-u})\,\mathrm{d}u+\int_{\mathcal{C}_{r}}u^{\sigma-1+it}(1-e^{-u})\,\mathrm{d}u.

We first estimate the integral over
\mathcal{C}_{R}
. Using that
|1-e^{-z}|\leqslant 2
for
\operatorname{Re}z\geqslant 0
, we see that on this contour we have
|1-e^{-u}|\leqslant 2
, and at
u=Re^{i\theta}
we have
|u^{\sigma-1+it}|=R^{\sigma-1}e^{-\theta t}\leqslant R^{\sigma-1}
for
t\geqslant 0
. The length of the contour is
\pi R/2
, and so the total contribution is
\ll R^{\sigma-1}\cdot R
which tends to zero as
R\rightarrow\infty
, since
\sigma<0
.

Next we estimate the integral over
\mathcal{C}_{r}
. Now we use
|1-e^{-u}|\ll|u|=r
and
|u^{\sigma-1+it}|\ll r^{\sigma-1}
. The length of the contour is
\pi r/2
, so the total contribution is
\ll r\cdot r^{\sigma-1}\cdot r=r^{\sigma+1}
, which tends to zero as
r\rightarrow 0
since
\sigma>-1
.

It follows that

I_{\sigma+it}=i\int^{\infty}_{0}(ui)^{\sigma-1+it}(1-e^{-ui})\,\mathrm{d}u=\int^{\infty}_{0}u^{\sigma-1+it}e^{\sigma i\pi/2-\pi t/2}(1-e^{-ui})\,\mathrm{d}u.

Taking absolute values,

\displaystyle|I_{\sigma+it}|\leqslant e^{-\pi t/2}\int^{\infty}_{0}u^{\sigma-1}|1-e^{-iu}|\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-\pi t/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{\sigma-1}(\min(u,2))\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=2^{\sigma+1}\big(\frac{1}{\sigma+1}-\frac{1}{\sigma}\big)e^{-\pi t/2}.

In the case of interest to us, where
\sigma=\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}
, we get a numerical value of less than
6
, so
|\Gamma(\sigma+it)|=|I_{\sigma+it}|\leqslant 6e^{-\pi t/2}
in this case. Since
\widehat{g}(m)=-\frac{1}{\log 2}I_{\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}+\frac{2\pi im}{\log 2}}
, the bound
Equation

10.2
follows immediately.

Finally we establish
Equation

10.3
. Using
Equation

10.2
and simple numerics we have

\sum_{|m|\geqslant 2}|\widehat{g}(m)|\leqslant\frac{12}{\log 2}\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}e^{-\pi^{2}m/\log 2}<7.5\times 10^{-12}.

By further numerical evaluations (using Wolfram Alpha) we have that

\widehat{g}(0)=-\frac{1}{\log 2}\Gamma\big(\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}\big)\approx 5.1278218186

and

|\widehat{g}(1)|=|\widehat{g}(-1)|=\Big|\frac{1}{\log 2}\Gamma\big(\frac{\log\log 2+2\pi i}{\log 2}\big)\Big|\approx 2.4479026947\times 10^{-7}.

Thus we conclude that

\frac{\max g}{\min g}\leqslant\frac{\widehat{g}(0)+\sum_{m\neq 0}|\widehat{g}(m)|}{\widehat{g}(0)-\sum_{m\neq 0}|\widehat{g}(m)|}\leqslant 1+2\cdot 10^{-7},

which is the desired bound
Equation

10.3
.
∎

Part III
The Poisson paradigm

11.
Approximate
\ell
-wise independence

The main result of this section is
Lemma

11.2
. We will describe it informally below, but in order to do so we set up some notation.

We will consider a random multiset
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
sampled conditioned on
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
for some fixed pair of upper and lower walks
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
. The general setup under discussion here is outlined in
Section

5
and the reader may wish to review that now. We recall in particular from
Equations

5.1
,
5.2
, and
5.3
that

\mathbf{A}=(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{i\in[n],j\in[b_{i}]}
, where

(11.1)

b_{i}:=1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}\;\;(i=1,\dots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)\;\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\;b_{n+1-i}:=1+\xi_{i}\;\;(i=1,\dots,\lceil n/2\rceil).

Throughout the section we will write

(11.2)

D=D(\beta,\beta^{\prime}):=\beta(\lceil n/2\rceil)-\beta^{\prime}(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}-n.

We occasionally refer to this quantity as the
discrepancy
of
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
.

From now on we fix a truncation parameter
M
; the walks under consideration will have

\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil}\beta(i),\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\beta^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-M,

and later we will let
M\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Associated to
M
we will also associate the following key truncation thresholds.

Definition 11.1
.

Given
M
we define

•

R(M):=20M
is a threshold for the
R
-boundedness of walks;

•

T(M):=e^{40M/\eta}
is a threshold for the
T
-positivity of walks;

•

L(M)
is an extremely large function of
M
. (How large it needs to be is reliant on the bounds in
Lemma

8.19
, the result on the existence of jump steps; the point at which we need
L(M)
to be very large is in the proof of
Lemma

13.4
.)

These thresholds will be in place for the next several sections. We will also require some more technical parameters namely
\ell_{*}(M),V(M),t_{\min}(M),t_{\max}(M)
. The parameter hierarchy will be

(11.3)

T(M)\lll\ell_{*}(M)\lll V(M)\lll t_{\min}(M)\lll t_{\max}(M)\lll L(M)<n/2.

and
V(M)
will be an integer. For the rest of this section we will drop the explicit mention of
M
when referring to
R
,
T
,
\ell_{*}
,
V
,
t_{\min}
,
t_{\max}
and
L
for notational brevity. We briefly discuss the purpose of the rest of these parameters.

The
\ell_{*}
parameter will control the depth to which we manage to run a crucial inclusion-exclusion principle during the argument.

The
V,t_{\min},t_{\max}
parameters are connected with the (rather technical) notion of jump step (
Definition

8.17
). Throughout the next two sections we will assume that
\beta
has a
V
-jump step at some index
t=t(\beta)
which satisfies
t_{\min}\leqslant t\leqslant t_{\max}
; for definiteness we take
t
minimal subject to these constraints. We informally remark that by
Lemma

8.19
this will be a generic property of
\beta
for appropriate choices of parameters. Note also that
b_{n+1-t}=1+\xi_{t}=V
. The jump step
t
is associated with the random elements
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
,
j\in[V]
, of
\mathbf{A}
, all of which have size
\asymp 2^{n-t}
; these will play a special role in what follows.

For technical reasons to do with calculations in the local limit theorem we also need to condition on the approximate distribution of these elements
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
. To set this up let us briefly recall from
Equation

5.2
that
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}=\tilde{\phi}(n-\mathbf{u}_{t,j})
, where the
\mathbf{u}_{t,j}
,
j\in[V]
, are independent uniform random variables on
(t-1,t]
. The definition of
\tilde{\phi}
may be found at
Definition

5.1
; recall in particular that
\tilde{\phi}(x)\asymp 2^{x}
, so that indeed
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t}\asymp 2^{n-t}
.

For the rest of the section we abuse notation by writing
V^{1/3}
for
\lfloor V^{1/3}\rfloor
for brevity. Partition

(11.4)

(t-1,t]=\bigcup_{m=1}^{V^{1/3}}I_{m},\qquad I_{m}:=\big(t-1+\frac{m-1}{V^{1/3}},t-1+\frac{m}{V^{1/3}}\big].

For each
\mathbf{u}
we record the ‘jump distribution’ which is the random tuple
\mathbf{f}=(\mathbf{f}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{f}_{V^{1/3}})
where
\mathbf{f}_{m}
is the number (frequency) of the
\mathbf{u}_{t,j}
in the interval
I_{m}
. Thus, in particular we have
\sum_{m}\mathbf{f}_{m}=V
deterministically. The conditioned variables
(\mathbf{u}_{t,j}\mid\mathbf{f}=f)
consist of
f_{m}
independent uniform samples from
I_{m}
for
m=1,\dots,V^{1/3}
.

For the remainder of this section and much of the next, we will condition not just to fixed choices
\bm{\beta}=\beta
,
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}
, but also to a fixed choice
\mathbf{f}=f
. The precise point at which the additional conditioning on
\mathbf{f}
will be removed is after
Equation

12.36
below. For notational brevity, until that point we write
\mathbf{A}
rather than
(\mathbf{A}\mid\bm{\beta}=\beta,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime},\mathbf{f}=f)
. The key result of the section is
Lemma

11.2
below. The main aim of this result (cf.
Equation

4.3
) is to understand the quantity
\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))
(with
\mathbf{A}
conditioned to
\bm{\beta}=\beta,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime},\mathbf{f}=f
as discussed above).

The statement of
Lemma

11.2
is a little technical, and the proof lengthy, so we start by offering some motivation. We set

(11.5)

\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}}:=(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L,j\in[b_{i}]},\quad\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}:=(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{L+1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t,j\in[b_{i}]},

and

\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}:=(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n,j\in[b_{i}]}.

Note in particular that the elements associated to the jump step of
\beta
are the elements of
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}
with the highest index
i=n+1-t
.
We remind the reader that
\Sigma(\mathbf{A})
is the set of sums
\{\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n,j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:\varepsilon_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}\}
, and (for example)
\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})
is defined analogously.

We consider the event
(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))
as the union of the events
X_{\varepsilon}=(k-\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n,j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}}))
, over all choices of
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{large}}:=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n,j\in[b_{i}]}
. The idea will be to compute
\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))
by using inclusion-exclusion on these events. To enable this, we need to show that they are approximately
\ell
-wise independent for
\ell\rightarrow\infty
.

We relabel the elements of
\mathbf{A}
associated to the jump step
t
as
(\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j})_{j\in[V]}
to be consistent with the conditioning to
\mathbf{f}=f
. Thus we write
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}=\tilde{\phi}(n-\mathbf{U}_{m})
where
m=m(j)
is the unique value of
m\in\{1,\dots V^{1/3}\}
for which
f_{1}+\cdots f_{m-1}<j\leqslant f_{1}+\cdots+f_{m}
,
\mathbf{U}_{m}
is uniform on
I_{m}
and
\tilde{\phi}
is defined in
Definition

5.1
. Unravelling the definitions, we thus have

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}=x)=V^{1/3}\big|(n-I_{m})\cap\big[\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{x-1},\frac{n}{H_{k}}H_{x}\big)\big|.

Thus, if we set
\alpha:=V^{1/3}n/H_{k}\approx V^{1/3}/\log 2
then the distribution of
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
is as follows. There are integers
x_{0},x_{1}\asymp 2^{n-t}
and
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}
with
0\leqslant\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\leqslant\alpha
(all potentially depending on
j
) such that:

(11.6)

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}=x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{$x<x_{0}$ or $x>x_{1}$};\\
\alpha_{0}/x_{0}&x=x_{0};\\
\alpha/x&x_{0}<x<x_{1};\\
\alpha_{1}/x_{1}&x=x_{1}.\end{array}\right.

Thus,
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
takes values in an interval of length
\asymp V^{-1/3}2^{n-t}
. A particular point to note is that we must replace the anticoncentration bound
Equation

5.8
for
i=n+1-t
by the weaker bound

(11.7)

\sup_{x}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}=x)\ll V^{1/3}2^{t-n}

(which follows immediately from
Equation

11.6
); for other values of
i
it is unchanged.

Define
\mu_{j}=\mathbb{E}\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
and write

(11.8)

\mu=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{V}\mu_{j}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{V}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}^{2}.

In various places in the analysis we will use that (deterministically, and uniformly in
f
)

(11.9)

\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j},\mu_{j}\asymp 2^{n-t},\qquad|\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}-\mu_{j}|\ll V^{-1/3}2^{n-t},\qquad\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t},

all of which are clear from the definitions. Finally let
g
be the Gaussian density with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
,

(11.10)

g(x):=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\big(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma}\big)^{2}},

noting that once again this depends on
f
.

Finally we come to the statement of
Lemma

11.2
. Recall
Definitions

4.1
and
4.2
.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
be upper and lower walks that are each
R
-bounded and
T
-positive up to lengths
\lceil n/2\rceil,\lfloor n/2\rfloor
respectively. Set
L:=L(M)<n/2
. Suppose that
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil}\beta(i)
,
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\beta^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-M
, and that
|D|\leqslant M/10
, where the discrepancy
D
is given by
Equation

11.2
.
Suppose that
\beta
has a
V
-jump step. Let
n^{\prime}
be an integer satisfying
L\leqslant n^{\prime}\leqslant n-L
. Suppose that
S\subset\mathbf{N}
is a set of size
\tau 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}b_{i}}
with
\max(S)\leqslant 2^{n-L/2}
and where
V^{-1}\leqslant\tau\leqslant 1
. Let
\ell\leqslant\ell_{*}
, and let
x_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,\ell
, be positive integers such that

(11.11)

|x_{i}-\mu|\leqslant(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma.

Furthermore suppose that we have the separation condition

(11.12)

|x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}}|>2^{n-L/2}

for
i\neq i^{\prime}
. Write
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>n^{\prime}}:=(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{n^{\prime}+1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t,j\in[b_{i}]}
. Then we have that

(11.13)

\mathbb{P}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}\in S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}^{>n^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{med}})\big)=\big(1+O(V^{-1/4})\big)\Big(\tau 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}\Big)^{\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x_{i}).

Remarks
.

Note that
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>L}=\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}
. Note that by
Equations

11.8
,
11.9
, and
11.11
we have

(11.14)

x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}\asymp V2^{n-t},

and so one typically expects the separation condition to be comfortably satisfied; see
Lemma

12.4
for more details on this.

The following simple lemma will feature a few times in the proof.

Lemma 11.3
.

Suppose that
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
are upper and lower walks. Suppose that
\beta
is
T
-positive up to length
\lceil n/2\rceil
, and that the discrepancy
D=D(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
satisfies
|D|\leqslant M/10
. Let notation be as in
Equation

11.1
. Then

(11.15)

-2t_{\max}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\leqslant-\tfrac{1}{2}t^{1/2-\eta},

and

(11.16)

\sum_{i=1}^{n-L}b_{i}<n-L.

Proof.

From
Equations

11.1
and
11.2
we have that if
m<\lceil n/2\rceil
then

(11.17)

\sum_{i=1}^{n-m}b_{i}=n+D-(m+\beta(m)).

We first prove
Equation

11.15
. We certainly have
t\leqslant t_{\max}\lll n/2
, and so by
Equation

11.17
we have
\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)=D+1-\beta(t-1)
.

For the lower bound, by the
T
-positive nature of the walk
\beta
it follows that
D-\beta(t-1)\geqslant D-T-(t-1)^{1/2+\eta}
, and the result follows from the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
, noting in particular that
t\leqslant t_{\max}
.

For the upper bound, by the
T
-positive nature of
\beta
it follows that
D-\beta(t-1)\leqslant D+T-(t-1)^{1/2-\eta}
, and the result again follows from the parameter hierarchy, specifically that
t\geqslant t_{\min}\ggg T
, and all these parameters are sufficiently large in terms of
M
. For
Equation

11.16
, an analogous proof works since
L\ggg T
.
∎

For the proof of
Lemma

11.2
(which is rather lengthy), the plan is to first establish a weighted version of
Equation

11.13
in which the number of representations in
S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{\geqslant n^{\prime}})
is counted. This is stated as
Lemma

11.4
below. However, in practice the number of representations is essentially always either 0 or 1, so we can later remove the weights. To do this rigorously a related technical result (
Lemma

11.5
below) is required. The deduction of
Lemma

11.2
from the weighted version is quite short and is given at the end of the section.

Fix
S
and
n^{\prime}
and define
R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)
to be the number of representations of
x
in
S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>n^{\prime}})
, that is to say as a sum of a
\{0,1\}
-combination of the
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
,
n^{\prime}+1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t,j\in[b_{i}]
, and an element of
S
.

Lemma 11.4
.

With the notation and assumptions of
Lemma

11.2
, and with
R_{\mathbf{A}}
as just defined, we have

(11.18)

\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})=\big(1+O(V^{-1/4})\big)\Big(\tau 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}\Big)^{\ell}g(x_{1})\cdots g(x_{\ell}).

Proof.

Denote by
\Xi
the set of pairs
(i,j)
with
n^{\prime}+1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t
and
j\in[b_{i}]
. Thus

(11.19)

|\Xi|=\sum_{i=n^{\prime}+1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}.

Note for future reference that, since
n^{\prime}\leqslant n-L
and
b_{i}\geqslant 0
for all
i
,

(11.20)

|\Xi|\geqslant\sum_{i=n+1-L}^{n+1-t}b_{i}=(L+\beta(L))-\big((t-1)+\beta(t-1)\big)>L/2

by the
T
-positive property
Definition

4.2
and the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
, and similarly, using the parameter hierarchy and that
n^{\prime}\geqslant L
,
L<n/2
and
|D|<M
,

(11.21)

|\Xi|\leqslant\sum_{i=L+1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}=n+D-\big((t-1)+\beta(t-1)\big)-(L-\beta^{\prime}(L))<n-L/2.

Recall that by definition
R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)
is the number of pairs
(\varepsilon,s)\in\{0,1\}^{\Xi}\times S
with

\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}+s=x.

Thus

(11.22)

\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})=\sum_{\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\in\{0,1\}^{\Xi}}\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell}\in S}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x_{u}-s_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big).

For each choice of
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}
consider the
\ell
-by-
|\Xi|
matrix
M(\varepsilon):=(\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j})_{u\in[\ell],(i,j)\in\Xi}
formed with the
\varepsilon^{(u)}
as rows. We will consider the columns
(\varepsilon_{i,j}^{(u)})_{u\in[\ell]}
to be ordered by decreasing
i
and increasing
j
; thus the first
V
columns of
M(\varepsilon)
correspond to
(i,j)=(n+1-t,j)
,
j\in[b_{n+1-t}]=[V]
, these being the ones associated to the jump step.

To estimate the inner probability in
Equation

11.22
, we will need to pay attention to the linear-algebraic structure of this matrix
M(\varepsilon)
. To this end, recall the definition of rank profile from
Section

9
and split the sum according to the rank profile. Write
E_{(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})}
for the contribution of the terms with
\operatorname{rp}(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})=(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})
to
Equation

11.22
.

Our first main task (which is already somewhat lengthy) will be to show that only terms with
r=\ell
and
q_{\ell}\leqslant V
contribute importantly; to this end will show that

(11.23)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})\\
r<\ell\mbox{\,\scriptsize or}\\
r=\ell\mbox{\, \scriptsize and \,}q_{\ell}>V\end{subarray}}E_{(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})}\ll 2^{-\Omega(V)}2^{\ell\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}.

To see that this is indeed negligible compared to the stated bound in
Lemma

11.2
we use the assumption that
\tau\geqslant V^{-1}
, the fact that
g(x_{i})\gg\sigma^{-1}e^{-C(\log V)^{1/2}}
(here we use the assumption
Equation

11.11
) and finally that
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
(see
Equation

11.9
) and that
V\ggg\ell
(from
Equation

11.3
).

The proof of the claim
Equation

11.23
will occupy the next few pages.
Consider to begin with a fixed choice of
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}
with
\operatorname{rp}(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})=(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})
. To bound the inner sum in
Equation

11.22
, first note that

(11.24)

\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell}\in S}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x_{u}-s_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big)\leqslant|S|^{r}\sup_{x^{\prime}_{u}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x^{\prime}_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big),

since (due to the rank of
M(\varepsilon)
being
r
) for fixed
x_{u}
the probability on the left can only be nonzero for at most
|S|^{r}
choices of
(s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell})
, since all
s_{u}
s are determined by some subset of
r
of them.

Consider the columns
(\varepsilon_{i,j}^{(u)})_{u\in[\ell]}
of
M(\varepsilon)
for
(i,j)\in\Xi
. We remind the reader that we order these first by decreasing
i
and then by increasing
j
. Let the columns corresponding to increments in the rank be
(n+1-i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(n+1-i_{r},j_{r})
where
t\leqslant i_{1}\leqslant i_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{r}
(note that these are completely determined by the rank profile). In particular observe that

(11.25)

\sum^{n+1-t}_{n+2-i_{d}}b_{i}<q_{d}\leqslant\sum^{n+1-t}_{n+1-i_{d}}b_{i},

a relation we will use several times. We claim that

(11.26)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x^{\prime}_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big)\ll V^{O(\ell)}\prod_{d=1}^{r}2^{i_{d}-n}.

To see this, first reveal all
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
with
(i,j)
not one of the
(n+1-i_{d},j_{d})
,
d\in[r]
. The remaining elements
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{n+1-i_{r},j_{r}}
are then determined (with the
\varepsilon
and
x^{\prime}_{u}
fixed). The claim
Equation

11.26
then follows from the anticoncentration bound
Equation

5.8
, unless
n+1-i_{d}=t
in which case we must instead use
Equation

11.7
, which potentially gives rise to the
V^{O(\ell)}
factors.

Combining
Equations

11.24
and
11.26
gives

(11.27)

\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell}\in S}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x_{u}-s_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big)\leqslant V^{O(\ell)}|S|^{r}\prod_{d=1}^{r}2^{i_{d}-n}.

From this,
Lemma

9.1
(i) and the definition of
E_{(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})}
(just after
Equation

11.22
) we have

\displaystyle E_{(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})}

\displaystyle\ll 2^{\ell^{2}}2^{r|\Xi|-\sum_{d=1}^{r}q_{d}}|S|^{r}V^{O(\ell)}\prod_{d=1}^{r}2^{i_{d}-n}

(11.28)

\displaystyle\ll V^{O(\ell)}2^{r\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)-\sum_{d=1}^{r}(q_{d}-i_{d}+t)}.

(Here, the
2^{\ell^{2}}
has been absorbed into the
V^{O(\ell)}
term due to the assumption
Equation

11.3
on parameters, and for the final step we used
Equation

11.19
and the crude bound
|S|\leqslant 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}b_{i}}
, which follows from the assumption that
\tau\leqslant 1
.) The bound
Equation

11.28
will be our key tool in the following analysis, the aim of which is to establish
Equation

11.23
. Below we will use it examine the contributions to
Equation

11.23
from various rank profiles.

Suppose that
d\in\{1,\dots,r\}
. Using
Equation

11.25
we have

(11.29)

q_{d}\geqslant\sum_{n+2-i_{d}}^{n+1-t}b_{i}=\sum_{n+2-i_{d}}^{n}b_{i}-(t-1+\beta(t-1))\geqslant\sum_{n+2-i_{d}}^{n}b_{i}-2t_{\max},

using the
T
-positive nature of
\beta
(see
Definition

4.2
) and that
t\leqslant t_{\max}
,
T\lll t_{\max}
.

Suppose first that
i_{d}\leqslant 1+\lceil n/2\rceil
. Then
Equation

11.29
gives
q_{d}\geqslant(i_{d}-1)+\beta(i_{d}-1)-2t_{\max}
. By the
T
-positive nature of
\beta
(and since
T\lll t_{\max}
) it follows that
q_{d}\geqslant i_{d}+i_{d}^{1/2-\eta}-3t_{\max}
, and therefore by
Lemma

D.2
we have
q_{d}\geqslant i_{d}+\frac{1}{2}q_{d}^{1/2-\eta}-3t_{\max}
.

Alternatively, suppose that
i_{d}>1+\lceil n/2\rceil
. Then
Equation

11.29
, the
T
-positive nature of
\beta^{\prime}
and the assumption that
|D|\leqslant M/10\lll t_{\max}
give
q_{d}\geqslant D-1+i_{d}+\beta^{\prime}(n-i_{d})-2t_{\max}\geqslant i_{d}+(n-i_{d})^{1/2-\eta}-3t_{\max}
and so
(n-q_{d})\leqslant(n-i_{d})-(n-i_{d})^{1/2-\eta}+3t_{\max}
. Note that by
Equation

11.21
and since
n+1-i_{d}\geqslant n^{\prime}+1
, we have
n-q_{d},n-i_{d}>0
. By
Lemma

D.3
it follows that
(n-q_{d})\leqslant(n-i_{d})-\frac{1}{2}(n-q_{d})^{1/2-\eta}+6t_{\max}
.

In both cases we have

(11.30)

q_{d}-i_{d}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\min(q_{d},n-q_{d})^{1/2-\eta}-O(t_{\max}).

We now turn to the promised analysis of the contributions to
Equation

11.23
from various rank profiles.

Step 1: the contribution from
q_{r}\geqslant t_{\max}^{10}
. Using the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
it follows from
Equations

11.28
and
11.30
that

E_{(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})}\ll 2^{O(\ell t_{\max})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{d=1}^{r}\min(q_{d},n-q_{d})^{1/2-\eta}}2^{r\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}.

By
Equation

11.15
we may replace the preceding with

E_{(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})}\ll 2^{O(\ell t_{\max})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{d=1}^{r}\min(q_{d},n-q_{d})^{1/2-\eta}}2^{\ell\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}.

We sum this over all rank profiles
(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})
with
r\leqslant\ell
and
\max_{d}\min(q_{d},n-q_{d})=q
, i.e. all
q_{d}
are either
\leqslant q
or
\geqslant n-q
, with at least one equality. The number of choices for these is
\leqslant\ell(2q)^{\ell}
, so the contribution of them to
Equation

11.23
is

\ll\ell(2q)^{\ell}2^{O(\ell t_{\max})-\frac{1}{2}q^{1/2-\eta}}2^{\ell\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}.

Taking into account the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
, the sum of this over
q\geqslant t_{\max}^{10}
is

\ll 2^{-t_{\max}^{4}}2^{\ell\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)},

which is completely negligible in comparison to the desired bound
Equation

11.23
since
t_{\max}
is much bigger than
V
.

To complete the analysis of Step 1, it suffices to show that if
q<t_{\max}^{10}
then
q_{r}<t_{\max}^{10}
. By the definition of
q
, it suffices to show that
q_{r}<n-t_{\max}^{10}
. This follows immediately from
Equation

11.21
and the parameter hierarchy, since
q_{r}\leqslant|\Xi|\leqslant n-L/2<n-t_{\max}^{10}
.

Step 2: The contribution to
Equation

11.23
from
r<\ell
and
q_{r}\leqslant t_{\max}^{10}
. We consider further the structure of the
\varepsilon^{(u)}
. Suppose first that there are only
r
distinct
\varepsilon^{(u)}
. Then in order for the system of equations
\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon_{i,j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x_{u}-s_{u}
,
u\in[\ell]
to have any solutions in the
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
at all, we must have
x_{u}-s_{u}=x_{u^{\prime}}-s_{u^{\prime}}
for some
u\neq u^{\prime}
, and therefore
|x_{u}-x_{u^{\prime}}|\leqslant\operatorname{diam}(S)\leqslant\max(S)\leqslant 2^{n-L/2}
by assumption. This is contrary to the separation assumption
Equation

11.12
.
Thus the contribution of these
(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})
to
Equation

11.22
is zero.

Therefore there are at least
r+1
different
\varepsilon^{(u)}
. Suppose first that one of these is the zero vector. Then we have
x_{u}=s_{u}
and so
x_{u}\leqslant\max(S)\leqslant 2^{n-L/2}
. However, as noted in
Equation

11.14
, we have
x_{u}\asymp V2^{n-t}
, so there is a contradiction since
L\ggg t
.

Suppose, then, that there are at least
r+1
different nonzero
\varepsilon^{(u)}
. However, we showed in
Lemma

9.1
(ii) that such matrices are relatively rare; there are at most
2^{\ell^{2}}2^{(r-\frac{1}{10})|\Xi|}
of them, noting that the condition
|\Xi|\geqslant 10\ell
required in
Lemma

9.1
(ii) is satisfied by
Equation

11.20
and the parameter hierarchy.

Using
Equation

11.27
(and recalling
Equation

11.22
), we see that the contribution of this case to the LHS of
Equation

11.23
is

(11.31)

\ll V^{O(\ell)}2^{(r-\frac{1}{10})|\Xi|}|S|^{r}\prod_{d=1}^{r}2^{i_{d}-n}\ll 2^{-|\Xi|/10}V^{O(\ell)}2^{r\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}2^{-rt+\sum_{d=1}^{r}i_{d}},

where in the last step we used
Equation

11.19
and the crude bound
|S|\leqslant 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}b_{i}}
.

To bound this we use
Equation

11.30
, which certainly implies that
i_{d}\leqslant q_{d}+O(t_{\max})\leqslant 2t_{\max}^{10}
, as well as
\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\geqslant-2t_{\max}
, which is the left-hand inequality in
Equation

11.15
. This gives that
Equation

11.31
is at most

\ll 2^{-|\Xi|/10}V^{O(\ell)}2^{O(\ell t_{\max}^{10})}2^{\ell\big(\sum_{i\leqslant n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}.

This is bounded by the RHS of
Equation

11.23
due to the fact that
|\Xi|>L/2
(which is
Equation

11.20
) and the parameter hierachy, which has
L
vastly larger than
V,\ell
and
t_{\max}
, and so the analysis of Step 2 is complete.

Step 3: The contribution to
Equation

11.23
from
r=\ell
and
V<q_{\ell}\leqslant t_{\max}^{10}
. Here we use that
t
is a
V
-jump step (
Definition

8.17
), which has not previously come in to the analysis. From
Equation

11.28
we see that it suffices to show that

(11.32)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1},\dots,q_{s}\leqslant V\\
V<q_{s+1},\dots,q_{\ell}\leqslant t_{\max}^{10}\end{subarray}}2^{-\ell t+\sum_{d=1}^{\ell}(i_{d}-q_{d})}\ll 2^{-V}.

for every
s<\ell
(and then sum over the
\ell
choices for
s
and the
V^{O(\ell)}
choices for
q_{1},\dots,q_{s}\leqslant V
, always bearing in mind that
\ell
is much smaller than
V
so that these factors contribute only
2^{o(V)})
. Now for
d\leqslant s
we have
q_{d}\leqslant V=b_{n+1-t}
and so
i_{d}=t
and hence
-t+(i_{d}-q_{d})\leqslant 0
, and so it suffices to show

(11.33)

\sum_{V<q_{d}\leqslant t_{\max}^{10}}2^{-t+(i_{d}-q_{d})}\ll 2^{-V}

for all
d=s+1,\dots,\ell
, whereupon the desired bound
Equation

11.32
follows by taking the product over
d
(since by the assumption of Step 3 there is at least one
d
in the range). Fix such a
d\in\{s+1,\dots,\ell\}
. We have
i_{d}>t
. By
Equation

11.30
we also have (comfortably)
i_{d}<n/2
, and so by
Equation

11.25
and
Equation

8.23
we have

\displaystyle q_{d}>\sum_{n+2-i_{d}}^{n+1-t}b_{i}

\displaystyle=V+\sum_{n+2-i_{d}}^{n-t}b_{i}=V+(i_{d}-1)+\beta(i_{d}-1)-t-\beta(t)

\displaystyle\geqslant V+(i_{d}-1-t)+(i_{d}-1-t)^{1/2-2\eta}.

The LHS of
Equation

11.33
is therefore bounded above by

(11.34)

\ll 2^{-V}\sum_{V<q_{d}\leqslant t_{\max}^{10}}2^{-(i_{d}-1-t)^{1/2-2\eta}}\ll 2^{-V}\sum_{V<q_{d}\leqslant t_{\max}^{10}}2^{-(i_{d}-t)^{1/2-2\eta}}.

To bound this we additionally use
Equation

8.24
(and
Equation

11.25
again). This gives that

q_{d}\leqslant\sum_{n+1-i_{d}}^{n+1-t}b_{i}\leqslant V+O\big(\sum_{u=1}^{i_{d}-t}u^{2\kappa}\big)\leqslant V+O\big((i_{d}-t)^{1+2\kappa}\big),

and so

(i_{d}-t)^{1/2-2\eta}\gg(q_{d}-V)^{(1/2-2\eta)/(1+2\kappa)}\geqslant(q_{d}-V)^{1/3}

(since
\eta=\kappa=\frac{1}{100}
). Therefore
Equation

11.34
is bounded by

2^{-V}\sum_{q_{d}>V}2^{-\Omega((q_{d}-V)^{1/3})}\ll 2^{-V}

and so the required bound
Equation

11.33
holds.

Steps 1,2 and 3 cover all cases in
Equation

11.23
and so the proof of that statement is now complete. The remaining argument required for the proof of
Lemma

11.2
concerns the contribution to
Equation

11.22
from
\operatorname{rp}(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})=(\ell;q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell})
with
q_{\ell}\leqslant V
. Equivalently, the matrix
(\varepsilon^{(u)}_{n+1-t,j})_{u\in[\ell],j\in[V]}
formed from the first
V
columns of
M(\varepsilon)
has full rank
\ell
. Beyond this point we will use only this property; the notion of rank profile will have no further role.

Before turning to the heart of the argument, we make one further reduction, which is to the case where at least
4^{-\ell}V
of the
V
columns
(\varepsilon_{n+1-t,j}^{(u)})_{u\in[\ell]}
,
j=1,\dots,V
are the basis vector
e_{i}\in\mathbf{Q}^{\ell}
, for every
i=1,\dots,\ell
. In this situation we say that
(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})
is
good
. We claim that (with the
\varepsilon
sampled independently at random)

(11.35)

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}((\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})\;\mbox{bad})\leqslant e^{-\Omega(2^{-\ell}V)}.

For fixed
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
denote by
X_{j}
the event that
(\varepsilon_{n+1-t,j}^{(u)})_{u\in[\ell]}=e_{i}\in\mathbf{Q}^{\ell}
, thus
X_{j}
is a Bernoulli random variable with mean
p:=2^{-\ell}
and
X:=\sum_{j=1}^{V}X_{j}
has the binomial
\operatorname{Bi}(V,p)
distribution with mean
pV
. By
Lemma

F.1
(and since
p\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
) we have
\mathbb{P}(X\leqslant p^{2}V)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(|X-\mathbb{E}X|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}X)\leqslant 2e^{-pV/12}
. Summing over
i=1,\dots,\ell
gives

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}((\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})\;\mbox{bad})\leqslant 2\ell e^{-pV/12}\leqslant e^{-\Omega(2^{-\ell}V)},

which is the desired claim
Equation

11.35
.

Thus the
number
of
(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})
which are bad is
\leqslant e^{-\Omega(2^{-\ell}V)}2^{\ell|\Xi|}
. For each such bad matrix, we bound the inner sum in
Equation

11.22
using
Equation

11.27
, as before. Now we have
r=\ell
and
i_{1}=\cdots=i_{\ell}=t
since all the rank jumps of
M(\varepsilon)
occur in the first
V
columns (with the ordering of columns described above, that is to say these first
V
columns are the ones indexed by
(n+1-t,1),\dots,(n+1-t,V)
). Thus we see that the contribution of these terms to
Equation

11.22
is

(11.36)

\ll e^{-\Omega(2^{-\ell}V)}2^{\ell|\Xi|}\cdot|S|^{\ell}V^{O(\ell)}2^{\ell(t-n)}.

By the parameter hierarchy we have
e^{-\Omega(2^{-\ell}V)}V^{O(\ell)}<e^{-V^{1/2}}
. Using the trivial bound
|S|\leqslant 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}b_{i}}
and
Equation

11.19
, we see that
Equation

11.36
is

\ll e^{-V^{1/2}}2^{\ell\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}.

This is negligible compared to the stated bound in
Lemma

11.2
by the discussion immediately following
Equation

11.23
.

It remains to analyse the contribution to
Equation

11.22
from good tuples
(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})
. To spell it out, we wish to show that

(11.37)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})\\
\operatorname{good}\\
s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell}\in S\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x_{u}-s_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big)=\big(1+O(V^{-1/4})\big)\Big(\tau 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}\Big)^{\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x_{i}).

We now reduce to handling only the indices with
i=n+1-t
, that is to say ‘at the jump step’. To this end write

h(\mathbf{A},\varepsilon^{(u)})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j)\in\Xi\\
i<n+1-t\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}.

Observe that using
Equations

5.7
and
8.24
that we have (for all choices of the
\varepsilon^{(u)}
, and deterministically in
\mathbf{A}
)

(11.38)

|h(\mathbf{A},\varepsilon^{(u)})|\ll 2^{n-t}\sum_{u\geqslant 1}b_{n+1-t-u}2^{-u}\ll 2^{n-t}\sum_{u\geqslant 1}u^{2\kappa}2^{-u}\ll 2^{n-t}.

Write
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(u)}=(\varepsilon_{n+1-t,j}^{(u)})_{j\in[V]}\in\{0,1\}^{V}
and also
\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}:=\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
. In the light of
Equation

11.38
, and since
|S|=\tau 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}b_{i}}
and
|\Xi|=\sum_{i=n^{\prime}+1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}=V+\sum_{i=n^{\prime}+1}^{n-t}b_{i}
, in order to establish
Equation

11.37
it suffices to show

(11.39)

\sum_{(\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(\ell)})\operatorname{good}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{V}\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump},j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}=x^{\prime}_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big)=\big(1+O(V^{-1/4})\big)2^{V\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x_{i})

uniformly for
|x^{\prime}_{u}-x_{u}|\ll 2^{n-t}
, applying this bound with the choice
x^{\prime}_{u}=x_{u}-s_{u}-h(\mathbf{A},\varepsilon^{(u)})
then summing over all
s_{u}
, all
\varepsilon_{i,j}^{(u)}
,
i<n+1-t,j\in[b_{i}]
, and revealing the remaining elements
(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{i<n+1-t}
of
(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\Xi}
. (Here there is a slight abuse of notation in talking about
(\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(\ell)})
being good, a notion which was originally defined with reference to the full vectors
\varepsilon^{(u)}
but in fact only depends on the
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(u)}
.)

Now we observe that

(11.40)

\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x_{i})=(1+O(V^{-1/4}))\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x^{\prime}_{i}).

This follows fairly simply from the analytic properties of
g(\cdot)
. Indeed, using
|x^{\prime}_{i}-x_{i}|\ll 2^{n-t}
and that
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
, we have
|\sigma^{-1}(x_{i}-\mu)-\sigma^{-1}(x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu)|\ll V^{-1/2}
, and so from the uniformly Lipschitz nature of
e^{-x^{2}/2}
we have
|g(x_{i})-g(x^{\prime}_{i})|\ll V^{-1/2}\sigma^{-1}
. Therefore
|\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x_{i})-\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x^{\prime}_{i})|\ll 2^{\ell}\sigma^{-\ell}V^{-1/2}\ll V^{-1/3}\sigma^{-\ell}
. Finally, using that
\sigma^{-1}|x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu|\leqslant\sigma^{-1}|x_{i}-\mu|+O(V^{-1/2})\leqslant 2(\log V)^{1/4}
we have
g(x^{\prime}_{i})\geqslant(2\pi)^{-1/2}\sigma^{-1}e^{-2(\log V)^{1/2}}
, and so
V^{-1/3}\sigma^{-\ell}\ll V^{-1/4}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x^{\prime}_{i})
. This completes the proof of
Equation

11.40
.
Thus
Equation

11.39
follows from

(11.41)

\sum_{(\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(\ell)})\operatorname{good}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{V}\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump},j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}=x^{\prime}_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big)=(1+O(V^{-1/4}))2^{V\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x^{\prime}_{i}),

where we may assume
\sigma^{-1}|x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu|\leqslant 2(\log V)^{1/4}
.
We phrase this task probabilistically, thus
Equation

11.41
is the statement that

(11.42)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{V}\bm{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{jump},j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}=x^{\prime}_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big)=\big(1+O(V^{-1/4})\big)\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x^{\prime}_{i}),

where the tuple
(\bm{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(\ell)})
is sampled uniformly from all good tuples, and the probability is taken over the random choices of the
\bm{\varepsilon}
and the
\mathbf{a}
. Here, we have noted that by our earlier estimate
Equation

11.35
on the number of bad tuples, and the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
, the sample space of
\bm{\varepsilon}
tuples has size
\big(1-O(e^{-\Omega(2^{-\ell}V)})\big)^{\ell}2^{V\ell}=(1+O(V^{-1/2}))2^{V\ell}
.

As before we have
g(x^{\prime}_{i})\geqslant(2\pi)^{-1/2}\sigma^{-1}e^{-2(\log V)^{1/2}}
and so in order to establish
Equation

11.42
it is enough to prove

(11.43)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{V}\bm{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{jump},j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}=x^{\prime}_{u},u\in[\ell]\Big)=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x^{\prime}_{i})+O(V^{-1/4}\sigma^{-\ell}).

We will prove this for
all

(x^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in[\ell]}\in\mathbf{R}^{\ell}
. For notational brevity, until the end of the proof (of
Lemma

11.4
) we drop the jump subscripts; thus we write simply
\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}:=\bm{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{jump},j}
,
\mathbf{a}_{j}:=\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}
. Define the random
\mathbf{R}^{\ell}
-valued random variable

(11.44)

X:=\frac{1}{\sigma}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{V}\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu\big)_{u\in[\ell]}.

Let

\gamma(x):=\sigma^{-\ell}(2\pi)^{-\ell/2}e^{-\|x\|_{2}^{2}/2}.

Then (dropping the dashes on the
x^{\prime}_{i}
) the desired bound
Equation

11.43
can be rephrased as the following local limit bound:

(11.45)

\sup_{x\in((\mathbf{Z}-\mu)/\sigma)^{\ell}}\big|\mathbb{P}(X=x)-\gamma(x)\big|\ll V^{-1/4}\sigma^{-\ell}.

As usual with statements of local limit type, we approach this task with a Fourier argument.
By orthogonality of characters we have that

\mathbb{P}(X=x)=(2\pi\sigma)^{-\ell}\int_{[-\pi\sigma,\pi\sigma]^{\ell}}e^{-i\theta\cdot x}\mathbb{E}(e^{i\theta\cdot X})\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

Also, by direct calculation we have

\gamma(x)=(2\pi\sigma)^{-\ell}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{\ell}}e^{-i\theta\cdot x}e^{-\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

Therefore the required bound
Equation

11.45
follows from

(11.46)

\Big|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{\ell}}e^{-i\theta\cdot x}e^{-\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{d}\theta-\int_{[-\pi\sigma,\pi\sigma]^{\ell}}e^{-i\theta\cdot x}\mathbb{E}(e^{i\theta\cdot X})\,\mathrm{d}\theta\Big|\ll V^{-1/4}.

We prove this by establishing the following three estimates:

(11.47)

\sup_{\theta\in[-\log V,\log V]^{\ell}}\big|\mathbb{E}(e^{i\theta\cdot X})-e^{-\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2}\big|\ll V^{-2/7};

(11.48)

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{\ell}\setminus[-\log V,\log V]^{\ell}}e^{-\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{d}\theta\ll V^{-10};

and

(11.49)

\int_{[-\pi\sigma,\pi\sigma]^{\ell}\setminus[-\log V,\log V]^{\ell}}|\mathbb{E}(e^{i\theta\cdot X})|\,\mathrm{d}\theta\ll V^{-10}.

These three estimates are easily seen to imply
Equation

11.46
, using here that
(2\log V)^{\ell}=V^{o(1)}
to handle the contribution from
\|\theta\|_{\infty}\leqslant\log V
.

Proof of
Equation

11.47
.
This is essentially a standard computation associated with proving a multivariate central limit theorem. Since the proportion
(\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}^{(\ell)})
of tuples in
(\{0,1\}^{V})^{\ell}
that are bad is exponentially small in
V
(see
Equation

11.35
), in this range we may for the purposes of proving
Equation

11.47
drop the ‘good’ condition; that is to say it suffices to prove
Equation

11.47
with
X
replaced by the variable
\tilde{X}
, defined as in
Equation

11.44
but with the
\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}
sampled independently at random from
\{0,1\}
. Let
\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell})
and for notational convenience in the rest of the proof write
e(\xi):=e^{i\xi}
. Assume (as in the estimate
Equation

11.47
we are trying to prove) that
|\theta_{u}|\leqslant\log V
for all
u
.

From this definition and
Equation

11.8
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(e^{i\tilde{X}\cdot\theta})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\varepsilon},\mathbf{a}}e\big(\frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{u=1}^{\ell}\sum_{j=1}^{V}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{j}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{j})\theta_{u}\big)

(11.50)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}\prod_{j=1}^{V}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}e\big(\frac{1}{2\sigma}(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})\theta_{u}\big)\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}\prod_{j=1}^{V}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}e\big(\frac{1}{\sigma}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}-\tfrac{1}{2})\mathbf{a}_{j}\theta_{u}\big).

To estimate the inner average over the
\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}
we use that

(11.51)

\frac{1}{2}\big(e(\xi)+e(-\xi)\big)=e^{-\xi^{2}/2}(1+O(|\xi|^{3}))

for
|\xi|\leqslant 1
. We apply this with
\xi=\mathbf{a}_{j}\theta_{u}/2\sigma
. Note (see
Equation

11.9
) that
\mathbf{a}_{j}\asymp 2^{n-t}
,
|\theta_{u}|\leqslant\log V
and
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
, so
|\xi|\ll V^{-1/2}\log V
. Therefore substituting
Equation

11.51
into
Equation

11.50
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}(e^{i\tilde{X}\cdot\theta})=\mathbb{E}(1+\eta(\mathbf{a}))\prod_{j=1}^{V}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}e\big(\frac{1}{2\sigma}(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})\theta_{u}\big)\cdot\exp\Big(-\sum_{j=1}^{V}\sum_{u=1}^{\ell}\frac{\mathbf{a}_{j}^{2}\theta_{u}^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\Big)

where
\|\eta\|_{\infty}\ll\ell V(V^{-1/2}\log V)^{3}\ll V^{-1/2+o(1)}
.
Now from
Equations

11.8
and
11.9
we have
\mathbf{a}_{j}^{2}=(1+O(V^{-1/3}))\mu_{j}^{2}=(1+O(V^{-1/3}))\mathbb{E}\mathbf{a}_{j}^{2}
and so
\sum_{j=1}^{V}\mathbf{a}_{j}^{2}=4\sigma^{2}(1+O(V^{-1/3}))
; since
\sum_{u=1}^{\ell}\theta_{u}^{2}=\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}
, this gives

\mathbb{E}(e^{i\tilde{X}\cdot\theta})=e^{-\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}(1+\eta^{\prime}(\mathbf{a}))\prod_{j=1}^{V}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}e\big(\frac{1}{2\sigma}(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})\theta_{u}\big)

where
\|\eta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\ll\ell V^{-1/3}\log V\ll V^{-2/7}
. Consequently

\mathbb{E}(e^{i\tilde{X}\cdot\theta})=O(V^{-2/7})+e^{-\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}\prod_{j=1}^{V}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}e\big(\frac{1}{2\sigma}(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})\theta_{u}\big).

Hence, in order to prove the desired result
Equation

11.47
it suffices to prove that

(11.52)

\mathbb{E}\prod_{j=1}^{V}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}e\big(\frac{1}{2\sigma}(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})\theta_{u}\big)=1+O(V^{-1/2}).

Note that the LHS here is

\prod_{j=1}^{V}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}_{j}}e\big(\lambda(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})\big),

where
\lambda:=\frac{1}{2\sigma}\sum_{u=1}^{\ell}\theta_{u}
. By
Equation

11.9
and the fact that
|\theta_{u}|\leqslant\log V
we have
|\lambda(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})|\ll(\ell\log V)V^{-5/6}\ll V^{-3/4}\lll 1
and so

e\big(\lambda(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})\big)=1+i\lambda(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})+O(\lambda^{2}(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})^{2});

taking expectations gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}_{j}}e\big(\lambda(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})\big)=1+O(\lambda^{2}(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mu_{j})^{2})=1+O(V^{-3/2}),

and
Equation

11.52
follows by taking the product over
j=1,\dots,V
.

Proof of
Equation

11.48
.
This is a straightforward exercise using
\int_{t>r}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt\leqslant r^{-1}\int_{t>r}te^{-t^{2}/2}dt=r^{-1}e^{-r^{2}/2}
.

Proof of
Equation

11.49
.
We split into dyadic ranges
K\leqslant\|\theta\|_{\infty}<2K
,
K\in\{2^{i}\log V:i=0,1,\dots\}
. The region of integration in
Equation

11.49
corresponding to such a range has volume
\leqslant(4K)^{\ell}
and so it suffices to show the pointwise bound

(11.53)

|\mathbb{E}(e^{iX\cdot\theta})|\ll(8K)^{-\ell}V^{-10}

uniformly for
K\leqslant\|\theta\|_{\infty}<2K
.

We first consider the smaller ranges
\log V\leqslant K\ll V^{1/2}/\log V
(say). As in the analysis of
Equation

11.47
it suffices to prove
Equation

11.53
with
X
replaced by
\tilde{X}
, defined as in
Equation

11.44
but without the ‘good’ condition, that is to say with the
\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}
sampled independently from
\{0,1\}
. From the definition
Equation

11.44
and the triangle inequality we have

\big|\mathbb{E}(e^{i\tilde{X}\cdot\theta})\big|\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}\Big|\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}e\big(\frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{u=1}^{\ell}\sum_{j=1}^{V}\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{j}\theta_{u}\big)\Big|=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}\prod_{j=1}^{V}\Big|\frac{1+e(\theta_{u}\mathbf{a}_{j}/\sigma)}{2}\Big|.

Note that, since
\mathbf{a}_{j}\asymp 2^{n-t}
,
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
and
|\theta_{u}|=o(V^{1/2})
, the arguments of the exponentials here are
o(1)
and we may use the inequality
\frac{1}{2}|1+e(\xi)|\leqslant e^{-|\xi|^{2}/8}
to give
|\mathbb{E}(e^{i\tilde{X}\cdot\theta})|\leqslant e^{-\Omega(K^{2})}
. This is bounded as in
Equation

11.53
for
K\geqslant\log V
, using here that
\ell
is much smaller than
V
by the parameter hierarchy.

Now we turn to the larger values
K\geqslant V^{1/2}/\log V
. Here, we apply the triangle inequality the other way around, obtaining

\big|\mathbb{E}(e^{iX\cdot\theta})\big|\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{\varepsilon}^{(\ell)})\operatorname{good}}\Big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}e\big(\frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{u=1}^{\ell}\sum_{j=1}^{V}\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}\mathbf{a}_{j}\theta_{u}\big)\Big|.

For
j\in[V]
denote by
\phi_{j}
the characteristic function
\phi_{j}(\xi):=\mathbb{E}e(\xi\mathbf{a}_{j})
. Then the preceding may be rewritten as

\big|\mathbb{E}(e^{iX\cdot\theta})\big|\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{\varepsilon}^{(\ell)})\operatorname{good}}\prod_{j=1}^{V}\Big|\phi_{j}\big(\frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{u=1}^{\ell}\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)}\theta_{u}\big)\Big|.

Consequently,

(11.54)

\big|\mathbb{E}(e^{iX\cdot\theta})\big|\leqslant\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\sup_{j}\big|\phi_{j}(\theta_{i}/\sigma)\big|^{4^{-\ell}V}\leqslant\sup_{j}\big|\phi_{j}(\|\theta\|_{\infty}/\sigma)\big|^{4^{-\ell}V}:

this follows by picking out just those indices
j
for which
(\bm{\varepsilon}_{j}^{(u)})_{u\in[\ell]}=e_{i}\in\mathbf{Q}^{\ell}
, for
i=1,\dots,\ell
; by the definition of ‘good’ there are at least
4^{-\ell}V
such
j
for each value of
i
. We may use the trivial bound
|\phi_{j}(\theta_{u}/\sigma)|\leqslant 1
everywhere else.

To obtain estimates for the quantities
\phi_{j}(\|\theta\|_{\infty}/\sigma)
, first recall from
Equation

11.6
how
\mathbf{a}_{j}
is distributed. With the notation used there (recalling that
\mathbf{a}_{j}=\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}=\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
), it follows that

\phi_{j}(\xi)=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{x_{0}}e(\xi x_{0})+\frac{\alpha_{1}}{x_{1}}e(\xi x_{1})+\big(1-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{x_{0}}-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{x_{1}}\big)\mathbb{E}(e^{i\xi X}),

where
X
is the random variable taking values in
(x_{0},x_{1})\subset\mathbb{Z}
, with
\mathbb{P}(X=x)\propto 1/x
. In our context
\alpha_{0}/x_{0}
,
\alpha_{1}/x_{1}
are minuscule and so we certainly have

|\phi_{j}(\xi)|\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}\big(1+|\mathbb{E}(e^{i\xi X})|\big).

We can estimate
|\mathbb{E}(e^{i\xi X})|
using
Lemma

C.1
, which is designed for this purpose. We take
N=x_{0}
,
R=x_{1}-x_{0}+1
and
\xi=\|\theta\|_{\infty}/\sigma
. One may easily check that
N\asymp 2^{n-t}
and
R\asymp V^{-1/3}2^{n-t}
. Since
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
, we have
\xi R\asymp\|\theta\|_{\infty}V^{-5/6}\asymp KV^{-5/6}
.

Lemma

C.1
then gives

(11.55)

\big|\phi_{j}(\|\theta\|_{\infty}/\sigma)\big|\leqslant\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1-cK^{2}V^{-5/3}&K\leqslant CV^{5/6};\\
\frac{3}{4}&K\geqslant CV^{5/6}.\end{array}\right.

Here,
c,C
are absolute constants with
0<c<1<C
(independent of
j
, and
c
may be smaller than the constant in
Lemma

C.1
).

For
V^{1/2}/\log V\leqslant K\leqslant CV^{5/6}
we use the first bound in
Equation

11.55
, obtaining from
Equation

11.54
that
|\mathbb{E}(e^{iX\cdot\theta})|\ll(1-c\frac{V^{-2/3}}{\log^{2}V})^{4^{-\ell}V}\ll e^{-V^{1/4}}
which is certainly a bound of strength
Equation

11.53
. For
K\geqslant CV^{5/6}
we instead use the second bound in
Equation

11.55
getting
|\mathbb{E}(e^{iX\cdot\theta})|\ll(\frac{3}{4})^{4^{-\ell}V}
, which is again (massively) of strength
Equation

11.53
.

We have now completed the proof of the three statements
Equations

11.47
,
11.48
, and
11.49
, and hence
Equation

11.46
. Tracing back through the successive reductions
Equation

11.45
,
Equation

11.41
,
Equation

11.39
and
Equation

11.37
, we see that the contribution to
Equation

11.22
from the good tuples
(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)})
provides the main term in
Equation

11.18
, and the proof of
Lemma

11.4
is (at last) complete. ∎

As previously stated, we will also require the following technical estimate, which will be used to deduce the unweighted
Lemma

11.2
from the version
Lemma

11.4
with weights.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let the notation and setup be as in
Lemma

11.2
and, as in
Lemma

11.4
, let
R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)
be the number of representations of
x
in
S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}^{>n^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{med}})
. Then we have

\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{2})\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})(R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})-1)\leqslant V^{O(\ell)}\Bigg(\frac{2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}}{\sigma}\Bigg)^{\ell+1}.

Proof.

The proof goes along similar lines to (part of) the proof of
Lemma

11.4
. In particular, define
\Xi
in the same way. We have the following modification of
Equation

11.22
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})(R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})-1)

(11.56)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell+1)}\in\{0,1\}^{\Xi}\\
\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\neq\varepsilon^{(\ell+1)}\end{subarray}}\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell+1}\in S}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{(i,j)\in\Xi}\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x_{u}-s_{u},u\in[\ell+1]\Big),

where
x_{\ell+1}:=x_{\ell}
. This follows by observing that
R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})(R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})-1)
counts pairs of distinct representations of
x_{\ell}
in
S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}^{>n^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{med}})
.

We now proceed completely analogously to the initial steps in the proof of
Lemma

11.4
, simply changing
\ell
to
\ell+1
in many places. In particular the first main task is to show

(11.57)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})\\
r<\ell+1\mbox{\,\scriptsize or}\\
r=\ell+1\mbox{\, \scriptsize and \,}q_{\ell+1}>V\end{subarray}}E_{(r;q_{1},\dots,q_{r})}\ll 2^{-\Omega(V)}2^{(\ell+1)\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}.

which is
Equation

11.23
with
\ell
replaced by
\ell+1
. Key tools for doing this will be
Equations

11.28
and
11.30
, which hold verbatim.

The analysis of Steps 1 and 3 proceeds essentially identically, changing
\ell
to
\ell+1
a few times.

The initial part of Step 2 requires a very small modification, in that if
r<\ell+1
and there are only
r
distinct
\varepsilon^{(u)}
, then we must have
\varepsilon^{(u)}=\varepsilon^{(u^{\prime})}
for some pair
(u,u^{\prime})
with
u<u^{\prime}
, and moreover we cannot have
(u,u^{\prime})=(\ell,\ell+1)
due to the stipulation that
\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\neq\varepsilon^{(\ell+1)}
in the range of summation in
Equation

11.56
. The rest of that argument proceeds as before, once again switching some instances of
\ell
to
\ell+1
.

This concludes the proof of
Equation

11.57
. Recalling that
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
, to conclude the proof of
Lemma

11.5
we need only show that

\sum_{q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell+1}\leqslant V}E_{(\ell+1;q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell+1})}\ll V^{O(\ell)}2^{(\ell+1)\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}.

Since the number of possibilities for
q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell+1}
is
V^{O(\ell)}
, it suffices to prove the same bound for each
E_{(\ell+1;q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell+1})}
individually.
For this we use
Equation

11.28
, where we can take all
i_{d}
to be equal to
t
since
q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell+1}\leqslant V
. This gives exactly the desired bound (in fact with an extra factor
2^{-\sum_{d=1}^{\ell+1}q_{d}}\leqslant 1
). This concludes the proof of
Lemma

11.5
.∎

The final task of this section is to establish the key result
Lemma

11.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

11.2
.

The upper bound is immediate from
Lemma

11.4
since
1_{x\in S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}^{>n^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{med}})}\leqslant R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)
pointwise.
For the lower bound, we use that
1_{x\in S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}^{>n^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{med}})}\geqslant R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)\mathbf{1}_{R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)\in\{0,1\}}
, and so

\mathbb{P}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}\in S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}^{>n^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{med}})\big)\geqslant\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})-\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})\big(1-\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{1}_{R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{i})\in\{0,1\}}\big).

However

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})\big(1-\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{1}_{R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{i})\in\{0,1\}}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{1}_{R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{i})>1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{1})\cdots R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{\ell})(R_{\mathbf{A}}(x_{i})-1).

Using
Lemma

11.5
, and noting that
g(x_{i})\geqslant V^{-o(1)}/\sigma
due to the hypotheses on the
x_{i}
, comparison with
Lemma

11.4
shows that it is enough to prove that

V^{O(\ell)}\frac{2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}}{\sigma}\ll V^{-1/4}.

This follows using
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
and the upper bound in
Equation

11.15
, together with the parameter hierarchy (in particular, that
t\geqslant t_{\min}\ggg V
).
∎

12.
Inclusion–exclusion

In this section we carry out the inclusion-exclusion analysis hinted at in the introduction to
Section

11
, using
Lemma

11.2
as our main tool. The following is the main result of the section.

As in the previous section, we have a main truncation parameter
M
, key further thresholds
R(M)
,
T(M)
and
L(M)
as described in
Definition

11.1
, and technical auxiliary thresholds
\ell_{*}(M)
,
V(M)
,
t_{\min}(M)
,
t_{\max}(M)
as in the hierarchy
Equation

11.3
.

The reader may also wish to recall the definitions of the random variables
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
,
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
, which may be found in
Definitions

7.1
and
6.1
. Here is the main result of the section.

Proposition 12.1
.

Fix upper and lower walks
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
which are
R
-bounded and
T
-positive up to lengths
\lceil n/2\rceil
,
\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, and which have
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil}\beta(i),\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\beta^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-M
. Write
D=D(\beta,\beta^{\prime}):=\beta(\lceil n/2\rceil)-\beta^{\prime}(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)
and suppose that
|D|\leqslant M/10
. Suppose that
\beta
has a
V(M)
-jump step at some
t\in[t_{\min}(M),t_{\max}(M)]
. Then

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)=1-\mathbb{E}\exp\big(-2^{D-\xi}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L(M))\big)+O(\ell_{*}(M)^{-1}).

Remark
.

Note that the final expression is independent of the location of the jump step
t
.

To avoid excessively cumbersome notation, we drop the explicit mention of
M
from the parameters
T,t_{\min},t_{\max},V,L,\ell_{*}
, as we did in the last section. For the rest of the section we will assume that
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
satisfy the hypotheses of
Proposition

12.1
without further comment; this applies in particular to the subsidiary
Lemma

12.2
.

For the initial analysis notation will be as in
Section

11
. Specifically, the reader should recall
Equation

11.1
, the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
and the fact that we are conditioning to
\bm{\beta}=\beta,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}
and also to a fixed choice of the jump distribution
\mathbf{f}=f
. In particular, from this point onwards in the section we write
\mathbf{A}
as shorthand for
(\mathbf{A}\mid\bm{\beta}=\beta,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime},\mathbf{f}=f)
.

Associated to this data are the parameters
\mu,\sigma^{2}
defined in
Equation

11.8
, and the Gaussian density
g(x)
with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
as in
Equation

11.10
.

As in
Equation

11.5
we will divide
\mathbf{A}
as
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}}\cup\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}\cup\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}
. For much of the section we will additionally condition to fixed choices of
A_{\operatorname{small}}=(a_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L;j\in[b_{i}]}
and
A_{\operatorname{large}}=(a_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
and sample only
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}
at random, with the randomly selected object being denoted by bold font as usual. Since
Equation

5.7
holds deterministically, we always assume

a_{i,j}\asymp 2^{i}.

12.1.
Consequences of
Section

11

In the proof of
Proposition

12.1
we will make crucial use of the main result of the last section,
Lemma

11.2
. We record the consequences we need here. For notational clarity in what follows we will write
E\approx E^{\prime}
as shorthand for
E^{\prime}=(1+O(V^{-\Omega(1)}))E
. Recall that
\Omega(1)
denotes a small positive constant, which may change from line to line.

First (in
Lemma

11.2
) take
n^{\prime}=L
and
S:=\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})
. As remarked after the statement of
Lemma

11.2
, in this case we have
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>n^{\prime}}=\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}
. The condition
\max(S)\leqslant 2^{n-L/2}
is satisfied by a large margin. Write
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})
for the parameter
\tau
in this application of
Lemma

11.2
, that is to say

(12.1)

\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})=2^{-\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}}|\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})|.

Here the notation
\Sigma(S)
is as defined in
Definition

2.1
.
Assume in what follows that
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\geqslant V^{-1}
. Suppose that
\ell\leqslant\ell_{*}
and that
x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}
satisfy
Equation

11.11
and the separation condition
Equation

11.12
. Then all the hypotheses of
Lemma

11.2
hold and the conclusion
Equation

11.13
is that

(12.2)

\mathbb{P}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)\approx\big(\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}\big)^{\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}g(x_{i})

(where
g
is a Gaussian density with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
as in
Equation

11.10
.)
Comparing this with the case
\ell=1
, we immediately obtain the key almost-independence statement

(12.3)

\mathbb{P}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)\approx\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{P}\big(x_{i}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big),

where here we used the fact that
(1+V^{-\Omega(1)})^{\ell}=1+O(V^{-\Omega(1)})
due to the hierarchy of parameters. This will be the first key consequence of
Lemma

11.2
that we will use later.

The second important consequence will be an alternative expression for the probabilities on the right in
Equation

12.3
. For this, note first that the case
\ell=1
of
Equation

12.2
gives that

(12.4)

\mathbb{P}\big(x\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)\approx\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}g(x),

still under the assumption that
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\geqslant V^{-1}
and also that

(12.5)

|x-\mu|\leqslant(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma.

(The separation condition
Equation

11.12
is redundant.) Leaving this aside temporarily, we now use
Lemma

11.2
differently in order to obtain an alternative expression for the right-hand side of
Equation

12.4
.
Now take
n^{\prime}=n-L
and
S
to be any subset of
[2^{-L}k]
of size
2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}b_{i}}
. That such a set exists follows from
Equation

11.16
(recalling that
k\geqslant 2^{n}
). The condition
\max(S)\leqslant 2^{n-L/2}
is again satisfied quite comfortably (noting that
k\asymp 2^{n}
), and in this case
\tau=1
. Take
\ell=1
in
Equation

11.13
, obtaining (for any
S
)

\mathbb{P}(x\in S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>n-L}))\approx 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}g(x).

Comparing with
Equation

12.4
gives

(12.6)

\mathbb{P}\big(x\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)\approx\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\mathbb{P}(x\in S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>n-L})).

We claim that

(12.7)
