\mathbb{P}(x\in S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>n-L}))\approx\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x).

where
R_{\mathbf{A}}
denotes the number of representations of
x
in
S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>n-L})
(as defined just prior to
Lemma

11.4
). First note that the LHS of
Equation

12.7
is simply
\mathbb{E}1_{R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)\geqslant 1}
, so clearly
\mathbb{P}(x\in S+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}^{>n-L}))\leqslant\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)
. Obtaining a bound in the other direction is only slightly more involved. By employing the inequality
R-R(R-1)\leqslant 1_{R\geqslant 1}
, it suffices to show that

(12.8)

\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)(R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)-1)\ll V^{-\Omega(1)}\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x).

To see that this is so, we note that
Lemma

11.4
(together with the lower bound
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\geqslant V^{-1}
,
Equations

11.9
and
12.5
) tells us that
\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)\gg V^{-1}2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}g(x)\gg V^{-O(1)}2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)}
, whilst
Lemma

11.5
gives
\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)(R_{\mathbf{A}}(x)-1)\ll V^{O(1)}2^{2\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-t}b_{i}-(n-t)\big)}
. The bound
Equation

12.8
then follows using
Equation

11.15
and the parameter hierarchy, and this concludes the proof of the claim
Equation

12.7
.

Combining
Equations

12.6
and
12.7
evidently gives

\mathbb{P}\big(x\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)\approx\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbf{A}}(x).

Expanding out the expectation term on the RHS we obtain

(12.9)

\mathbb{P}\big(x\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)\approx\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\sum_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(x\in S+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n-L<i\leqslant n+1-t\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\big),

where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})_{n-L<i\leqslant n+1-t,j\in[b_{i}]}
ranges over all tuples with
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}
, and the use of
\varepsilon^{\prime}
as a dummy variable is to avoid conflict later.
Recall that
S
is an arbitrary subset of
[2^{-L}k]
of size
2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-L}b_{i}}
. Noting that the LHS of
Equation

12.9
is independent of
S
, we average over all such
S
and thereby obtain (assuming
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\geqslant 1/V
and
Equation

12.5
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(x\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})

\displaystyle+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)\approx

(12.10)

\displaystyle\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})2^{\sum_{i\leqslant n-L}b_{i}}(2^{-L}k)^{-1}\sum_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}(x\in[2^{-L}k]+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n-L<i\leqslant n+1-t\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}).

This is the second key result that we will require in the proof of
Proposition

12.1
.

12.2.
The Poisson paradigm

In this section we establish the key ingredient in the proof of
Proposition

12.1
, which we state as
Lemma

12.2
below. We retain the notation in force so far, in particular that we are conditioning to fixed choices of
A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}}
. In what follows we write
\varepsilon:=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
and denote

(12.11)

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(A_{\operatorname{large}}):=\big\{\varepsilon:\big|k-\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}-\mu\big|\leqslant(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma\big\}

and

\Sigma^{\prime}(A_{\operatorname{large}}):=\{\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}:\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}(A_{\operatorname{large}})\}.

Here,
\mu,\sigma
are defined in
Equation

11.8
. In particular, it is important to note that the definition of
\mathcal{E}
depends on the jump distribution vector
f
that we conditioned to at the beginning of the last section, and not just on the walks
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
.

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
be as in the statement of
Proposition

12.1
. Let
A_{\operatorname{small}}=(a_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L;j\in[b_{i}]}
and
A_{\operatorname{large}}=(a_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
be multisets. Define
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(A_{\operatorname{large}})
as in
Equation

12.11
.
Suppose that
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\geqslant 1/V
and that
A_{\operatorname{large}}
satisfies the following separation condition: as
\varepsilon
ranges over
\{0,1\}^{\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n}b_{i}}
, the numbers
\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}
are
2^{n-L/2}
-separated. Then, writing
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}:=(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{L+1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t;j\in[b_{i}]}

(
chosen at random
)
, we have

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma^{\prime}(A_{\operatorname{large}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}}))=1-e^{-S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})}+O(\ell_{*}^{-1}),

where

(12.12)

S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}}):=\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon}),

with

X_{\varepsilon}
the event that

(12.13)

k-\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}}).

Proof.

In this proof all instances of
\mathbb{P}
are probabilities with respect to the random choice of
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}
. We retain the convention that
E\approx E^{\prime}
is shorthand for
E^{\prime}=(1+O(V^{-\Omega(1)}))E
.
Write
S:=S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})
for brevity. We have

(12.14)

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma^{\prime}(A_{\operatorname{large}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\bigcup_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}X_{\varepsilon}\big).

Let
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{E}
be distinct. We claim that
Equation

12.3
holds with the
x_{u}
being the quantities
k-\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon^{(u)}_{i,j}a_{i,j}
. Indeed, the latter statement was proven assuming that the
x_{u}
satisfy
Equation

11.11
and the separation condition
Equation

11.12
. In the present context the first of these conditions is a consequence of the restriction to
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}
, and the separation condition is one of the assumptions of
Lemma

12.2
. The claim follows.

This instance of
Equation

12.3
gives the key almost-independence statement

(12.15)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bigcap_{u=1}^{\ell}X_{\varepsilon^{(u)}}\big)\approx\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon^{(u)}}),

provided that
\ell\leqslant\ell_{*}
.

We pause to show that the
\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon})
are individually rather small. To do this we first note by the definition
Equation

12.13
of
X_{\varepsilon}
and
Equation

12.10
we have

(12.16)

\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon})\approx\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-L}b_{i}}(2^{-L}k)^{-1}\sum_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(k-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i>n+1-t\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=n+1-L\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}^{n+1-t}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\in[2^{-L}k]\big),

where (as before)
\varepsilon^{\prime}=(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})_{n+1-L\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t;j\in[b_{i}]}
. Now if
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}
and if, for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}
, the inner probability is not zero then for some instance
(a_{i,j})_{n+1-L\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t;j\in[b_{i}]}
of
(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{n+1-L\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t;j\in[b_{i}]}
we have

(12.17)

\big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=n+1-L\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}^{n+1-t}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}a_{i,j}\big|\geqslant\mu-2^{-L}k-(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma.

We claim that this forces at least one of the
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{n+1-t,u}
(that is, associated to the jump step) to not be zero. To see this claim, we may use
Equations

5.7
and
8.24
to bound

(12.18)

\big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=n+1-L\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}^{n-t}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}a_{i,j}\big|\ll\sum_{i=n+1-L}^{n-t}b_{i}2^{i}\ll 2^{n-t}\sum_{i=1}^{L}(1+i^{2\kappa})2^{-i}\ll 2^{n-t}.

However, recalling that
\mu\asymp V2^{n-t}
,
k\asymp 2^{n}
,
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
and
L\ggg t
, the RHS of
Equation

12.17
is
\gg V2^{n-t}
, which is much larger than
Equation

12.18
; this establishes the claim.

Using the anticoncentration estimate
Equation

11.7
(revealing all
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
other than
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,u}
), we have that

\mathbb{P}\Big(k-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i>n+1-t\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=n+1-L\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}^{n+1-t}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x\Big)\ll V^{1/3}2^{t-n},

uniformly for
x\in[2^{-L}k]
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}
, and
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}
.

Applying this to
Equation

12.16
, summing over the
2^{\sum_{i=n+1-L}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}
choices of
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and using the trivial bound
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\leqslant 1
and the upper bound in
Equation

11.15
we obtain

(12.19)

\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon})\ll 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-L}b_{i}}\cdot 2^{\sum_{i=n+1-L}^{n+1-t}b_{i}}\cdot V^{1/3}2^{t-n}\ll V^{1/3}2^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{1/4}}\leqslant 2^{-\frac{1}{3}t_{\min}^{1/4}}.

We set this aside for later use, and resume the main line of argument. We divide into two cases according to the value of
S=S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})
.

Case 1:

S\leqslant\ell_{*}/100
. This is the main case. To estimate the RHS of
Equation

12.14
we use the inclusion-exclusion principle (“Poisson paradigm”). Let
\ell_{0}\in[\ell_{*}/2,\ell_{*}]
be even. Then by the Bonferroni inequalities we have

1-\mathbb{P}\big(\bigcup_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}X_{\varepsilon}\big)\leqslant\sum_{0\leqslant\ell\leqslant\ell_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon^{(1)}<\cdots<\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\\
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ell}\mathbb{P}(\bigcap_{u=1}^{\ell}X_{\varepsilon^{(u)}}).

By
Equation

12.15

it follows that

\displaystyle 1-

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\bigcup_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}X_{\varepsilon}\big)\leqslant\sum_{0\leqslant\ell\leqslant\ell_{0}}(-1)^{\ell}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon^{(1)}<\cdots<\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\\
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}(1+O(V^{-\Omega(1)}))\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon^{(u)}})

(12.20)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{0\leqslant\ell\leqslant\ell_{0}}(-1)^{\ell}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon^{(1)}<\cdots<\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\\
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon^{(u)}})+O(V^{-\Omega(1)})\sum_{0\leqslant\ell\leqslant\ell_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon^{(1)}<\cdots<\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\\
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon^{(u)}}).

The error term here is bounded by
\ll V^{-\Omega(1)}\sum_{0\leqslant\ell\leqslant\ell_{0}}\frac{1}{\ell!}\big(\sum_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon})\big)^{\ell}<V^{-\Omega(1)}e^{S}<e^{-\ell_{*}}
(provided
\ell_{*}
is small enough in terms of
V
), and so
Equation

12.20
implies that

(12.21)

1-\mathbb{P}\big(\bigcup_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}X_{\varepsilon}\big)\leqslant\sum_{0\leqslant\ell\leqslant\ell_{0}}(-1)^{\ell}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon^{(1)}<\cdots<\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\\
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon^{(u)}})+O(e^{-\ell_{*}}).

We also have

(12.22)

\big|e^{-S}-\sum_{\ell=0}^{\ell_{0}}(-1)^{\ell}\frac{1}{\ell!}S^{\ell}\big|\leqslant\sum_{\ell>\ell_{0}}\frac{1}{\ell!}S^{\ell}\ll\big(\frac{eS}{\ell_{0}}\big)^{\ell_{0}}\ll\big(\frac{e}{50}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}\ell_{*}}\ll e^{-\ell_{*}}.

Also,
Equations

12.19
and
D.1
give that

(12.23)

\Big|S^{\ell}-\ell!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon^{(1)}<\cdots<\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\\
\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}\prod_{u=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon^{(u)}})\Big|\leqslant\ell(\ell-1)2^{-\frac{1}{3}t_{\min}^{1/4}}S^{\ell-1}<2^{-\frac{1}{4}t_{\min}^{1/4}}<e^{-10\ell_{*}}.

Combining
Equations

12.21
,
12.23
, and
12.22
with
Equation

12.14
gives

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma^{\prime}(A_{\operatorname{large}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)\geqslant 1-e^{-S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})}+O(e^{-\ell_{*}}).

A corresponding upper bound follows using the lower bound Bonferroni inequalities (taking
\ell_{0}
odd) in an entirely analogous manner. This completes the proof of
Lemma

12.2
in Case 1.

Case 2:

S>\ell_{*}/100
. For this we use a second moment method. We have

(12.24)

\mathbb{E}\big|\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}(1_{X_{\varepsilon}}-\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon}))\big|^{2}=\sum_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon}\cap X_{\varepsilon^{\prime}})-S^{2}.

Now by
Equation

12.15
with
\ell=2
we have when
\varepsilon\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}
that

\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon}\cap X_{\varepsilon^{\prime}})=\big(1+O(V^{-\Omega(1)})\big)\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon})\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}).

Summing over
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
gives

\sum_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon}\cap X_{\varepsilon^{\prime}})=\big(1+O(V^{-\Omega(1)})\big)\Big(S^{2}-\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon})^{2}\Big)+S.

Therefore from
Equation

12.24
we have

\mathbb{E}\big|\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}(1_{X_{\varepsilon}}-\mathbb{P}(X_{\varepsilon}))\big|^{2}\ll S+V^{-\Omega(1)}S^{2}.

It follows by Markov’s inequality that the complement of
\bigcup_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}
, that is to say the event
\sum_{\varepsilon}1_{X_{\varepsilon}}=0
, has probability
\ll S^{-1}+V^{-\Omega(1)}\ll\ell_{*}^{-1}
.

This concludes the analysis of Case 2 and hence the proof of
Lemma

12.2
.
∎

12.3.
Proof of the main result

We now turn to the task of proving the main result of the section,
Proposition

12.1
. To do this, we will use
Lemma

12.2
and undo the conditioning to
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}}=A_{\operatorname{small}}
,
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}=A_{\operatorname{large}}
.
We first need to establish that the application of
Lemma

12.2
is generically valid. That this is so is the content of
Lemmas

12.3
and
12.4
below.

Lemma 12.3
.

Define
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})
as in
Equation

12.1
. Then, with the setup as in
Proposition

12.1
, the probability that
\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})\leqslant 1/V
is at most
2^{T}/V
.

Proof.

Writing
r_{\mathbf{A}}(x)
for the multiplicity of
x
in
\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})
, we have
\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})=2^{-\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}}\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{A}})
. By Cauchy–Schwarz it follows that if
|\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})|\leqslant 1/V
then

(12.25)

\sum_{x}r_{\mathbf{A}}(x)^{2}\geqslant 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}}V.

On the other hand, we have

\mathbb{E}\sum_{x}r_{\mathbf{A}}(x)^{2}=\sum_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\big),

where now
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L;j\in[b_{i}]}
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}=(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L;j\in[b_{i}]}
.
For each pair
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime})
in the above sum, consider the largest
\ell
for which there is some
m
(which we take minimal) such that
\varepsilon_{\ell,m}\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\ell,m}
. Then by revealing all
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
except
\mathbf{a}_{\ell,m}
, we see from
Equation

5.8
that the inner probability is
\ll 2^{-\ell}
.

The number of pairs
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime})
corresponding to a given
(\ell,m)
is
2^{2\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}-\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{L}b_{i}-(m-1)}
.
Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sum_{x}r_{\mathbf{A}}(x)^{2}\ll\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}

\displaystyle 2^{-\ell}\sum_{m\in[b_{\ell}]}2^{2\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}-\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{L}b_{i}-(m-1)}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}2^{-\ell+2\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}-\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{L}b_{i}}=2^{\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}2^{-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)}.

Since
\beta^{\prime}
is a
T
-positive walk,
\beta^{\prime}(\ell)\geqslant-T+\ell^{1/4}
and so it follows that

\mathbb{E}\sum_{x}r_{\mathbf{A}}(x)^{2}\ll 2^{T+\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}}.

By Markov,

\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{x}r_{\mathbf{A}}(x)^{2}\geqslant 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{L}b_{i}}V\big)\ll 2^{T}/V,

and so the lemma follows from the remarks leading to
Equation

12.25
.∎

The next lemma controls the separation property required in
Lemma

12.2
. Here, very crude estimates suffice.

Lemma 12.4
.

With the setup in
Proposition

12.1
, the probability that there exist distinct
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}=(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
for which
\big|\sum_{i,j}(\varepsilon_{i,j}-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\big|\leqslant 2^{n-L/2}
is
\leqslant 2^{-L/8}
.

Proof.

The number of pairs
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
is
<2^{2\sum_{i=n+2-t}^{n}b_{i}}<2^{L/4}
, using the
R
-boundedness of
\beta
and the parameter hierarchy for the second bound. For each pair, choose some index with
\varepsilon_{u,v}\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{u,v}
, and reveal all
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
except for
\mathbf{a}_{u,v}
. By
Equation

5.8
,
\mathbb{P}\big(\big|\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}(\varepsilon_{i,j}-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\big|\leqslant 2^{n-L/2}\big)\ll 2^{t-n}\cdot 2^{n-L/2}
. Summing over all pairs
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
and using the parameter hierarchy again gives the result.
∎

Now we begin the proof of
Proposition

12.1
in earnest.
From
Lemma

12.2
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma^{\prime}(A_{\operatorname{large}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big)

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})})1_{\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\geqslant 1/V,\,A_{\operatorname{large}}\in\mathscr{S}}

(12.26)

\displaystyle+O\big(1_{\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\leqslant 1/V}+1_{A_{\operatorname{large}}\notin\mathscr{S}}+\ell_{*}^{-1}\big),

where
\mathscr{S}
denotes the separation condition in the statement of
Lemma

12.2
.
Now from the definition
Equation

12.12
of
S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})
and
Equation

12.13
we have

S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})\ =\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{P}\big(k-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1-t<i\leqslant n\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(A_{\operatorname{small}})\big).

If
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\geqslant 1/V
we now apply
Equation

12.10
with
x=k-\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n,j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}
; the relevant condition
Equation

12.5
holds precisely because
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}(A_{\operatorname{large}})
(recall the definition
Equation

12.11
). This gives that if
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\geqslant 1/V
then

S(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})=(1+O(V^{-\Omega(1)}))S^{\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})

where
S^{\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})
equals

(12.27)

\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-L}b_{i}}(2^{-L}k)^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}(A_{\operatorname{large}})\\
\varepsilon^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\Big(k-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i>n+1-t\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=n+1-L\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}^{n+1-t}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\in[2^{-L}k]\Big).

where
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}=(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})_{n+1-L\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t;j\in[b_{i}]}
range over all tuples of elements from
\{0,1\}
(subject to the restriction
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}(A_{\operatorname{large}})
).
Substituting into
Equation

12.26
, removing the conditioning on
A_{\operatorname{large}},A_{\operatorname{small}}
and applying
Lemmas

12.3
and
12.4
(noting that all error probabilities, as well as the
V^{-\Omega(1)}
term, are much less than
\ell_{*}^{-1}
by the parameter hierarchy) we obtain

(12.28)

\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})\big)=1-\mathbb{E}e^{-S^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}+O(\ell_{*}^{-1}).

Our next task is to relate the LHS here to
\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))
. We pause to recall here that
\mathbf{A}
still means that we are conditioning to
\bm{\beta}=\beta,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{f}=f
.

In carrying out the analysis (and also later on) we will use the following lemma.

Lemma 12.5
.

Suppose that
s
is an integer with
0\leqslant s\leqslant n+1-L
. Write
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
, and
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{large}}:=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
. Then

\sum_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}\big(k-\sum_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\in[2^{s-n-1}k],\,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{large}}\notin\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})\big)\ll R^{2}2^{\sum_{i=s}^{n}b_{i}-(n-s)}e^{-\Omega((\log V)^{1/2})}.

Proof.

Suppose
k\in\sum_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}+[2^{s-n-1}k]
with
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{large}}\notin\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})
, that is to say

\Big|k-\sum_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}-\mu\Big|\geqslant(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma.

Then

\Big|\sum_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}-\mu\Big|\geqslant(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma-2^{s-n-1}k>\tfrac{1}{2}(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma,

where the last step follows using that
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
whilst
2^{s-n}k\ll 2^{s}\leqslant 2^{n-L}\lll 2^{n-t}
.

By
Equation

12.18
(truncated to
i=s
at the lower end of the summation) it follows that the contribution from
i<n+1-t
is negligible and hence that

\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{V}\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump},j}\textbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}-\mu\big|=\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{V}\varepsilon_{n+1-t,j}\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}-\mu\big|\geqslant\tfrac{1}{4}(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma,

where here and henceforth we write
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump},j}:=\varepsilon_{n+1-t,j}
and
\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}:=\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
for
j\in[V]
.

Next we observe that if some
\varepsilon
contributes nontrivially to the LHS in the lemma then it must lie in the set
\mathcal{E}_{0}
consisting of those
\varepsilon
for which at least one
\varepsilon_{i,j}
,
i\geqslant n-2\log_{2}R
, is not zero. This follows immediately from the
R
-boundedness of
\beta
and
Equation

7.10
, and the fact that
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\ll 2^{i}
deterministically (see
Equation

5.7
).

Putting these observations together, the LHS in the lemma is bounded above by

\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}\mathbb{P}\Big(k-\sum_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\in[2^{s-n-1}k],\,\big|\sum_{j=1}^{V}\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump},j}\textbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}-\mu\big|\geqslant\tfrac{1}{4}(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma\Big).

Denoting
\tilde{\varepsilon}:=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n,i\neq n+1-t;j\in[b_{i}]}
(that is, all the
\varepsilon_{i,j}
other than the
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump},j}
), this is

\ll 2^{s-n}k\sum_{\tilde{\varepsilon}:\tilde{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}\sup_{x}\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\leqslant i\leqslant n\\
i\neq n+1-t\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x\big)\cdot\sum_{\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\sum_{j=1}^{V}\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump},j}\textbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}-\mu\big|\geqslant\tfrac{1}{4}(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma\Big).

Here,
\tilde{\varepsilon}
ranges over the set
\{0,1\}^{\sum_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n,i\neq n+1-t}b_{i}}
, and
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump}}=(\varepsilon_{\operatorname{jump},j})_{j\in[V]}
ranges over
\{0,1\}^{V}=\{0,1\}^{b_{n+1-t}}
. Additionally, we have implicitly used that the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
implies that
t\ggg 2\log_{2}R
, so the conditions
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
are the same.
By revealing all
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
except for one corresponding to an index
(i,j)
with
i\geqslant n-2\log_{2}R
for which
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i,j}\neq 0
, we see from
Equation

5.8
that the
\sup_{x}\mathbb{P}(\sum\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=x)
term is
\ll R^{2}2^{-n}
.

Thus, writing
\bm{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{jump},j}
,
j=1,\dots,V
, for i.i.d. random
\{0,1\}
variables (independent of the
\mathbf{a}
variables) and using that
k\asymp 2^{n}
, the LHS in the lemma is bounded above by

\displaystyle 2^{s-n}k\cdot

\displaystyle 2^{\sum_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n,i\neq n+1-t}b_{i}}R^{2}2^{-n}\cdot 2^{V}\mathbb{P}\big(\big|\sum_{j=1}^{V}\bm{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{jump},j}\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}-\mu\big|>\tfrac{1}{4}\sigma(\log V)^{1/4}\big)

(12.29)

\displaystyle=R^{2}2^{\sum_{i=s}^{n}b_{i}-(n-s)}\mathbb{P}\big(\big|\sum_{j=1}^{V}\mathbf{X}_{j}-\mu\big|>\tfrac{1}{4}(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma\big),

where
\mathbf{X}_{j}:=\bm{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{jump},j}\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}
.
Now recall that by definition
Equation

11.8
,
\mu=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{V}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{a}_{\operatorname{jump},j}
, and so
\sum_{j=1}^{V}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{X}_{j}=\mu
. Now by Hoeffding’s inequality (see
Lemma

F.2
), if
H
is an upper bound for
\max_{j}|\mathbf{X}_{j}|
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\big|\sum_{j=1}^{V}\mathbf{X}_{j}-\mu\big|\geqslant\tfrac{1}{4}(\log V)^{1/4}\sigma\big)\leqslant 2\exp\big(-\sigma^{2}(\log V)^{1/2}/(32VH^{2})\big)\ll e^{-\Omega((\log V)^{1/2})},

where in the last step we used the fact that we can take
H\asymp 2^{n-t}
, and that
\sigma\asymp V^{1/2}2^{n-t}
. The proof of
Lemma

12.5
is complete.
∎

Finally we complete the proof of
Proposition

12.1
.

Proof of
Proposition

12.1
.

From the case
s=1
of
Lemma

12.5
, noting that in this case we have
\sum_{s\leqslant i\leqslant n}b_{i}-n=D
, we see that the probability that
k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A})
, but
k\notin\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}})+\Sigma(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})
, is
\ll R^{2}2^{D}e^{-\Omega((\log V)^{1/2})}\ll\ell_{*}^{-1}
. By this and
Equation

12.28
we have

(12.30)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))=1-\mathbb{E}e^{-S^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}+O(\ell_{*}^{-1}).

Recall that
S^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})
is defined in
Equation

12.27
. Define
S^{\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})
as for
S^{\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})
, but without the constraint
(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\in\mathcal{E}(A_{\operatorname{large}})
. That is,

\displaystyle S^{\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},

\displaystyle A_{\operatorname{large}}):=\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-L}b_{i}}(2^{-L}k)^{-1}\times

(12.31)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-L\leqslant i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}}\mathbb{P}\big(k-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1-t<i\leqslant n\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}a_{i,j}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=n+1-L\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}^{n+1-t}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\in[2^{-L}k]\big).

Using the trivial bound
\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\leqslant 1
, we have that
\mathbb{E}\big(S^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})-S^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})\big)
is bounded by

\ll 2^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-L}b_{i}-(n-L)}\sum_{\varepsilon_{i,j}}\mathbb{P}\big(k-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1-L\leqslant i\leqslant n\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\in[2^{-L}k],\,(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\notin\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})\big)

where the first sum is over all
(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n+1-L\leqslant i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
with components in
\{0,1\}
.

Applying
Lemma

12.5
with
s=n-L+1
, and using that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}=n+D
, it follows using the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
that

(12.32)

\mathbb{E}\big(S^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})-S^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})\big)\ll R^{2}2^{D}e^{-\Omega((\log V)^{1/2})}\ll\ell_{*}^{-1}.

Since
x\mapsto e^{-x}
is Lipschitz with constant
1
on
[0,\infty)
, it follows from
Equations

12.30
and
12.32
that we have

(12.33)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))=1-\mathbb{E}e^{-S^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}+O(\ell_{*}^{-1}).

Recall once more that, throughout the preceeding analysis,
\mathbf{A}
means
(\mathbf{A}\mid\bm{\beta}=\beta,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime},\mathbf{f}=f)
.

From the definition
Equation

12.31
, using again that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}=n+D
, recalling that
\sum_{i>n-L}b_{i}=L+\beta(L)
and
k=2^{n+\xi}
, and rescaling the
a
-variables by dividing by
k
, we have that
S^{\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},A_{\operatorname{large}})
equals

2^{D-\xi}\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})2^{-\beta(L)}\sum_{\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{i\geqslant n+1-L;j}}\mathbb{P}\big(1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i>n+1-t\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{i,j}\frac{a_{i,j}}{k}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=n+1-L\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}^{n+1-t}\varepsilon_{i,j}\frac{\mathbf{a}_{i,j}}{k}\in[2^{-L}]\big).

Therefore

S^{\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})=2^{D-\xi}\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})2^{-\beta(L)}\sum_{\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{i\geqslant n+1-L;j}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}}\big(1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=n+1-L\\
j\in[b_{i}]\end{subarray}}^{n}\varepsilon_{i,j}\frac{\mathbf{a}_{i,j}}{k}\in[2^{-L}]\big).

In this expression, note carefully that the probability is taken only over random choices of
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}=(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{L+1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1-t;j\in[b_{i}]}
, which means that
S^{\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})
still depends nontrivially on the random variables
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}=(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{n+1-t<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
.
Recalling
Definition

7.1
, this may be written

S^{\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})=2^{D-\xi}\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}}\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L),

where
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}=(\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L,j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}
is defined by

(12.34)

2^{-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{i,j}}=\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{a}_{n+1-i,j}

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant L
and
j\in[1+\xi_{i}]
. Recall here that
b_{n+1-i}=1+\xi_{i}
for
i\leqslant\lceil n/2\rceil
, where the
\xi_{i}
are the increments of
\beta
, and note that
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}
depends on
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}
and
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}
.
Set

(12.35)

S^{\prime\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}):=2^{D-\xi}\tau(A_{\operatorname{small}})\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1);

note that
\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)
depends only on
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}
and not on
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}
, so this is well-defined. By the Lipschitz nature of
e^{-x}
and the triangle inequality, and since
|D|\leqslant M/10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}}\big|e^{-S^{\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}-e^{-S^{\prime\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}\big|\ll\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}}\big|S^{\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})-S^{\prime\prime\prime}(A_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})\big|

\displaystyle\ll 2^{M/10}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}}\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|\leqslant 2^{M/10}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|,

and so

\mathbb{E}\big|e^{-S^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}-e^{-S^{\prime\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}\big|\ll 2^{M/10}\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|,

where here averages are taken over the whole random choice of
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}}\cup\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}\cup\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}}
, conditioned to
\bm{\beta}=\beta
,
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{f}=f
. Therefore by
Equation

12.33
we have

(12.36)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))=1-\mathbb{E}e^{-S^{\prime\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}+O(\ell_{*}^{-1})+O\big(2^{M/10}\big)\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|.

Recall that
\mathbf{f}
is the ‘jump distribution’ from the previous section; specifically,
f_{m}
is the number of the
\mathbf{u}_{t,j}
in the interval
I_{m}
(see
Equation

11.4
). However, the term
S^{\prime\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})
is independent of
\mathbf{f}
, using
Equation

5.2
and that the jump indices with
\mathbf{a}_{n+1-t,j}
are part of
\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{med}}
. Thus it is tempting to remove the conditioning on
\mathbf{f}
at this juncture. To this end we note the simple comparison estimate

\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{f}=f)^{-1}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|\leqslant V^{V/3}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|

where (as above)
\mathbb{E}
denotes averaging with the conditioning to
\mathbf{f}=f
in place, and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
is the average with no condition on
\mathbf{f}
(but still conditioning to
\bm{\beta}=\beta
and
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}
). The second computation here comes from

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{V^{1/3}}))=\frac{V!}{f_{1}!\cdots f_{V^{1/3}}!}V^{-V/3}\geqslant V^{-V/3}.

Thus, at the expense of a slightly worse error term, we can remove the conditioning on
\mathbf{f}
in
Equation

12.36
to conclude that

(12.37)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}))=1-\mathbb{E}e^{-S^{\prime\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}+O(\ell_{*}^{-1})+O\big(2^{M/10}V^{V/3}\big)\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|.

Here, and from now on in this section, we are conditioning only to
\bm{\beta}=\beta,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}
, thus
\mathbf{A}
denotes
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
and the averages on the right are with no conditioning on
\mathbf{f}
(we called this averaging
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
above), with the
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{i,j}
still defined as in
Equation

12.34
.

From the definition
Equation

12.35
we have

\mathbb{E}e^{-S^{\prime\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}=\mathbb{E}\exp\big({-}2^{D-\xi}\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)\big).

Since
e^{-2^{D}x}
is Lipschitz on
[0,\infty)
with constant at most
2^{D}\leqslant 2^{M/10}
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}e^{-S^{\prime\prime\prime}(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}},\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{large}})}

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\exp\big(-2^{D-\xi}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\big)\big|

(12.38)

\displaystyle\ll 2^{M/10}\mathbb{E}\big|\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)-\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)\big|.

Applying the inequality
|x_{1}x_{2}-x_{3}x_{4}|\leqslant x_{3}|x_{4}-x_{5}|+x_{3}|x_{5}-x_{2}|+x_{2}|x_{3}-x_{1}|
(trivially valid for non-negative reals
x_{1},\dots,x_{5}
) with
x_{1}:=\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)
,
x_{2}:=\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)
,
x_{3}:=\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})
,
x_{4}:=\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)
and
x_{5}:=\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(t-1)
, we may bound the RHS of
Equation

12.38
by
2^{M/10}(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})
, where

E_{1}:=\mathbb{E}\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(t-1)\big|,\qquad E_{2}:=\mathbb{E}\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\big|,

and

E_{3}:=\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\cdot\mathbb{E}|\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})-\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)|.

We also introduce

E_{4}:=\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)\big|

(which appears in
Equation

12.37
). We now proceed to bound
E_{1},\dots,E_{4}
in turn.

Bounding
E_{1}
.

To bound
E_{1}
, we first use that
\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})\leqslant 1
deterministically, and so

(12.39)

E_{1}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(t-1)\big|.

To bound this we use
Lemma

5.6
and
Equation

12.34
, which give that
|2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}-2^{-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{i,j}}|\ll 2^{-i}i/n\ll\frac{1}{n}
deterministically for
1\leqslant i\leqslant L
and
j\in[1+\xi_{i}]
. Given
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1,j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}
, set
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1,j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1,j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{i,j}}
. Then we have

(12.40)

\big|\sigma_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}\big|\ll\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}(1+\xi_{i})\lll n^{-1/2},

with the final bound following very comfortably from the parameter hierarchy and since
\beta
is
R
-bounded. Now we have by definition

\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(t-1)=2^{-\beta(t-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon}1\big(\sigma_{\varepsilon}\in[1-2^{1-t},1]),\quad\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)=2^{-\beta(t-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon}1\big(\tilde{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}\in[1-2^{1-t},1]),

and so using
Equations

12.39
and
12.40
we obtain

E_{1}\leqslant 2^{-\beta(t-1)}\sum_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}\in[1-O(n^{-1/2}),1+O(n^{-1/2})]\cup[1-2^{1-t}-O(n^{-1/2}),1-2^{1-t}+O(n^{-1/2})]\big).

As in the proof of
Lemma

7.2
, using the
R
-boundedness of
\beta
we see that if
\sigma_{\varepsilon}>\frac{1}{2}
then
\varepsilon_{a,b}\neq 0
for some
a\leqslant 2\log_{2}R
. In particular, the probability term above is zero unless this is the case.
Revealing all
\mathbf{u}_{i,j}
except
\mathbf{u}_{a,b}
, it follows that, for each
\varepsilon
, the probability term above is bounded by
\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}\in\cdots\big)\ll 2^{a}n^{-1/2}
. Since the number of
\varepsilon
is
2^{\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}(1+\xi_{i})}<2^{Rt_{\max}^{2}}<n^{1/10}
and since
2^{a}<n^{1/10}
, we have the bound

(12.41)

E_{1}\ll n^{-1/4}.

Bounding
E_{2}
.
We again use that
\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})\leqslant 1
, and for the second term we use
Equation

7.16
and a telescoping sum. This gives a bound of

(12.42)

E_{2}\ll 2^{3T-\Omega(t_{\min}^{1/4})}.

Bounding
E_{3}
.
By
Equation

7.5
we have
\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\ll R^{2}
. To bound the
\mathbb{E}|\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})-\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)|
term, define
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
by
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i,j}:=\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
for
i\in[L]
and
j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]
. Thus by the definitions of the various quantities (see
Equation

12.1
and
Definition

6.1
) we have

(12.43)

\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})=\tau^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L).

We showed in
Lemma

5.7
that
\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{x}_{i,j}\neq\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i,j})\ll L/n
, uniformly for
i\in[L]
and
j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]
; thus with probability
1-O(L^{3}/n)
we have
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i,j}
for all
i\in[L]
and
j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]
, using here the crude bound
\sum_{i=1}^{L}(1-\xi^{\prime}_{i})\ll L^{2}
, which follows from the
R
-boundedness of
\beta^{\prime}
and the parameter hierarchy.

In the event that
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i,j}
for all
i\in[L]
and
j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]
, it follows from
Equation

12.43
that
\tau(\mathbf{A}_{\operatorname{small}})=\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)
. Since all
\tau^{*}
quantities are
\leqslant 1
deterministically, we obtain from this analysis that

(12.44)

E_{3}\ll R^{2}\frac{L^{3}}{n}.

Bounding
E_{4}
.
Finally, to bound
E_{4}
, we use

E_{4}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(t-1)\big|+\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(t-1)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\big|+\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}\mid\beta}(L)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\big|.

The first term we already bounded during the analysis of
E_{1}
. The third term may be bounded analogously, replacing
t-1
by
L
throughout. Due to the parameter hierarchy we have the bound
n^{-1/4}
for both of these terms. Finally, as we already saw during the analysis of
E_{2}
, the second term may be bounded by
\ll 2^{3T-\Omega(t_{\min}^{1/4})}
by use of
Equation

7.16
. Thus

(12.45)

E_{4}\ll 2^{3T-\Omega(t_{\min}^{1/4})}.

Putting the bounds
Equations

12.41
,
12.42
,
12.44
, and
12.45
together with
Equations

12.36
and
12.38
(recalling that the RHS of
Equation

12.38
is bounded by
2^{M/10}(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})
) and using the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
concludes the proof of
Proposition

12.1
.∎

Part IV
Putting everything together

13.
Expressing
p(k)
as an average over walks

We turn now to the proof of our main results. At this point we drop the convention (in force for the preceding two sections) that
\mathbf{A}
is shorthand for either
(\mathbf{A}\mid\bm{\beta}=\beta,\,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime},\,\mathbf{f}=f)
or
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
. From now on,
\mathbf{A}
will denote the unconditioned multiset as defined in
Equation

3.1
, and we will explicitly write
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
for the conditioned sets as described in
Section

5.3
. There should be little danger of confusion since the only result we will need from the preceding two sections is
Proposition

12.1
, which is stated with the longhand notation. Our starting point will be the expression
Equation

4.2
for
p(k)
.

As in the previous two sections, we have a main truncation parameter
M
and various further key further thresholds
R(M)
,
T(M)
,
L(M)
as described in
Definition

11.1
, as well as technical auxiliary thresholds
\ell_{*}(M)
,
V(M)
,
t_{\min}(M)
,
t_{\max}(M)
as in the hierarchy
Equation

11.3
. We will always assume that
L(M)
is much less than
n=\lfloor\log_{2}k\rfloor
; later we will let
M\rightarrow\infty
as
k\rightarrow\infty
sufficiently slowly so that this holds.

To shorten some expressions it is convenient to set
N:=\lceil n/2\rceil
and
N^{\prime}:=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
througout this section.

13.1.
Reducing to walks with large minima and small discrepancy

As before the
discrepancy
of a pair of walks
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
, which we denote by
D(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
, is
\beta(N)-\beta^{\prime}(N^{\prime})
. For
D\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
denote

(13.1)

\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}:=\{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}:D(\beta,\beta^{\prime})=D,\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\beta(i)=-m,\,\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m^{\prime}\},

where we declare
\beta(0)=\beta^{\prime}(0)=0
. It follows from
Lemma

8.10
with
\mathcal{E}
being the trivial (always satisfied) event that

(13.2)

\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\big)\ll(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}(\log k)^{-3/2}.

Write

(13.3)

E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}:=\sum_{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}}\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big).

Then
Equation

4.2
gives

(13.4)

p(k)=(1+o(1))e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}k^{-\delta}\sum_{|D|\leqslant 20\log n}(\log 2)^{D-\xi}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\geqslant 0}E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}+O(k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-2}).

We now wish to show that the most important contribution to this sum comes from
|D|,m,m^{\prime}=O(1)
. In this direction, our first result shows that the contribution from large
\max(m,m^{\prime})
is negligible.

Lemma 13.1
.

We have

\sum_{|D|\leqslant 20\log n}(\log 2)^{D}\sum_{\max(m,m^{\prime})\geqslant(\log n)^{2}}E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\ll(\log k)^{-100}.

Proof.

We first bound the contribution from the (extremely rare) pairs of walks for which
\max_{i\leqslant n}b_{i}
is greater than or equal to
n
. Since
b_{i}\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(1)
, the probability that
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})
have this property is
\ll n\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(1)\geqslant n)<e^{-n}
. Bounding
\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))
trivially by
1
, and
(\log 2)^{D}
by
(\log 2)^{-20\log n}\ll n^{O(1)}
, we see that the contribution from these walks is negligible.

Henceforth, we restrict to pairs of walks with
\max_{i\leqslant n}b_{i}\leqslant n
. Fix a pair of walks
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
, where
|D|\leqslant 20\log n
. We consider the contributions from
m\geqslant(\log n)^{2}
and from
m^{\prime}\geqslant(\log n)^{2}
separately.

Suppose first that
m\geqslant(\log n)^{2}
. Let
q
,
0\leqslant q\leqslant N
, be the smallest value such that
\beta(q)=-m
. Since
\xi_{i}\geqslant-1
for all
i
, we have
\beta(q)\geqslant-q
, and so
q\geqslant m\geqslant(\log n)^{2}
. Writing
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
for the elements of
(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})
as usual (see
Equation

5.2
), it follows using
Equation

5.8
that

\max\{\sum_{i\leqslant n-q;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:\varepsilon_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}\}\ll n2^{n-q}.

Since
q\geqslant(\log n)^{2}
, this is certainly
o(k)
.
Therefore the event
(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))
is contained in the union of the events
(\sum_{n-q<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=k-x)
, over all
x\ll n2^{n-q}=o(k)
and all
2^{q+\beta(q)}
choices of
(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n-q<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
. Note moreover that for such an event to be nontrivial, we must have
\varepsilon_{i,j}\neq 0
for some
i\geqslant n-2\log_{2}n
; otherwise,

\sum_{n-q<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\leqslant\sum_{i\leqslant n-2\log_{2}n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\ll n2^{n-2\log_{2}n}<k/2<k-x.

By revealing all
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
except for such a pair of indices, we see that
\mathbb{P}(\sum_{n-q<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=k-x)\ll 2^{2\log_{2}n-n}=n^{2}2^{-n}
for each choice of
x
and the
\varepsilon_{i,j}
. Summing over these choices gives

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))\ll n2^{n-q}\cdot 2^{q+\beta(q)}\cdot n^{2}2^{-n}\ll n^{3}2^{-(\log n)^{2}},

and so
(\log 2)^{D}\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))\ll n^{O(1)}2^{-(\log n)^{2}}\ll(\log k)^{-100}
.
Summing over all
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
lying in some
\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
with
|D|\leqslant 20\log n
and
m\geqslant(\log n)^{2}
, weighted by
\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)
, gives a bound of the strength claimed in
Lemma

13.1
.

Now we consider the possibility that
m^{\prime}\geqslant(\log n)^{2}
; the analysis here is very similar. Let
q^{\prime}
,
0\leqslant q^{\prime}\leqslant N^{\prime}
, be the smallest value such that
\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})=-m^{\prime}
. Similarly to before, it follows using
Equation

5.8
that

\max\{\sum_{i\leqslant q^{\prime};j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:\varepsilon_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}\}\ll n2^{q^{\prime}}.

Since
q^{\prime}\leqslant N^{\prime}
, this is vastly smaller than
k
. The event
(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))
is contained in the union of the events
\big(\sum_{q^{\prime}<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=k-x\big)
, over all
x\ll n2^{q^{\prime}}
and all
2^{n+D-q^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})}
choices of
(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{q^{\prime}<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
, noting here that
\sum_{q^{\prime}<i\leqslant n}b_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{q^{\prime}}b_{i}=n+D-(q^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime}))
. As before,
\mathbb{P}(\sum_{q^{\prime}<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=k-x)\ll n^{2}2^{-n}
for each choice of
x
and the
\varepsilon_{i,j}
. Summing over these choices gives

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))\ll n2^{q^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{n+D-q^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})}\cdot n^{2}2^{-n}\ll n^{O(1)}2^{-(\log n)^{2}},

using here that
|D|\leqslant 20\log n
. Summing over all
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
lying in some
\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
with
|D|\leqslant 20\log n
and
m^{\prime}\geqslant(\log n)^{2}
, weighted by
\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)
, again gives a bound of the strength claimed in
Lemma

13.1
. This completes the proof.
∎

To prepare for our study of the more modest values of
m
,
m^{\prime}
, we establish the following lemma, which should be thought of as very roughly suggesting that
\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))
behaves like
\min(2^{-m},2^{-m^{\prime}})
when
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 13.2
.

Suppose that
D\in\mathbb{Z}
and that
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. Let
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
be walks such that
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
. Let
q
,
0\leqslant q\leqslant N
, be minimal such that
\beta(q)=-m
, and
q^{\prime}
be minimal such that
\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})=-m^{\prime}
. Let
r
be the least positive integer such that
\beta
is
r
-bounded to length
N

(
see
Definition

8.11
)
, and let
r^{\prime}
be defined similarly for
\beta^{\prime}
. Then we have

(13.5)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))\ll r^{O(1)}(1+q)^{\kappa}2^{-m},

and

(13.6)

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))\ll(r^{\prime})^{O(1)}(1+q^{\prime})^{\kappa}2^{D-m^{\prime}}.

Proof.

By replacing
r
by
\max(100,r)
if necessary (and similarly for
r^{\prime}
) we may assume
r,r^{\prime}\geqslant 100
. We begin with the first bound
Equation

13.5
.
If
m\leqslant 2\log_{2}r
, the bound is trivial, so assume henceforth that
m\geqslant 2\log_{2}r
. We may also assume that
m
is sufficiently large. In particular
q\geqslant 1
. The argument is very similar to that in the previous lemma. Since
\beta
is
r
-bounded, we have
|b_{n+1-i}|\leqslant ri^{\kappa}+1
and so using
Equation

5.7
we have

(13.7)

\max\{\sum_{i\leqslant n-q;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:\varepsilon_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}\}\ll rq^{\kappa}2^{n-q}.

As in the previous lemma, we have
q\geqslant m
, and so
q\geqslant 2\log_{2}m
; therefore (since also
q
is sufficiently large) the bound in
Equation

13.7
is
\leqslant k/2
. Now we proceed as in the previous lemma, writing the event
(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))
as the union of the events
(\sum_{n-q<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=k-x)
, over all
x\ll rq^{\kappa}2^{n-q}<k/2
and all
2^{q+\beta(q)}
choices of
(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{n-q<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
.

For such an event to be nontrivial, we must have
\varepsilon_{i,j}\neq 0
for some
i\geqslant n-2\log_{2}r
; otherwise,

\sum_{n-q<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\leqslant\sum_{i\leqslant n-2\log_{2}r;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\ll\sum_{i\leqslant n-2\log_{2}r}(ri^{\kappa}+1)2^{i}<k/2<k-x.

By revealing all
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
except for such a pair of indices, we see that
\mathbb{P}(\sum_{n-q<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}=k-x)\ll 2^{2\log_{2}r-n}\ll r^{O(1)}2^{-n}
for each choice of
x
and the
\varepsilon_{i,j}
. Summing over these choices gives

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))\ll rq^{\kappa}2^{n-q}\cdot 2^{q+\beta(q)}\cdot r^{O(1)}2^{-n}\ll r^{O(1)}q^{\kappa}2^{-m},

which is the required result.

Now we turn to the second bound, whose proof is again very similar. The
r^{\prime}
-boundedness of
\beta^{\prime}
gives
|b_{i}|\leqslant 1+r^{\prime}i^{\kappa}
, and so using
Equation

5.8
we have

\max\{\sum_{i\leqslant q^{\prime};j\in[b_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:\varepsilon_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}\}\ll r^{\prime}(q^{\prime})^{\kappa}2^{q^{\prime}}.

The rest of the argument proceeds as before; as in the proof of
Lemma

13.1
the number of choices for
(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{q^{\prime}<i\leqslant n;j\in[b_{i}]}
is
2^{n+D-q^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})}
. We obtain

\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))\ll r^{\prime}(q^{\prime})^{\kappa}2^{q^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{n+D-q^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})}\cdot(r^{\prime})^{O(1)}2^{-n}=(r^{\prime})^{O(1)}(q^{\prime})^{\kappa}2^{D-m^{\prime}},

which is the required result.
∎

It follows from
Equation

13.5
and the definition
Equation

13.3
that

\displaystyle E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\ll 2^{-m}\sum_{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}}\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)r_{\beta}^{O(1)}(1+q_{\beta})^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant q\leqslant N\\
r\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}r^{O(1)}(1+q)^{\kappa}\mathbb{P}(\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=-m^{\prime},\,D(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=D,

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204ptR_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})=r,\,Q_{m}(\bm{\beta})=q),

where
q_{\beta},r_{\beta}
are the
q,r
associated to
\beta
as in the statement of
Lemma

13.2
,
R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)
is the least positive integer
r
such that
\beta
is
r
-bounded to length
N
, and
Q_{m}(\bm{\beta})
is the least non-negative integer such that
\bm{\beta}(q)=-m
. To estimate this, we apply the first estimate in
Lemma

8.10
with the event
\mathcal{E}
being that
R_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})=r
and that
Q_{m}(\bm{\beta})=q
. This gives (recalling that
n\sim\log_{2}k
)

\displaystyle E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\ll(1+m+m^{\prime})^{2}2^{-m}(\log k)^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant q\leqslant N\\
r\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}r^{O(1)}

\displaystyle(1+q)^{\kappa}\mathbb{P}\big(R_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})=r,

\displaystyle Q_{m}(\bm{\beta})=q,\,\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big).

Applying the AM-GM inequality
r^{O(1)}(1+q)^{\kappa}\leqslant r^{O(1)}+(1+q)^{2\kappa}
and summing out the redundant variables gives

\displaystyle E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\ll(1+m+m^{\prime})^{2}2^{-m}(\log k)^{-1}\sum_{0\leqslant q\leqslant N}(1+q)^{2\kappa}\mathbb{P}\big(Q_{m}(\bm{\beta})=q,\,\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)

(13.8)

\displaystyle+(1+m+m^{\prime})^{2}2^{-m}(\log k)^{-1}\sum_{r\geqslant 1}r^{O(1)}\mathbb{P}\big(R_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})=r,\,\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big).

By
Lemma

8.7
(relaxing the condition
Q_{m}(\bm{\beta})=q
to
\bm{\beta}(q)=-m
, and the condition
\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m
to
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leqslant q\leqslant N}(1+q)^{2\kappa}\mathbb{P}\big(Q_{m}(\bm{\beta})=q,\,\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)

\displaystyle\ll(1+m)\sum_{0\leqslant q\leqslant N}(1+q)^{2\kappa-3/2}(N+1-q)^{-1/2}

(13.9)

\displaystyle\ll(1+m)N^{-1/2}\ll(1+m)(\log k)^{-1/2}.

By
Lemma

8.12
(relaxing the condition
R_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})=r
to
R_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})>r-1
) we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r\geqslant 1}r^{O(1)}\mathbb{P}\big(R_{\leqslant N}(\bm{\beta})=r,\,

\displaystyle\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)\ll N^{-1/2}\sum_{r\geqslant 1}r^{O(1)}(m+r)e^{-r}

(13.10)

\displaystyle\ll(1+m)N^{-1/2}\ll(1+m)(\log k)^{-1/2}.

It follows from
Equations

13.8
,
13.9
, and
13.10
that

(13.11)

E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\ll(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}2^{-m}(\log k)^{-3/2}.

An essentially identical analysis starting from
Equation

13.6
yields

(13.12)

E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\ll(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}2^{D-m^{\prime}}(\log k)^{-3/2}.

From this we deduce the following estimate concerning the contribution to
Equation

13.4
from walks with small minima or large discrepancy.

Lemma 13.3
.

The sum of
(\log 2)^{D}E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
over all
D,m,m^{\prime}
with
|D|\leqslant 20\log n
,
0\leqslant m,m^{\prime}\leqslant(\log n)^{2}
and either
|D|\geqslant M/10
or
\max(m,m^{\prime})\geqslant M
is bounded by
\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}(\log k)^{-3/2}
.

Proof.

Given the estimates
Equations

13.11
and
13.12
, the task is to show that

(13.13)

\sum_{D,m,m^{\prime}}(\log 2)^{D}(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}\min(2^{-m},2^{D-m^{\prime}})\ll e^{-\Omega(M)},

where the sum is over triples
D,m,m^{\prime}
,
D\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, satisfying one of (1)
|D|\geqslant M/10
, (2)
|D|\leqslant M/10
but
\max(m,m^{\prime})\geqslant M
.

Contribution from
(1). First we consider the contribution from
D\geqslant M/10
. For this we use the estimate
\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\geqslant 0}(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}\min(2^{-m},2^{D-m^{\prime}})\ll D^{4}
. (To verify this, first note that the contribution from
m^{\prime}\leqslant 2D
is
\ll D\sum_{m\geqslant 0}(D+m)^{3}2^{-m}
, which is acceptable; for
m^{\prime}\geqslant 2D
we have
\min(2^{-m},2^{D-m^{\prime}})\leqslant\min(2^{-m/2},2^{-m^{\prime}/2})
, so by symmetry the sum is
\ll\sum_{m\geqslant m^{\prime}}(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}2^{-m/2}\ll\sum_{m}(1+m)^{4}2^{-m/2}
, which is again acceptable.) Including the factor
(\log 2)^{D}
and summing this estimate over
D\geqslant M/10
gives
\sum_{D\geqslant M/10}(\log 2)^{D}D^{4}\ll(M/10)^{4}(\log 2)^{M/10}
, which is acceptable.

Next we consider the contribution from
D\leqslant-M/10
. For this we use the estimate
\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\geqslant 0}(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}\min(2^{-m},2^{D-m^{\prime}})\ll|D|^{4}2^{-|D|}
. (To verify this, first note that the contribution from
m\leqslant 2|D|
is
\ll|D|\sum_{m^{\prime}\geqslant 0}(|D|+m^{\prime})^{3}2^{-|D|-m^{\prime}}
, which is acceptable; for
m\geqslant 2|D|
we have
\min(2^{-m},2^{D-m^{\prime}})\leqslant 2^{-|D|}\min(2^{-m/2},2^{-m^{\prime}/2})
, and we can conclude as before.) Including the factor
(\log 2)^{D}
and summing this estimate over
D\leqslant-M/10
gives
\ll\sum_{D\leqslant-M/10}|D|^{4}(\log 2)^{-|D|}2^{-|D|}\ll(M/10)^{4}(2\log 2)^{-M/10}
, so this contribution is also acceptable.

Contribution from
(2). The contribution to
Equation

13.13
from
|D|\leqslant M/10
and
\max(m,m^{\prime})\geqslant M
is

\ll M(2/\log 2)^{M/10}\sum_{\max(m,m^{\prime})\geqslant M}(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}\min(2^{-m},2^{-m^{\prime}}).

By symmetry it is enough to sum this over
m\geqslant\max(m^{\prime},M)
. We then note, in turn, that

\sum_{m^{\prime}\leqslant m}(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}\min(2^{-m},2^{-m^{\prime}})\ll(1+m)^{4}2^{-m},

then that
\sum_{m\geqslant M}(1+m)^{4}2^{-m}\ll M^{4}2^{-M}
. The result follows.
∎

It follows, substituting into
Equation

13.4
, that

\displaystyle p(k)

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))e^{\gamma(\frac{1}{\log 2}-1)}k^{-\delta}\sum_{|D|\leqslant M/10}(\log 2)^{D-\xi}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M}E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}+

(13.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt+O\big(e^{-\Omega(M)}k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}\big).

This is the desired reduction to the consideration of walks with large minimum and small discrepancy.

13.2.
Passing to ‘good’ walks

We now continue with the analysis, the next aim being to refine
Equation

13.14
so that
E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
(see
Equation

13.3
) only sums over walks which are
R(M)
-bounded,
T(M)
-positive and for which
\beta
has a jump step, at which point we will be able to apply
Proposition

12.1
.

Recall the definition
Equation

13.1
of
\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
. We now define two slightly different subsets of
\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
. Let
C_{0}
be a (sufficiently large) constant to be specified later. Set

\mathscr{B}^{(1)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}:=\{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}:\beta,\beta^{\prime}\;\mbox{are $R(M)$-bounded and $T(M)$-positive to length $L(M)$ and}

(13.15)

\mbox{there are $q,q^{\prime}\leqslant e^{C_{0}M}$ with $\beta(q)=-m$, $\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})=-m^{\prime}$}\}.

By a
jump step at scale
M
we mean a
V(M)
-jump step in the sense of
Definition

8.17
which lies in the interval
[t_{\min}(M),t_{\max}(M)]
, with the parameters
V(M),t_{\min}(M),t_{\max}(M)
as in the hierarchy
Equation

11.3
. Define
\mathscr{B}^{(2)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
to be the set of all
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
such that
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
are
R(M)
-bounded and
T(M)
-positive to lengths
N
,
N^{\prime}
respectively, there are
q,q^{\prime}\leqslant e^{C_{0}M}
with
\beta(q)=-m
,
\beta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})=-m^{\prime}
, and such that
\beta
has a jump step at scale
M
.
We clearly have

(13.16)

\mathscr{B}^{(2)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathscr{B}^{(1)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}.

We claim that both these sets are ‘generic’ in the following sense.

Lemma 13.4
.

Suppose that
m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M
and that
|D|\leqslant M/10
. We have for
i=1,2
that

\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\setminus\mathscr{B}^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\big)\ll e^{-10M}(\log k)^{-3/2}.

Proof.

By
Equation

13.16
it is enough to handle the case
i=2
. We use
Lemma

8.10
with
\mathcal{E}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{E}^{(i)}
being the event that at least one of the following occurs: (1)
\bm{\beta}
is not
R(M)
-bounded to length
N
; (2)
\bm{\beta}
is not
T(M)
-positive to length
N
; (3) there is
q
with
e^{C_{0}M}\leqslant q\leqslant N
such
\beta(q)=-m
and (4)
\bm{\beta}
has no jump step at scale
M
. There are also (except for (4)) symmetric events involving
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
to be considered, which may be handled in exactly the same way and about which we shall make no further comment. Applying
Lemma

8.10
, we see that it is enough to show that

(13.17)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{E}^{(i)},\,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\ll e^{-10M}N^{-1/2}.

We handle the case
i=1,2,3,4
in turn.

Case
i=1
.
By
Lemma

8.12
it follows that
Equation

13.17
is
\ll R(M)e^{-R(M)}N^{-1/2}
. Recalling from
Definition

11.1
that
R(M)=20M
, this is acceptable.

Case
i=2
.
By
Lemma

8.15
it follows that
Equation

13.17
is
\ll M^{O(1)}T(M)^{-\eta/2}N^{-1/2}
. Recalling from
Definition

11.1
that
T(M)=e^{40M/\eta}
, this is acceptable.

Case
i=3
.
By
Lemma

8.7
,
Equation

13.17
is

\ll M\sum_{e^{C_{0}M}\leqslant q\leqslant N}q^{-3/2}(N+1-q)^{-1/2}\ll Me^{-C_{0}M/2}N^{-1/2},

which is acceptable if
C_{0}>20
.

Case
i=4
.
In this case the bound follows from
Lemma

8.19
with
\delta=e^{-10M}
and
Equation

8.4
, provided parameters are chosen appropriately. Note here that for any choice of
V(M)
there are appropriate choices of
t_{\min}(M)
and
t_{\max}(M)
for which
Equations

8.27
and
8.28
both hold.
∎

For
i=1,2
define
E^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
analogously to
E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
(cf.
Equation

13.3
), that is to say
write

E^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}:=\sum_{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}}\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime})).

If we replace
E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
by
E^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
in
Equation

13.14
, the error is bounded by

(13.18)

\ll k^{-\delta}\sum_{|D|\leqslant M/10}(\log 2)^{D}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M}\mathbb{P}((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\setminus\mathscr{B}^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}})\ll e^{-5M}k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}

by
Lemma

13.4
. Thus we may replace
E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
by
E^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
in
Equation

13.14
with changes only in the implied constant in the error term.

Define also

\tilde{E}^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}:=\sum_{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}^{(i)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}}\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)\Big(1-\mathbb{E}\exp\big({-}2^{D-\xi}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L(M))\big)\Big).

The purpose of this definition is that we have replaced
\mathbb{P}(k\in\Sigma(\mathbf{A}\mid\beta,\beta^{\prime}))
by the main term in the formula obtained in
Proposition

12.1
. Indeed, by
Proposition

12.1
it follows that

E^{(2)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}=\tilde{E}^{(2)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}+O(\ell_{*}(M)^{-1}\mathbb{P}((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}})).

Thus, using
Equation

13.2
, we see that the error if we further replace
E^{(2)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
by
\tilde{E}^{(2)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
in
Equation

13.14
is bounded by

\displaystyle\ll\ell_{*}(M)^{-1}k^{-\delta}\sum_{|D|\leqslant M/10,m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M}(\log 2)^{D}\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle\ll\ell_{*}(M)^{-1}k^{-\delta}\sum_{|D|\leqslant M/10,m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M}(\log 2)^{D}(1+m+m^{\prime})^{3}(\log k)^{-3/2}\ll e^{-10M}k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}

if an appropriate choice of
\ell_{*}(M)
is made in the parameter hierarchy
Equation

11.3
. Finally, we replace
\tilde{E}^{(2)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
by
\tilde{E}^{(1)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
in
Equation

13.14
. The error in doing this is again bounded as in
Equation

13.18
by a very similar argument using
Lemma

13.4
.

Let us state the final conclusion of the above analysis, and the only one we will need subsequently, which is that with negligible error we can replace
E_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
by
\tilde{E}^{(1)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
in
Equation

13.14
, and therefore we have

(13.19)

p(k)=(c_{0}+o(1))k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}\big(f_{M,n}(\xi)+O(e^{-\Omega(M)})\big),

where
c_{0}
is the constant in
Theorem

1.1
, and for
0\leqslant\theta\leqslant 1
we define
f_{M,n}(\theta)
to be

(13.20)

\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}n^{3/2}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|D|\leqslant M/10\\
m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M\\
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}^{(1)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt(\log 2)^{D-\theta}\mathbb{P}\big((\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\big)\big(1-\mathbb{E}\exp\big({-}2^{D-\theta}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)\big)\big),

with
L=L(M)
and
\mathscr{B}^{(1)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
defined in
Equation

13.15
. (Here we have used that
(\log k)^{3/2}=(1+o(1))(\log 2)^{3/2}n^{3/2}
, but it is natural to write things in terms of
\log k
in
Equation

13.19
and in terms of
n
in
Equation

13.20
.)

14.
Decoupling the walks

The notation in this section follows that of the previous three sections and in particular we continue to write
L=L(M)
and
N=\lceil n/2\rceil
,
N^{\prime}=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
for brevity. It is also convenient to write
\beta_{\leqslant L}
for
(\beta(i))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant L}
, and similarly for
\beta^{\prime}
. We always have
\beta(0)=\beta^{\prime}(0)=0
. Our aim now is to show that the function
f_{M,n}
in
Equation

13.20
essentially does not depend on
n
. The average in
Equation

13.20
is over pairs of walks
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
satisfying the conditions

(14.1)

D(\beta,\beta^{\prime}):=\beta(N)-\beta^{\prime}(N^{\prime})=D,\quad\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\beta(i)=-m,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m^{\prime}.

Note that membership of
(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
in
\mathscr{B}^{(1)}_{D,m,m^{\prime}}
is defined by the conditions
Equation

14.1
and the following four conditions which depend only on each of
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
individually and in fact only on the values
\beta_{\leqslant L},\beta^{\prime}_{\leqslant L}
, namely

(1)

\beta
is
R(M)
-bounded to length
L
;

(2)

\beta
is
T(M)
-positive to length
L
;

(3)

\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant L}\beta(i)=-m
;

(4)

There is some
q\leqslant e^{C_{0}M}
such that
\beta(q)=-m
,

and similar conditions on
\beta^{\prime}
. Note that the redundant condition (3) is deliberately retained here as it will be relevant to a later definition. It is also the case that the random variables
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)
,
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)
depend only
\beta_{\leqslant L}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{\leqslant L}
respectively.

Define
\mathscr{C}_{m,M}
to be the set of all tuples
\vec{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{L})\in\mathbb{Z}^{L}
such that
\beta
satisfies (1) to (4) above iff
\beta_{\leqslant L}\in\mathscr{C}_{m,M}
. Define
\mathscr{W}_{m,M}
to be the set of all walks
\beta
satisfying (1) to (4) above; thus
\beta\in\mathscr{W}_{m,M}
if and only if
\beta_{\leqslant L}\in\mathscr{C}_{m,M}
.

We may then write
Equation

13.20
as

\displaystyle f_{M,n}(\theta)=\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}n^{3/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|D|\leqslant M/10\\
m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{c}\in\mathscr{C}_{m,M}\\
\vec{c}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{m^{\prime},M}\end{subarray}}(\log 2)^{D-\theta}\mathbb{E}\big(1-\exp\big({-}2^{D-\theta}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\vec{c}}(L)\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\vec{c}^{\prime}}(L)\big)\big)\times

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N^{\prime}}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=-m^{\prime},\;\bm{\beta}(N)-\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(N^{\prime})=D,\,\bm{\beta}_{\leqslant L}=\vec{c},\,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{\leqslant L}=\vec{c}^{\prime}\big).

Applying
Lemma

8.9
, we obtain

\displaystyle f_{M,n}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|D|\leqslant M/10\\
m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{c}\in\mathscr{C}_{m,M}\\
\vec{c}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{m^{\prime},M}\end{subarray}}(\log 2)^{D-\theta}\mathbb{E}\big(1-\exp\big({-}2^{D-\theta}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\vec{c}}(L)\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\vec{c}^{\prime}}(L)\big)\big)\times

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\big(h(m+c_{L})h^{\prime}(m^{\prime}+c^{\prime}_{L})+O(n^{-\varepsilon_{1}})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}_{\leqslant L}=\vec{c},\,\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{\leqslant L}=\vec{c}^{\prime}\big)

(14.2)

\displaystyle=f_{M}(\theta)+O(n^{-\varepsilon_{1}/2}),

where
\varepsilon_{1}
is the (positive) exponent in
Lemma

8.9
and

(14.3)

f_{M}(\theta):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|D|\leqslant M/10\\
m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M\end{subarray}}(\log 2)^{D-\theta}\iint h(m+\beta(L))h^{\prime}(m^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}(L))\Psi_{m,m^{\prime},M,D,\theta}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})

with

(14.4)

\Psi_{m,m^{\prime},M,D,\theta}(\beta,\beta^{\prime}):=\mathbb{E}\big(1-\exp\big({-}2^{D-\theta}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)\big)\big)1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)1_{\mathscr{W}_{m^{\prime},M}}(\beta^{\prime}),

and the integrations being with respect to the path measures of
\bm{\beta}
,
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
respectively.
We comment briefly on the application of
Lemma

8.9
. The required condition
\max c_{i},\max c^{\prime}_{i}\leqslant N^{\varepsilon_{1}}
is comfortably implied by the definition of
\mathscr{W}_{m,M}
, in particular by the fact that
\vec{c}
must be the initial segment of an
R(M)
-bounded walk. We also note that the
O(n^{-\varepsilon_{1}/2})
error term in
Equation

14.2
follows using the fact that
(\log 2)^{-D}
is much smaller than
n^{\varepsilon_{1}/2}
(and that the
\mathscr{W}_{m,M}
are disjoint as
m
varies).

The crucial point to note is that
f_{M}(\theta)
no longer depends on
n
; in other words
f_{M}(\theta)
is a ‘universal’ object which does not see
k,n,\xi
.

Combining
Equations

13.19
,
13.20
, and
14.2
gives

(14.5)

p(k)=(c_{0}+o(1))k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}\big(f_{M}(\xi)+O(e^{-\Omega(M)})\big),

where
\xi=\{\log_{2}k\}
as usual, the
o(1)
denotes a quantity tending to zero as
k\rightarrow\infty
, and
M
is any quantity satisfying
M\leqslant M_{0}(k)
for some fixed function
M_{0}
with
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}M_{0}(k)=\infty
. The function
M_{0}
is specified by the requirement that it must be possible to choose parameters as in
Equation

11.3
.

Let us pause to remark on how
Corollary

1.2
already follows from this and the existing literature.

Proof of
Corollary

1.2
.

If
k
is a power of two then
\xi=\{\log_{2}k\}=0
. By applying
Equation

14.5
for fixed
M
and
k\rightarrow\infty
, we see that
|f_{M}(0)-f_{M+1}(0)|\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}
, and therefore we have
f_{M}(0)=c_{1}+O(e^{-\Omega(M)})
for some
c_{1}
. Substituting in to
Equation

14.5
, the corollary follows (with
C=c_{0}c_{1}
) except we have not shown that
C>0
. This does not follow directly from this argument, but is a consequence of
[
15
]
and will also be shown in a self-contained fashion in
Sections

15.4
and
15.5
below.
∎

15.
Proof of the main theorem

In this section we prove
Theorem

1.1
. The main task is to show that the measures
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
exist and are not zero. The more detailed characterisations described in
Propositions

1.3
and
1.4
must wait until later.

Recall that
\mathscr{W}_{m,M}
denotes the set of walks
\beta
satisfying conditions (1) – (4) at the start of
Section

14
. Throughout the section
c:=-\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}
.

15.1.
Approximating
f_{M}
by a convolution

The starting point for this section is
Equation

14.5
, with
f_{M}
defined in
Equation

14.3
. Our aim is to show convergence of
f_{M}
to a function
f
, which we will ultimately describe as a convolution
g\ast\mu\ast\mu^{\prime}
. As a stepping stone to this result, our main goal in this subsection is to approximate
f_{M}
by a convolution; in particular, the function
g
(defined in
Equation

1.2
) will enter the picture for the first time. Specifically, we will prove the following.

Lemma 15.1
.

Let
M
be a sufficiently large positive integer. There is a function
\tilde{f}_{M}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
with

(15.1)

\|f_{M}-\tilde{f}_{M}\|_{\infty}\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}

and such that
\tilde{f}_{M}
may be written as

(15.2)

\tilde{f}_{M}=g\ast\mu_{M}\ast\mu^{\prime}_{M},

g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
the function in
Equation

1.2
,
\mu_{M}
is the Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
given by

(15.3)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{M}=\sum_{m\leqslant M}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))^{c}\psi\big(\log_{2}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))\big)1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)

for test functions
\psi\in C(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, and
\mu^{\prime}_{M}
is defined by

(15.4)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}_{M}=\sum_{m\leqslant M}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\mathbb{E}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L(M))^{c}\psi\big(\log_{2}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L(M))\big)1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m}(\beta^{\prime}).

Here, the convention is that
x^{c}\psi(\log_{2}x)=0
if
x=0
.

Proof.

We again write
L=L(M)
for brevity.
With
Equation

14.3
in mind, consider first any expression of the form

\iint h(\beta(L)+m)h^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}(L)+m^{\prime})\Psi(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})

where
\Psi:\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is such that
\Psi(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
depends only on the initial segments
\beta_{\leqslant L},\beta^{\prime}_{\leqslant L}
. (Note that the functions
\Psi_{m,m^{\prime},M,D,\theta}
in
Equation

14.4
are of this type due to the nature of the
1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}
cutoffs.) By
Equation

8.9
this expression can be written

\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}}}\Psi(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}(\beta^{\prime}).

Returning to
Equation

14.3
, we therefore have

(15.5)

f_{M}(\theta)=\sum_{|D|\leqslant M/10}(\log 2)^{D-\theta}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}}}\Psi_{m,m^{\prime},M,D,\theta}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}(\beta^{\prime}).

Suppose in all that follows that
\theta\in[0,1]
. The next part of our analysis is to show that we can extend the sum over
D
in
Equation

15.5
to all of
\mathbb{Z}
with small error. First note that, immediately from the definition
Equation

14.4
, we have
\Psi_{m,m^{\prime},M,D,\theta}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\leqslant 1
always and so by
Proposition

8.1
the integral in
Equation

15.5
is
\ll\omega_{m}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m})\omega^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}})=h(m)h(m^{\prime})\ll M^{2}
.

We immediately see that the error incurred by adding in the terms
D>M/10
to
Equation

15.5
is
\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}
.

When
D
is very negative we need an alternative argument. By the inequality
1-e^{-x}\leqslant x
we have
1-\exp\big({-}2^{D-\theta}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)\big)\ll 2^{D}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)
. Thus, since
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)\leqslant 1
, it follows from
Lemma

7.2
and the definition
Equation

14.4
that we additionally have
\Psi_{m,m^{\prime},M,D,\theta}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\ll 2^{D}r^{2}
if
\beta
is
r
-bounded up to
L
, and hence certainly if
\beta
is
r
-bounded. Thus, defining
R(\beta)
to be the minimal integer
r
for which
\beta
is
r
-bounded (as in
Definition

8.11
), we have using
Corollary

8.13
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}}}\Psi_{m,m^{\prime},M,D,\theta}

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\ll 2^{D}\omega^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}})\sum_{r}r^{2}\omega_{m}(R(\beta)=r)

\displaystyle\ll 2^{D}M\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}}(M+r+1)e^{-r}\ll M^{2}2^{D}.

From this (and since
2\log 2>1
) we see that the error from adding the terms
D<-M/10
to
Equation

15.5
is also
\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}
.

Thus, up to error
e^{-\Omega(M)}
, we can replace
f_{M}(\theta)
by the completed sum
