\tilde{f}_{M}(\theta)=\sum_{D\in\mathbb{Z}}(\log 2)^{D-\theta}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}}}\Psi_{m,m^{\prime},M,D,\theta}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}.

Recalling the definition
Equation

14.4
, and temporarily writing
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}=\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)
,
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}=\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(L)
for brevity, it follows that
\tilde{f}_{M}(\theta)
equals

\sum_{D\in\mathbb{Z}}(\log 2)^{D-\theta}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}}}\mathbb{E}\big(1-e^{-2^{D-\theta}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}}\big)1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)1_{\mathscr{W}_{m^{\prime},M}}(\beta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}(\beta^{\prime}).

Taking the sum over
D
on the inside and using
Equation

2.19
with
\xi=\theta
and
\lambda=\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}
, we see that
\tilde{f}_{M}(\theta)
equals

\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\leqslant M}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m^{\prime}}}\mathbb{E}\big(\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}\big)^{c}g\big(\theta-\log_{2}(\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}})\big)1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)1_{\mathscr{W}_{m^{\prime},M}}(\beta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}(\beta^{\prime}).

Recalling the definitions
Equation

15.3
and
Equation

15.4
, we see that
Equation

15.2
indeed holds, and this concludes the proof.∎

15.2.
Convergence of the measures
\mu_{M}

The remaining task is to examine the convergence of the sequences of measures
(\mu_{M})_{M=1}^{\infty}
and
(\mu^{\prime}_{M})_{M=1}^{\infty}
. In this section we handle the first of these sequences in detail. Recall that
\mu_{M}
is defined in
Equation

15.3
, which we can write as

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{M}=\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))^{c}\psi\big(\log_{2}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))\big)1_{m\leqslant M}1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

Set

(15.6)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{M}=\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))^{c}\psi\big(\log_{2}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))\big)\,1_{\min\beta=-m}1_{\argmin\beta\leqslant L(M)}\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta),

where
\min\beta=\min_{i\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}}\beta(i)
and
\argmin\beta
denotes the smallest index
q\geqslant 0
for which
\beta(q)=\min\beta
. Our main result is the following. Here,
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu;\nu]}
denotes the bounded Lipschitz distance between two measures; for a full discussion of this concept, see
Appendix

E
.

Lemma 15.2
.

We have
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu_{M};\tilde{\mu}_{M}]}\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}
.

In the proof of this lemma we will use the following auxiliary bounds.

Lemma 15.3
.

Uniformly for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and
N\in[\ell,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}
we have the following bounds:

(15.7)

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\ll 2^{-m/4};

(15.8)

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)=r\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\ll r^{2}(m+r+1)e^{-r};

(15.9)

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)=r\\
\argmin\beta=q\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\ll r^{3}(1+q)^{\kappa-3/2}

for
q\leqslant\ell
, and

(15.10)

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)=r\\
T(\beta)>T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\ll r^{2}(1+m)^{O(1)}T^{-\eta/2}.

Here we define
R_{\leqslant\infty}(\beta):=R(\beta)
, the smallest integer
r
for which
\beta
is
r
-bounded.

Proof.

The key estimate will be
Lemma

7.2
. From this, Hölder’s inequality and AM-GM we have that, if
\min\beta=-m
and
\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\leqslant\big(\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\big)^{c}\mathbb{P}(\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0)^{1-c}

\displaystyle\ll R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)^{3}(1+\argmin\beta)^{\kappa}2^{-m/3}

\displaystyle\leqslant(R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)^{6}+(1+\argmin\beta)^{2\kappa})2^{-m/3}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)

\displaystyle\ll 2^{-m/3}\sum_{q\geqslant 0}(1+q)^{2\kappa}\omega_{m}\{\argmin\beta=q\}+2^{-m/3}\sum_{r}r^{6}\omega_{m}\{R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)=r\}.

To estimate the two terms on the right we use
Equations

8.16
and
8.13
respectively, obtaining that the above is

\ll(1+m)2^{-m/3}\big(\sum_{q}(1+q)^{2\kappa-3/2}+\sum_{r}r^{6}(r+1)e^{-r}\big)\ll(1+m)2^{-m/3}\ll 2^{-m/4},

which gives
Equation

15.7
.
For
Equation

15.8
we use Hölder as above, but now using the trivial bound
\mathbb{P}(\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0)\leqslant 1
. This gives
\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\ll R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)^{2}\leqslant R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)^{2}
, and so

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)=r\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\ll r^{2}\omega_{m}\{R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)=r\}.

The bound
Equation

15.8
follows using
Corollary

8.13
.

For
Equation

15.9
we proceed as for
Equation

15.7
, except we replace
R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)
by
R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)
and avoid the temptation to apply AM-GM. We then have

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)=r\\
\argmin\beta=q\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\ll r^{3}(1+q)^{\kappa}2^{-m/3}\omega_{m}\{\argmin\beta=q\},

and the result follows from
Lemma

8.6
and the crude estimate
(1+m)2^{-m/3}\ll 1
.

Finally, for
Equation

15.10
we proceed as for
Equation

15.8
, obtaining

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)=r\\
T(\beta)>T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\ll r^{2}\omega\{R_{\leqslant N}(\beta)=r,T(\beta)>T\}\leqslant r^{2}\omega_{m}\{T(\beta)>T\},

which gives the required bound using
Corollary

8.16
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

15.2
.

Let
\psi
be a function on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
with
\|\psi\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant 1
. In particular,
\|\psi\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
and so we have

\displaystyle\big|\int\psi\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mu_{M}-\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{M}\big|

(15.11)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L(M))^{c}\big|1_{\min\beta=-m,\argmin\beta\leqslant L(M)}-1_{m\leqslant M}1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)\big|\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

Recall that
\mathscr{W}_{m,M}
is the set of walks
\beta
satisfying items (1) – (4) in
Section

14
, and so we may write

1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)=1_{R_{\leqslant L(M)}(\beta)\leqslant R(M)}1_{T_{\leqslant L(M)}(\beta)\leqslant T(M)}1_{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant L(M)}\beta(i)=-m}1_{\min\beta^{-1}(-m)\leqslant e^{C_{0}M}}.

Restricted to walks
\beta
in
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}
, for some
m\geqslant 0
, we have

1_{m\leqslant M}1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)=1_{m\leqslant M}1_{R_{\leqslant L(M)}(\beta)\leqslant R(M)}1_{T_{\leqslant L(M)}(\beta)\leqslant T(M)}1_{\min\beta=-m}1_{\argmin\beta\leqslant e^{C_{0}M}}.

Note that, when written this way, it is clear that
1_{m\leqslant M}1_{\mathscr{W}_{m,M}}(\beta)\leqslant 1_{\min\beta=-m}1_{\argmin\beta\leqslant L(M)}
pointwise for
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-m}^{\mathbb{N}}
(and so the absolute value signs in
Equation

15.11
are unnecessary). Writing
L=L(M)
for brevity, by a telescoping sum we may bound
Equation

15.11
as a sum of four terms

(15.12)

\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-m}^{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)^{c}1_{m>M}1_{\min\beta=-m}1_{\argmin\beta\leqslant L}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta),

(15.13)

\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-m}^{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)^{c}1_{m\leqslant M}1_{\min\beta=-m}1_{\argmin\beta\leqslant L}1_{R_{\leqslant L}(\beta)>R(M)}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta),

(15.14)

\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-m}^{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)^{c}1_{m\leqslant M}1_{\min\beta=-m}1_{e^{C_{0}M}<\argmin\beta\leqslant L}1_{R_{\leqslant L}(\beta)\leqslant R(M)}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta),

and

(15.15)

\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-m}^{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(L)^{c}1_{m\leqslant M}1_{\min\beta=-m}1_{\argmin\beta\leqslant e^{C_{0}M}}1_{R_{\leqslant L}(\beta)\leqslant R(M)}1_{T_{\leqslant L}(\beta)>T(M)}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

We bound the contributions from these pieces using
Lemma

15.3
, showing that in each case we get a bound
\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}
. In what follows, recall from
Definition

11.1
that
R(M):=20M
and that
T(M):=e^{40M/\eta}
.

To bound
Equation

15.12
we use
Equation

15.7
and sum over
m>M
. This clearly gives a bound
\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}
.

To bound
Equation

15.13
we use
Equation

15.8
. Summing over
m\leqslant M
and
r>R(M)
, this gives a bound

\ll\sum_{m\leqslant M}\sum_{r>R(M)}r^{2}(m+r+1)e^{-r}\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}.

To bound
Equation

15.14
we use
Equation

15.9
. Summing this over
m\leqslant M
,
r\leqslant R(M)
, and
e^{C_{0}M}<q\leqslant L
gives

\ll M\sum_{r\leqslant R(M)}\sum_{e^{C_{0}M}<q\leqslant L}r^{3}(1+q)^{\kappa-3/2}\ll M^{5}(e^{C_{0}M})^{\kappa-1/2}\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}.

Finally, to bound
Equation

15.15
we use
Equation

15.10
, noting that
T(\beta)\geqslant T_{\leqslant L}(\beta)
. Summing this over
m\leqslant M
and
r\leqslant R(M)
gives a bound of
M^{O(1)}T(M)^{-\eta/2}\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

15.2
.∎

We now define measures
\mu^{(\ell)}
by

(15.16)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

Thus from the definition
Equation

15.6
we see that

(15.17)

\tilde{\mu}_{M}=\mu^{(L(M))}.

We now claim the following.

Proposition 15.4
.

There is a Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
with
\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}={\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu;0]}<\infty
such that
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{(\ell)};\mu]}\rightarrow 0
as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

Since the space of measures is complete with respect to the bounded Lipschitz metric (
Lemma

E.1
) it is enough to show that the
\mu^{(\ell)}
are a Cauchy sequence in the bounded Lipschitz norm. For the rest of the section suppose that
\psi\in C(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is a test function with
\|\psi\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant 1
.
We first observe the inequality

(15.18)

\big|x^{c}\psi(\log_{2}x)-y^{c}\psi(\log_{2}y)\big|\ll|x-y|^{c}

for
x,y\in[0,\infty)
. To prove this, suppose wlog that
y\geqslant x
and write
y=x+h
. If
x=O(h)
then the result is trivial. Otherwise, we have

x^{c}\big(\psi(\log_{2}x)-\psi(\log_{2}(x+h))\big)=x^{c}\big(\psi(\log_{2}x)-\psi(\log_{2}x+O(\frac{h}{x}))\big)\ll x^{c}\frac{h}{x}\ll h^{c}

by the Lipschitz property. Also

\big((x+h)^{c}-x^{c}\big)\psi(\log_{2}(x+h))\leqslant\big|(x+h)^{c}-x^{c}\big|\ll x^{c}\big(1+O(\frac{h}{x})-1\big)\ll h^{c}\big(\frac{h}{x}\big)^{1-c}\ll h^{c},

and together these statements give
Equation

15.18
.
We note also that

(15.19)

|x-y|^{c}\leqslant x^{c}+y^{c}.

Let
\ell<\ell^{\prime}
. Then by the definition
Equation

15.16
and from
Equation

15.18
we have

\displaystyle\big|

\displaystyle\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{(\ell)}-\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{(\ell^{\prime})}\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{m}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell))-\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime})^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime}))\Big|\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{m}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\ell<\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime})^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)

(15.20)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{m}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime})\big|^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)+\sum_{m}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\ell<\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime})^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

To bound the first term we split into two parts. As usual,
R(\beta)
denotes the least integer so that
\beta
is
R(\beta)
-bounded, and
T(\beta)
the least integer such that
\beta
is
T(\beta)
-bounded. The ‘main’ region of integration is over
m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
and
\beta
for which
R(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
and
T(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{1/8}
, where here
\eta_{0}\in(0,\eta)
is an absolute constant to be specified. On this region, it follows from
Equation

7.16
with a telescoping sum that
\mathbb{E}|\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime})|\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{1/4})}
, and hence
\mathbb{E}|\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime})|^{c}\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{1/4})}
by Hölder’s inequality. Since
\omega_{m}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m})\ll m
, the contribution of the main region of integration to the first term in
Equation

15.20
is
\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{1/4})}
.

To estimate the contribution to the first term from the remaining portions, we first apply
Equation

15.19
, thereby reducing matters to estimating

(15.21)

\sum_{m>\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\tilde{\ell})^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta),\qquad\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
R(\beta)>\ell^{\eta_{0}}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\tilde{\ell})^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta),

and

(15.22)

\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}R(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}\\
T(\beta)>\ell^{1/8}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\tilde{\ell})^{c}1_{\min\beta=-m}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)

for
\tilde{\ell}\in\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}
. (We dropped the
\argmin
conditions in the second estimate in
Equation

15.21
and in
Equation

15.22
since we will not need these in the estimations.)

The contribution from the first integral in
Equation

15.21
is
\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{\eta_{0}})}<\ell^{-10}
by
Equation

15.7
.

The contribution from the second integral in
Equation

15.21
can be estimated using
Equation

15.8
with
N=\infty
, which gives a bound of

(15.23)

\ll\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{r>\ell^{\eta_{0}}}r^{2}(m+r+1)e^{-r}\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{\eta_{0}/2})}<\ell^{-10}.

Finally, the contribution from
Equation

15.22
can be estimated using
Equation

15.10
, which gives a bound of

\ll\ell^{-\eta/16}\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{r\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}(r(1+m))^{O(1)}\ll\ell^{-\eta/32},

provided that
\eta_{0}
is chosen appropriately. This will be the weakest of the various estimates we obtain.

We also need to estimate the second term in
Equation

15.20
. Using
Equation

15.7
, the contribution from
m>\ell^{\eta_{0}}
is
<\ell^{-10}
similarly to before.

The contribution from
m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
and
r\geqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
is
<\ell^{-10}
as in the previous estimation of the second integral in
Equation

15.21
.

The final remaining contribution is at most

\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{r\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
\ell<\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell^{\prime})^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

By
Equation

15.9
with
N=\infty
this is

\ll\ell^{5\eta_{0}}\sum_{q>\ell}(1+q)^{\kappa-3/2}\ll\ell^{5\eta_{0}+\kappa-1/2}<\ell^{-1/10}.

Putting everything together, it follows that

\big|\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{(\ell)}-\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{(\ell^{\prime})}\big|\ll\ell^{-\eta/32}.

Since
\psi
was an arbitrary Lipschitz test function, it follows that
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{(\ell)};\mu^{(\ell^{\prime})}]}\ll\ell^{-\eta/32}
, which establishes that the
\mu^{(\ell)}
are indeed a Cauchy sequence.
∎

The following proposition summarises what we have shown.

Proposition 15.5
.

Let measures
\mu_{M}
be defined as in
Equation

15.3
. Then there is a Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
with
\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}={\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu;0]}<\infty
such that
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu_{M};\mu]}\rightarrow 0
as
M\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover,
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{(\ell)};\mu]}\rightarrow 0
as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
, where the measures
\mu^{(\ell)}
are defined as in
Equation

15.16
.

Proof.

This follows by combining
Lemma

15.2
,
Equation

15.17
and
Proposition

15.4
.
∎

15.3.
Convergence of the measures
\mu^{\prime}_{M}

In this section we examine the convergence of the sequence of measures
(\mu^{\prime}_{M})_{M=1}^{\infty}
, where the
\mu^{\prime}_{M}
are defined in
Equation

15.4
. The analysis is very similar to that in the previous section, so we restrict ourselves to stating the main results and indicating the points at which the arguments differ in minor details. Analogously to
Proposition

15.5
, the main statement is the following.

Proposition 15.6
.

Let measures
\mu^{\prime}_{M}
be defined as in
Equation

15.4
. Then there is a Borel measure
\mu^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
such that
\|\mu^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{BL}}={\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{\prime};0]}<\infty
and
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{\prime}_{M};\mu^{\prime}]}\rightarrow 0
as
M\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover,
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{\prime(\ell)};\mu^{\prime}]}\rightarrow 0
as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
, where the measures
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}
are defined by

(15.24)

\int\psi d\mu^{\prime(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta^{\prime}=-m\\
\argmin\beta^{\prime}\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m}(\beta^{\prime}),

where
c=-\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}
.

Proof.

(Sketch.)
For the most part the argument is the same as the one in the previous section, replacing
\beta
by
\beta^{\prime}
,
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}
by
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}
, and
\omega_{m}
by
\omega^{\prime}_{m}
throughout. In place of the estimates
Equation

7.5
and
Equation

7.6
from
Lemma

7.2
, we instead use (respectively) the trivial bound
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\leqslant 1
and
Equation

6.7
. With very minor modifications this allows us to recover analogues of all the statements in
Lemma

15.3
. Later on in the proof, at the point where we applied a telescoping sum to
Equation

7.16
, we instead apply
Lemma

6.4
, the
T
-positivity of
\beta^{\prime}
, and a telescoping sum, which gives a similar bound
\mathbb{E}|\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)-\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime})|\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{1/4})}
at the appropriate point.
∎

15.4.
\mu
is not zero

In this section we prove that the measure
\mu
is not zero. Whilst this follows from
Theorem

1.1
and the main result of Ford
[
21
]
, it does not take long to provide a direct proof using the machinery we have set up.

Our starting point is
Equation

15.16
. We take
\psi=1
and we restrict attention to walks
\beta
which satisfy
\xi_{1}=C
,
\xi_{2}=\cdots=\xi_{C+1}=-1
, and which are
R
-bounded and
T
-positive, where
R:=C^{2}
and
T:=e^{C^{2}}
. Here
C
is a sufficiently large constant to be specified later. Denote by
\mathscr{B}_{C}
the collection of such walks which additionally satisfy
\min\beta=0
(and hence
\argmin\beta=0
). Ignoring all terms in
Equation

15.16
with
m>0
, it suffices to prove that there is some choice of
C
(independent of
\ell
) such that

(15.25)

\omega_{0}(\mathscr{B}_{C})>0

and that

(15.26)

\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\gg 1

uniformly for
\beta\in\mathscr{B}_{C}
and all
\ell
(the implied constant may depend on
C
).

We begin with the first statement
Equation

15.25
. Denote by
\mathscr{B}_{C}^{*}
the event
\mathscr{B}_{C}
without the
R
-bounded and
T
-positive conditions. By
Lemma

8.3
we have, for all sufficiently large
N
,

\omega_{0}(\mathscr{B}^{*}_{C})\gg N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}\in\mathscr{B}^{*}_{C},\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0\big).

The event that
\bm{\beta}\in\mathscr{B}^{*}_{C}
and that
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0
has probability
\frac{e^{-C-1}}{(C+1)!}\mathbb{P}(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-C-1}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant 0)
, where
\bm{\gamma}
is another
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
walk. This is
\sim\frac{e^{-C-1}}{(C+1)!}h(0)(N-C-1)^{-1/2}\gg C^{-C-3/2}N^{-1/2}
. It follows that
\omega_{0}(\mathscr{B}_{C}^{*})\gg C^{-C-3/2}
. The desired result
Equation

15.25
is immediate from this and
Corollaries

8.13
and
8.15
and the choice of
R
and
T
.

For
Equation

15.26
, it suffices by Hölder’s inequality and
Corollary

7.5
to show that

(15.27)

\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\gg 1

and that
uniformly for
\beta\in\mathscr{B}_{C}
and all
\ell
sufficiently large. Again the implied constant can depend on
C
. Let us recall the definition
Equation

7.1
, which is

\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)=2^{-\beta(\ell)}\sum_{\varepsilon_{\leqslant\ell}}1\Big(\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in[1-2^{-\ell},1]\Big).

We can now prove
Equation

15.27
. We restrict to
\varepsilon_{\leqslant\ell}
satisfying

(15.28)

\varepsilon_{1,1}=1,\,\varepsilon_{1,2}=\cdots=\varepsilon_{1,C+1}=0,

and the other
\varepsilon_{i,j}
can be arbitrary. Note there are no
\varepsilon_{i,j}
for
2\leqslant i\leqslant C+1
since
1+\xi_{i}=0
for these
i
. Deterministically, we have that

0\leqslant\sum_{i\geqslant C+2}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\leqslant\sum_{i\geqslant C+2}(1+\xi_{i})2^{1-i}<\tfrac{1}{10}

for
C
large enough, using here the fact that
\bm{\beta}
is
C^{2}
-bounded and so
|\xi_{i}|\leqslant C^{2}i^{\kappa}
. Therefore for any such
\varepsilon
we have

(15.29)

\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1+\xi_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}2^{-\mathbf{u}_{i,j}}\in[1-2^{-\ell},1]\big)\geqslant\inf_{x\in[0,\frac{1}{10}]}\mathbb{P}(2^{-\mathbf{u}_{1,1}}\in 1-x-[2^{-\ell}]\big)\gg 2^{-\ell}.

(To justify this last step observe that if
f(x)
is the density function of
2^{-\mathbf{u}_{1,1}}
(where, recall,
\mathbf{u}_{1,1}
is uniform on
[0,1]
) and
g(x)
is the density function of a uniform
\operatorname{U}[\frac{1}{2},1]
variable, then a short calculation gives that
f(x)/g(x)\geqslant 1/2\log 2>\frac{1}{2}
.) The number of choices for
\varepsilon_{\leqslant\ell}
satisfying
Equation

15.28
is
2^{-C-1}2^{\sum_{i\leqslant\ell}(1+\xi_{i})}=2^{-C-1}2^{\ell+\beta(\ell)}
. Summing
Equation

15.29
over these choices immediately gives
Equation

15.27
.

15.5.
\mu^{\prime}
is not zero

In this subsection we show that
\mu^{\prime}
is not zero.

Our starting point is
Equation

15.24
. We take
\psi
to be the constant function
1
, and we remove all terms from the sum except
m=0
, noting that they are all non-negative. It thus suffices to prove that

(15.30)

\int\mathbb{E}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{0}(\beta^{\prime})\gg 1

uniformly in
\ell
. Note that both the
\min\beta^{\prime}=0
condition and the
\argmin
condition are trivial here since
\omega^{\prime}_{0}
is supported on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{\mathbb{N}}
and the minimum of
\beta^{\prime}
occurs at
0
. In order to prove this we introduce the representation function

r_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime},\ell}(t):=\#\{\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i,j})_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}:\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}\varepsilon_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=t\}.

Thus by definition
Equation

6.1
we have

(15.31)

\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)=2^{\beta^{\prime}(\ell)-\ell}|\operatorname{Supp}(r_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime},\ell})|.

Note also that

(15.32)

\sum_{t}r_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime},\ell}(t)=2^{\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)}.

We proceed via a second moment argument essentially identical to the one in the proof of
Lemma

12.3
(which is unsurprising since our task here is closely analogous to the one there). By opening up the second moment we have

(15.33)

\mathbb{E}\sum_{t}r_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime},\ell}(t)^{2}=\sum_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{i\leqslant\ell;j\in[1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}]}(\varepsilon_{i,j}-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i,j})\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=0\big).

We foliate the sum over
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
according to the largest index
r
for which there is some
s
such that
\varepsilon_{r,s}\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{r,s}
. For any such pair
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
, the inner probability is
\ll 2^{-r}
; this follows by revealing all
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}
except
\mathbf{x}_{r,s}
and applying the anti-concentration estimate
Equation

5.6
. The number of pairs
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
with a given value
r
is
\leqslant 2^{2\sum_{i\leqslant r}(1-\xi^{\prime}_{i})+\sum_{r<i\leqslant\ell}(1-\xi^{\prime}_{i})}=2^{\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)+r-\beta^{\prime}(r)}
. Therefore from
Equation

15.33
we obtain

\mathbb{E}\sum_{t}r_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime},\ell}(t)^{2}\ll 2^{\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)}\sum_{r\leqslant\ell}2^{-\beta^{\prime}(r)}.

If we assume that
\beta^{\prime}
is
T
-positive (for some
T
) then
\beta^{\prime}(r)\geqslant-T+r^{1/4}
and so

\mathbb{E}\sum_{t}r_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime},\ell}(t)^{2}\ll 2^{\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)+T}.

In particular, with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
in
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
we have

\sum_{t}r_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime},\ell}(t)^{2}\ll 2^{\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)+T}.

For any such
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
it follows from this,
Equations

15.31
and
15.32
that

\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\gg 2^{-T}
, and so

\mathbb{E}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\gg 2^{-cT}.

The desired result
Equation

15.30
now follows from the fact that

\omega^{\prime}_{0}\{\beta^{\prime}:\beta^{\prime}\mbox{ is $T$-positive}\}>0

for sufficiently large
T
, which is an immediate consequence of
Corollary

8.16
.

15.6.
Conclusion of the proof of
Theorem

1.1

It is now a fairly straightforward matter to complete the proof of
Theorem

1.1
. We begin with
Equation

14.5
, which we repeat here:

(15.34)

p(k)=(c_{0}+o(1))k^{-\delta}(\log k)^{-3/2}\big(f_{M}(\xi)+O(e^{-\Omega(M)})\big).

This was established under the assumption that
M\leqslant M_{0}(k)
for some fixed function
M_{0}
with
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}M_{0}(k)=\infty
.
We showed in
Equations

15.1
and
15.2
that

(15.35)

\|f_{M}-\tilde{f}_{M}\|_{\infty}\ll e^{-\Omega(M)}

where

(15.36)

\tilde{f}_{M}=g\ast\mu_{M}\ast\mu^{\prime}_{M}

with
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
being the function in
Equation

1.2
. We have also shown that
\mu_{M}\rightarrow\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}_{M}\rightarrow\mu^{\prime}
in bounded Lipschitz norm. To complete the proof we set
f:=c_{0}g\ast\mu\ast\mu^{\prime}
. Then
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
by two applications of
Lemma

E.3
. By
Equations

15.34
,
15.35
, and
15.36
, it suffices to show that

\|g\ast\mu_{M}\ast\mu^{\prime}_{M}-g\ast\mu\ast\mu^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\rightarrow 0.

To show this, we use the triangle inequality and
Lemma

E.2
to bound

\displaystyle\|g\ast\mu_{M}\ast\mu^{\prime}_{M}-g\ast\mu\ast\mu^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|g\ast\mu_{M}\ast(\mu^{\prime}_{M}-\mu^{\prime})\|_{\infty}+\|g\ast(\mu-\mu_{M})\ast\mu^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu_{M}^{\prime};\mu^{\prime}]}\|g\ast\mu_{M}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}+{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu_{M};\mu]}\|g\ast\mu^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}.

We have
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu_{M}^{\prime};\mu^{\prime}]},{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu_{M};\mu]}\rightarrow 0
, whilst
\|g\ast\mu_{M}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}
and
\|g\ast\mu^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}
are bounded uniformly in
M
by
Lemma

E.3
. This concludes the proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

Part V
Description of the measures

We have completed the proof of
Theorem

1.1
, but the measures
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
have only been described as rather complicated limits over random walks satisfying certain technical conditions. Our task in this, the final part of the main paper, is to remove these conditions (and the random walks!) and prove the characterisations of these measures in
Propositions

1.3
and
1.4
.

16.
Description of
\mu

The proof of
Proposition

1.3
is the somewhat more difficult of these two tasks. At the moment, we have characterised
\mu
as the (bounded Lipschitz) limit of measures
\mu^{(\ell)}
, defined as in
Equation

15.16
. Once again, in this section
\eta_{0}\in(0,\eta)
denotes a sufficiently small absolute constant.

16.1.
From walks to sequences

Recall the definition
Equation

8.1
of
h(m)
and that (see
Proposition

8.1
(2))
h(m)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}(m+1)
for
m\geqslant 0
. By a slight abuse of notation we now write
h(x):=(2/\pi)^{1/2}(x+1)^{+}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. (That is,
h(x)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}(x+1)
for
x>-1
, and
0
otherwise.)
We observe the relation

(16.1)

e^{-a}h(s-a)=\int^{s+1}_{a}e^{-u}h(s+1-u)\,\mathrm{d}u

for
a\leqslant s+1
.

Definition 16.1
.

Denote by
\Omega_{m}
the space of all sequences
x=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
of real numbers with
\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}x_{i}=\infty
and satisfying
x_{i}\leqslant i+m
for all
i
.

We consider the
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega_{m}
generated by sets of the form
\{x\in\Omega_{m}:(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in B\}
for some
r
and for some Borel measurable
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{r}
, which we can and do assume is contained in
\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{r}):x_{i}\leqslant i+m\;\mbox{for $i=1,\dots,r$}\}
. We define

(16.2)

\nu_{m}\{x:(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in B\}:=\int_{B}h(m+r-x_{r})e^{-x_{r}}1_{0<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{r}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{r}

We remark that without the factor
h(m+r-x_{r})
, this would be the usual density functional for the Poisson process. It turns out that
\nu_{m}
extends to give a well-defined measure on
\Omega_{m}
with the stated
\sigma
-algebra. This is a continuous variant of the Doob conditioning construction given in
Lemma

8.2
.

Lemma 16.2
.

The measure
\nu_{m}
is well-defined and
\nu_{m}(\Omega_{m}):=h(m)
.

Proof.

The essential point is to check compatibility of
Equation

16.2
across different values of
r
, that is to say we must show that the measures of
\{x:(x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1})\in B\}
and
\{x:(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in B\times[0,r+m]\}
coincide whenever
B\subset\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}[0,i+m]\subset\mathbb{R}^{r-1}
. To this end, if
r\geqslant 2
we have

\displaystyle\nu_{m}\{x:

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in B\times[0,r+m]\}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r-1}}1_{B}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1})\big(\int_{x_{r-1}}^{r+m}h(m+r-x_{r})e^{-x_{r}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{r}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}x_{r-1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r-1}}1_{B}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1})h(m+r-1-x_{r-1})e^{-x_{r-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}x_{r-1}

\displaystyle=\nu_{m}\{x:(x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1})\in B\},

where the middle step follows from
Equation

16.1
with
a=0
and
s=m+r-1-x_{r-1}
upon making the substitution
u=x_{r}-x_{r-1}
. If
r=1
we instead take the lower limit of the inner integral down to
0
; this confirms that
\nu_{m}(\Omega_{m})=\nu_{m}\{x:x_{1}\in[0,m+1]\}=h(m)
.
∎

Given
x=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\in\Omega_{m}
, we may define the walk
\beta_{x}
by
\beta_{x}(j)=\#\{i:x_{i}\leqslant j\}-j
. Thus, the number of elements of
x
in
(j-1,j]
is
\beta(j)-\beta(j-1)+1
. Observe that the condition that an increasing sequence
x
lies in
\Omega_{m}
is equivalent to
\min\beta_{x}\geqslant-m
. Indeed, if
x\in\Omega_{m}
then
x_{i}\leqslant i+m
for all
i
, and so
x_{j-m}\leqslant j
for
j>m
, which means that
\#\{i:x_{i}\leqslant j\}\geqslant j-m
and so
\beta_{x}(j)\geqslant-m
. Conversely if
x\notin\Omega_{m}
then
x_{j}>j+m
for some
j
, which means that
\#\{i:x_{i}\leqslant j+m\}\leqslant j-1
and so
\beta_{x}(j+m)\leqslant-m-1
.

Lemma 16.3
.

Let
F:\Omega_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be an integrable function. Then

\int_{\Omega_{m}}F(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{m}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m}}\mathbb{E}F(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

Here,
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
is the upper Poisson process
\mathbf{u}
conditioned to
\bm{\beta}=\beta
as described in
Section

5.3
.

Proof.

By a limiting argument we may assume that
F
depends only on the elements of
x
in
[0,L]
for some
L
(the functions we consider will in any case have this property). We evaluate the LHS by splitting up according to the values of
\beta_{x}(j)
,
j=1,\dots,L
. Note that
\beta_{x}(j)=c_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,L
is the condition that
x_{i}\in(j-1,j]
for
j-1+c_{j-1}<i\leqslant j+c_{j}
,
j\leqslant L
, and also that
x_{L+c_{L}+1}>L
. Therefore, writing
r:=c_{L}+L
for brevity, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{m}}

\displaystyle F(x)\prod_{j=1}^{L}1_{\beta_{x}(j)=c_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{m}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i}\in(j-1,j]\;\mbox{\scriptsize for}\\
j-1+c_{j-1}<i\leqslant j+c_{j},\\
L<x_{r+1}\leqslant m+r+1\end{subarray}}F(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})h(m+r+1-x_{r+1})1_{x_{1}<\dots<x_{r}}e^{-x_{r+1}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}x_{r+1}

\displaystyle=e^{-L}h(m+c_{L})\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i}\in(j-1,j]\;\mbox{\scriptsize for}\\
j-1+c_{j-1}<i\leqslant j+c_{j}\end{subarray}}F(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})1_{x_{1}<\dots<x_{r}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}x_{r}

\displaystyle=e^{-L}h(m+c_{L})\prod_{j=1}^{L}\frac{1}{(c_{j}-c_{j-1}+1)!}\mathbb{E}F(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta),

where
\beta
is any walk with
\beta(j)=c_{j}
for
j\leqslant L
(it does not matter which). Here, to get the middle step we integrated out the
x_{r+1}
variable and applied
Equation

16.1
. This is exactly

\omega_{m}\{\beta:\beta(j)=c_{j}\;\mbox{for $j=1,\dots,L$}\}\mathbb{E}F(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta),

where here we used the definition
Equation

8.6
, recalling that the increments of
\bm{\beta}
are
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
. Summing over all choices of
c_{1},\dots,c_{L}
gives the result.
∎

16.2.
Description of the measure
\mu

We turn now to the main analysis and proof of
Proposition

1.3
. The argument will proceed as follows. In
Proposition

15.5
, we have already characterised
\mu
as the BL-limit of the measures
\mu^{(\ell)}
defined in
Equation

15.16
. We have

(16.3)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

Here, and for the rest of the section,
c=-\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}
. Note that
Equation

16.3
differs slightly in appearance from
Equation

15.16
; we have replaced the condition
(\min\beta=-m)\cap(\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell)
by
\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m
, which is a seemingly weaker condition. However there is no contribution to
Equation

16.3
from any walks with
\min\beta<-m
, since
\omega_{m}
is supported on walks with
\min\beta\geqslant-m
, so the two definitions are equivalent.

Define
\mu^{(\ell)}_{*}
by

(16.4)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{(\ell)}_{*}:=\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}\mathbb{E}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)),

where
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)
is defined as in
Equation

1.3
. To prove
Proposition

1.3
, it is enough to show that
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{(\ell)};\mu^{(\ell)}_{*}]}=o_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}(1)
. We will do this in steps, specifying intermediate measures
\mu_{i}^{(\ell)}
,
i=0,1,\dots,5
, with
\mu_{0}^{(\ell)}:=\mu^{(\ell)}
and
\mu_{5}^{(\ell)}:=\mu_{*}^{(\ell)}
, and show that
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{(\ell)}_{i};\mu^{(\ell)}_{i-1}]}=o_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}(1)
for
i=1,\dots,5
; an application of the triangle inequality then concludes the argument. For the remainder of the analysis,
\psi
denotes an arbitrary function on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
with
\|\psi\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant 1
. Then our task is to prove that

(16.5)

\big|\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}^{(\ell)}-\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i-1}^{(\ell)}\big|=o_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}(1),

uniformly in
i\in\{1,\dots,5\}
and
\psi
.

Step 1.
First we compare
\mu^{(\ell)}
to the measure
\mu_{1}^{(\ell)}
in which we restrict the integral in
Equation

16.3
to walks
\beta
which are suitably bounded, positive and for which
m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
. More precisely, define
\mu_{1}^{(\ell)}
by

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}^{(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m\\
R(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}\\
T(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{1/8}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

We claim that
Equation

16.5
holds for
i=1
, with a bound of
\ll\ell^{-\eta/32}
on the RHS. To prove this claim, we first restrict
Equation

16.3
to
m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
. The error in doing this has already been handled via the first estimate in
Equation

15.21
. The rest of the error comes from the contribution of
\beta
with

\min\beta=-m
,
m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
, and
\argmin\beta\leqslant\ell
but either
R(\beta)>\ell^{\eta_{0}}
or
T(\beta)>\ell^{1/8}
; that these contributions are appropriately small follows in exactly the same way we bounded the second sum in
Equation

15.21
and the sum in
Equation

15.22
.

Step 2.
To obtain
\mu_{2}^{(\ell)}
from
\mu_{1}^{(\ell)}
we replace the two copies of
\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
by
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
(which is defined in
Equation

7.4
). Note that for
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
to be defined we need
Equation

7.2
to be satisfied, that is to say
\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}(i+\beta(i))=\infty
. This will be the case for all walks under consideration here, which (since we can restrict to the support of
\omega_{m}
) have
\min\beta=-m
. Moreover, by the definition of
T
-positive walk and the fact that
T(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{1/8}
, we have
-\ell^{1/8}+i^{1/4}\leqslant\beta(i)\leqslant\ell^{1/8}+i^{3/4}
. Therefore the quantity
\ell^{\prime}
, defined to be minimal such that
\ell^{\prime}+\beta(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
, will satisfy

(16.6)

\ell^{\prime}\asymp\ell.

We thus define
\mu_{2}^{(\ell)}
by

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}^{(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m\\
R(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}\\
T(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{1/8}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

By
Equation

15.18
, and since
\omega_{m}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m})=h(m)\ll 1+m
, in order to establish
Equation

16.5
with
i=2
it is sufficient to show

(16.7)

\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\big|^{c}\ll\ell^{-10},

uniformly for
\beta
with
T(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{1/8}
. However from
Equations

7.16
and
7.17
, Cauchy-Schwarz, and the triangle inequality, for such walks
\beta
we have

\mathbb{E}\big|\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)-\varrho^{*}_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\big|\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{1/4})}.

Here we used
Equation

16.6
in the application of
Equation

7.17
. The required statement
Equation

16.7
then follows using Hölder’s inequality.

Step 3.
To obtain
\mu_{3}^{(\ell)}
from
\mu_{2}^{(\ell)}
we remove the constraints
R(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
and
T(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{1/8}
again. However we retain the constraint
\beta(\ell)\geqslant 0
(which is implied by
T(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{1/8}
, since
\beta(\ell)\geqslant-\ell^{1/8}+\ell^{1/2-\eta}>0
). Thus we define

(16.8)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{3}^{(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m\\
\beta(\ell)\geqslant 0\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

To establish
Equation

16.5
with
i=3
, the argument is similar to the case
i=1
. Bounding
\psi
pointwise by
1
, it suffices to bound

(16.9)

\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m\\
R(\beta)>\ell^{\eta_{0}}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)

and

(16.10)

\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m\\
R(\beta)\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}\\
T(\beta)>\ell^{1/8}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta).

For
Equation

16.9
we use
Equation

7.7
and
Corollary

8.13
;
Equation

16.9
may then be bounded exactly as in
Equation

15.23
, and is seen to be
\ll\ell^{-10}
. For
Equation

16.10
, we note that we have an exact analogue of
Equation

15.10
for
\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)
, namely

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min\beta=-m\\
R(\beta)=r\\
T(\beta)>T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{m}(\beta)\ll r^{O(1)}(1+m)^{O(1)}T^{-\eta/2}.

The proof is the same, using the other estimates in
Lemma

7.2
. We can then proceed exactly as in the estimation of
Equation

15.22
. This concludes the proof that
Equation

16.5
holds for
i=3
.

Using
Lemma

16.3
, we can rewrite the definition
Equation

16.8
of
\mu_{3}^{(\ell)}
. For a sequence
x
, denote by
S_{m}
the condition that
\#(x\cap[0,i])\geqslant i-m
for
i\leqslant\ell
, or equivalently
x_{i-m}\leqslant i
for all
m<i\leqslant\ell
, and
\tilde{S}
the condition that
\#(x\cap[0,\ell])\geqslant\ell
, or equivalently
x_{\ell}\leqslant\ell
. (These definitions also depend on
\ell
, but this is fixed through the argument so we suppress this.) We include
S_{-1}
in the definition, with
S_{-1}
being the empty set.

Now (deterministically in
\mathbf{u}
conditioned to
\bm{\beta}=\beta
) we have that
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)\geqslant-m
iff
\#((\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)\cap[0,i])\geqslant i-m
for
i\leqslant\ell
, which is so if and only if
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)\in S_{m}
. Therefore we have (deterministically)

(16.11)

1_{S_{m}\setminus S_{m-1}}(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)=1_{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m}.

Also (again deterministically) we have that
\beta(\ell)\geqslant 0
iff
\#((\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)\cap[0,\ell])\geqslant\ell
, which is so if and only if
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)\in\tilde{S}
. Therefore

(16.12)

1_{\tilde{S}}(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)=1_{\beta(\ell)\geqslant 0}.

Thus, if for
m\geqslant 0
we define

(16.13)

F_{m}(x):=\varrho_{x}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho_{x}(\ell))1_{S_{m}\setminus S_{m-1}}(x)1_{\tilde{S}}(x)

(with
\varrho_{x}(\ell)
defined as in
Equation

1.3
) and apply
Lemma

16.3
, then it follows using
Equation

16.8
and
Equations

16.11
and
16.12
that we have

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{3}^{(\ell)}=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int F_{m}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{m}(x).

Since
F_{m}
depends only on
x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}
we can write in the definition
Equation

16.2
of
\nu_{m}
, obtaining

\displaystyle\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{3}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int F_{m}(x)h(m+\ell-x_{\ell})e^{-x_{\ell}}1_{0<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\ell}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{\ell}

(16.14)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\mathbb{E}F_{m}(\mathbf{u})h(m+\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell}).

Here, as usual,
\mathbf{u}
is the upper process (a rate
1
Poisson process on
[0,\infty)
).

Step 4.
For this step, we replace
h(m+\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})
in
Equation

16.14
by
(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}
, that is we define

(16.15)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{4}^{(\ell)}=\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\mathbb{E}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}F_{m}(\mathbf{u}).

For this we recall that
h(x)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}(x+1)^{+}
, which implies that

h(m+\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})=O(m+1)+\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}.

(Here consider the cases
\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell}\geqslant 0
and
\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell}<0
separately; in the latter case, the LHS is zero unless
m+\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell}\in(-1,m]
).

Thus, to show
Equation

16.5
with
i=4
, it is enough to show that the contribution from the
O(m+1)
term is small. Since
\eta_{0}\lll\frac{1}{10}
, it therefore suffices to show

(16.16)

\mathbb{E}|F_{m}(\mathbf{u})|\ll(1+m)^{2}\ell^{-1/2}

for all
m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
.
We prove this by conditioning on the embedded walk
\bm{\beta}
of
\mathbf{u}
. The average on the LHS of
Equation

16.16
is

\int\mathbb{E}|F_{m}(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\beta),

where
\mathbb{P}
denotes measure on the path space of
\bm{\beta}
. By the definition
Equation

16.13
of
F_{m}
and
Equations

16.11
and
16.12
(and since
|\psi|\leqslant 1
), this is

\ll\int_{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m,\beta(\ell)\geqslant 0}\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\beta).

By
Equation

7.7
(noting here that
\beta(\ell)\geqslant 0
gives
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell
), this is

\ll\int_{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m}R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\beta)=\sum_{r}r^{2}\mathbb{P}(R_{\leqslant\ell}(\bm{\beta})=r,\;\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m).

By
Lemma

8.12
, this is

\ll\ell^{-1/2}\sum_{r}r^{2}(m+r+1)e^{-r}\ll(1+m)^{2}\ell^{-1/2},

which is exactly
Equation

16.16
. This completes the analysis of the case
i=4
.

Step 5.
For the final step, we begin with the claim that if we extend the sum over
m
in
Equation

16.15
to all
m\geqslant 0
then we obtain
\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{5}^{(\ell)}=\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{*}^{(\ell)}
, where
\mu_{*}^{(\ell)}
is defined in
Equation

16.4
. Indeed, from the definition
Equation

16.13
of
F_{m}
and a telescoping sum we have

\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\mathbb{E}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}F_{m}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbb{E}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}}(\ell))1_{\tilde{S}}(\mathbf{u}).

However, the cutoff
1_{\tilde{S}}(\mathbf{u})
is equivalent to
1_{\mathbf{u}_{\ell}\leqslant\ell}
and so is redundant, and this establishes the claim.

Therefore to prove
Equation

16.5
when
i=5
, it is enough to show that

\sum_{m>\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\mathbb{E}(\ell-\mathbf{u}_{\ell})^{+}F_{m}(\mathbf{u})=o_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}(1).

Once again we sample using the embedded walk. By the definition
Equation

16.13
of
F_{m}
and
Equations

16.11
and
16.12
, it is enough to bound

(16.17)

\sum_{m>\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=-m\\
\beta(\ell)\geqslant 0\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}(\ell-(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)_{\ell})^{+}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\beta).

Here, for clarity we note that
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)_{\ell}
is the
\ell
th element of
(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)
when listed in increasing order.
Recall that, in the context of
Lemma

7.2
,
\ell^{\prime}
is defined to be minimal so that
\ell^{\prime}+\beta(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
. Since
\beta(\ell)\geqslant 0
, we have
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell
. Since all steps of
\beta
are
\geqslant-1
, for any
s\geqslant 0
we have
\beta(\ell^{\prime}+s)\geqslant\ell-\ell^{\prime}-s
, and so in particular
\min_{\ell^{\prime}\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)\geqslant 0
, and hence
\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta(i)=\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}}\beta(i)
.

Denote by
R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)
the least integer
R
for which
\beta
is
R
-bounded to length
\ell
. We use the bounds
Equations

7.7
and
7.8
. Since
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell
, these give
\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\ll R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)^{2}
and
\mathbb{P}(\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0)\ll\ell^{3}2^{-m}
respectively. By Hölder’s inequality,

\mathbb{E}\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)^{c}\leqslant R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)^{2c}\mathbb{P}(\varrho_{\mathbf{u}\mid\beta}(\ell)\neq 0)^{1-c}\leqslant R_{\leqslant\ell}(\beta)^{2}\ell^{2}2^{-m/3}.

Using the trivial bound
(\ell-(\mathbf{u}\mid\beta)_{\ell})^{+}\leqslant\ell
,
Equation

16.17
is bounded by

\ell^{3}\sum_{m>\ell^{\eta_{0}}}2^{-m/3}\sum_{r}r^{2}\mathbb{P}\big(R_{\leqslant\ell}(\bm{\beta})=r,\,\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big).

By
Lemma

8.12
, this is bounded by

\ll\ell^{3}\sum_{m\geqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}2^{-m/3}\sum_{r}r^{2}(m+r+1)e^{-r}\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{\eta_{0}})}.

This completes the proof of
Equation

16.5
for
i=5
, and thus the whole proof of
Proposition

1.3
.

17.
Description of
\mu^{\prime}

In this section we establish
Proposition

1.4
, which is a description of the measure
\mu^{\prime}
. Recall that we have currently identified
\mu^{\prime}
as the bounded Lipschitz limit of the measures
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}
, defined in
Equation

15.24
, as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
. Throughout this section,
\ell
will denote a large integer parameter. The reader may also want to recall the definition of the lower process
\mathbf{x}
, defined just before
Proposition

1.4
, and the conditioned lower process
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
, which is described in
Section

5.3
. Throughout the section we will assume that
\beta^{\prime}
is a walk with increments
\xi^{\prime}_{i}
bounded above by
1
for which
Equation

6.2
holds. Given
\ell
and
\beta^{\prime}
, we denote by
\ell^{\prime}
the least integer such that
\ell^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
, as in the statement of
Corollary

6.3
. In some cases where
\beta^{\prime}
is not clear from context, we will write this as
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})
(which of course still depends on
\ell
).

17.1.
The first
\ell
arrivals

In this subsection we assemble some ingredients for the main argument, having to do with the distribution of the first
\ell
arrivals of
(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})
.

Lemma 17.1
.

We have the following statements.

(1)

\beta^{\prime}(\ell)>0
if and only if
\ell^{\prime}>\ell
;

(2)

The distribution of
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
depends only on the values of
\beta^{\prime}(1),\dots,\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})
;

(3)

We have
\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}=\ell+\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})+O(1+|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}|)
.

Proof.

(1) Indeed if
\beta^{\prime}(\ell)>0
then
\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)<\ell
and so, since
i-\beta^{\prime}(i)
is a non-decreasing function of
i
, we have
\ell^{\prime}>\ell
. Conversely if
\ell^{\prime}>\ell
then
\ell
does not satisfy
\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell)\geqslant\ell
, which means
\beta^{\prime}(\ell)>0
.

To prepare for (2) and (3) we begin by reviewing the coupling of the lower process
\mathbf{x}
to the lower walk
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
, as described in
Section

5.3
. The key features of the coupling are as follows. There is an auxiliary rate
1
Poisson process
\mathbf{s}
such that
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=i-\#(\mathbf{s}\cap[0,i])
, and the conditioned process
(\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime}):=(\mathbf{s}\mid\bm{\beta}^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime})
consists of sampling
1-\xi^{\prime}_{i}
uniform random elements from
(i-1,i]
, which are then ordered. We then define
\mathbf{x}=\phi(\mathbf{s})
, where
\phi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is defined in
Definition

5.1
. In particular to establish (2) it is enough to show that the distribution of the first
\ell
elements
(\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,\ell
, depends only on the values
\beta^{\prime}(1),\dots,\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})
. This statement is essentially the purpose of
\ell^{\prime}
. Indeed, since
\#((\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime})\cap[0,i])=i-\beta^{\prime}(i)
, we see that (deterministically)

(17.1)

\ell^{\prime}-1<(\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}\leqslant\ell^{\prime},

and (2) then follows.

Finally we turn to (3). Recall that by
Equation

5.4
we have
\log_{2}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{s}+O(1)
. Thus to prove (3) it suffices to show that

(17.2)

(\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}=\ell+\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})+O(1+|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}|).

From the definition of
\ell^{\prime}
we have

(17.3)

(\ell^{\prime}-1)-\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime}-1)\leqslant\ell-1.

Using
Equations

17.1
and
17.3
(and
\ell^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
) one may check that

\beta(\ell^{\prime})-1\leqslant(\mathbf{s}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell\leqslant\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime}-1),

and so (since
\beta^{\prime}(\ell)=\beta(\ell^{\prime}-1)+\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}
) the desired statement
Equation

17.2
follows immediately.
∎

Recall that the embedded walk
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
has
1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)
steps. The following lemma records that
\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})
is usually
O(\ell)
.

Lemma 17.2
.

Suppose that
j\geqslant 1
is an integer. Then we have

\mathbb{P}(2^{j}\ell\leqslant\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})<2^{j+1}\ell)\ll 2^{j}\ell e^{-2^{j-4}\ell}.

Proof.

Suppose that
\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime}-1)<\ell^{\prime}/2
. Then, since
\ell-1\geqslant(\ell^{\prime}-1)-\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime}-1)>\ell^{\prime}/2-1
, we have
\ell^{\prime}<2\ell
. Thus if
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})\geqslant 2^{j}\ell
then
\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime}-1)\geqslant\ell^{\prime}/2>(\ell^{\prime}-1)/2
.

Now for any fixed
t
we have
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(t)\geqslant t/2)=\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(t)\leqslant t/2)\leqslant e^{-t/8}
by the first estimate in
[
4
, Theorem A.1.15]
. Thus if
j\geqslant 1
then

\mathbb{P}(2^{j}\ell\leqslant\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})<2^{j+1}\ell)\leqslant\sum_{2^{j}\ell-1\leqslant t<2^{j+1}\ell-1}\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(t)\geqslant t/2)\ll 2^{j}\ell e^{-2^{j-4}\ell}.\qed

17.2.
The main argument

Recall from
Proposition

15.6
that
\mu^{\prime}
is the bounded Lipschitz limit of the measures
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}
defined by

(17.4)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\int_{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m}\mathbb{E}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m}(\beta^{\prime}).

Here,
\omega^{\prime}_{m}
is defined as in
Section

8.1
, and
c=-\frac{\log\log 2}{\log 2}
as usual. Note that walks
\beta^{\prime}
in the support of
\omega^{\prime}_{m}
are lower, that is to say they have steps bounded above by
1
rather than below by
-1
.

Define a measure
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{*}
by

(17.5)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{*}:=(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}\mathbb{E}(\log_{2}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}}(\ell)).

Proposition

1.4
is equivalent to the statement that
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{*}\rightarrow\mu^{\prime}
in bounded Lipschitz norm.

As in the last section, we will prove this by specifying intermediate measures
\mu_{i}^{\prime(\ell)}
,
i=0,1,\dots,5
, with
\mu_{0}^{\prime(\ell)}:=\mu^{\prime(\ell)}
and
\mu_{5}^{\prime(\ell)}:=\mu_{*}^{\prime(\ell)}
, and show that

(17.6)

{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{i};\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{i-1}]}=o_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}(1)

for
i=1,\dots,5
, and this is enough to complete the proof.

Step 1.
In what follows we let
\psi
be an arbitrary function with
\|\psi\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant 1
. First we replace
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}
by
\mu_{1}^{\prime(\ell)}
in which we restrict the integral in
Equation

17.4
to walks
\beta^{\prime}
which are suitably bounded, positive and for which
m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
. More precisely, define
\mu_{1}^{\prime(\ell)}
by

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}^{\prime(\ell)}:=\sum_{0\leqslant m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
R(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}\\
T(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant\ell^{1/8}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m}(\beta^{\prime}).

That
Equation

17.6
holds for
i=1
follows in essentially the same way as before.

Step 2.
To obtain
\mu_{2}^{\prime(\ell)}
from
\mu_{1}^{\prime(\ell)}
we replace the two copies of
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
by
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
, where here the relevant definitions are
Equations

6.1
and
6.3
. Thus we define
\mu_{2}^{\prime(\ell)}
by

(17.7)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}^{\prime(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
R(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}\\
T(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant\ell^{1/8}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m}(\beta^{\prime}).

By
Equation

15.18
, and since
\omega^{\prime}_{m}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}_{\geqslant-m})=h^{\prime}(m)\ll 1+m
, it is sufficient to show

(17.8)

\mathbb{E}\big|\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)-\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\big|^{c}\ll\ell^{-10}.

Now since
\beta^{\prime}
is
\ell^{1/8}
-positive, we have
\beta^{\prime}(\ell)\geqslant\ell^{1/2-\eta}-\ell^{1/8}>0
. Therefore by
Equation

6.4
and the monotonicity property
Equation

6.5
we have
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)=\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell-\beta^{\prime}(\ell))\geqslant\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
. We also have
\beta^{\prime}(2\ell)\leqslant(2\ell)^{1/8}+(2\ell)^{1/2+\eta}<\ell
, and so by further applications of
Equation

6.4
and
Equation

6.5
we have
\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(2\ell)=\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(2\ell-\beta^{\prime}(2\ell))\leqslant\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
. Combining these facts and then applying
Lemma

6.4
and a telescoping sum, we have

\mathbb{E}|\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)-\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)|\leqslant\mathbb{E}|\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(2\ell)-\tau^{*}_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)|\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{1/4})}.

The required bound
Equation

17.8
then follows from Hölder’s inequality.

Step 3.
To obtain
\mu_{3}^{\prime(\ell)}
from
\mu_{2}^{\prime(\ell)}
we remove the constraints
R(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
and
T(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant\ell^{1/8}
again. However we retain the constraint
\beta^{\prime}(\ell)>0
(which is implied by
T(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant\ell^{1/8}
as noted above).

Thus we define

(17.9)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{3}^{\prime(\ell)}:=\sum_{0\leqslant m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
\beta^{\prime}(\ell)>0\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m}(\beta^{\prime}).

The proof that
Equation

17.6
holds with
i=3
is very similar to, but easier than, the handling of Step 3 in the last section. Bounding
\psi
and
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}
pointwise by
1
, it suffices to bound

\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\omega^{\prime}_{m}\{\beta^{\prime}:R(\beta^{\prime})>\ell^{\eta_{0}}\}\quad\mbox{and}\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\omega^{\prime}_{m}\{\beta^{\prime}:T(\beta^{\prime})>\ell^{1/8}\}

That these are bounded by
o_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}(1)
follows using
Corollary

8.13
and
Corollary

8.16
respectively, assuming that
\eta_{0}
is sufficiently small.

Now, as explained at the start of the proof of
Lemma

17.1
, the constraint
\beta^{\prime}(\ell)>0
is equivalent to
\ell^{\prime}>\ell
. Thus we may replace
Equation

17.9
by

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{3}^{\prime(\ell)}:=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{t>\ell}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{m}(\beta^{\prime}).

Here, recall that we have written
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})
for
\ell^{\prime}
to emphasise that this quantity depends on
\beta^{\prime}
.
Observe that the conditions
\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m
and
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t
depend only on
\beta^{\prime}(1),\dots,\beta^{\prime}(t)
(since
t\geqslant\ell
), and so too does the integrand (since
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
is involves only the first
\ell
elements of
\mathbf{x}
, and the number of elements of
\mathbf{x}
up to
t
is
t-\beta^{\prime}(t)\geqslant\ell
). Recalling the definition (see
Section

8.1
) of
\omega^{\prime}_{m}
, and in particular
Equation

8.9
, it follows that

(17.10)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{3}^{\prime(\ell)}=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{t>\ell}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant t}\beta^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m\\
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t\end{subarray}}h^{\prime}(m+\beta^{\prime}(t))\mathbb{E}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}),

where here
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
denotes measure on the path space of
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.
Note that from
Equation

17.3
we have
\beta^{\prime}(t-1)\geqslant t-\ell
. Since all steps of
\beta^{\prime}
are bounded above by
1
, it follows that
\beta^{\prime}(t-i)\geqslant t-\ell-(i-1)
for all
i\geqslant 1
, and in particular
\min_{\ell\leqslant i\leqslant t-1}\beta^{\prime}(i)\geqslant 1
. Therefore we may replace
Equation

17.10
by

(17.11)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{3}^{\prime(\ell)}=\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{t}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t\end{subarray}}1_{t>\ell}1_{\beta^{\prime}(t)\geqslant-m}h^{\prime}(m+\beta^{\prime}(t))\mathbb{E}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}).

Step 4.
We use the fact that

(17.12)

h^{\prime}(r)=(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}r+O(1)

for
r\geqslant 0
, which is
Proposition

8.1
(2).
Suppose that
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t
and
\beta^{\prime}(t)\geqslant-m
. Then from
Equation

17.12
and
Lemma

17.1
(3) we have

(17.13)

h^{\prime}(m+\beta^{\prime}(t))=(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}\big(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell\big)+O(m+1+|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|).

Suppose that
\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}<\ell
. Then from
Lemma

17.1
we have
\beta^{\prime}(t)\leqslant O(1+|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|)
and so by
Equation

17.12

h^{\prime}(m+\beta^{\prime}(t))=O(1+m+|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|)
.
Thus we may replace
Equation

17.13
by

h^{\prime}(m+\beta^{\prime}(t))=(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}+O(1+m+|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|).

Next we multiply by the cutoff
1_{t>\ell}1_{\beta^{\prime}(t)\geqslant-m}
appearing in
Equation

17.11
; we claim that (still assuming
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t
)

(17.14)

1_{t>\ell}1_{\beta^{\prime}(t)\geqslant-m}h^{\prime}(m+\beta^{\prime}(t))=(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}+O(1+m+|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|).

This is trivial if
t>\ell
and
\beta^{\prime}(t)\geqslant-m
, so suppose that either
t\leqslant\ell
, or that
\beta^{\prime}(t)<-m
. In either case, the LHS is zero. In both cases,
\beta^{\prime}(t)\leqslant 0
(in the first case this is since
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t
and so
t-\beta^{\prime}(t)\geqslant\ell
by the definition of
\ell^{\prime}
). It follows from
Lemma

17.1
(3) that
((\log_{2}\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}=O(1+|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|)
, so the claim follows in this case also.

Now we define
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{4}
itself. We set

(17.15)

\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{4}=(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m}\mathbb{E}(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}).

This is the same as
Equation

17.11
except that we have replaced
1_{t>\ell}1_{\beta^{\prime}(t)\geqslant-m}h^{\prime}(m+\beta^{\prime}(t))
by the main term in
Equation

17.14
; note that we have removed the sum over
t
, since nothing on the right in
Equation

17.15
references this variable.

To prove
Equation

17.6
in the case
i=4
, we must handle the contribution from the error term in
Equation

17.14
. We split this error term into two parts: the contribution from
O(1+m)
and the contribution from
O(|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|)
. These terms are, by definition,

(17.16)

\ll\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}(1+m)\sum_{t}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})

and

(17.17)

\ll\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{t}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t\end{subarray}}|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|\mathbb{E}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}).

Bounding
Equation

17.16
.
We bound the
\tau
and
\psi
terms trivially by
1
; thus this contribution is

\ll\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}(1+m)\sum_{t}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t\end{subarray}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})\ll\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}(1+m)\mathbb{P}(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m)

From the definition
Equation

8.2
and the fact that
h^{\prime}(m)\ll 1+m
, this is
\ll\ell^{3\eta_{0}-1/2}
, which is acceptable.

Bounding
Equation

17.17
.
Again we bound the
\tau
and
\psi
terms trivially by
1
, so the contribution is

(17.18)

\ll\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{t}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m\\
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})=t\end{subarray}}|\xi^{\prime}_{t}|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})\ll\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{t}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=-m,\,\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=t,\,|\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{t}|>s\big).

We divide our sum over
t
dyadically. For the sum over
t\leqslant 2\ell
we have the upper bound

\ll\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=-m,\;\sup_{i\leqslant 2\ell}|\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}|>s).

The contribution from
s\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
is
\ll\ell^{\eta_{0}}\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)=-m)\ll\ell^{3\eta_{0}-1/2}
, as before. The contribution from
s>\ell^{\eta_{0}}
is
\ll\ell^{O(1)}\sum_{s>\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\mathbb{P}(|1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)|>s)<\ell^{-10}
.

Turning now to the higher dyadic ranges of
t
in
Equation

17.18
, we drop the
\min
condition completely and bound this above by

\sum_{m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\sum_{j\geqslant 1}\sum_{2^{j}\ell\leqslant t<2^{j+1}\ell}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=t,|\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{t}|>s\big).

The contribution from
s>2^{j}\ell
is bounded by

\ll\ell^{O(1)}\sum_{j\geqslant 1}2^{j}\sum_{s>2^{j}\ell}\mathbb{P}(|1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)|>s),

which is negligible. The contribution from
s\leqslant 2^{j}\ell
is

\ll\ell^{O(1)}\sum_{j\geqslant 1}2^{j}\mathbb{P}(2^{j}\ell\leqslant\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})\leqslant 2^{j+1}\ell),

which is negligible by
Lemma

17.2
.

Putting these estimates together completes the proof of
Equation

17.6
in the case
i=4
.

Step 5.
Note that to obtain
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{5}=\mu_{*}^{\prime(\ell)}
from
\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{4}
we just need to drop the restriction
m\leqslant\ell^{\eta_{0}}
in
Equation

17.15
. Indeed, this then gives an unrestricted integral

(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}\int\mathbb{E}(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\psi(\log_{2}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}),

which is equal to
\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime(\ell)}_{*}
as defined in
Equation

17.5
by removing the conditioning on
\beta^{\prime}
.
To estimate the difference between
\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{4}^{\prime(\ell)}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{5}^{\prime(\ell)}
, by bounding the
\psi
term trivially by
1
it is sufficient to bound

(17.19)

\sum_{m>\ell^{\eta_{0}}}\int_{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m}\mathbb{E}(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}).

Clearly, we may restrict the integral to those
\beta^{\prime}
for which
\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}\geqslant\ell
for some instance of
\mathbf{x}
. By
Lemma

17.1
(3) this implies that
\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant-O(1+|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}|)
. From the definition of
\ell^{\prime}
it follows that

(17.20)

\ell^{\prime}\geqslant\ell+\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell-O(1+|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}|).

We now distinguish two cases: Case 1 (the typical case) is that
\min_{i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1}\beta^{\prime}(i)\leqslant-m/2
. Case 2 (the exceptional case) is that
\min_{i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1}\beta^{\prime}(i)>-m/2
(but still
\min_{i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m
).

Case 1.
For
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)
we use the bound
\ll R\cdot(\ell^{\prime})^{\kappa}2^{-m/2}
, which follows from
Corollary

6.3
. Here
R
is a (minimal) positive integer parameter for which
|\xi^{\prime}_{i}|\leqslant Ri^{\kappa}
for all
i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1
, where (as usual)
\ell^{\prime}
is minimal so that
\ell^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime})\geqslant\ell
. Suppose that
\min_{i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1}\xi^{\prime}_{i}=-t
. Then, since all
\xi^{\prime}_{i}
are
\leqslant 1
, we have
\beta^{\prime}(\ell^{\prime}-1)\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-2-t
. By
Equation

17.3
, it follows that
t\leqslant\ell-2
, and so we can take
R:=\ell
. We thus have the bound

(17.21)

\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\ll\ell\ell^{\prime}2^{-m/2}.

For the
(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}
term we can use crude bounds. By
Lemma

17.1
(3) and the fact that the steps of
\beta^{\prime}
are bounded above by
1
we have

(17.22)

(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime})_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}\ll\ell^{\prime}+|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}|.

Combining
Equations

17.21
and
17.22
(crudely) gives

(\log_{2}(\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}))_{\ell}-\ell)^{+}\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)^{c}\ll\ell 2^{-cm/2}((\ell^{\prime})^{2}+\ell^{\prime}|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}|)\ll\ell 2^{-cm/2}((\ell^{\prime})^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}|^{2}).

It follows that the contribution of Case 1 to
Equation

17.19
is bounded by

(17.23)

\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{\eta_{0}})}\int\big(\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})}|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}).

We have
\int\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})\ll\ell^{2}
; to prove this we partition into dyadic ranges according to the value of
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})
, using the trivial bound
\ll\ell^{2}
for the range
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant 2\ell
, and
Lemma

17.2
to show that the contribution from the higher dyadic ranges
2^{j}\ell\leqslant\ell^{\prime}<2^{j+1}\ell
,
j\geqslant 1
, is negligible.

For the second term in
Equation

17.23
, we have

\int|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant\ell+\sum_{j\geqslant 1}\sum_{2^{j}\ell\leqslant t<2^{j+1}\ell}\sum_{s}s\mathbb{P}(\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})=t,|\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{t}|>s),

where the first term is the contribution from
\ell^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})\leqslant 2\ell
, which is bounded above by
\sum_{i\leqslant 2\ell}\mathbb{E}|\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}\ll\ell
. Consider the contribution to the sum from some fixed
j\geqslant 1
. The contribution from
s>2^{j}\ell
is

\ll 2^{j}\ell\sum_{s>2^{j}\ell}s\mathbb{P}(|1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)|>s)\ll 2^{j}\ell e^{-2^{j}\ell}.

The contribution from
s\leqslant 2^{j}\ell
is

\ll(2^{j}\ell)^{2}\mathbb{P}(2^{j}\ell\leqslant\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime})<2^{j+1}\ell)\ll\ell^{3}2^{3j}e^{-2^{j-4}\ell},

by
Lemma

17.2
. Summed over
j
, we get a tiny contribution of
\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell)}
in both cases.

It follows from this analysis that
Equation

17.23
is bounded by
\ll e^{-\Omega(\ell^{\eta_{0}})}
, and hence that the contribution of Case 1 to
Equation

17.19
is similarly bounded.

Case 2.
We remind the reader that this is the case that
\min_{i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}-1}\beta^{\prime}(i)>-m/2
and
\min_{i\leqslant\ell}\beta^{\prime}(i)=-m
. This implies that
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell
and that
\sum_{i=\ell^{\prime}}^{\ell}(1+|\xi^{\prime}_{i}|)>m/2
. Therefore either (i)
\ell-\ell^{\prime}\gg\sqrt{m}
, or (ii) there is some
i
,
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
, with
|\xi^{\prime}_{i}|\gg\sqrt{m}
. If (i) holds then, by
Equation

17.20
, we have
|\xi^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}|\gg\sqrt{m}
, and so in both cases we have
\max_{\ell^{\prime}\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}|\xi^{\prime}_{i}|\gg\sqrt{m}
. Since the sum in
Equation

17.19
is restricted to
m>\ell^{\eta_{0}}
, this implies
\max_{i\leqslant\ell}|\xi^{\prime}_{i}|\gg\ell^{\eta_{0}/2}
.

To bound the contribution of Case 2 to
Equation

17.19
, we use
Equation

17.22
, the fact that
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell
in Case 2, and the trivial bound
\tau_{\mathbf{x}\mid\beta^{\prime}}(\ell)\leqslant 1
, to bound the contribution by

\ll\sum_{u\gg\ell^{\eta_{0}/2}}(\ell+u)\mathbb{P}\big(\max_{i\leqslant\ell}|\xi^{\prime}_{i}|=u\big)\leqslant\ell\sum_{u\gg\ell^{\eta_{0}/2}}(\ell+u)\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(1)=u+1)<\ell^{-10}.

Thus, the contribution to
Equation

17.19
from Case 2 is minuscule.

Cases 1 and 2 together establish
Equation

17.6
in the case
i=5
, and this completes the proof of
Proposition

1.4
.

Appendices

Appendix A
Almost positive random walks

In this appendix we give the proofs of some results in
Section

8
. The main references for this material are various parts of Feller
[
18
]
. We also benefitted from consulting
[
3
, Section 2]
.

As in the main part of the paper, we consider a random walk
\bm{\beta}(N)=\bm{\xi}_{1}+\cdots+\bm{\xi}_{N}
and a complementary random walk
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(N)=\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{N}
, where
\bm{\xi}_{i}\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
and
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}_{i}\overset{d}{=}1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)
. Much of the material under discussion here is, however, valid much more generally.

A.1.
Positive random walks

In this subsection
n
is a dummy variable. The first task is to understand
h(m)
and
h^{\prime}(m)
for
m\in\{-1,0\}
. Important ingredients in the analysis are the following identities of Sparre Andersen
[
36
]
(subsequently generalised by Spitzer
[
37
]
). Denote
p_{n}:=\mathbb{P}(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\bm{\beta}(i)>0)
and
\tilde{p}_{n}:=\mathbb{P}(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0)
. Then

(A.1)

f(z):=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}p_{n}z^{n}=\exp\big(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n}\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)>0)\big)

and

(A.2)

\tilde{f}(z):=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\tilde{p}_{n}z^{n}=\exp\big(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n}\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)\geqslant 0)\big).

A good source for the proof is
[
18
, XII. 7]
. Since the
\bm{\xi}_{i}
have mean zero it makes sense to work with the following consequences of
Equations

A.1
and
A.2
:

f(z)(1-z)^{1/2}=\exp\big(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{n}z^{n}\big)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{f}(z)(1-z)^{1/2}=\exp\big(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\alpha}_{n}z^{n}\big),

where here

\alpha_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\big(\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)>0)-\frac{1}{2}\big)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{\alpha}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\big(\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)\geqslant 0)-\frac{1}{2}\big)

Applying a Tauberian theorem
[
18
, XII. 8, Theorem 1a]
we obtain

(A.3)

p_{n}\sim e^{\alpha}(\pi n)^{-1/2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{p}_{n}\sim e^{\tilde{\alpha}}(\pi n)^{-1/2}.

where
\sim
means ‘asymptotic to’ and

(A.4)

\alpha:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\big(\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)>0)-\frac{1}{2}\big)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{\alpha}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\big(\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)\geqslant 0)-\frac{1}{2}\big)

In particular, from
Equation

A.3
we see that
h(-1)
and
h(0)
exist and that

(A.5)

h(-1)=e^{\alpha}\pi^{-1/2},\quad h(0)=e^{\tilde{\alpha}}\pi^{-1/2}.

Similar statements hold for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
and the associated probabilities
p^{\prime}_{n},\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{n}
. In particular

(A.6)

h^{\prime}(-1)=e^{\alpha^{\prime}}\pi^{-1/2},\quad h^{\prime}(0)=e^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}}\pi^{-1/2},

where
\alpha^{\prime},\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
are defined analogously with reference to
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
. Observe that

(A.7)

\alpha=-\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime},\qquad\tilde{\alpha}=-\alpha^{\prime}.

We now recall a result of Spitzer
[
38
, Theorem 3.4]
(see also
[
18
, XVIII.5, Theorem 1]
). This states the following. Let
Z_{+}
be the first strict ascending ladder height of
\bm{\beta}
, that is to say
\bm{\beta}(\tau_{+})
where
\tau_{+}:=\inf\{i\geqslant 1:\bm{\beta}(i)>0\}
is the first strict ascending ladder epoch of
\bm{\beta}
. (The use of the letter
\tau
here is unrelated to that in
Section

6
.) Define
Z^{\prime}_{+}
analogously with reference to
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
. Define also
\tilde{\tau}_{+}:=\inf\{i\geqslant 1:\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0\}
and
\tilde{Z}_{+}:=\bm{\beta}(\tilde{\tau}_{+})
, and similarly for
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}_{+}
.

Then Spitzer’s result is that

(A.8)
