\mathbb{E}Z_{+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{-\alpha},\quad\mathbb{E}\tilde{Z}^{\prime}_{+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{\alpha},\quad\mathbb{E}Z^{\prime}_{+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{-\alpha^{\prime}},\quad\mathbb{E}\tilde{Z}_{+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{\alpha^{\prime}}.

Since the steps of
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
are
1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)
variables bounded above by
1
, we have
Z^{\prime}_{+}=1
deterministically and therefore

(A.9)

\alpha^{\prime}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\log 2.

One may also observe the identity

(A.10)

\tilde{\alpha}-\alpha=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)=0)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{n-1}e^{-n}}{n!}=1,

which goes back to Borel
[
7
]
and is equivalent to the fact that the Borel distribution with parameter
1
is well-defined.

Relations
Equations

A.5
,
A.6
,
A.7
,
A.8
,
A.9
, and
A.10
allow for a complete evaluation of all the constants above, and one may easily check that

\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}\log 2-1,\quad\tilde{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{2}\log 2,\quad\alpha^{\prime}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\log 2,\quad\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}=1-\tfrac{1}{2}\log 2

and thus that
\mathbb{E}Z_{+}=e/2
and

(A.11)

h(-1)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}e^{-1},\quad h(0)=(2/\pi)^{1/2},\quad h^{\prime}(-1)=1/\sqrt{2\pi},\quad h^{\prime}(0)=e/\sqrt{2\pi}.

Remarks
.

Most of the above discussion holds with minor modification for arbitrary random walks with i.i.d. steps with mean zero and variance 1. The identities
Equations

A.1
and
A.2
make critical use of the i.i.d. assumption. From
Equation

A.9
onwards we made critical use of the fact that
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
is ‘skip-free’, that is to say has increments bounded above by
1
. In the general skip-free case the analogue of
Equation

A.10
is that the RHS is
-\log\mathbb{P}(\bm{\xi}^{\prime}=1)
(we omit the proof of this fact, which is not needed in the paper).

A.2.
Renewal theorem

In the proof of
Proposition

8.1
we will also require some basic renewal theory. In particular we will need
Proposition

A.2
below, which is a quantitative version of the renewal theorem in a particular setting. The result is essentially due to Gel’fond
[
23
]
but we give a self-contained proof (closely related to that of Gel’fond) in our setting.

Consider a random variable
X
with the following properties:

(1)

X
takes values in
\{1,2,\dots\}
;

(2)

\mathbb{P}(X=1)>0
;

(3)

We have
\mathbb{E}(1+\delta_{0})^{X}<\infty
for some
\delta_{0}>0
.

The key example for us will be when
X
is the strict ascending ladder height
Z_{+}
of the walk
\bm{\beta}
, as defined above. With this example, (1) above is obvious. Item (2) is also obvious;
\mathbb{P}(X=1)\geqslant\mathbb{P}(\bm{\xi}_{1}=1)
. Finally,
\mathbb{P}(X=j)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\bm{\xi}\geqslant j)
which decays faster than exponential, so in this case we have (3) for all
\delta_{0}
.

Write
p_{j}=\mathbb{P}(X=j)
for brevity in what follows. Consider the moment generating function
f(z):=\mathbb{E}z^{X}
; this will be holomorphic in
|z|<1+\delta_{0}
.

Lemma A.1
.

There is some
0\leqslant\delta_{1}<\delta_{0}
such that the only zero of
f(z)-1
in
|z|\leqslant 1+\delta_{1}
is a simple zero at
z=1
.

Proof.

If
|z|<1
we have
|f(z)|<1
and so there are no zeros of
f(z)-1
with
|z|<1
. By a compactness argument it suffices to check that the only zero of
f(z)-1
with
|z|=1
is at
z=1
, and that it is simple. Suppose that
z=e^{i\theta}
. Then

\operatorname{Re}f(z)=\sum_{j}p_{j}\cos(j\theta)\leqslant\sum_{j\neq 1}p_{j}+p_{1}\cos\theta=1+p_{1}(\cos\theta-1).

Since
p_{1}>0
, this is
<1
unless
\cos\theta=1
, that is to say
\theta
is a multiple of
2\pi
. Thus the only zero of
f(z)-1
on
|z|=1
is at
z=1
. We have
f^{\prime}(1)=\mathbb{E}X>0
and so the zero at
z=1
is simple.
∎

Remark
.

The use of a compactness argument means that
\delta_{1}
is ineffective. In the particular case corresponding to the walk
\bm{\beta}
, an effective
\delta_{1}
could be obtained without undue difficulty if required.

Proposition A.2
.

Suppose that
X
satisfies
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
above for some
\delta_{0}
. Let
\delta_{1}
be as in the conclusion of
Lemma

A.1
, and set
\lambda:=\mathbb{E}X
. Then uniformly for integers
m>0
we have

\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{1}+\cdots+X_{r}=m)=\frac{1}{\lambda}+O\big((1+\delta_{1})^{-m}\big).

Proof.

We have

\mathbb{P}(X_{1}+\cdots+X_{r}=m)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{|z|=1/2}f(z)^{r}z^{-m-1}dz.

Note that for uniformly for
|z|=\frac{1}{2}
we have
|f(z)|\leqslant\mathbb{P}(X=0)+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(X>0)<1
. Summing over
r
gives

\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{1}+\cdots+X_{r}=m)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{|z|=1/2}\frac{f(z)}{1-f(z)}z^{-m-1}dz.

Now move the contour to
|z|=1+\delta_{1}
. In doing this we pick up a contribution of
1/\lambda
from the pole of the integrand at
z=1
. There are no other poles by
Lemma

A.1
. It follows that

\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{1}+\cdots+X_{r}=m)=\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{|z|=1+\delta_{1}}\frac{f(z)}{1-f(z)}z^{-m-1}dz.

The result follows since
\frac{f(z)}{1-f(z)}
is uniformly bounded on
|z|=1+\delta_{1}
, and
|z|^{-m}=(1+\delta_{1})^{-m}
.
∎

A.3.
Proof of
Proposition

8.1
and
Lemma

8.4

We are now ready to prove the main statements needed in the paper concerning the existence of
h(m),h^{\prime}(m)
and the asymptotic behaviour of these quantities. We mostly consider the (harder) case of
h^{\prime}
, indicating how the analysis simplifices in the much simpler case of
h
as we go along.

We first give a reference for the proof of
Proposition

8.1
(3), or more precisely of
Equation

8.3
, since
Equation

8.4
follows immediately from it. This is the statement that

(A.12)

N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big),\;N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m\big)\asymp m+1,

uniformly for
0\leqslant m\leqslant\sqrt{N}
. For
m=0
this is immediate from the existence of
h(0)
and
h^{\prime}(0)
. For
1\leqslant m\leqslant\sqrt{N}
, items (i) (upper bound) and (iii) (lower bound) of
[
31
, Lemma 3.3]
give the result.

Remark
.

As noted in
[
31
]
, the upper bound is due to Kozlov
[
30
, Theorem A, equation (13)]
; however the proof in
[
31
]
is different and easier. The lower bound is due to Zhang
[
40
, Lemma 1]
. Finally we observe that the upper bound in
Equation

A.12
(that is, the LHS is
\ll m+1
) holds without any restriction on
m
for trivial reasons.

We now sketch the proof of
Lemma

8.4
following
[
1
]
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.4
(sketch).

Recall that this is the statement that
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 0)\ll\min\big(N^{-1},(1+x)N^{-3/2}\big)
. The argument in
[
1
]
goes essentially as follows: consider the first, last and middle thirds of the walk up to
N
, with the last third of the walk reversed. The first third must be nonnegative to time
\lfloor N/3\rfloor
, an event with probability
\ll N^{-1/2}
by the existence of
h(0)
. The final third (reversed) must be bounded below by
-x
, an event with probability
\ll\min\big(1,(1+x)N^{-1/2}\big)
by
Equation

A.12
. Conditioned on the first and last thirds, the middle third of the walk sums to some fixed value, and the probability of this is
\ll N^{-1/2}
by a result of Kesten (which in our particular case is an easy computation, see
Equation

B.5
below).
∎

Before turning to the proof of items (1) and (2) of
Proposition

8.1
, we prove the following (standard) auxiliary estimate on strict ascending ladder times.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\tau_{+}:=\inf\{i\geqslant 1:\bm{\beta}(i)>0\}
be the first strict ascending ladder epoch of
\bm{\beta}
. We have
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{+}=t)\ll t^{-3/2}
uniformly in
t
. An analgous estimate holds for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We may assume
t\geqslant 2
. Couple
\bm{\beta}
to a copy of
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
via
\bm{\beta}(i)=-\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)
. Then
\tau_{+}=t
is precisely the event that
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant 0
and
\bm{\beta}(t)>0
, the probability of which is

\sum_{x\geqslant 0}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(t-1)=x,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant 0\big)\mathbb{P}(\bm{\xi}_{t}>x).

By
Lemma

8.4
, the first probability is
\ll(1+x)t^{-3/2}
. Since
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\xi}_{t}>x)\ll(1+x)^{-3}
, the result follows.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

8.1
(1) and (2).

We have already established the cases
m=-1,0
in
Section

A.1
, so we assume
m\geqslant 1
in what follows. We first give the proof for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}
, which is harder. Couple
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
to a copy of
\bm{\beta}
via
\bm{\beta}=-\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
. Let
N
be a large positive integer (
N>m^{20}
will do).

Associated to
\bm{\beta}
are the strict ascending ladder epochs
T_{1},T_{2},\dots
, defined by
T_{j}=\inf\{i:\bm{\beta}(i)>\bm{\beta}(T_{j-1})\}
, and the strict ascending ladder heights
H_{j}:=\bm{\beta}(T_{j})
. The variable
T_{j}
is distributed as
\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{j}
where the
\tau_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of the first strict ascending ladder epoch
\tau_{+}
and
H_{j}
is distributed as
X_{1}+\cdots+X_{j}
where the
X_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of the first strict ascending ladder height
Z_{+}=\bm{\beta}(\tau_{+})
. Then, foliating according to
r
, the number of ladder epochs up to time
N
, we have

(A.13)

\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\leqslant m\big)=\sum_{r=0}^{m}\sum_{t=0}^{N}\mathbb{P}(T_{r}=t,H_{r}\leqslant m)\mathbb{P}(\tau_{+}>N-t).

Here we adopt the convention that
T_{0}=H_{0}=0
deterministically. Note also that the number of ladder epochs is bounded above by
m
.

We assemble various facts. First, by the existence of
h^{\prime}(0)
we have for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
that

(A.14)

\mathbb{P}(\tau_{+}>\ell)=\mathbb{P}\big(\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\bm{\beta}(i)\leqslant 0\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant 0\big)=(h^{\prime}(0)+o(1))\ell^{-1/2}.

Next note that uniformly for
1\leqslant r\leqslant m
and
t\geqslant 1
we have

(A.15)

\mathbb{P}(T_{r}=t)\leqslant r\sup_{x\geqslant t/r}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{+}=x)\ll r^{5/2}t^{-3/2}\ll m^{5/2}t^{-3/2},

where here we used
Lemma

A.3
.

We first estimate the contribution to
Equation

A.13
from
t\geqslant\sqrt{N}
. For this we use
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{+}>N-t)\ll(N+1-t)^{-1/2}
(a consequence of
Equation

A.14
) and
Equation

A.15
, and we ignore the
H_{r}\leqslant m
condition. This gives a bound of

\ll m^{7/2}\sum_{\sqrt{N}<t\leqslant N}t^{-3/2}(N+1-t)^{-1/2}\ll m^{7/2}N^{-3/4}=o(N^{1/2}).

In the remaining range
t\leqslant\sqrt{N}
in
Equation

A.13
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{+}>N-t)=(h^{\prime}(0)+o(1))N^{-1/2}
by
Equation

A.14
. Putting these facts together we obtain

\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m\big)=(h^{\prime}(0)+o(1))N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{m}\sum_{t\leqslant\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{P}(T_{r}=t,H_{r}\leqslant m)+o(N^{-1/2}).

We can add back in the sum over
t>\sqrt{N}
, at a cost of a further error of
o(N^{-1/2})
, by further use of
Equation

A.15
. The sum over
t
then becomes redundant and we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}(i)\geqslant-m\big)

\displaystyle=(h^{\prime}(0)+o(1))N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{m}\mathbb{P}(H_{r}\leqslant m)+o(N^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=(h^{\prime}(0)+o(1))N^{-1/2}\big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}(X_{1}+\cdots+X_{r}\leqslant m)\big)+o(N^{-1/2}).

By
Proposition

A.2
it now follows that
h^{\prime}(m)
exists and that

(A.16)

h^{\prime}(m)=h^{\prime}(0)\big(\frac{m}{\mathbb{E}Z_{+}}+O(1)\big).

By
Equations

A.6
,
A.7
, and
A.8
we have
h^{\prime}(0)/\mathbb{E}Z_{+}=(2/\pi)^{1/2}
. Together with
Equation

A.16
, this completes the proof of
Proposition

8.1
(1) and (2) in the case of
h^{\prime}
.

The analysis of
h(m)
is much easier. By exactly the same argument with the roles of
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
switched, we obtain

\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)=(h(0)+o(1))N^{-1/2}\big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}(X^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+X^{\prime}_{r}\leqslant m)\big)+o(N^{-1/2}).

Here,
X^{\prime}_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of
Z^{\prime}_{+}
, the first strict ascending ladder height of
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
. However
Z^{\prime}_{+}=1
deterministically, and so we obtain
h(m)=h(0)(m+1)
. By
Equation

A.11
we have
h(0)=(2/\pi)^{1/2}
and this completes the proof of
Proposition

8.1
(1) and (2) in the case of
h
.∎

A.4.
Local limit theorems

In this subsection we give the proof of
Proposition

8.8
. The main ingredient of the proof will be (a weakening of) a result of Denisov, Tarasov and Wachtel
[
11
]
, which we quote as
Theorem

A.5
below; we will give a self-contained proof of that in
Appendix

B
. The proofs of
Equations

8.18
and
8.19
are identical (we will not use the fact that
h(m)
is exactly
(2/\pi)^{1/2}(m+1)
for
m\geqslant 0
, which does not hold for
h^{\prime}(m)
), so we establish only
Equation

8.18
.

Recall that

h(-1):=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant 1\big)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{1/2}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)>0\big).

We showed in
Equation

A.11
that
\tilde{h}(-1)
exists and

(A.17)

\tilde{h}(-1)=e^{\alpha}\pi^{-1/2}=(2/\pi)^{1/2}e^{-1},

with
\alpha
defined as in
Equation

A.4
. We need the following auxiliary lemma.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
m\geqslant 0
be an integer. We have

h(m)-h(m-1)=h(-1)\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\bm{\beta}(q)=-m\big).

Proof.

Let
N
be some very large positive integer. By summing over the position
q
of the last visit to
-m
up to time
N
we have

(A.18)

\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)=\sum_{q=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\bm{\beta}(q)=-m\big)\cdot\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-q}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant 1\big)

where
\bm{\gamma}
is the shifted walk
\bm{\gamma}(i):=\bm{\beta}(q+i)+m
, and the second probability term on the right is absent when
q=N
. The contribution to the sum from
q\geqslant N^{1/2}
is, using
Equation

8.14
and the existence of
h(-1)
,

\ll(1+m)\sum_{q>N^{1/2}}q^{-3/2}(N+1-q)^{-1/2}\ll(1+m)N^{-3/4}.

By the existence of
h(-1)
again, uniformly for
q\leqslant N^{1/2}
we have
\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-q}\bm{\gamma}(i)\geqslant 1\big)=(h(-1)+o(1))N^{-1/2}
. Thus
Equation

A.18
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)=(h(-1)+o(1))N^{-1/2}\sum_{q=1}^{N^{1/2}}\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}\bm{\beta}(i)

\displaystyle=-m,\;\bm{\beta}(q)=-m\big)

\displaystyle+O\big((1+m)N^{-3/4}\big).

Since

\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-m\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-(m-1)\big),

multiplying by
N^{1/2}
and sending
N
to infinity gives the result.
∎

Now we quote the main external input to the proof of
Proposition

8.8
(which is essentially the case
m=-1
of the result). As previously mentioned, it is a weak version of a result of Denisov, Tarasov and Wachtel
[
11
, Theorem 1]
.

Theorem A.5
.

There is a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that the following holds. Uniformly for positive integers
N,x
we have

(A.19)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)>0\big)=N^{-1}h(-1)W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O(N^{-1-\varepsilon}).

Proof.

See
Appendix

B
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

8.8
.

We prove
Equation

8.18
; the proof of
Equation

8.19
is identical.
Theorem

A.5
is the case
m=-1
, so we assume that
m\geqslant 0
in what follows. We may assume
N
sufficiently large throughout. Set
\varepsilon_{0}:=\min(\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\frac{1}{10})
, where
\varepsilon
is as in
Theorem

A.5
. We remind the reader of the desired statement, which, replacing the dummy variable
m
by
M
, is that uniformly for
M,x\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)\geqslant-M\big)=N^{-1}h(M)W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O\big((1+M)^{O(1)}N^{-1-\varepsilon_{0}}\big).

We may assume throughout that
M\leqslant N^{\varepsilon_{0}}
, since otherwise the result has no content by choosing a suitably large
O(1)
exponent.
By a telescoping sum argument, it suffices to show

(A.20)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m\big)=N^{-1}(h(m)-h(m-1))W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O\big((1+m)N^{-1-\varepsilon_{0}}\big)

uniformly for
0\leqslant m\leqslant N^{\varepsilon_{0}}
; to see this, sum
Equation

A.20
over
m=0,1,2\dots,M
together with
Equation

A.19
.

Henceforth we focus on
Equation

A.20
. If
x>N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{0}}
then both the LHS and the first term on the RHS of
Equation

A.20
are
\ll N^{-10}
; for the LHS this follows by dropping the
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m
condition and using the large deviation bound
Lemma

F.3
to bound
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N)=x)
, while for the first term on the RHS we use the bound
h(m)-h(m-1)=O(1)
, which follows from
Proposition

8.1
(2).

Suppose from now on that
x\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{0}}
. We foliate the event on the LHS of
Equation

A.20
according to the largest value of
q
,
1\leqslant q\leqslant N
, such that
\bm{\beta}(q)=-m
. Set
Q:=\lfloor N^{2\varepsilon_{0}}\rfloor
. The contribution from
Q<q\leqslant N
is, using
Equation

8.14
, bounded above by

\displaystyle\sum_{Q<q\leqslant N}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\;\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\bm{\beta}(q)=-m\big)

\displaystyle\ll(1+m)\sum_{Q<q\leqslant N}q^{-3/2}(N+1-q)^{-1}

\displaystyle\ll(1+m)\big(\frac{1}{Q^{1/2}N}+\frac{\log N}{N^{3/2}}\big).

This may be absorbed into the error term in
Equation

A.20
due to the choice of
Q
.
The remaining contribution to the LHS of
Equation

A.20
may be written as
\sum_{q=1}^{Q}p_{1}(q)p_{2}(q)
where

p_{1}(q):=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\gamma}(N-q)=x+m,\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-q}\bm{\gamma}(i)>0\big),\;\quad p_{2}(q):=\mathbb{P}\big(\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}\bm{\beta}(i)=-m,\;\bm{\beta}(q)=-m\big),

and where
\bm{\gamma}(i)=\bm{\beta}(q+i)
is the shifted walk, which is a
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
walk independent of
(\bm{\beta}(i))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}
. Thus to complete the proof of
Equation

A.20
our task is to show, assuming
x\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{0}}
, that

(A.21)

\sum_{q=1}^{Q}p_{1}(q)p_{2}(q)=N^{-1}(h(m)-h(m-1))W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O\big((1+m)N^{-1-\varepsilon_{0}}\big).

We first dispense with the case
x<N^{1/2-\varepsilon_{0}}
. By
Lemma

8.4
,
p_{1}(q)\ll(1+x+m)N^{-3/2}\ll N^{-1-\varepsilon_{0}}
, and by
Equation

8.10
, independent of any assumption on
x
we have

(A.22)

p_{2}(q)\ll(1+m)q^{-3/2}.

Thus
\sum_{q=1}^{Q}p_{1}(q)p_{2}(q)\ll(1+m)N^{-1-\varepsilon_{0}}
, which is the size of the error term in
Equation

A.21
. Using
Proposition

8.1
(2), the term
N^{-1}(h(m)-h(m-1))W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}})
is bounded by
\ll N^{-1-\varepsilon_{0}}
, which may also be absorbed into the error term in
Equation

A.21
. Thus
Equation

A.21
holds in this regime.

For the remainder of the proof we assume
N^{1/2-\varepsilon_{0}}\leqslant x\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon_{0}}
. We apply
Theorem

A.5
with
\tilde{x}:=x+m
and
\tilde{N}:=N-q
. This application of
Theorem

A.5
implies that if
1\leqslant q\leqslant Q
, then

p_{1}(q)=(N-q)^{-1}h(-1)W\big(\frac{x+m}{\sqrt{N-q}}\big)+O(N^{-1-\varepsilon}).

We have
\frac{x+m}{\sqrt{N-q}}=\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}+O(N^{\varepsilon_{0}-1/2})
and so, since
W^{\prime}
is uniformly bounded,
W\big(\frac{x+m}{\sqrt{N-q}}\big)=W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O(N^{\varepsilon_{0}-1/2})
. Also
(N-q)^{-1}=N^{-1}+O(QN^{-2})=N^{-1}+O(N^{2\varepsilon_{0}-2})
. Since
\varepsilon_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{10}
, we see that

(A.23)

p_{1}(q)=N^{-1}h(-1)W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O(N^{-1-\varepsilon}).

Using
Lemma

A.4
, it follows that

\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}p_{1}(q)p_{2}(q)=N^{-1}(h(m)-h(m-1))W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O(N^{-1-\varepsilon})\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}p_{2}(q),

and hence that

\sum_{q=1}^{Q}p_{1}(q)p_{2}(q)=N^{-1}(h(m)-h(m-1))W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O(N^{-1-\varepsilon})\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}p_{2}(q)+O\big(\sum_{q>Q}p_{1}(q)p_{2}(q)\big).

By
Equation

A.22
the second term here is
\ll(1+m)N^{-1-\varepsilon}
, which may be absorbed into the error term in
Equation

A.21
. Finally, by
Lemma

8.4
(or
Equation

A.23
) we have
p_{1}(q)\ll N^{-1}
, and from this and
Equation

A.22
we see that
\sum_{q>Q}p_{1}(q)p_{2}(q)\ll(1+m)N^{-1}Q^{-1/2}
, which may again be absorbed into the error term in
Equation

A.21
since
Q=\lfloor N^{2\varepsilon_{0}}\rfloor
.
∎

Appendix B
On results of Denisov, Tarasov and Wachtel

In this appendix we give a more-or-less self-contained proof of
Theorem

A.5
. This result (and in fact a stronger statement) follows essentially immediately from
[
11
, Theorem 1]
in the case
r=1
, specialised to our situation. However, that reference is rather lengthy and technical (as befits the authors’ aims of great precision and generality) and moreover depends on the earlier paper
[
13
]
, which has similar properties.

The argument we give here is much shorter and is in part very closely based on
[
12
]
. Our need for less precision, as well as the fact that our random walks have specific rather nicely-behaved increments, is what allows for a much more compact treatment.

Throughout this appendix, we work with the walk
\bm{\beta}
with
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
steps, but we write the argument such that it would also work for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
with only trivial changes. In particular we never rely on the fact that the steps of
\bm{\beta}
are bounded below by
-1
.

B.1.
Berry-Esséen Theorem for positive random walks

The main result of this section,
Proposition

B.1
, as well as the proof, are exactly that of
[
12
]
except we can remove a technical smoothing device (as we have a discrete random variable) and we do not need to carefully track explicit constants. Additionally, we remark on how one could, if desired, replace appeals to some of the literature on general random walks with computations using Stirling’s formula.

Denote

(B.1)

\tau:=\inf\{i\geqslant 1:\bm{\beta}(i)\leqslant 0\}.

Observe that, with the notation of the previous appendix,
\tau=\tilde{\tau}^{\prime}_{+}
, and so in particular

(B.2)

|\bm{\beta}(\tau)|=\tilde{Z}^{\prime}_{+}.

Here is the main result of this subsection.

Proposition B.1
.

Uniformly for integers
N\geqslant 1
and
x>0
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\geqslant x,\;\tau>N\big)=\tilde{h}(0)N^{-1/2}e^{-x^{2}/2N}+O(N^{-1}).

Before giving the proof, we begin by assembling some preliminaries. Define

(B.3)

p(N,x):=\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N)=x)=\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Pois}(N)=N+x).

Lemma B.2
.

Uniformly for integers
N\geqslant 1
and
x
, we have

(B.4)

p(N,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi N}}e^{-x^{2}/2N}+O(N^{-1}).

In particular

(B.5)

p(N,x)\ll N^{-1/2}

uniformly.
We have

(B.6)

\sum_{x}\big|p(N,x)-p(N,x+\ell)\big|\ll|\ell|N^{-1/2},

uniformly in
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Uniformly for
0\leqslant k\leqslant N/2
and all
x
we have

(B.7)

\big|p(N-k,x)-p(N,x)\big|\ll kN^{-3/2}+N^{-1}.

Finally, for
-N\leqslant x\leqslant N
we have

(B.8)

p(N,x)\leqslant e^{-x^{2}/4N}.

Proof.

Equation

B.4
is an instance of the local limit theorem for which the standard general reference is the book of Petrov
[
32
]
. In our specific case we can compute explicitly. In the range
|x|\leqslant N^{2/3}
this can be handled by Stirling’s formula in the form
m!=(m/e)^{m}(2\pi m)^{1/2}(1+O(1/m))
and a computation. The tails
|x|\geqslant N^{2/3}
follow from the large deviation estimate
Lemma

F.3
.
Equation

B.5
follows immediately from
Equation

B.4
.

For
Equation

B.6
, by telescoping it suffices to handle the case
\ell=1
. We compute that
p(N,x+1)=p(N,x)\frac{N}{N+x+1}
, so in particular
p(N,x)
is monotonic decreasing for
x=0,1,2,\dots
and also for
x=-1,-2,\dots
, with
p(N,0)=p(N,-1)
. By telescoping, the LHS in
Equation

B.6
(with
\ell=1
) is simply
2p(N,0)-p(N,-N)
, and the stated bound follows from
Equation

B.4
.

The inequality
Equation

B.7
follows from
Equation

B.4
, the inequality
\sup_{x}\big|e^{-x^{2}/2n}-e^{-x^{2}/2(n-1)}\big|\ll n^{-1}
(for
n\geqslant 2
) and a telescoping sum; for details see
[
12
, Equation (32)]
.

Finally, the large deviation estimate
Equation

B.8
follows from
Lemma

F.3
, where a reference is given.
∎

Corollary B.3
.

Let
N\geqslant 1
and let
x,y
be integers with
y\geqslant 0
. Let
k
,
0\leqslant k\leqslant N/2
, be an integer. Uniformly in these parameters we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N-k)\in(x,x+y]\cup(-x,-x+y]\big)=\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}\frac{y}{\sqrt{N}}e^{-x^{2}/2N}+O\big(\frac{y^{2}}{N}+\frac{ky}{N^{3/2}}\big).

Proof.

The result is trivial when
y=0
, so assume
y\geqslant 1
. Using
Equation

B.7
one may reduce to handling the case
k=0
. This in turn follows from
Equation

B.4
using the mean value estimate
\big|e^{-x^{2}/2N}-e^{(x+i)^{2}/2N}\big|\ll iN^{-1/2}
.
∎

Recall the definition
Equation

B.1
of
\tau
.

Lemma B.4
.

Uniformly for
N\geqslant 1
and
y\geqslant 0
we have the bound

(B.9)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=-y,\;\tau=N\big)\ll N^{-3/2}(1+y)^{-10}.

In particular

(B.10)

\mathbb{P}(\tau=N)\ll N^{-3/2}.

Proof.

Equation

B.10
is an immediate consequence of
Equation

B.9
. The result is clear when
N=1
, so suppose
N\geqslant 2
. To prove
Equation

B.9
we use
Lemma

8.4
(and the fact that
\{\tau=N\}
is contained in the event
\{\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N-1}\bm{\beta}(i)>0\}
), which gives
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N-1)=z,\;\tau=N)\ll zN^{-3/2}
, uniformly for
z>0
. This gives

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=-y,\;\tau=N\big)\ll N^{-3/2}\sum_{z>0}z\mathbb{P}(\bm{\xi}^{\prime}=y+z)\ll N^{-3/2}(1+y)^{-10}.\qed

We record one more preliminary lemma. Let
x\geqslant 0
and
N\geqslant 1
be integers. Denote

F_{N}(x):=\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N)\geqslant x)-\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N)\leqslant-x).

Lemma B.5
.

We have the following bounds:

(1)

F_{N}(x)\ll N^{-1/2}
uniformly;

(2)

|F_{N}(x)-F_{N-k}(x)|\ll kN^{-3/2}
uniformly for integer
k
with
0\leqslant k<N/2
.

Proof.

(1) follows from the Berry-Esséen theorem. (2) By telescoping it suffices to check this for
k=1
. This is done using characteristic functions in
[
12
, Lemma 10]
. Both parts could also be done directly using Stirling’s formula.
∎

We are now ready for the proof of the main result.

Proof of
Proposition

B.1
.

Let
x>0
. By considering the time
k\geqslant 1
at which the walk is first non-positive, we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\geqslant x,\,\tau>N\big)=\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N)\geqslant x)-\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(k)=-y,\,\tau=k\big)\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N-k)\geqslant x+y).

(Note that since
x>0
, we cannot have
\tau=N
.)
Similarly we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\leqslant-x,\,\tau>N)=\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N)\leqslant-x)

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(k)=-y,\,\tau=k\big)\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(N-k)\leqslant-x+y)-\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\leqslant-x,\,\tau=N\big).

Subtracting the above two expressions and rearranging gives the identity

(B.11)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\geqslant x,\,\tau>N\big)=E_{1}-E_{2}+E_{3}-E_{4},

where

E_{1}=F_{N}(x)\mathbb{P}(\tau\geqslant N),\qquad E_{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\mathbb{P}(\tau=k)\big(F_{N-k}(x)-F_{N}(x)\big),

E_{3}=\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(k)=-y,\tau=k)\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N-k)\in[x,x+y)\cup(-x,-x+y]\big),

and
E_{4}=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\leqslant-x,\,\tau=N\big)
.
We have
E_{1}\ll N^{-1}
by
Lemma

B.5
(1) and
Equation

B.10
. To estimate
E_{2}
, we split into
1\leqslant k<N/2
and
N/2\leqslant k\leqslant N-1
. In the first range we use
Lemma

B.5
(2) and
Equation

B.10
, obtaining a bound
\ll N^{-3/2}\sum_{1\leqslant k<N/2}k^{-1/2}\ll N^{-1}
. In the second range we use
Lemma

B.5
(1),
Equation

B.10
and the triangle inequality, obtaining a bound
\ll\sum_{N/2\leqslant k\leqslant N-1}k^{-3/2}(N-k)^{-1/2}\ll N^{-1}
. Immediately from
Equation

B.10
we have the bound
E_{4}\ll N^{-3/2}
.

From this discussion and
Equation

B.11
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\geqslant x,\,\tau>N\big)=E_{3}+O(N^{-1}).

The analysis of
E_{3}
, which provides the main term in the asymptotic, is the remaining task.
We first bound the contribution from
N/2<k\leqslant N-1
. Using
Equations

B.5
and
B.9
, this contribution is

\ll\sum_{N/2<k\leqslant N-1}(N-k)^{-1/2}\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}y\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(k)=-y,\,\tau=k\big)\ll\sum_{N/2<k\leqslant N-1}(N-k)^{-1/2}k^{-3/2}\ll N^{-1}.

For the remaining terms (with
k\leqslant N/2
) in
E_{3}
we use
Corollary

B.3
. We first bound the contribution of the error terms there. Using
Equation

B.9
, this is

\ll\sum_{k\leqslant N/2}\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(k)=-y,\tau=k)\big(\frac{y^{2}}{N}+\frac{ky}{N^{3/2}}\big)\ll\sum_{k\leqslant N/2}k^{-3/2}\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}(1+y)^{-10}\big(\frac{y^{2}}{N}+\frac{ky}{N^{3/2}}\big)\ll N^{-1}.

Finally we come to the contribution from the main terms in
Corollary

B.3
, which is

\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}e^{-x^{2}/2N}\sum_{k\leqslant N/2}\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}y\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(k)=-y,\,\tau=k\big).

By
Equation

B.9
we can complete the sum over
k
back out to
k=\infty
at a cost of
O(N^{-1})
. The remaining expression is then

\big(\frac{2}{\pi}\big)^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}e^{-x^{2}/2N}\mathbb{E}|\bm{\beta}(\tau)|.

To conclude the proof of
Proposition

B.1
we need the fact that
(\frac{2}{\pi})^{1/2}\mathbb{E}|\bm{\beta}(\tau)|=h(-1)
. This follows from
Equations

A.8
,
A.17
, and
B.2
.∎

B.2.
Local limit from global

We will now show that
Proposition

B.1
implies
Theorem

A.5
, the crucial local limit theorem for random walks conditioned to stay positive. Note that
Proposition

B.1
and the fact that the derivative of
f(x)=e^{-x^{2}/2N}
is
\ll N^{-1/2}
pointwise already gives the crude bound

(B.12)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\,\tau>N)=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\geqslant x,\,\tau>N)-\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)\geqslant x+1,\,\tau>N)\ll N^{-1}

uniformly in
x
; we will use this in the arguments.

Proof of
Theorem

A.5
.

Set
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{40}
.
We may assume
N
sufficiently large throughout. We first restrict to the case

(B.13)

N^{1/2-\varepsilon}\leqslant x\leqslant N^{1/2+\varepsilon}.

If
x>N^{1/2+\varepsilon}
then
Theorem

A.5
is essentially immediate from the large deviation estimate
Lemma

F.3
. If
x<N^{1/2-\varepsilon}
then
Theorem

A.5
follows from
Lemma

8.4
.

Henceforth, we assume that
Equation

B.13
holds. Set
L^{\prime}:=\lfloor N^{1/4}\rfloor
and
L:=\lfloor N^{3/4}\rfloor
(there is scope for flexibility in these parameters).
First observe that

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\,\tau>N\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\,\tau>N-L)+O(N^{-10}).

Indeed if
\tau\in(N-L,N]
then
\inf_{i\in(N-L,N]}\bm{\beta}(i)\leqslant 0
and so if
\bm{\beta}(N)=x
then
|\sum_{i=N-L+1}^{N}\bm{\xi}_{i}|\geqslant|x|\geqslant N^{1/2-\varepsilon}>L^{3/5}
, and the probability of this can be seen to be tiny using
Lemma

F.3
.

We have (with
p(\cdot,\cdot)
defined as in
Equation

B.3
)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N)=x,\,\tau>N-L)=\sum_{h}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N-L)=x-h,\,\tau>N-L\big)p(L,h).

We first replace the RHS here with

(B.14)

\sum_{h}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(N-L)=x-h,\,\tau>N-L\big)\frac{1}{L^{\prime}}\sum_{h^{\prime}\in[L^{\prime}]}p(L,h+h^{\prime}).

The error involved in doing this is, using
Equations

B.6
and
B.12
,

\ll\sup_{h^{\prime}\in[L^{\prime}]}\sum_{h}|p(L,h)-p(L,h+h^{\prime})|\cdot\sup_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(n-L)=x^{\prime},\,\tau>N-L\big)\ll\frac{L^{\prime}}{NL^{1/2}}\ll N^{-9/8}.

This is an acceptable error and so henceforth we may work with
Equation

B.14
. Substituting
u=h+h^{\prime}
and retaining the variables
u,h^{\prime}
gives the equivalent expression

\frac{1}{L^{\prime}}\sum_{u}p(L,u)\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{\beta}(n)\in x-u+[L^{\prime}],\,\tau>N-L\big).

Now we replace the right-hand probability term by the asymptotic from
Proposition

B.1
. The contribution of the
O(N^{-1})
error is

\ll N^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{L^{\prime}}\sum_{u}p(L,u)\ll\frac{1}{NL^{\prime}}\ll N^{-5/4},

which is acceptable. It remains to show that the contribution from the main term is given by the claimed asymptotic in
Theorem

A.5
, that is to say that

(B.15)

\frac{h(-1)}{(n-L)^{3/2}L^{\prime}}\sum_{u}p(L,u)\int^{x-u+L^{\prime}}_{x-u}te^{-t^{2}/2(n-L)}\,\mathrm{d}t=N^{-1}h(-1)W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big)+O(N^{-1-\varepsilon}).

The contribution from
|u|\leqslant L^{3/5}
is negligible using
Equation

B.8
. For
|u|\leqslant L^{3/5}\approx N^{9/20}
, since
x\geqslant N^{1/2-\varepsilon}
we have
x-u,x-u+L^{\prime}\asymp x
. The derivative of
te^{-t^{2}/(N-L)}
with respect to
t
in the range
[x-u,x-u+L^{\prime}]
is therefore
\ll 1+|x|^{2}/N\ll N^{2\varepsilon}
, and thus

\frac{1}{L^{\prime}}\int^{x-u+L^{\prime}}_{x-u}te^{-t^{2}/(N-L)}\,\mathrm{d}t=(x-u)e^{-(x-u)^{2}/2(N-L)}+O(N^{2\varepsilon}L^{\prime}).

When substituted into the LHS of
Equation

B.15
, the contribution of the error term is
\ll N^{-3/2+2\varepsilon}L^{\prime}\ll N^{-9/8}
. Thus we are left with a main contribution to the LHS of
Equation

B.15
of

(B.16)

\frac{h(-1)}{(N-L)^{3/2}}\sum_{|u|\leqslant L^{3/5}}p(L,u)(x-u)e^{-(x-u)^{2}/2(N-L)}.

We replace the first
x-u
with
x
. The error in doing this is

\ll N^{-3/2}\sum_{|u|\leqslant L^{3/5}}p(L,u)u\ll L^{3/5}N^{-3/2}\ll N^{-1-1/20},

which is acceptable. Thus we may replace
Equation

B.16
by

(B.17)

\frac{h(-1)x}{(N-L)^{3/2}}\sum_{|u|\leqslant L^{3/5}}p(L,u)e^{-(x-u)^{2}/2(N-L)}.

Now

\displaystyle\big(

\displaystyle\frac{N}{N-L}\big)^{3/2}e^{-(x-u)^{2}/2(N-L)}=e^{-x^{2}/2N}\Big(e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\big(\frac{1}{N-L}-\frac{1}{N}\big)+\frac{xu}{N-L}-\frac{u^{2}}{2(N-L)}}\Big)(1+O(L/N))

\displaystyle\qquad=e^{-x^{2}/2N}\Big(1+O\big(\frac{|x|^{2}L}{N^{2}}+\frac{|x|L^{3/5}}{N}+\frac{L^{6/5}}{N}\big)\Big)=e^{-x^{2}/2N}\big(1+O(N^{-1/40})\big).

Since
|xe^{-x^{2}/2N}|\ll N^{1/2}
, the total contribution of the
O(N^{-1/40})
error term to
Equation

B.17
is
\ll N^{-1-1/40}
, which is acceptable. Thus we may replace
Equation

B.17
in turn by

\big(\sum_{|u|\leqslant L^{3/5}}p(L,u)\big)\cdot h(-1)N^{-1}W\big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\big).

To complete the proof, all that remains is to extend the sum back over all
u
, which can be done at negligible cost by
Equation

B.8
.
∎

Appendix C
Fourier estimates

Lemma C.1
.

Let
R,N
be integer parameters with
R\leqslant N
, and
R
sufficiently large. Let
X
be sampled from
(N,N+R]
, with
\mathbb{P}(X=j)
being proportional to
1/j
for all
j
. Consider the characteristic function
\phi_{X}(\xi):=\mathbb{E}e^{i\xi X}
for
\xi\in[-\pi,\pi]
. Then we have the following estimates:

(1)

for
|\xi|\leqslant 16/R
we have
|\phi_{X}(\xi)|\leqslant 1-c\xi^{2}R^{2}
for some absolute
c>0
;

(2)

for
16/R\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant\pi
we have
|\phi_{X}(\xi)|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

(1) The following argument, given by ChatGPT Pro 5.4 (but shortened, rewritten and checked by us) is cleaner than the one we originally wrote. For
1\leqslant r\leqslant R
, write
p_{r}:=\mathbb{P}(X=N+r)
. Thus
p_{r}=\frac{1}{(N+r)L}
where
L:=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\frac{1}{N+r}
. We have
L\leqslant\frac{R}{N+1}
, thus
p_{r}\geqslant\frac{N+1}{R(N+r)}\geqslant\frac{1}{2R}
, using that
r\leqslant R\leqslant N
in the second inequality.
We have
|\phi_{X}(\xi)|=\big|\sum_{r=1}^{R}p_{r}e^{i\xi r}\big|
. Since
|\phi_{X}(\xi)|\leqslant 1
we have (after a short calculation)

1-|\phi_{X}(\xi)|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\big(1-|\phi_{X}(\xi)|^{2}\big)=2\sum_{1\leqslant r<s\leqslant R}p_{r}p_{s}\sin^{2}\big(\frac{(s-r)\xi}{2}\big)=2\sum_{h=1}^{R}\sin^{2}\big(\frac{h\xi}{2}\big)\sum_{1\leqslant r\leqslant R-h}p_{r}p_{r+h}.

For
h\leqslant R/2
we have

\sum_{1\leqslant r\leqslant R-h}p_{r}p_{r+h}\geqslant\frac{R-h}{4R^{2}}\geqslant\frac{1}{8R}.

Therefore

1-|\phi_{X}(\xi)|\geqslant\frac{1}{4R}\sum_{h=1}^{\lfloor R/2\rfloor}\sin^{2}\big(\frac{h\xi}{2}\big).

To bound this below, we use only the terms with
h\leqslant\pi R/16
. For these, using the fact that
\sin x\geqslant\frac{2}{\pi}x
for
0\leqslant x\leqslant\frac{\pi}{2}
, we have
\sin^{2}(h\xi/2)\gg h^{2}|\xi|^{2}
. The result follows.

(2) For this we use partial summation, noting that

L\phi_{X}(\xi)=\frac{1}{N+R}\sum_{j=N+1}^{N+R}e^{i\xi j}+\int^{N+R}_{N+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{2}}\Big(\sum_{j=N+1}^{t}e^{i\xi j}\Big).

From this and the geometric series bound
|\sum_{j\in I}e^{i\xi j}|\leqslant\pi|\xi|^{-1}
(for any interval
I
) we obtain
|\phi_{X}(\xi)|\leqslant\pi/(NL|\xi|)
and so, since
L\geqslant R/(N+R)\geqslant R/2N
,
|\phi_{X}(\xi)|\leqslant 2\pi/R|\xi|
. The result follows.
∎

Appendix D
Various real-variable inequalities

Lemma D.1
.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\geqslant 0
be real. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\big|\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\big)^{\ell}-\ell!\sum_{1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}\leqslant n}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}\big|\leqslant\ell(\ell-1)(\max_{i}x_{i})\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\big)^{\ell-1}.

Proof.

By expanding out, we have

\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\big)^{\ell}-\ell!\sum_{1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}\leqslant n}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}

is the sum
\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}
in which there is at least one repeated index. The contribution from (for example)
i_{\ell-1}=i_{\ell}
is

\leqslant(\max x_{i})\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell-1}}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell-1}}=(\max x_{i})\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\big)^{\ell-1}.

Summing over all possibilities for the position of the repeated indices gives the result.∎

Lemma D.2
.

Let
c\in(0,1]
. Suppose that
x,y\geqslant 1
and
M\geqslant 0
are real numbers with
y\geqslant x+x^{c}-M
. Then
y\geqslant x+\frac{1}{2}y^{c}-M
.

Proof.

First suppose that
x<M
; in this case
y\geqslant y^{c}\geqslant x+y^{c}-M
trivially. Now suppose that
x\geqslant M
. We have
2^{1/c}x\geqslant 2x>x+x^{c}-M
. The RHS is non-negative, so we may raise to the power
c
giving
x^{c}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(x+x^{c}-M)^{c}
and hence
(x+x^{c}-M)-\frac{1}{2}(x+x^{c}-M)^{c}\geqslant x-M
. The result now follows from the assumption and the fact that
y-\frac{1}{2}y^{c}
is an increasing function of
y
for
y\geqslant 1
.∎

Lemma D.3
.

Let
c\in(0,1]
. Suppose that
x,y\geqslant 1
and
M\geqslant 0
are real numbers with
y\leqslant x-x^{c}+M
. Then
y\leqslant x-\frac{1}{2}y^{c}+2M
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
x\leqslant M
. Then
y+\frac{1}{2}y^{c}\leqslant\tfrac{3}{2}y\leqslant\tfrac{3}{2}(x+M)\leqslant x+2M
. Alternatively, suppose
x\geqslant M
. Then

\displaystyle y+\tfrac{1}{2}y^{c}\leqslant(x-x^{c}+M)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}(x-x^{c}+M)^{c}

\displaystyle\leqslant(x-x^{c}+M)+\tfrac{1}{2}(x+M)^{c}\leqslant(x-x^{c}+M)+x^{c}=x+M,

where we used
2^{1/c}\geqslant 2
and
x\geqslant M
in the penultimate step.
∎

Appendix E
Bounded Lipschitz distance

If
\psi\in C(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is a function, define

\operatorname{Lip}(\psi):=\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{|\psi(x)-\psi(y)|}{\|x-y\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}}.

If this supremum is finite, we say that
\psi
is Lipschitz. In this case we define the Lipschitz norm of
\psi
by

\|\psi\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}:=\|\psi\|_{\infty}+\operatorname{Lip}(\psi),

where
\|t\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}
denotes distance to the nearest integer. This norm is clearly translation-invariant.

Suppose that
\mu
is a Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. In our paper
\mu
will always be positive (meaning
\mu(E)\geqslant 0
for any measurable set
E
). Given two such measures
\mu,\nu
we define

{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu;\nu]}:=\sup_{\|\psi\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant 1}\big|\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu-\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu\big|.

This is the
bounded Lipschitz
distance on the space
\mathcal{M}^{+}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
of positive Borel measures. That is satisfies symmetry and the triangle inequality is clear. That
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu;\nu]}=0
only when
\mu=\nu
follows from the fact that the characteristic function
1_{A}
of any closed set
A\subset\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
may be approximated monotonically by Lipschitz functions
f_{n}(x):=\max(1-n\operatorname{dist}(x,A),0)
, so by the monotone convergence theorem we have, if
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu;\nu]}=0
, that
\mu(A)=\nu(A)
for all
A
.

Lemma E.1
.

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is complete with respect to the bounded Lipschitz distance.

Proof.

We use the Riesz representation theorem, namely that
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
may be identified with the positive linear functionals on
\Lambda:C(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\Lambda(1)=1
.
Suppose that
(\mu_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of probability measures which is Cauchy in the bounded Lipschitz distance. By the Riesz theorem and Alaoglu’s theorem that the unit ball of
C(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{*}
is sequentially compact, there is some probability measure
\mu
such that
\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}\rightarrow\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu
along a subsequence of the
\mu_{n}
, for all
f\in C(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
. By the Cauchy property it then follows that
\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}\rightarrow\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu
for all Lipschitz
f
. We need this convergence to hold uniformly for all
f
in the unit ball
\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant 1
. This follows from the compactness of the latter in the uniform topology, which is an instance of the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem.∎

Now we prove that the space of positive (but not necessarily probability-) measures is complete in the bounded Lipschitz metric. Suppose that
(\mu_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence. The sequence
\int 1\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}=\mu_{n}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is then Cauchy and so tends to some limit
\ell
. If
\ell=0
, the result is trivial; we have
\mu_{n}\rightarrow 0
. Set
\ell_{n}:=\mu_{n}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
. Consider (after discarding any small values of
n
for which
\ell_{n}=0
) the probability measures
\mu^{\prime}_{n}:=\frac{1}{\ell_{n}}\mu_{n}
.
If
\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant 1
then

\displaystyle\Big|\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}_{n}-\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}_{m}\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\ell_{n}}\Big|\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}-\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{m}\Big|+\Big|\frac{1}{\ell_{n}}-\frac{1}{\ell_{m}}\Big|\Big|\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{m}\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\ell_{n}}{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu_{n};\mu_{m}]}+\Big|\frac{1}{\ell_{n}}-\frac{1}{\ell_{m}}\Big|\ell_{m}\rightarrow 0

as
m,n\rightarrow\infty
. Thus the
\mu^{\prime}_{n}
are a Cauchy sequence and so by
Lemma

E.1
there is a probability measure
\mu^{\prime}
such that
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{\prime}_{n};\mu^{\prime}]}\rightarrow 0
. We claim that
{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu_{n};\ell\mu^{\prime}]}\rightarrow 0
. This is from the fact that if
\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant 1
then

\displaystyle\Big|\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}-\ell\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\ell_{n}\Big|\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}_{n}-\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}\Big|+|\ell_{n}-\ell|\Big|\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\ell_{n}{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu^{\prime}_{n};\mu^{\prime}]}+|\ell_{n}-\ell|\rightarrow 0.

This completes the proof.

Convolutions.
Recall that if
f\in C(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
and
\mu
is a measure then
f\ast\mu(x):=\int f(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
.

Lemma E.2
.

Let
f
be a Lipschitz function on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. Then

\|f\ast(\mu-\nu)\|_{\infty}\leqslant{\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu;\nu]}\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}.

Proof.

This is essentially immediate from the definition and the fact that
f\ast(\mu-\nu)(x)=\int f_{x}\,\mathrm{d}\mu-\int f_{x}\,\mathrm{d}\nu
where
f_{x}(y):=f(x-y)
has the same Lipschitz norm as
f
.
∎

We conclude with the following lemma. Here,
\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}:={\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{BL}}[\mu;0]}
.

Lemma E.3
.

Suppose that
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
and that
\mu
is a positive Borel measure with
\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}<\infty
. Then
f\ast\mu\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
and

(E.1)

\|f\ast\mu\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leqslant\big(\|f\|_{\infty}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\big)\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}.

Proof.

We have

(E.2)

\mu(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})=\int 1\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leqslant\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}<\infty.

One may check that
f\ast\mu
is differentiable with derivative
(f\ast\mu)^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\ast\mu
by using

\int\big|f(x-y+h)-f(x-y)-hf^{\prime}(x-y)\big|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\ll|h|^{2}\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\mu(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}).

The fact that
f\ast\mu\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
then follows by induction.

For
Equation

E.1
we first observe that using
Equation

E.2
we have
\|f\ast\mu\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|f\|_{\infty}\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}
.
Furthermore, we have

\operatorname{Lip}(f\ast\mu)\leqslant\|(f\ast\mu)^{\prime}\|_{\infty}=\|f^{\prime}\ast\mu\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|\mu\|_{\operatorname{BL}}.

Putting these bounds together gives the result.
∎

Appendix F
Large deviation bounds

In this appendix we collect various large deviation bounds of a standard type.

Lemma F.1
.

Let
X\overset{d}{=}\operatorname{Bi}(n,p)
be a binomial random variable, and let
\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{3}{2}
. Then

\mathbb{P}\big(|X-\mathbb{E}X|\geqslant\varepsilon\mathbb{E}X\big)\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{E}X}.

Proof.

This is
[
29
, (2.9)]
.
∎

Lemma F.2
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent random variables such that
|X_{i}|\leqslant H
for all
i
and some
H\in(0,\infty)
. Set
X:=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n}
. Then for all
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(|X-\mathbb{E}X|\geqslant\lambda\big)\leqslant 2e^{-\lambda^{2}/(2H^{2}n)}.

Proof.

This is Hoeffding’s inequality; see for instance
[
8
, Theorem 2.8]
.∎

Now let
\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
be random walks with
\operatorname{Pois}(1)-1
and
1-\operatorname{Pois}(1)
increments respectively.

Lemma F.3
.

For
n
sufficiently large and
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
we have
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)\geqslant i)\ll e^{-i^{2}/4n}
and similarly for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is a very standard type of large deviation bound. The second estimate in
[
4
, Theorem A.1.15]
gives that

(F.1)

\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)\geqslant i)\leqslant e^{-n\phi(i/n)}

where
\phi(x):=(1+x)\log(1+x)-x
. The particular statement given here then follows using
\phi(x)\geqslant x^{2}/4
, valid for
x\in(0,1)
.

For
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
we have a similar statement to
Equation

F.1
but with
\tilde{\phi}(x):=x^{2}/2
, so in this case the result follows straight away.
∎

Corollary F.4
.

With probability
\geqslant 1-N^{-10}
,
|\bm{\beta}(j_{2})-\bm{\beta}(j_{1})|\leqslant N^{1/2-\eta}
whenever
j_{1},j_{2}\in[N/8,N]
and
|j_{2}-j_{1}|\leqslant N^{1-5\eta/2}
, and similarly for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality,
j_{2}>j_{1}
.
By
Lemma

F.3
applied with
n=j_{2}-j_{1}
, for each fixed
j_{1},j_{2}
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(|\bm{\beta}(j_{2})-\bm{\beta}(j_{1})|\geqslant N^{1/2-\eta}\big)\ll e^{-N^{\eta/2}/4}\ll N^{-20}.

Summing over the
O(N^{2})
choices of
j_{1},j_{2}
gives the result.
∎

The following slight variant of the large deviation bound was required in the proof of
Lemma

8.15
.

Lemma F.5
.

We have
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)=i)\leqslant e^{-i/10}
uniformly for
i>n/2
, and similarly for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We can again use
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\beta}(n)\geqslant i)\leqslant e^{-n\phi(i/n)}
, but now use the inequality
\phi(x)\geqslant x/6
, valid for
x\geqslant 1/2
. The inequality holds by a massive margin. A similar proof works for
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
.
∎
